PREFACE 

The many changes in the Civil Practice Act and allied Acts since 1934 have made necessary, especially for bar review purposes, new material on the subject of Pleading and Practice. The object of the present sixth revised edition of this outline is to furnish a comprehensive, but condensed, treatment of the subject so as to make resort to outside sources unnecessary. Those important amendments and additions which became effective after this book went to press, or which will become effective September 1, 1938, will be found in the

Appendix. 

The author's experience in conducting bar review courses, has made it sufficiently apparent to him that most students, in preparing for school and bar examinations in a statutory subject such as Pleading and Practice, content themselves with abstract generalizations without a sufficient background of case interpretations. The "outline" form of presentation has therefore been used in order, more conveniently, to permit the tying up of illustrative, factual situations, found in leading cases, with the pertinent sections of the Act. 

Brooklyn, N. Y. 

April 15, 1938.

I. W. B.

To: M. M. B.

CHAPTER I. 

COURTS AND THEIR JURISDICTION 

1. Introduction: The American colonists brought with them the two systems of procedure then obtaining in England; viz., the common law system and the chancery (or equity) system.

A. Common Law Procedure: The most ancient of the two, common law procedure, was essentially a system of writs. If A wished to sue B, he went to the "Registra

Brevium," the King's chancery office, for that purpose, paid a fee and obtained a writ fitting his particular form of action. This writ commanded the sheriff to summon 

B "by good summoners" to appear in court to answer for his default in satisfying A's claim. If A did not present a claim in conformance with one of the established writs, such as covenant, debt, detinue, replevin, ejectment or trespass, none would issue. In 1285, the statute of Westminster II was passed. This statute provided that if the clerks in the chancery office, where the common law writs were obtained, could not find a particular writ to fit a particular type of case presented, they "shall agree in forming a new one, and if they cannot agree, it shall be adjourned to the next Parliament where a writ shall be framed by the consent of the Learned in the Law, lest it happen in the future that the court of our Lord, the King, is deficient in doing justice to the suitors." The effect of the statute was to increase greatly the common law writs, but the utter rigidity of the system still remained.

B. Chancery Procedure: It was only in the nature of things that this inflexible adherence to form and precedent should fail to give satisfaction. Aggrieved suitors who could find no form of action to fit their case would petition the King, as the "fountain head of all justice," to give them relief as a matter of grace. Invariably, these petitions were referred by His Majesty to his Chancellor, an ecclesiastic charged with the keeping of the King's conscience. Relief was granted only in such cases where the common law courts could give no redress or inade-

quate redress, notably in such cases as specific performance, rescission, cancellation, reformation, re-execution, foreclosure, injunction, accounting, bills of discovery and quia timet, writs of ne exeat, etc. There were no common law writs for these. Gradually, the respective jurisdictions of the common law courts and the chancery or equity courts became more and more settled. 

C. Statutory Changes in New York: After the American 

Revolution, there was no English King in America and it soon became patent that the English systems of procedure, founded as they were on royal prerogatives, were incompatible with a republican form of government. New 

York was the first State in the Union to make a change. 

In 1848 and 1849, pursuant to a provision in the State Constitution of 1846, a commission of three, headed by David Dudley Field, reported to the Legislature a statutory "Code of Procedure" which was subsequently made law. The Code was enlarged in 1877. In 1920, The 

Code of Procedure was replaced by the present Civil 

Practice Act and allied acts; viz., City Court Act, Surro gate's Court Act, Court of Claims Act and the Justice's 

Court Act. In June 1921, the Rules of Civil Practice, supplementing the Civil Practice Act, were adopted by a joint judiciary and bar convention. Sec. 82 of the Judiciary Law gives a majority of the justices of the Appellate Division on order of the four presiding justices of the four judicial departments of the state, the power to adopt, amend or rescind any of the Rules. (1) The "Rules of Civil Practice" are not Statutes. 

Practice is regulated by the following in the order named: (a) Provisions of the State Constitution, 

(b) Statutory provisions of the Civil Practice Act, Judiciary Law, Surrogate's Court Act, City 

Court Act, Mun. Ct. Code, etc., (c) Rules of Civil Practice (and in the case of the 

Court of Appeals, rules of that court), (d) Rules of the various courts.

From this it is apparent that where there is a conflict between a provision of the Civil Practice Act,

or a provision of the City Court Act, etc., and a provision of the State Constitution the latter must govern. American Historical Society v. Glenn, 248

N. Y. 445. By the same token, if there is a conflict between a Rule of Civil Practice and a provision of the Civil Practice Act the latter will govern. Winter v. Winter, 256 N. Y. 113 (1931).

In the case last cited the Court of Appeals considered a conflict between R.C.P. 51 and C.P.A. 

233. The last sentence of the rule then provided that "service without the state in lieu of publication is complete 10 days after proof thereof is filed." 

The court held that since the legislature made personal service without the state the full equivalent of publication, the convention which adopted the rules of practice had no constitutional right to provide that before such service should be deemed complete, there should be another prerequisite, to wit, the expiration of 10 days after personal service. Therefore, in cases of service under C.P.A. 

233, in lieu of publication, service was held complete upon delivery of a copy of the summons, complaint and notice to defendant without the state; defendant's time to answer running from said point. Moreover, by analogy, in cases of substituted service under C.P.A. 231, it seemed that service was complete either upon delivery to a person of proper age or upon affixing, etc. and depositing in the post office, in spite of the fact that R.C.P. 49 then provided for the additional requisite that the original order and the papers upon which it was granted, together with proof of service in compliance with the order, be first filed with the clerk before service should be deemed complete. Since September 1, 1935 amendments to the Civil Practice Act, as we shall see when we discuss "Service," have cleared up this anomalous situation. 

There are other instances of obvious conflict between statutory provisions and Rules of Civil Practice, to which neither judicial nor legislative atten-

tion has yet been directed, but with which we are not now concerned, other than in knowing the general axiom that the repugnant Rule should yield to the statute.

D. Distinctions Between Equity Suits and Law Actions Abolished: 

There is now only one form of civil action.

Both under the Civil Practice Act (Sec. 8) and under the old Code which it replaced, the distinctions between actions at law and suits in equity and the forms of these actions and suits have been abolished. This does not mean that equitable principles have been abolished. It means only that there is no longer any separate procedure for a suit in equity than in an action at law. Under the present system, all the rights of the parties whether legal or equitable, may be adjudicated, wherever practicable, in one action. Hahl v. Sugo, 169 N. Y. 109. In the pages that follow, our chief concern will be with the Civil 

Practice Act and the Rules of Civil Practice. Wherever deemed necessary, pertinent provisions of the City Court Act and Municipal Court Code will be noted, but it should be remembered, at the outset, that unless a contrary intent is expressed, or plainly implied from the context of the acts governing procedure in those courts, the provisions of law, or of rules, applicable to practice or procedure in the Supreme Court, apply as well to actions in the City or Municipal Court and to proceedings in the Surrogate's Court. Mun. Ct. Code Sec. 15; City

Ct. Act Sec. 80; Surrog. Ct. Act Sec. 316. 

2. Courts and Their Jurisdiction: 

A. Constitutional and Legislative Courts: (1) Constitutional courts are those established or continued by the State Constitution (the one now in force was adopted in 1894) e.g.: Court for the Trial of Impeachments (a criminal court), the Court of Appeals, the Supreme Court, the County Courts. (established in 1691 as Courts of Common Pleas), the Surrogate's Court, Courts of Justices of the 

Peace, and certain City courts. 

(2) Legislative Courts: Under our State Constitution

(Art. VI, Sec. 18) as amended January 1, 1895, the 

Legislature may establish new, inferior, local courts of civil and criminal jurisdiction subject to the following important limitations: (a) such court must be a local court (i.e., a court established for and within one of the recognized territorial divisions of the state as distinguished from a court established for the entire state. 

Peo. ex rel Townsend v. Porter, 90 N. Y. 68), (b) it cannot be a court of record, (c) it cannot have equitable jurisdiction, (d) it cannot have any greater jurisdiction in other respects than is conferred upon the County 

Courts by the Constitution. 

(e) By a further amendment to Art. VI, Sec. 18, in effect January 1, 1926, the territorial jurisdiction in civil cases of any inferior or local court in a city, may be made by the Legislature to extend throughout the county or counties in which the city may be located. See Rochester Exposition 

Assn. v. Bogorad, 267 N. Y. S. 723. 

B. Courts of Record: Courts are either courts of record or not of record. A court of record is one the records of whose proceedings are permanent and unquestionable. 

Hutkoff v. Demorest, 103 N. Y. 377. Section 2 of the 

Judiciary Law (as amended Mar. 3, 1938) provides which courts are of record. The following are courts of record: the Court for the Trial of Impeachments, the Court of Appeals, the Appellate Division in each department, the Supreme Court, the Court of Claims, the County Courts, the Surrogate's Court, the Court of General Sessions of New York County, the City Court and the Municipal 

Court of New York City, the City Courts of Albany, Mt.

Vernon, Schenectady, Troy, Utica and Yonkers and the Municipal Court of Syracuse. C.P.A. 63 defines the general powers of courts of record. 

C. Original and Appellate Jurisdiction: Courts have either original or appellate jurisdiction or both. (1) Original: A court of original jurisdiction is one in which an action may be commenced, e.g. the Su-

preme Court.

In a court of original jurisdiction, witnesses are heard and a stenographic record made. (2) Appellate: A court of appellate jurisdiction is one which merely reviews determinations of courts of original jurisdiction and cannot entertain an action at its inception, e.g. the Court of Appeals. An appellate court makes its decisions on the "record" of the trial court and upon briefs and arguments submitted by opposing counsel. It rarely, if ever, hears testimony. (a) The Appellate Division of the Supreme Court has original, or quasi-original, jurisdiction in some matters, such as the determination of real controversies upon submission of agreed statements of fact (C.P.A. 546-548) and the regulation and control of attorneys, who are considered officers of the court. Judiciary Law, Sec. 88. Peo. ex rel Karlin v. Culkin, 248 N. Y. 465.

D. Limited and General Jurisdiction: Courts have either limited or general jurisdiction. (1) Limited: A court of limited jurisdiction can entertain only such actions as the law creating it gives it power to adjudicate, and the facts necessary to give it the jurisdiction which it exercises must appear in the record of its proceedings. In Thomas. v. Harmon, 122 N. Y. 84, it was held that the statutory power of a county court (a court of limited, original and appellate jurisdiction) to foreclose a mortgage, did not vest that court with authority to reform such mortgage. Again, the Municipal Court would have no jurisdiction of a matrimonial or partition action because the Mun. Ct. Code does not give it such jurisdiction; nor may the parties confer it by mutual consent. 

(2) General: A court of general jurisdiction has power vested in it to hear and determine actions of every sort, irrespective of the nature of the case, character of the parties, or amount involved. New York's only court having general, original jurisdiction is

the Supreme Court. C.P.A. 64; Mussen v. Ausable Granite Works, 63 Hun. 367. The Supreme Court, naturally, has no jurisdiction over matters which Congress has given exclusively to the jurisdiction of the Federal Courts, for example, an action for damages for infringement of patent. Shea v. Ex port S. S. Corp., 253 N. Y. 17. But the Supreme

Court would have jurisdiction of an action for damages for unfair competition, where P alleges that D, illegally claiming patent rights to an article marketed by P, was threatening P's customers with damage suits in the event that they continued to buy from P, even though in such action the legality or illegality of the patent under which D claimed. would be a matter for incidental adjudication by the Supreme Court. Zenie v. Miskind, 284 N. Y. S. 

63. Would the Supreme Court have jurisdiction to try a case exclusively involving ambassadors, consuls or public ministers, or an admiralty case or one in which a State is a party, or one exclusively involving interpretation of the U. S. Constitution, or federal laws or treaties? The answer is No, because as to such actions the federal courts have exclusive jurisdiction. U. S. Const. Art. III, Sec. 2, subd. 1 

and 2. Patterson v. Seneca Nation of Indians, 245 

N. Y. 433. 

3. Civil Courts of Record and Their Jurisdiction. 

A. Municipal Court of the City of New York: This court is a court of record having limited, original jurisdiction defined by Sec. 6 of the Mun. Ct. Code. Its process may be served and its mandates executed only within the territorial confines of the city. It has jurisdiction of the following law actions when the amount claimed in the summons does not exceed $1,000, exclusive of interest and costs: (1) On contract, express or implied, except contracts to marry (a) See C.P.A. 61a to i (infra, pg. 19) with respect to abolition of causes of action for breach of contract to marry, seduction, criminal conversation, etc. (b) The Municipal Court has no jurisdiction of an action

to recover accrued alimony (Schafer v. Schafer, 193

N. Y. S. 43) nor of an action, based upon a separation agreement under which D agreed in a certain contingency to increase P wife's allowance thereunder in lieu of alimony, the complaint not being based upon a breach of the contract, but asking the court to determine that the stated contingency had occurred. Gerken v. Gerken, 199 N. Y. S. 169. (2) To recover a fine or penalty. (3) To establish (not foreclose) a mechanic's lien on realty and to recover a personal judgment for the amount due. (4) To foreclose a lien on a chattel. (5) To recover damages for escape from county jail liberties of any of the counties of New York City. (6) To recover damages for fraud or deceit, injury to person or property (including real property) except assault, battery, malicious prosecution, false imprisonment, defamation, criminal conversation, seduction or loss of consortium (this last would not include a cause of action for loss of a wife's services because of D's negligence). (a) The court has jurisdiction of an action to recover for wrongful death. Green v. Dowling, 270 N. Y. S. 811. (b) But it has no jurisdiction of an action to recover damages for wrongful attachment, since such an action cannot be classified as one for injury to property, but is for malicious prosecution over which the court has no jurisdiction. Barsotti v. Peirano, 157 N. Y. S. 844. 

(7) For an accounting between partners after the termination of the partnership relationship, up to $1,000 plus interest and costs. (a) This portion of Sec. 6 has been held unconstitutional as seeking to confer equitable powers upon an inferior local court in violation of Art. VI, Sec. 18 of the State 

Constitution. Schmitt v. Querengaesser, 158 N. Y. S. 

575. See also Rosenthal v. Garber, 202 N. Y. S. 703; 

Menitto v. Iacovacci, 206 N. Y. S. 589. (b) But if a balance has already been struck between the partners and an account stated, the Municipal court would have jurisdiction of an action to recover the sum found due. (c) Tenants in common are not partners and if one sues another to recover his share of the estate rents, the

Municipal Court has jurisdiction. O'Connell v. Kelly, 

220 N. Y. S. 161. (8) To recover a chattel or chattels of an aggregate value not exceeding $1,000, with or without damages for the wrongful taking or detention thereof. (9) To recover, by summary proceedings, possession of real property (C.P.A. 1410 et seq). The court in such case may render judgment for any amount for arrears in rent even though it exceeds $1,000. Byrne v. Padden, 248 N. Y. 243. (10) Equitable Jurisdiction: The court may not give general, affirmative, equitable relief, such as reformation, rescission, compelling arbitration, etc., but may give effect to equitable defenses. Kulerban 

Holding Corp. v. Blauner, 190 N. Y. S. 484, (defense of fraud in action for rent, under lease, given effect); 

DeVita v. Pianisani, 217 N. Y. S. 438 (defense, in summary proceedings to dispossess, that petitioner was not in fact the landlord but really a mortgagee holding under a deed intended as a mortgage, given effect); Alumor Garage, Inc. v. Stivers, Inc., 218 

N. Y. S. 683 (equitable defense to a counterclaim for conversion given effect). But see Simon v. 

Schmitt, 137 A. D. 625; see also Epstein v. Kaplan, 269 N. Y. S. 604, where in an action for money loaned it was held the court had jurisdiction to determine equitable objections to a defense of release. (11) Counterclaims: The court may render judgment in favor of a defendant upon a proper counterclaim for any sum not exceeding $1,000 plus interest and costs. Mun. Ct. Code Sec. 86. If in any action D interposes a counterclaim in excess of the jurisdictional amount, the Municipal Court may render affirmative judgment in his favor on the counterclaim up to the jurisdictional limit of the court; as to the excess, because of the wording of Sec. 86, and because of the rule against splitting indivisible causes of action, D runs the risk of the Municipal Court judgment constituting a bar to further action. Silberstein v. Begun, 232 N. Y. 319. 

(a) In Summary Proceedings to Dispossess: Sec. 86 above

referred to, does not apply to special proceedings, such as summary proceedings to dispossess. It has been held, therefore, that in such proceedings, the court may grant affirmative judgment on a properly interposed counterclaim even in excess of $1,000 plus interest and costs. 

Harfried Realty Co. v. Amusement Corp., 270 N. Y. 

S. 692; Broadway-94th Street Realty Co. v. C. Y. L. 

Lunch Co., 196 N. Y. S. 549. But see Peerless Candy 

Co. v. Halbreich, 211 N. Y. S. 676, contra, (App. Term,

Second Dept.). 

(12) Small Claims Part of the New York City Municipal Court: Title XI (Secs. 179 to 187) of the Mun. Ct. 

Code, in effect September 1, 1934, has for its purpose the establishment of a simple, informal and prompt procedure for the determination of claims. within the jurisdiction of the court (except summary proceedings to recover real property) where the sum involved, or the value of the property involved, does not exceed $50.00, exclusive of interest and costs. No corporation, partnership, association, or assignee may sue in the Small Claims Part. (a) Procedure: The claimant pays a filing fee of $1.25 to the clerk of the court (no summons or complaint need be served unless the court so orders) and states his cause of action to the clerk who reduces the same to a concise, written form and records it in a docket. Notice of the claim, plus notice of hearing, is then sent to the person complained against, by registered mail, return receipt requested. Hearings are conducted informally with a view to substantial justice. The court or referee is not bound by statutory rules of practice, procedure, pleading or evidence except statutory provisions relating to privileged communications and relating to transactions with a deceased or an incompetent (C.P.A. 347). 

Sec. 184 makes provision for trial by jury in certain cases. Sec. 185 provides that claimant shall be deemed to have waived his right to appeal except that either party may appeal (to the Appellate Term) on the sole ground that substantial justice has not been done between the parties according to substantive law principles. Sec. 186 provides that a "small claim” judgment may be pleaded as res adjudicata only as to the amount involved in the particular action and shall not otherwise be deemed an adjudication of any fact at issue, or found therein, in any other action or court. 

(13) Joinder of Plaintiffs-Amount Demanded In Sum-

mons: Several Ps join as plaintiffs in one action, under C.P.A. 209, separately stating and numbering their causes of action, individually claiming within the jurisdictional limit, but in the aggregate, demanding in excess thereof. Has the Municipal 

Court jurisdiction? In the Second Department, Dilworth v. Yellow Taxi Co., 221 N. Y. S. 813, held in the negative. In the First Department, Sirop v. 

Bernard Greenwood Co., 236 N. Y. S. 588, following the City Court rule in such case (Spetler v. Jogel 

Realty Co., 231 N. Y. S. 517) held in the affirmative. To the same effect see Murtagh v. Keystone 

Transportation Co. Inc., 269 N. Y. S. 903. The case of Merten v. Queen Rental Corp., 271 N. Y. S. 271, in overruling the Dilworth case now reconciles the divergence of opinion in the two departments and makes the rule in the Spetler case applicable to 

Municipal court actions. (a) But the court does not have jurisdiction where one P asks for less than $1,000 against each of two Ds by separate causes of action where the aggregate exceeds $1,000 plus interest and costs. Fader v. Silverman, 267 

N. Y. S. 782. 

(14) Appeals: Appeals from the Municipal Court are. 

taken to an Appellate Term of the Supreme Court which is composed of three justices of the Supreme Court.

B. City Court of City of New York: This court is a court of record having limited, original jurisdiction. Its process and mandates (i. e. executions, subpoenas, orders, etc.) may be served and executed only within the limits of New York City. American Historical Society v. 

Glenn, 248 N. Y. 445 (declaring unconstitutional a provision of the City Ct. Act that all process and mandates of the court could be executed in any part of the State insofar as such provision purported to apply to money judgment actions). The City Court exists in each of the five counties of the City of New York, and has taken over the civil jurisdiction of County Courts in each of these counties. It has jurisdiction of the following actions:

(1) To recover a sum of money where the judgment demanded in the complaint does not exceed $3,000 plus interest and costs. (a) The test of jurisdiction is the amount demanded in the complaint. Hence, where the complaint is served with the summons and the amount demanded in the complaint exceeds the limitation, there is no jurisdiction in the court to permit an amendment reducing the amount within the jurisdictional limit; but where the summons is served without any complaint and contains no statement of the amount sued for, the court acquires jurisdiction and may thereafter amend the complaint so as to come within the jurisdictional limit of the court. 

Dobrikin v. Union Ry. Co., 225 N. Y. S. 376. See also 

Connelly v. Oceanic Navigation Co., 257 N. Y. S. 735. (b) Moreover, where several Ps unite in one complaint (C.P.A. 209), each demanding judgment not exceeding $3,000, plus interest and costs on his separate cause of action, separately stated and numbered, the City Court has jurisdiction even though the aggregate amount is in excess of the jurisdictional amount. Dobrikin v. 

Union Ry. Co., supra; Spetler v. Jogel Realty Co., 231 

N. Y. S. 517. (c) But where one P sets out in his complaint against one D several causes of action each asking for less than the jurisdiction amount, but the aggregate amount exceeding that sum, the City Court does not acquire jurisdiction. City Trust Co. v. Ricci Realty Co., 241 N. Y. S. 

481. Marcus v. Bader, 282 N. Y. S. 503. (d) If the causes of action in the case above were each against different Ds, (properly joined) the rule, it seems, would be different. Curtain Mfg. Co. v. Law Union 

Co., 248 N. Y. S. 488 (consolidation of actions held proper). Cf. Fader v. Silverman, 267 N. Y. S. 782. (2) To recover or replevy one or more chattels having an aggregate value not in excess of $3,000, and interest, with or without damages for the wrongful taking or detention thereof. (a) If the complaint asks for damages for the detention of the property in an amount exceeding $3,000 and interest, the court nevertheless, retains jurisdiction if, in fact, the value of the property is within the jurisdictional limit. "Replevin is a possessory action. Its object is the recovery of the property. The alternative relief of the fixation of its value and also the recovery of damages for its detention are but incidents to the primary purpose of the suit, the right to possession. The statute,

while making the amount demanded in the complaint a test of the right to institute the action, did not confine the right to cases coming within such test, but made the further provision that the right to institute actions to recover a chattel or of replevin shall be governed by the value of the chattel." Lippe, J., in Scharfman v. 

May Claire Costume Co., 232 N. Y. S. 516. (3) For a marine cause of action as defined by Sec. 17, City Ct. Act (unlimited jurisdiction). (4) To recover upon a bond or undertaking given in an action or special proceeding in the City Court (unlimited jurisdiction). (5) To foreclose a mechanic's lien, or lien on personal property, where the amount sought to be recovered does not exceed $3,000 plus interest and costs. (a) In an action to foreclose a mechanic's lien on real property, it is immaterial that the realty value exceeds $3,000 as long as the lien does not exceed $3,000. Kaplan v. 

Antonelli, 132 Misc. 572. (6) To recover under any special code of ordinances affecting the area leased by New York City to New 

York World's Fair 1939 Inc. 

(7) Counterclaims: On a proper counterclaim interposed in an action to recover a sum of money only the City Court has jurisdiction to render judgment for any sum. New York City Court Act, Sec. 18. 

Schreiber v. Noe, 243 N. Y. S. 360. 

(8) Equitable Jurisdiction: The equitable jurisdiction of the court is confined to actions to foreclose mechanics' liens and liens on chattels, where the amount sought to be recovered does not exceed $3,000 plus interest and costs. Smith v. Solomon, 172 N. Y. S. 

515. The court may dispose of equitable defenses but cannot grant affirmative relief on a purely equitable counterclaim. Oppenheimer v. Trebla Realty 

Co., 139 N. Y. S. 894; Schreiber v. Noe, 243 N. Y. S. 

360. The court has no jurisdiction in an action for a partnership accounting. Laskey v. Coverdale, 145 

N. Y. S. 894. (9) Appeals: Appeals from the City Court are taken to an Appellate Term of the Supreme Court. 

C. County Courts: These courts are courts of record hav-

ing original and limited jurisdiction, and also appellate jurisdiction (as to appeals from courts of Justices of the Peace). There is a County Court in every county but 

New York county. 

(1) In New York City: In the counties of New York 

City, except New York county, the County Courts have criminal jurisdiction only. In New York county, the Court of General Sessions of the Peace exercises the criminal jurisdiction of the County 

Court. Jud. Law Sec. 2; C.P.A. 67; State Const.

Art. VI, Sec. 11. 

(2) Civil Jurisdiction Outside New York City: 

The County Courts, outside New York City, have civil jurisdiction over law actions to recover a sum of money. Before such jurisdiction attaches the following (a and b) must appear from the allegations of the complaint: (a) The amount demanded by way of judgment must not exceed $3,000 and costs (except in actions on county court judgments). (1) If interest is demanded in addition the court gets no jurisdiction. Manson v. Nostrand, 170 N. Y. S. 285. (2) Where the summons is served with a complaint that demands judgment in excess of the jurisdictional limit, even though such excess be interest only, the court acquires no jurisdiction to permit an amendment. Teall v. Roeser, 201 N. Y. S. 241. (b) All the defendants must be residents of the county when the action is commenced, or defendant, if a non-resident of the county (e. g. a foreign corporation) must have an office for the transaction of business within the county, in which latter case, the cause of action must also arise therein. 

(1) The jurisdictional facts as to residence etc. must be alleged in the complaint. Peck v. Dickey, 24 

N. Y. S. 834. (2) But defendant, if a non-resident or a foreign corporation having no place of business within the county, may, nevertheless, waive the court's lack of jurisdiction over his or its person by appearing generally in the action. Lindeman v. Wolf, 254 

N. Y. S. 274; Meyers v. American Locomotive Co., 201 N. Y. 163 (as to former rule denying jurisdiction in the County Court over a foreign corpora-

tion, see Wachtel v. Diamond, 213 N. Y. S. 77). (c) Replevin Actions: The county court has jurisdiction of actions to recover chattels, with or without damages, where the aggregate value of the chattels does not exceed $3,000. The defendant in such case need not be a resident of the county. 

(d) Equitable Actions: The court has jurisdiction of the following actions relating to real property within the county: partition, for dower, foreclosure of, redemption from, satisfaction of, a mortgage; to foreclose an equitable lien arising from a contract to sell real estate, to compel specific performance of such a contract, and to foreclose a mechanic's lien upon real estate. 

It has also jurisdiction of an action to foreclose a lien not exceeding $3,000 upon chattels within the county. C.P.A. Sec. 67. (1) In an action to foreclose a mortgage the county court may enter deficiency judgment for any sum found due. Hawley v. Whalen, 19 N. Y. S. 521. 

(e) Special Proceedings: The county court has con current jurisdiction with the Supreme Court over the care of the person and property of resident infants or incompetents. C.P.A. 67 subd. 4. Summary proceedings to recover possession of real property may be maintained in the county court. C.P.A. 1413. 

(f) Counterclaims: The court has jurisdiction to adjudicate a properly interposed counterclaim, irrespective of the amount demanded therein. 

Howard Iron Works v. Buffalo Elevator Co., 

176 N. Y. 1. But if for more than $3,000 and costs, the Supreme Court, on application of either party, may remove the cause to itself. 

C.P.A. 67 subd. 3. This right may be waived. 

Brinks Express Co. Inc. v. Burns, 245 N. Y. S. 649. 

(g) Service of Process: Process of the County Court may be served throughout the state (C.P.A. 72) except that a domestic corporation or joint

stock association having a place of business within the county (so as to be deemed a resident thereof) must be served within the county. 

C.P.A. 68. The County Court, having properly acquired jurisdiction, may render any judgment or grant either party any relief which the Supreme Court might render or grant in a like case and may enforce its mandates in like manner as the Supreme Court. C.P.A. 69. (h) Appeals: Appeals are taken to the Appellate

Division of the Supreme Court. C.P.A. 622. 

D. Supreme Court: This court is the highest court of the state having general and original jurisdiction. Its branches, the Appellate Term and Appellate Division, have appellate jurisdiction. (1) Jurisdiction: It has unlimited jurisdiction of any legal or equitable action, irrespective of the amount involved. Should the legislature create new litigable causes of action, the jurisdiction of the Supreme 

Court would attach. (a) However, statutory provisions (C.P.A. 1474) make the prosecution of small claims unprofitable in the Supreme Court. (1′) Thus, if A sues in the Supreme Court in any coun. ty of New York City serving the defendant in New

York City, when he might have brought the action in an inferior court (City or Municipal 

Courts), and his recovery is less than $2,000, he recovers no costs or disbursements. C.P.A. 1474. (2′) So, too, plaintiff gets no costs or disbursements if he recovers less than $500. in the City Court of the City of New York, where the defendant was served in said city. The action should have been brought in the Municipal Court. C.P.A. 1474. (2) Generally: A Supreme Court justice may act lawfully in any part of the state. The state is divided into nine judicial districts. The Supreme Court justices are elected by the electors of their respective judicial districts. (The first judicial district comprises the counties of New York and Bronx. The second judicial district comprises the counties of

Richmond, Kings, Queens, Nassau and Suffolk.) 

(3) Appellate Term of the Supreme Court: This court consists of three Supreme Court justices sitting to hear appeals from the New York City Court and the Municipal Court. The judges are appointed by the Appellate Division, from among the Supreme Court justices who are residents of the department. 

Concurrence of two justices is necessary to a decision. Appeals are taken to the Appellate Division of the same department. 

(4) Appellate Division of the Supreme Court: This is a distinct branch of the court created by the Consti tution (Art. VI, Sec. 2) to exercise appellate juris diction (in some cases final) in matters originating in the Trial (jury) and Special (non-jury) terms of the Supreme Court and in certain other courts. In the first and second judicial departments, the Appellate Division consists of seven justices, and in the third and fourth judicial departments, of five justices. The justices are designated by the Governor from all the justices of the Supreme Court for five year terms. The Presiding Justice of each Appellate Division, who must be a resident of the department, is also appointed by the Governor. No more than five justices of the Appellate Division sit in any case; the concurrence of three is necessary to a decision. Appeals in a proper case go to the

Court of Appeals. 

E. Court of Appeals: The jurisdiction of this court is des ignated and created by law exclusively. (1) Jurisdiction: It has no original jurisdiction and in civil cases, it is limited to the review of questions of law, as to which it is the highest state tribunal. 

Where the appeal in a criminal case is from sentence of death the court may review both law and facts. 

Again, where the Appellate Division, in reversing or modifying a final judgment or order, makes new findings of fact and renders final judgment, on appeal therefrom the Court of Appeals may review both law and facts.

(a) But not all cases may be appealed to the Court of Appeals as a matter of right. In many cases the Appellate Division has final appellate power, since the primary function of the Court of Appeals is not "of declaring justice between man and man, but of settling the law." The Constitution (Art. VI, Sec. 7) expressly provides that the legislature may restrict the jurisdiction of the Court of Appeals and the right to appeal thereto, but the right to so appeal must not be made to depend upon the amount involved. In this connection, it should be noted that the right to appeal, in any case, is not a natural, inherent right but, with the exception of a few constitutional provisions (e.g. appeal from death sentence in a criminal case) rests upon the legislative will and may be abrogated or limited by the legislature. Scolina v. Erie Preserving Co., 151 N. Y. 50. (b) Constitutional Question Involved: Where the only question involved in an inferior court of record is a constitutional one, an appeal may be taken directly to the Court of Appeals. C.P.A. 588, subd. 3. The same is true in a criminal proceeding where the sentence is one of death. 

(2) Composition: The court consists of a Chief Justice and six associate justices, chosen by the electors of the state. In addition, the Governor may designate not more than four Supreme Court Justices to serve in the Court of Appeals whenever pressure of business before that tribunal warrants. In any case, however, no more than seven judges may sit (five justices constitute a quorum) and the concurrence of four is necessary to a decision.

F. Court of Claims: The State of New York being a sov ereignty, may not be sued in its courts, without its consent. 

(1) Jurisdiction: The Court of Claims, consisting of five judges, has power to hear and determine private claims against the state. Its jurisdiction and practice is governed by the Court of Claims Act. (2) Appeals: Appeals from this court are taken to the

Appellate Division of the Supreme Court (Third or Fourth Departments). 

G. Surrogate's Court: Its jurisdiction includes the power to administer and distribute decedent's estates, and those

of infants and incompetents, subject to the provisions of the Surrogate's Court Act. Proceedings in this court are not actions but special proceedings commenced by the filing of a petition, and the parties are brought before the court by service of a "citation." (1) Appeals: Appeals from this court are taken to an

Appellate Division of the Supreme Court. 

4. Courts Not of Record and Their Jurisdiction. 

A. Courts of Justices of the Peace: 

(1) Civil Jurisdiction: These courts have only original jurisdiction expressly conferred by statute (Justice 

Court Act, Sec. 2). (a) They have jurisdiction of actions for damages for breach of contract (except to marry) where the sum claimed does not exceed $200. Sum- mary proceedings to dispossess may be maintained before a Justice of the Peace. (b) They have no jurisdiction of such ex delicto actions as assault and battery, false imprisonment, malicious prosecution, defamation, seduction or criminal conversation, or actions involving title to real property. (2) Appeals: Appeals are taken to the County Court in the county in which the judgment was rendered. 

B. Other Inferior Courts: These courts are created by special statutes or city charters, and exist in many other cities of the state, outside of New York City. 

5. Actions Against Public Policy: As bearing upon the jurisdiction of the courts hereinbefore mentioned, C.P.A. 61a to i 

(Art. 2-A added by L. 1935 ch. 263 in effect March 29) 

should be noted. Causes of action to recover money damages for alienation of affections, criminal conversation, seduction or breach of contract to marry are abolished (61b).

Existing causes of action for the above must be commenced before May 29, 1935, and if not so commenced are abolished (61c). All actions to recover damages for a breach of a presently existing contract to marry must be commenced within 60 days after the cause of action has accrued, and if not so commenced are barred forever (61c subd. 2-3).

No act done after March 29, 1935, within this State, shall

give rise to any of the rights of action abolished. No contract to marry, thereafter made, shall give rise to any cause of action for the breach thereof (61d). After March 29, 1935 it shall be unlawful for any person (including an attorney) to file or serve or to threaten to file and serve any process or pleading in any court of this State, upon any of the outlawed causes of action, whether such cause of action arose within or without the State (61e). All contracts and instruments, executed in New York, after March 29, 1935, in payment, satisfaction or settlement of any cause of action or claim abolished, are void (except as to a negotiable instrument in the hands of a holder in due course); nor shall it be lawful to commence suit on any such contract or instrument, knowing it to be such. It shall be unlawful to induce the settlement of an outlawed claim or cause of action or to accept payment by way of satisfaction or compromise (61f). Any person who violates any of these provisions is guilty of a felony punishable by fine and imprisonment or both (61g). 

In Fearon v. Treanor, 272 N. Y. 268, the Court of Appeals, sustaining the Appellate Division, First Department, upheld the constitutionality of the abolition of the causes of action for seduction and breach of promise to marry, as a valid exercise of the legislative power to regulate the marriage relation for the public welfare. Later, in Hanfgarn v. Marks, 274 N. Y. 22, the Court of Appeals, in reversing the Appellate 

Division, Second Department, held that Art. 2A of the Civil 

Practice Act in so far as it abolishes the causes of action for alienation of affections and criminal conversation is constitutional, since both of these causes of action are founded upon the marriage relation and are subject to legislative control.

CHAPTER II. 

1. Generally: If a claim could be prosecuted at any time after its inception, no matter how remote, there would be danger of miscarriages of justice arising from the loss of evidence, loss of witnesses, failing memory of witnesses, etc. 

Besides, in the interest of peace and repose of society, it is best that disputed claims be brought to a speedy determination. Campbell v. Holt, 115 U. S. 630. Hence, the legislature has enacted time limitations within which certain actions must be brought. Parties, by written agreement, may, however, provide for a shorter period of limitation; but a party cannot in advance wholly waive the benefit of the statute. Shapely v. Abbott, 42 N. Y. 443. When the statute of limitations has run, the obligation is not paid or extinguished (in the absence of statute so providing) but the remedy, by the form of action to which the statute applies, is barred. If there is any other form of remedy not barred, the creditor may avail himself of it. Dodds v. McColgan, 241 N. Y. S. 584. 

2. Statute of Limitation as a Vested Right: The federal rule is that a debtor has no vested property right in the statute of limitations and that a debt once barred may constitutionally be revived. "No man promises to pay money with a view to being released from that obligation by lapse of time. It violates no right of his, therefore, when the legislature says time shall be no bar, though such was the law when the contract was made." Campbell v. Holt, supra. 

In New York there are conflicting dicta (Hulbert v. Clark, 128 N. Y. 295; House v. Carr, 185 N. Y. 453) but it is agreed that revival of a cause of action by the legislature, once it has been outlawed by the running of the statute is an extreme exercise of legislative power. Hopkins v. Lincoln Trust Co., 233 N. Y. 213; Robinson v. Robins Dry Dock 

& Repair Co., 238 N. Y. 271. Matter of Decker v. Powail smith Corp., 252 N. Y. 1. It should be noted, however, that the owner of the cause of action does have a property right in the statute of limitations in that no shorter period may be constitutionally imposed without giving him a reasonable

time within which to commence action on his accrued claim, if such shorter period of limitation would work to his prejudice. C.P.A. 59 makes a writing signed by the party to be charged thereby the only competent evidence to take a case out of the operation of the statute of limitations (other than payment of principal or interest). 

3. Effect of Statute of Limitations Upon Collateral: Where a principal obligation is secured by collateral, the fact that the principal indebtedness is unenforceable becaue the period of limitations has expired does not affect the right to proceed on the collateral. Hulbert v. Clark, supra, (where an action was brought to foreclose a mortgage given to secure the payment of promissory notes within twenty years from the date when the last note became due, but more than six years from such due date, HELD, the foreclosure action was not barred). See also Reynolds v. Doyle, 211 N. Y. S. 509. 

4. Periods of Limitation in Civil Actions: 

A. No Limitation 

(1) Action upon a bill or note or other evidence of debt, circulating as money. C.P.A. 58. 

(2) Action brought by the United States of America. 

U. S. v. Brown, 247 N. Y. 211.

B. Forty Years: 

(1) Action affecting real property brought by State or by some one claiming under it. C.P.A. 31.

C. Twenty Years: 

(1) Action upon a sealed instrument. C.P.A. 47. (2) Action on a judgment of a court of record within the United States of America or elsewhere. C.P.A. 44. (3) Action on a judgment of a court not of record but docketed in the County Clerk's office. C.P.A. 44. (4) Action for dower. R.P.L. 460.

D. Fifteen Years: 

(1) Action by private person to recover real property adversely held. C.P.A. 34. (a) Adverse Possession how affected by landlord-tenant relationship: Where the relation of landlord and tenant

has existed between any persons the possession of the tenant is deemed the possession of the landlord until the expiration of fifteen years after the termination of the tenancy; or, where there has been no written lease, until the expiration of fifteen years after the last payment of rent; notwithstanding that the tenant has acquired another title or has claimed to hold adversely to his landlord. But this presumption does not exist after the prescribed periods (C.P.A. 41). (2) Action to redeem from a mortgagee. C.P.A. 46.

E. Ten Years: 

(1) Actions for which no other limitation is expressly prescribed. C.P.A. 53. (a) This classification includes nearly all exclusively equitable actions (except for actual fraud). In re Dietz's 

Estate, 235 N. Y. S. 756; McKenzie v. Wappler, 213 

N. Y. S. 389. (b) Where equity and law have concurrent jurisdiction, equity will follow the statute of limitation binding on the law court. Butler v. Johnson, 111 N. Y. 204; Rundle v. Allison, 34 N. Y. 180. (c) Where one receives money for the use of another under circumstances which make it his duty to pay it over at once, no trust relationship arises, and the statute of limitations applicable to money had and received actions (six years under C.P.A. 48) applies and not C.P.A. 

53. In such case, too, no demand is necessary, the six year statute starts to run from the day of the receipt of the money. Mills v. Mills, 115 N. Y. 80. (d) Again, where P places funds with Ds, brokers, for investment, and brings an action demanding an accounting as to his principal and profits, C.P.A. 48 (six years) and not C.P.A. 53 (ten years) applies. Keyes v. Leopold, 241 N. Y. 189 rev'g 210 N. Y. S. 406, where the

Court of Appeals said: "The mere fact that this is an action for an accounting is not determinative of this question. When a legal and an equitable remedy exists as to the same subject-matter, the latter is under the control of the same statutory bar as the former. (Rundle v. Allison, 34 N. Y. 180). Nor is the fact that the defendants received this money in a fiduciary capacity and may, therefore, hold it under such a trust as the law may imply for the purpose of justice. (Mills v. 

Mills, 115, N. Y. 80.) Our construction of the complaint answers the question. It states facts on which a recovery might be had at law. That the plaintiff was ignorant of what precisely she was entitled to claim is

immaterial. There are remedies provided for such a situation. We hold that under Civil Practice Act, section 48, this action should at least have been begun within six years after 1916." (e) Remember, too, that the question of laches in equity actions is considered wholly independent of the statute of limitations. Groesbeck v. Morgan, 206 N. Y. 385; 

Harvey v. Guaranty Trust Co., 236 N. Y. S. 37.

F. Six Years (C.P.A. 48): 

(1) Action on contract, express or implied, except a judgment or sealed instrument or contract. (a) A contract is under seal even though there is only one seal provided both parties adopt it and agree that it is to serve for both signatures. Zeiss v. Wurster, 247 N. Y. S. 811. (2) Action to recover upon a statutory liability, except a penalty or forfeiture. (3) Action to recover damages for injury to person except as otherwise prescribed (e. g. except as otherwise prescribed by C.P.A. 49 in negligence actions). (4) Action to recover a chattel. (5) Action to procure a judgment (in law or in equity) on the ground of actual fraud; the cause of action, in such case, accruing on the discovery by plaintiff, or the person under whom he claims, of the facts. constituting the fraud. See Hobaica v. Byrne, 205 

N. Y. S. 7. Pitcher v. Sutton, 264 N. Y. S. 488. 

(a) What Constitutes "Discovery" of Fraud: A new cause of action for fraud does not accrue each time a plaintiff discovers new elements of fraud in a transaction or new evidence to prove such fraud. Where there is a knowledge of facts sufficient "to suggest to a person of ordinary intelligence the probability that he has been defrauded, a duty of inquiry arises," and may thus start the running of the statute. Sielcken-Schwartz v. American Factors, Ltd., 265 N. Y. 239; Coffin v. Barber, 101 

N. Y. S. 147. (6) Action to establish a will. Where the will has been lost, concealed or destroyed, the cause of action is not deemed to have accrued till the discovery of the facts upon which its validity depends. (See 

Surrog. Ct. Act 142-143 as to method of proof.) (7) An action upon a judgment of a court not of record

which judgment has not been docketed in a county clerk's office in the State. 

G. Five Years: Action for a divorce; or annulment on the ground of physical incapacity. C.P.A. 1141; C.P.A. 

1153. The five year period of limitation applies also to an act of adultery set up by D in the divorce action by way of defense (recrimination) as well as by way of counterclaim. Ackerman v. Ackerman, 200 N. Y. 72.

H. Three Years (C.P.A. 49): 

(1) Action against a sheriff, coroner, constable or other officer for the non-payment of money collected upon execution. (2) Action against a constable upon any other liability incurred in his official capacity, except for an escape. (3) Action upon a statute for a penalty or forfeiture given by the statute to the person aggrieved, unless the statute fixes a different limitation. (4) Action against a director or stockholder of a moneyed or banking corporation to recover a penalty or forfeiture or to enforce a liability created by statute or common law (cause of action accrues upon discovery by P of the facts creating the liability). (5) Action against an executor, administrator or receiver, or against the trustee of an insolvent debtor, where the action is brought to recover a chattel, with or without damages for the taking, detention or injury of such chattel. (6) Action to recover damages for injuries to person resulting from negligence. (7) Action to recover damages for injury to property resulting from negligence or otherwise, except where differently prescribed. (As amended Sept. 1, 1936, formerly six years.) (a) Even before the 1936 amendment, supra, it was held that an action to recover damages for the loss of a wife's services as a result of personal injuries inflicted upon her through D's negligence was an action to recover for a personal injury under C.P.A. 49 and not one for injury to property (six years under former C.P.A. 48). Maxon

v. D. L. & W. RR. Co., 112 N. Y. 559. (b) Whether an action asking damages for personal injuries, is in form, ex delicto or ex contractu is not determinative of whether the six year statute or three year statute applies. The court will look to the "gravamen of the action and foundation of the claim," and if in fact, based on negligence, the shorter statute will apply. 

Hermes v. Westchester Racing Assn., 213 A. D. 147 where P bought a ticket to D's race track and while lawfully therein sustained personal injuries through D's negligence, HELD, an action brought three and one half years later, to recover damages for breach of the implied contract on the part of D to safely protect P while on D's premises, was barred by the running of the three year statute. See also Monahan v. Deviney, 223 A. D. 547; Tulloch v. Haselo, 218 N. Y. S. 139; 

Webber v. Rrd, 109 N. Y. 311.

I. Two Years (C.P.A. 50): 

(1) Action to recover damages for assault, battery, seduction, criminal conversation, false imprisonment, malicious prosecution, or malpractice. (2) Action to recover a statutory penalty or forfeiture, in favor of the people of the State. (3) Action to recover damages for wrongful death. 

D.E.L. 130.

J. One Year (C.P.A. 51-2): 

(1) Action to recover damages for libel or slander (as amended April 8, 1936, formerly two years.) (2) Action against a sheriff or coroner for official misconduct except non-payment of money upon execution. (3) Action against any other officer for escape of a civil prisoner. (4) Action upon a statute for a penalty or forfeiture, given wholly or partly to any person (e. g. common informer) who will prosecute for the same. (Prosecution must be commenced within one year after the commission of the offense and if not so commenced by the private person, it may be commenced within two years thereafter on behalf of the people of the state.) 

5. Computing the period of Limitation: The periods of limi-

tation, except as otherwise specifically prescribed, must be computed from the time of the accrual of the right to relief, to the time when the claim to relief is actually interposed by the party as plaintiff or defendant. C.P.A. 11.

In computing any period of time from a specified event, the day upon which the event happened is deemed the day from which the reckoning is made, but the day itself is excluded in making the computation. Gen. Const. Law 

Sec. 20.

A. When Action Deemed Commenced: "An action is com menced against a defendant, within the meaning of any provision of this act which limits the time for commencing an action, when the summons is served on him or a co-defendant who is a joint contractor or otherwise united in interest with him." C.P.A. 16. The service must be valid. Erickson v. Macy, 236 N. Y. 412. • (1) Illustration: I sue A, B and C, co-partners for a partnership debt on a contract. I name all as defendants (as I must, since under the Partnership Law they are jointly liable) but serve A only. With respect to the running of the statute of limitations, the action is "commenced" against A, B and C.

B. Delivery of Summons to Sheriff: C.P.A. 17 provides: 

"An attempt to commence an action in a court of record is equivalent to the commencement thereof against each defendant, within the meaning of each provision of this act which limits the time for commencing an action, when the summons is delivered, with the intent that it shall be actually served, to the sheriff, or, where the sheriff is a party, to a coroner of the county, in which that defendant, or one of two or more co-defendants who are joint contractors or otherwise united in interest with him, resides or last resided; or, if the defendant is a corporation, to a like officer of the county in which it is established by law or wherein its general business is or was last transacted, or wherein it keeps, or last kept an office for the transaction of business. But in order to entitle a plaintiff to the benefit of this section, the delivery of the summons to an officer must be followed within sixty days after the expiration of the time limited for the actual commencement of the action by personal

service of the summons or by service thereof without the state, upon the defendant sought to be charged, or by the first publication of the summons, as against that defendant, pursuant to an order for service upon him in that manner, or by substituted service of the summons on such defendant within the state pursuant to an order." 

The statute of limitation ceases to run only if service is effected as prescribed in the above section. In re Whitcher, 243 N. Y. S. 720. See C.P.A. 18 as to procedure in a court not of record.

C. Where Demand Necessary to Start Statute Running: 

Where a right exists, but a demand is necessary to entitle a person to maintain an action, the period of limitation is computed from the time when the right to make the demand is complete (e. g. the right to make the demand on a demand note accrues at the time of the execution thereof; as against the maker the statute of limitations runs from that point). C.P.A. 15. (1) Where the right grows out of the receipt or detention of money or property by an agent or fiduciary, the time must be computed from the time when the person having the right to make the demand has actual knowledge of the facts upon which that right depends. C.P.A. 15 subd. 1. (a) For example, if P entrusts D, his agent, with money for investment and D retains such money for himself, the cause of action in P's favor accrues as soon as he discovers the facts of the wrongful detention and the period of limitations starts to run from that point. King v. Mackellar, 109 N. Y. 215. (b) The same is true where D, an agent, acting under agreement with P (principal) undertakes to collect money belonging to P and to hold it for the latter, and then fails to turn same over; the cause of action for accounting in favor of P and against D accrues as soon as P learns of the facts (since at such time he would have the right to demand his money). Dumbadze v. Lignante, 244 N. Y. 1. (c) But where one receives money for the use of another under circumstances which make it his duty to pay it over at once, the statute begins to run immediately without demand. Mills v. Mills, 115 N. Y. 80 (where D, mortgagee, sold the mortgaged lands and failed to ac-

count to the mortgagor for surplus proceeds, HELD, in an action to compel such accounting, the six year statute (C.P.A. 48) applies and begins to run as soon as 

D received P's money, D being a debtor merely and not a trustee). See also Wood v. Young, 141 N. Y. 211. (d) The same is true where P, owner of fifty shares of the capital stock of D bank, seeks to compel D bank to deliver said stock to him or to pay the value thereof and to account for earned dividends. Under a forged power of attorney presented to it, D bank had negligently accepted the surrender of said stock and had cancelled it.

HELD, no demand was necessary to entitle P to maintain this action; the cause of action arose (and the statute of limitations started to run) when the shares were wrongfully transferred, and not when the owner of the stock acquired knowledge of the facts. Glover v. Bank, 141 N. Y. S. 409. (2) Where the right grows out of a deposit of money or property not to be repaid or returned at a fixed time but only on special demand, the time is computed from such demand. C.P.A. 15, subd. 2. (a) Money deposited in a bank to be checked out, or deposits evidenced by certificates of deposit are "deposits" within the meaning of C.P.A. 15, subd. 2, not to be repaid at a fixed time, and hence, a demand is necessary to start the statute running. Howell v. Adams, 68 

N. Y. 314; See also Payne v. Gardiner, 29 N. Y. 146. (b) In an action for breach of contract for failure to deliver goods pursuant to a warehouse receipt, the duty to present the receipt and demand the goods is a continuing one, and the period of limitation does not begin to run until a demand is made. Williams v. Flagg 

Storage Warehouse Co., 223 N. Y. S. 925.

D. Accrual of Cause of Action in Specific Actions: 

(1) Breach of Contract: Where no date is fixed as the date of performance, the statute begins to run from the expiration of a reasonable time within which defendant should have performed; the question as to what constitutes a reasonable time in a given case is one of fact for the jury. Ahrens v. Guaranty Trust Co.,. 211 N. Y. S. 283. 

(2) Breach of Agent's Warranty of Authority: The statute begins to run from the date plaintiff is made aware of the absence of authority. Moore v. Maddock, 251 N. Y. 420.

(3) Action by Principal Against Agent for Latter's Mis conduct: Where an injury to a third party results from the act or omission of an agent or deputy, the time within which the principal may commence an action to recover against the agent or deputy is computed from the time when a judgment against the principal is first recovered, and a subsequent reversal does not extend the time (C.P.A. 14). Before C.P.A. 14 can apply the injured third party must recover against the principal. Town of Greenburgh v. Coyne, 236 N. Y. 296. (It is to be remembered, in this connection, that while in a proper case the principal sued by a third party for the agent's wrongful act in the scope of the agency, could implead the agent under subd. 2 of C.P.A. 193, the exercise of this privilege is optional with the principal.) (4) Malicious Prosecution Action: Plaintiff's right to sue for malicious prosecution does not accrue until the termination in his favor of the prior action brought against him; hence, the two year period of limitation (C.P.A. 50) starts to run from that point. 

Psaty v. 5th Ave. etc. Corp., 229 N. Y. S. 384. 

(5) Equity Actions Based on Mistake (No Fraud): In an equity action to reform a written instrument such as a deed, on the ground of mistake, no fraud being present, the statute (ten year) does not run from the time of the discovery of the mistake, but from the time of the execution of the written instrument, or, in the case of a deed, from the time of its delivery. Exkorn v. Exkorn, 37 N. Y. S. 68. It has been held, however, that where the plaintiff (grantee) is in possession under the deed sought to be reformed on the ground of mistake, the statute runs from the time plaintiff first learns of the mistake. Burlingham v. Hanrahan, 251 N. Y. S. 55, and cases cited therein. 

(6) Action to Recover Balance on Mutual Account: 

Where plaintiff seeks to recover a balance due upon a mutual, open and current account, where there 1

have been reciprocal demands between the parties, the cause of action is deemed to have accrued from the time of the last item proved in the account on either side. C.P.A. 56. 

(a) "Mutual Account" Defined: 

To constitute a mutual account of reciprocal demands, a defendant, when sued, must have an account against the plaintiff, which he can interpose as a set-off to the extent thereof. "The cause of action upon such account is really in law for a balance due, and that party only is the debtor against whom the balance is found." Green v. 

Disbrow, 79 N. Y. 1; Minion v. Warner, 238 

N. Y. 413. 

(1') Illustration: Suppose P and D sell merchandise to each other over a long period of time. The total value of goods furnished by P to D is greater than that furnished by D to P. Whether there is a mutual and current account between the parties, depends upon the intent of the parties. If 

D delivered merchandise to P as an immediate payment of an obligation then due, then there is no open account. If, on the other hand, D delivered goods to P and simply expected a credit therefor, as one of a series of transactions, knowing that a balance would eventually be struck, then there is a mutual, open and current account. 

Crow v. Gleason, 141 N. Y. 489. (7) Malpractice Actions: The two year period of limitation prescribed by C.P.A. 50 applies only to malpractice actions, involving personal injuries, brought against physicians, surgeons, dentists, chiropractors and professional persons, licensed or not, who perform similar services. It does not apply to a nurse. Isenstein v. Malcomson, 236 N. Y. S. 641.

Nor does it apply to an action against an accountant for his negligence in making an audit resulting in property damages (American Exchange Bank v. 

Touche, 227 N. Y. S. 218) nor to an action against an attorney for professional negligence, resulting in property damages. Camp v. Reeves, 240 N. Y. 672.

In the two cases last given, the now three year

statute (C.P.A. 49) would apply. 

(a) Accrual of the Cause of Action: 

Generally speaking the cause of action accrues when the negligent or unskilful act constituting malpractice is committed. (1′) Thus where D, surgeon, left his forceps in P's abdominal cavity after an appendectomy, the cause of action for malpractice in P's favor is barred two years after the operation and not two years after P discovers the facts. Conklin v. Draper, 241 N. Y. S. 529, aff'd without op. 254 N. Y. 620. 

(2′) “Continuing" Cause of Action: But consider the following case: P employed D, physician, who diagnosed P's illness as appendicitis and advised an operation. The operation was performed but P failed to recover as expected, although D continued to attend him. 

It was finally discovered that D had negligently left a sponge in P at the time of the operation. When 

P discovered this, he ceased his relations with D and brought an action for malpractice against him.

D set up the statute of limitations in bar, claiming that the statute began to run from the date of the operation. It was held that the complaint charged D with a continuing cause of action in that not only did he negligently operate but that he negligently failed to discover the source of P's difficulty thereafter, and that, therefore, the period of limitation did not start to run until the professional relations had ceased. Sly v. Van Lengen, 198 N. Y. S. 608. (b) “Cloaking" the Barred Malpractice Action: If the action is based on malpractice, the gravamen of the cause of action being unskilful treatment causing personal injuries, the two year statute applies with respect to the tort elements involved, even though the action is brought as though to enforce a contract. Horowitz v. Bogart, 217 N. Y. S. 881 (where P sought $50,000 damages for pain and suffering, alleging that D failed to remove an ulcer in P's body which “D had agreed to do," the complaint not alleging D's negligence or lack of skill, HELD, the cause of action was still one for malpractice and barred by the running of the two year statute). 

In Tulloch v. Haselo, 218 N. Y. S. 139, D, dentist, extracted P's tooth, while P was under an anaesthetic, and negligently permitted the tooth to slip down P's

esophagus. D kept this fact from P, although it is possible that he honestly believed the tooth would be expelled from P's system through natural channels. As a matter of fact, the tooth lodged in P's lung. After the two year period had expired, P brought an action for damages for fraud. The action was brought within six years of the discovery of the alleged fraud. It was held that P's personal injuries were caused, not by D's fraud, assuming it to be such, but by his act of malpractice, and that P's cause of action, despite the wording of the complaint, was one for malpractice and consequently barred by the running of the statute. 

(1′) Damages Flowing From Breach of the Contract: 

If there was a consensual relation between P and D in the malpractice action, it seems, that it would be possible for P, even after the running of the two year statute, to obtain partial relief by suing strictly on the contract (six year period under C.P.A. 48).

Recovery of damages could not be had for the wrong involving unskilful treatment, such as pain and suffering, but P might be entitled to recover sums paid to D on the contract to furnish proper medical aid, and for sums paid out for nurses and medicines or other damages that flow naturally from the breach of whatever contract was made between the parties. Monahon v. Deviney, 223 A. D. 547; 

Frankel v. Wolper, 181 A. D. 485. 

(8) Actions For Wrongful Death: The personal rep resentative of a decedent may maintain an action to recover damages for a wrongful act causing decedent's death against a person or corporation who would have been liable to an action in favor of decedent if death had not ensued. The action must be commenced within two years after the decedent's death. D.E.L. 130. (a) This does not mean that it may be commenced within two years after the granting of letters of administration. Luen v. Brimmer, 197 N. Y. S. 3. (b) If decedent's death is caused by a wrongful act, and if he dies within three years after the injury, his personal representative may still bring an action for such wrongful death within two years thereof. But if decedent dies after three years from the date of the injury, never having commenced an action therefor, his personal representative cannot bring an action thereafter under 

D.E.L. 130, since, had the decedent lived, he could not

have recovered for the personal injuries after the three year period of limitation had expired as to him. C.P.A. 

49. See Emery v. Rochester Telephone Co., 271 N. Y. 306. (c) NOTE: Do not confuse the non-derivative action for wrongful death under D.E.L. 130 with an action to recover damages for personal injuries accruing to a deceased, before this death, which cause of action has been saved from abatement by new laws, (D.E.L. 118, 119, 120), in effect September 1, 1935, to be discussed later. 

See Kwiatkowisky v. Lowry Inc., 276 N. Y. 126. 

(9) Actions for Personal Injuries: Although C.P.A. 49 

provides for a three year period of limitation for actions brought to recover for personal injuries resulting from defendant's negligence, C.P.A. 48 provides that an action to recover for personal injuries. is subject to a six year period of limitation; except as otherwise provided, i.e. as excepted in C.P.A. 49. (a) Thus, it is possible to frame a complaint for personal injuries resulting from a nuisance maintained by D and come within the six year period of limitation (C.P.A. 

48) instead of the three year period (C.P.A. 49) by bringing an action in nuisance. Smith v. Earle, 195 

N. Y. S. 342. If a street railway corporation, maintaining tracks in a city street, creates a depression in such street and knowingly allows it to remain and if P, a pedestrian, is injured thereby, he has a choice of two causes of action; viz., for nuisance and for negligence. But if others create the nuisance and D is guilty of negligence in allowing it to remain, the cause of action is only for negligence and not nuisance. Hayes v. Brooklyn 

Heights Rrd. Co., 200 N. Y. 183. 

6. Waiver of Statute of Limitations: 

A. By a Writing: To revive a claim which is barred or to extend the statute of limitations, there must be in writing, either: an express promise to pay (absolute or conditional) or, an acknowledgment of the debt as subsisting, not inconsistent with an intention on the part of the debtor to pay it. Manchester v. Braender, 107 N. Y. 346. An offer to buy a note which has been outlawed by the statute of limitation "for some small sum" is not a sufficient acknowledgment sufficient to waive the bar of the statute. 

Connecticut Trust etc. Co. v. Wead, 172 N. Y. 497. such cases, the statute runs only from the date of the In

new promise, or the acknowledgment (C.P.A. Sec. 59). Such a writing is good although made after the expiration of the statutory period (Willis v. Wileman, 53 Misc. 462) and although failing to specifically describe the debt in character or amount. Zinn v. Stamm, 78 Misc. 567. 

B. By Part Payment of Interest or Principal: 

Part payment of interest or principal operates to waive the protection of the statute of limitations as to a claim which has already been barred under it (i.e. revive the claim) or to extend the statute. 

(1) Payment Made by One Joint Contractor: A pay ment made by a joint contractor (e. g. a payment made by one of two joint and several makers of a note) may be availed of by the other joint contractor, without the latter losing his right to insist upon the bar of the statute of limitations as to himself unless said payment was previously authorized or subsequently ratified by him. McMullen v. Rafferty, 89 N. Y. 456; Hoover v. Hubbard, 202 N. Y. 289. (a) Illustration: A and B are partners and jointly contract a partnership debt in 1928. In 1932, the partnership is dissolved. In 1935, A makes an initial payment to be applied on the debt. The bar of the statute of limitations is waived by A only, and B is no longer liable. When the partnership was dissolved so was the implied agency. 

The subsequent payment could be binding on B only if he previously authorized or subsequently ratified it. (b) Payment by Principal as Against Guarantor: Such payment does not extend the statute of limitations as against the guarantor unless the latter previously authorizes or subsequently ratifies the payment. People's 

Trust Co. v. O'Neil, 273 N. Y. 312. 

(2) Payment Made By One Co-Surety. The question we are now about to discuss arises most frequently in the case where one co-surety has paid more than his pro rata share and seeks to compel contribution from the other sureties. Suppose in 1928, the principal indebtedness of $1,000 was incurred and S1 and S2 become sureties thereon, each for the full amount thereof. In 1935, after the debt is barred 

S1, pays $750 to the creditor. The payment is with-

out the authorization or ratification of S2. Of course, the creditor cannot look to S2 for the balance. But, moreover, S1, although he has paid more than his pro rata share, cannot compel contribution from $2. This is because, in paying the $750, S1 was under no legal (albeit moral) obligation to do so, and he cannot revive the outlawed debt as to S2 without the latter's consent. Hard v. Mingle, 206 N. Y. 179. But suppose S1 paid $250 in 1933, while the obligation was still valid and subsisting, such payment not being authorized or ratified by S2. In 1935 S1 paid $500 additional under the same circumstances. Granting that the creditor cannot hold S2 for the remaining $250 may S1 compel contribution from S2? The answer is Yes, because when

S1 paid the $500 in 1935 (thus paying more than his pro rata share) the statute of limitations had been extended as to him (by the 1933 payment) and hence the 1935 payment was in recognition of a legal, subsisting claim as distinguished from a merely moral obligation. 

(3) When Part Payment May Be Made: Such payment may be made prior to or after the statutory period has expired. For example A makes a note payable in 1925. The statute of limitations expires in 1931.

A makes his first payment of interest (or principal) in 1930. The statute is extended to 1936. Suppose

A makes the same payment in 1932 and the payment is accepted. The debt is revived and the statute extended to 1938. (4) Test of Intention: The test is not how the creditor applies the payments, but how the debtor intended them to be applied. Lupfer v. State, 194 N. Y. S. 171. (a) Thus, where the debtor never directed as to how the payments should be applied, there is no waiver of the bar of the statute, unless the creditor can show that they were made by the debtor with intent to apply to the whole debt. Crow v. Gleason, 141 N. Y. 489. 

7. Tolling and Suspension of Statute of Limitations: Certain time is excluded from the computation of the periods of

limitation, based upon the fact that some reason exists during the excluded period why the person entitled to assert a right, fails to assert it.

A. Absence from State: If the party liable is outside the state, when the cause of action accrues, the statute of limitations does not run in his favor until he returns or designates some person within the state upon whom service of a summons may be made. If, after the cause of action has accrued, the party liable, leaves the state and remains continuously absent therefrom for a year, or more, or if, unknown to the person to whom he is liable, he resides within the state under a false name, the time of his absence or residence under a false name is excluded from the period of limitation, unless he has made a designation of a person to accept service on his behalf. If the defendant is a foreign corporation having no officers in the state who may be served (C.P.A. 229), the statute of limitations does not run in its favor until it has designated a person within the state for service of the summons. C.P.A. 19. Under this last section the "period of limitations runs whenever a debtor is physically present in the state, though he may be resident elsewhere. The question is not one of domicile, but of personal presence." Mack v. Mendels, 249 N. Y. 356. (1) After removing to another state D's return to New York for temporary, casual, visits will not prevent his being continuously absent within the meaning of C.P.A. 19. Connecticut 

Trust etc. Co. v. Wead, 172 N. Y. 497. (2) But where a non-resident maintains an office in New York which he visits daily for the open and notorious transaction of business, he is not "absent from the state" and C.P.A. 

19 does not toll the running of the statute as to him. Mack v. Mendels, supra; Costello v. Downer, 19 A. D. 434. (3) C.P.A. 19 tolls the statute even though D may never before have been in New York, but at all times was a non-resident. Laurencelle v. Laurencelle, 216 N. Y. S. 384; Meyers v. Credit Lyonaise, 259 N. Y. 399. However, as soon as D non-resident comes into the state the statute begins to run. 

Olcott v. Tioga Rry. Co., 20 N. Y. 210; Nat'l Surety Co. v. 

Ruffin, 242 N. Y. 413. 

(a) Illustration: A cause of action accrues in favor of P 

against D on January 1, 1930, having a six year statute of limitations. D enters New York for the first time

on January 1, 1931. The period of limitation expires 

January 1, 1937, it having been tolled one year. Suppose the same cause of action accrues to P, as above, on July 1, 1930, D, a resident, absents himself from the state from 1932 to 1934. The period of limitation is tolled two years and expires in 1938.

B. Foreign Cause of Action: Where a cause of action arises outside New York, it cannot be enforced in New York after the period of limitation in the foreign state has expired, except where the cause of action originally accrued to a New York resident. C.P.A. 13. Where a cause of action (not affecting realty within New York) accrues against a non-resident, it cannot be enforced against him in New York after the expiration of the applicable period of limitation in his own state, but if such period of limitation is shorter than our own statute, the latter applies. 

C.P.A. 55. The purpose of these two sections is to prevent a non-resident plaintiff from coming here and prosecuting a claim, whether against a resident or non-resident, under our statute, if it is more favorable to him than the statute of limitations where the cause of action arose. 

Walz v. Mansfield, 258 N. Y. S. 620. (In Nat. Surety
