Co. v. Ruffin, 242 N. Y. 413, a cause of action accrued in Virginia in 1914, having a five year period of limitation there and a six year period in New York, D being a resident of Virginia and P a resident of New York. 

P commenced action in New York in 1925; defense that under C.P.A. 55 the action was barred, HELD, under 

C.P.A. 19 our statute was tolled by reason of D's nonresidence, hence P could properly maintain this action under C.P.A. 13.) See also Meyers v. Credit Lyonaise, supra. 

8. Death of Claimant Before Expiration of Period: If a person entitled to maintain an action dies before the expiration of the time limited for the commencement thereof, and the cause of action, survives (see discussion of new abatement laws, post), an action may be commenced by his representative after the expiration of that time, and within one year after his death. C.P.A. 20; Dyckman v. Dyckman, 243

N. Y. S. 663. Thus, where a cause of action accrues to P on a simple contract and P dies one month before the ex-

piration of the period of limitation (six years) his personal representative may commence an action after the expiration of the period but within one year of P's death. (C.P.A. 

20 should not be confused with D.E.L. 130 which has reference only to actions for wrongful death and provides that such action must be brought within two years after decedent's death.) 

9. Death of Debtor or Person Liable (C.P.A. 21): 

A. If a person liable, dies within the state, an extension of eighteen months, is given beyond the statutory period within which to sue his representatives. 

B. If letters upon his estate are not issued in New York at least six months before the expiration of the time to bring the action (as extended by "A" above), then the term of one year after such letters are issued is not a part of the time limited for the commencement of such an action.

C. Where the person liable dies without the state, the statute of limitations is suspended until eighteen months after the issuance of letters in New York. C.P.A. 12. 

10. Effect of Reversal of Judgment or Termination of Action 

(C.P.A. 23): If an action is commenced within the time limited therefor, and the judgment is reversed on appeal without awarding a new trial, or if the action is terminated in any other manner (e. g. by non-suit) than by (a) voluntary discontinuance (b) dismissal of the complaint for failure to prosecute, or (c) a final judgment on the merits, then, plaintiff or his personal representatives (if the action survives) may commence a new action for the same cause after the expiration of the period of limitation, but within one year of such reversal or termination. The section applies to workmen's compensation proceedings. The words "for the same cause" as used in C.P.A. 23 do not mean the same form of action (i. e. remedy). It is sufficient that the second action brought within the extended period, be "based upon the same transaction as the former one, without regard to technical form" of the second action. Titus v. 

Poole, 145 N. Y. 414. In the case last cited, P sued D, executor, for fraudulent representations in the sale of stock

made by the latter's testator. P's claim had been rejected by D and within the time then limited by law (six months) P started suit. He was non-suited because he failed to prove "scienter." More than six months after the rejection of the claim, but within one year of the termination of the first action, P commenced a second action against D, based on the same transaction but this time for damages for breach of warranty, HELD, the statute of limitations was not a defense, because it was extended by C.P.A. 23. It is necessary, however, that the second action be between the same parties (or their representatives or assigns) as the first. 

Streeter v. Graham Co., 263 N. Y. 39. 

11. Effect of Stay on Commencement of Action (C.P.A. 24): 

Where the commencement of an action has been stayed by injunction, or other order, of a court or judge, or by statute, the time of the continuance of the stay is not a part of the time limited for the commencement of the action. A requirement that negligence claims against a city be presented to the common council (or comptroller) first, and that no such claim be prosecuted unless and until that body, or officer, neglects to pay or adjust same, is generally not part of P's cause of action. Hence, the statute starts to run against P's cause of action when the injury accrues and not when the claim is rejected. Dickinson v. Mayor, 92 

N. Y. 584; Cf. Fisher v. Mayor, 67 N. Y. 76; also, Winter v. City of Niagara Falls, 190 N. Y. 198; Murphy v. Village of Fort Edward, 213 N. Y. 397; Conway v. City of New 

York, 139 A. D. 446.

A. Actions Affecting Real Property (C.P.A. 43): If a per son who might maintain an action to recover real property or possession thereof, or make an entry, or defend or counterclaim on the title to real property, is at the time of the accrual of the cause of action, an infant, insane, or imprisoned on a criminal charge for a term less than life, the period of such disability is not part of the period of limitation (15 years, C.P.A. 34) except that the time so limited cannot be extended more than ten years after the disability ceases. (1) In Howell v. Leavitt, 95 N. Y. 617, the Court of Appeals construing this section in connection with C.P.A. 34, which at that time provided for a twenty year period of limitation,

said: C.P.A. 43 "relates to the extension of the time limited, and puts restraint only upon that extension. It means that the disability shall not add more than ten years to the time limited after the disability has ended. Practically, in a case of infancy, it makes the extreme possible limitation a period of thirty-one years. If the cause of action accrues to an infant on the day of its birth for twenty-one years the running of the statute is suspended; then it begins to run; but the time limited—that is, the twenty years considered as a period—having in fact elapsed, it is an extension of that period which is in progress, and the exception limits that added time to not more than ten years after full age; that is until the expiration of thirty-one years. 

But for the exception, the infant could have had forty-one years. **** What there is of difficulty in the section lies in the phrase ‘after the disability ceases.' That relates only to the extended time, and has no effect in any case to cut down or lessen the limitation of twenty years. To that the party is always entitled, and, in case of disability, to as much more as the period of disability would add, except that such addition must not be longer than ten years added after the disability has ended. Any unexpended part of the period or time fixed by the general rule of limitation belongs to the party entitled to sue, after the disability has ended and so much added time as will not extend the original limit beyond ten years more after the end of the disability." See also Muller v. Ry. Co., 195 N. Y. 539. (2) If the right of action originally accrues to a competent adult, his death and the passing of the right of action to an infant heir will not permit the latter to avail himself of his infancy under C.P.A. 43. Scallon v. Ry. Co., 185 N. Y. 359. 

B. Actions other than to Recover Realty (C.P.A. 60): If a person entitled to maintain an action, other than for the recovery of real property, is under a disability when the cause of action accrues the statute of limitations is tolled; but the time so limited cannot be extended more than five years, except in case of infancy; or in any case. more than one year after the disability ceases. (a) Purpose: The purpose of C.P.A. 60 is to add to and not to lessen the usual statutory periods applicable to adults, and it will not be applied when the result would be harmful, as in the case of infants. (1′) Thus, a minor of twenty years, having a right of action for negligence, to which a three year period would apply if he were an adult, may have this three year

period, whereas if he invoked his disability, he would have only one year of infancy and one year of maturity. 

(b) Insanity: Continuing insanity of the person entitled to sue, extends the usual period five years only.

C.P.A. 60.

CHAPTER III. 

THE SUMMONS 

1. Generally: The authority of a court must be invoked by the parties themselves or, by one of them. An "action" is almost invariably commenced by the service of a summons, as distinguished from a "proceeding", which is usually commenced by the filing of a petition. (An action ends in a judgment; a proceeding in a decree or final order.) Where a provisional remedy (such as arrest, or attachment) is granted before the service of the summons, or a chattel replevied in a replevin action, before such service, the court acquires a conditional jurisdiction of the action, which may be divested by failure to properly effect service. C.P.A. 

825, 1092; Daly v. Amberg, 126 N. Y. 490. 

2. What is a Summons: A summons is a mandate of the court, issued by an officer thereof (plaintiff's attorney). 

3. Form: R.C.P. 45 (permitted by C.P.A. 218) prescribes the form of summons. Secs. 19-20, Mun. Ct. Code provide the form of summons to be used in that court. (See "Forms" in Appendix.) The summons consists of three parts; the title, the body and the subscription.

A. The Title: This consists of the name of the court, in cluding the county which plaintiff designates as the place of trial (e. g. "Supreme Court: Kings County") and the names of the parties to the action. C.P.A. 191. 

(1) Naming Parties Ordinarily: Full names should be set out. Middle names are not necessary. Initials are insufficient. Grant v. Birdsall, 48 Super. Ct., 427. 

Abbreviations should be avoided. 

(a) Married Woman: A married woman should be named as "Mary Smith", not "Mrs. James Smith." C.P.A. 200. (b) Partnership: The names of the partners should be given, not merely the firm name, e. g. "John Doe and

Richard Roe, co-partners doing business under the firm name of American Paint Co., Plaintiffs." Mayor v. 

Frankfeld, 32 N. Y. S. 1007; De Witt v. Abraham Bros. 

Co., 156 N. Y. S. 658. (c) Corporation: The corporate name should appear, e. g. "The American Paint Co., Inc., Plaintiff." (d) Infant as Party: A guardian ad litem must be appointed for an infant in an action brought by or against him.

In the case of an infant plaintiff the title would read: "John Doe, an infant, by Richard Doe, his guardian ad litem, Plaintiff." 

(2) Naming Parties in a Representative Capacity: 

Where a party sues or is sued in a representative capacity, a description of such capacity should follow his name in the title (Leonard v. Pierce, 182 

N. Y. 431) e. g. "John Doe as executor (or administrator) of William Blank, deceased," or "John Doe as trustee (or receiver) in bankruptcy of William 

Blank, bankrupt." (a) If the "as" is omitted but it appears from the allegations of the pleadings that the action is being brought, or defended, in a representative capacity, the error is deemed cured by such allegations. Higgins v. Applebaum, 170 

N. Y. S. 228; Beers v. Shannon, 73 N. Y. 292. (b) If P omits "as" but describes his capacity (e. g. "administrator") he may nevertheless recover individually. 

Gatti-McQuade Co. v. Flynn, 140 N. Y. S. 135. (c) Unincorporated Association: Actions may be prosecuted by or against unincorporated associations in the name of the president or treasurer thereof. Any partnership or any association of persons having a president or treasurer comes within the same rule. Gen. Assn. Law 

Secs. 12-13 (the requirement as to "seven or more members" was dropped by amendment in 1932). The title would read "John Doe as President of the Amal gamated Brotherhood, etc., Plaintiff." See Miller v. 

Blood, 217 N. Y. 517. 

(3) Defendant's Name Unknown: 

(a) Entire Name Unknown: A fictitious name is used fol lowed by a description sufficient to identify him, e. g. "John Doe, the name, 'John Doe', being fictitious, the real name of defendant being unknown to plaintiff, the party intended being the lessee in possession of premises 

No. 380 South Street, Brooklyn, New York." See also 

Snyder v. Parezo, 206 N. Y. 689; Sherman v. People, 154 N. Y. S. 484. 

(b) Part of Name Unknown: If part of the name is known only, such of it as is known is inserted followed by a description of the person intended, e. g. “'Mary' Doe, the name 'Mary' being fictitious, the true first name, etc." (as above). 

(4) Necessity of Ascertaining True Name: A judgment becomes a lien against the real property of a defendant only if it is docketed against him in his

THE SUMMONS 

45 true name. C.P.A. 510; Stark v. Weisner, 214 N. Y. 

S. 292. Hence when the true name of a fictitiously named defendant is discovered, application should be made to the court to substitute the true name for the fictitious one. C.P.A. 215. (A judgment against "Bess Hedges" is a lien against real property owned by "Mary Elizabeth Hedges" where there is some evidence of doing business under the name of “Bess 

Hedges." H. R. & C. Inc. v. Smith, 242 N. Y. 267.)

B. The Body of the Summons: R.C.P. 45 gives the form of the body applicable to Supreme Court actions and County Court actions. If the action is brought in the City 

Court the same form is applicable except that defendant is given six days instead of twenty to appear or answer. If the City Court summons is served by publication the defendant must be given ten days to answer. 

City Ct. Act, Sec. 49. The Municipal Court summons is materially different in form (Mun. Ct. Code Sec. 20) from the form prescribed by R.C.P. 45. In the Municipal Court, defendant has only five days in which to appear and answer, the summons being made returnable in the office of the clerk of the court. 

C. Subscription of the Summons: The summons must be subscribed by the plaintiff's attorney (the signature may be typewritten, printed or written) and must bear his office and post office address, and the date of its issue. 

R.C.P. 11, 45. Under C.P.A. 236 a summons may be subscribed by the plaintiff in person, where he is prosecuting his own action. Horter v. De Mesa, 188 N. Y. 

S. 20; O'Brien v. Lashar, 236 N. Y. 602. There is authority that, under C.P.A. 236, a domestic corporation, suing for its own benefit, may subscribe the summons and complaint in its corporate name as plaintiff in person. 

Sellent-Repent Corp. v. Queens Borough Gas Co., 290 

N. Y. S. 887. But the seemingly sounder view is to the contrary. Mortgage Commission v. Great Neck Dev. 

Co., 295 N. Y. S. 107. See also Mullin-Johnson Co. v. 

Ins. Co., 9 F. Supp. 175; Binnie v. Triangle Ranch Co., 73 Colo. 586. 

4. Defects in the Summons: Variation from the prescribed

form may be an irregularity merely, subject to amendment, or may make the summons absolutely void giving the court no jurisdiction to permit an amendment. Elder v. Morse, 212 N. Y. S. 581. While, perhaps, no definitive test can be laid down, it may be stated that the summons, varying from the prescribed form, is void, unless (a) it sufficiently conveys to defendant the fact that an action is being commenced against him, (b) warns him of the legal consequences of his defaulting, and (c) advises him what he must legally do to avoid default. Stuyvesant v. Weil, 167 

N. Y. 421; Sicardi v. Ajello, 190 N. Y. S. 704.

A. Instances of Amendable "Irregularities”: C.P.A. 105 

provides that any mistake, omission, or irregularity may be corrected or supplied, with the court's permission. 

Moreover, if a substantial right of any party is not prejudiced thereby such mistake, omission or irregularity may be disregarded. Schack v. Bryan, 193 N. Y. S. 548. The following "irregularities" in the summons may be ordered amended or disregarded: (1) Omitting address of P's attorney. Sullivan v. Harney 

103 N. Y. S. 177. (2) Omitting designation of county in action in Supreme 

Court. Thomson v. Tilden, 53 N. Y. S. 920. 

(3) Using old form of summons authorized by Code of Civil Procedure. Jarvis v. Lavine, 200 A. D. 552. 

(4) Omitting of P's attorney's name from the copy of the summons, (the address being given) where D had access to the original. Hull v. Canandaigua Electric Co., 66 N. Y. S. 865.

B. Instances of Fatal Defects: 

(1) Making summons in Supreme Court "returnable forthwith." Tucci v. Romeo, 158 N. Y. S. 262. (2) Omitting name of court both in summons and accompanying complaint. Ward v. Stringham, 1 Code Rep. 118. (3) Omitting the words "or if the complaint is not served with this summons, to serve a notice of appearance," where no complaint was so served. Sicardi v. Ajello, 190 N. Y. S. 704; cf. Kenngott v. Kenngott, 190 N. Y. S. 82. 

5. Summons in Matrimonial Actions: If the summons is served without the complaint, there must be legibly endorsed on the face thereof, "Action to Annul a Marriage," "Action for

THE SUMMONS 

47 a Divorce" or "Action for a Separation," as the case may be. C.P.A. 1167; R.C.P. 47. See also R.C.P. 53, subd. 10. 

6. Summons with Notice: In a money judgment action on contract where the amount sought by way of damages is liquidated and certain, or capable of ascertainment by computation only, plaintiff may serve with his summons, instead of a complaint, a notice stating the sum of money for which default judgment will be taken. R.C.P. 46. 

A. Form of Notice: "TAKE NOTICE that upon your default, judgment in this action will be taken against you for the sum of Ten Thousand ($10,000.00) Dollars, with interest thereon from January 1, 1935, together with costs. 

Dated, Brooklyn, New York, January 1, 1936. 

James Jameson, Attorney for Plf.

Office and P. O. Address 

50 Court Street, Brooklyn, New York.

B. Effect of Notice: Where defendant appears the notice means nothing, the subsequent complaint determining the nature and extent of the cause of action asserted. 

Sharp v. Clapp, 44 N. Y. S. 451. But if defendant defaults after personal service, plaintiff may enter judg ment before the clerk without application to the court. 

C.P.A. 486; R.C.P. 46. In such case, however (as in the case where an unverified complaint is served) the clerk will examine plaintiff under oath (unless plaintiff submits a verified complaint) in order to assess the amount of the judgment. C.P.A. 487; Leroy Arnold Inc. v. Mackey, 222 N. Y. S. 225. 

7. Notice of No Personal Claim: In some cases such as a mortgage foreclosure, or partition action, it is necessary for the plaintiff to make defendants of persons against whom he has no personal claim (e. g. tenants on premises). In such cases, plaintiff may serve with the summons a properly subscribed notice setting forth (generally) the object of the action, a description of the property affected, and that a personal claim is not being made against such defendant. 

If such defendant unreasonably defends the action, costs

may be awarded against him. C.P.A. 1478. 

8. Other Indorsements on Summons: 

A. Action to recover Statutory Penalties: Where an ac tion is brought to recover on a forfeiture or statutory penalty, either in the Municipal Court or Justice's Court, the summons must be so endorsed and must refer to the pertinent statute or ordinance. Mun. Ct. Code Sec. 24;

Justice Court Act, Sec. 49. 

B. Automobile Negligence Action in Municipal Court: In such action if plaintiff endorses on the summons, or alleges in his "long form" complaint, the registration number of a vehicle involved in the accident, ownership and control thereof is deemed admitted unless the answer specifically denies same. Mun. Ct. Code, Sec. 92a. See also Sec. 92c and 92d (as to similar rule with respect to street cars and buildings).

C. Where Body Execution Issuable in Municipal Court: In such case, the summons, if no verified complaint is served with it, must be indorsed to the effect that plaintiff claims defendant to be liable to arrest and imprisonment. Mun. Ct. Code, Sec. 25, 139. 

9. Supplemental and Amended Summons: 

A. Supplemental Summons: Where the court directs a new defendant to be brought in upon the application of the plaintiff, or otherwise, (e. g. C.P.A. 193, subd. 2) a supplemental summons must be served on such new defendant. C.P.A. 219; R.C.P. 48. The court's authorization by order is necessary since a summons once issued cannot be changed as a matter of right. Bell v. Good, 19 N. Y. S. 693. (Do not confuse with right to serve an amended pleading as of course. C.P.A. 244.) The supplemental summons, if ordered issued, must be in the same form as the original summons, except that the impleaded party's name is added, and he is required to answer the original or amended or supplemental complaint as the case may be. R.C.P. 48; Baum v. Stockell, 153 N. Y. S. 1004. 2 B. Amended Summons: We saw that where the summons contains a curable mistake, omission, irregularity or defect the court may permit an amendment. C.P.A. 105.

THE SUMMONS 

49 Where defendant has appeared, he is entitled to notice of the application to amend. Stuyvesant v. Weil, 167 

N. Y. 421. 

(1) Correcting Misnomers under C.P.A. 105: This is important because such an amendment is sometimes. illegally asked in order to obviate the necessity of service on a new defendant sought to be brought into the action. This cannot be done. A few cases should be considered, on this point. For example, if I name "Emma J. Stockton" as defendant, and actually serve the person intended, but who, in reality, is named "Mary J. Stockton," the court gets jurisdiction over "Mary," because the summons was sufficient to apprise her that an action was being commenced against her, and the court will permit an amendment of the title to set forth the correct name. Stuyvesant v. Weil supra. But if I intend to sue and serve "Morris" Jackson, but erroneously serve "Meyer" Jackson who appears as "Meyer

Jackson, sued herein as 'Morris' Jackson," the court gets no jurisdiction to award a judgment against "Morris" because "Meyer" is an interloper and there is no law that permits a stranger, because erroneously served with process intended for someone else to come into the record and defend upon the merits on a cause of action with which plaintiff never intended to charge him and perhaps prejudice the real defendant intended. Of course no amendment of the title under C.P.A. 105 would be possible here. Smith v. Jackson, 20 Abb. N. C. 422, aff'd 1 

N. Y. S. 13.

Again, suppose A, B and C, brothers, are co-partners doing business as "Abraham Bros. Co." I intend to sue the partners on a firm debt due me. I entitle my action against "Abraham Bros. Co., Defendant" and serve the summons on A. I should be permitted to amend my title so as to read against "A, B and C, co-partners d/b as Abraham Bros. 

Co. Defendants". Since the partnership, by service upon the partner, (C.P.A. 1197) was before the court, although erroneously named, the amendment

does not substitute a new party. De Witt v. Abraham Bros. Co., 156 N. Y. S. 658; cf. Gray v. Vought & Co., 216 A. D. 230. But suppose I serve A, in an action entitled against "A, B and C, co-partners d/b as American Paint Co." I then discover the 

American Paint Co. to be a corporation, of which 

A is president. Is an amendment proper under 

C.P.A. 105? No, because the corporation as such is not the party before the court. It is true that A, who was served is an officer of the corporation, but he was not served in that capacity. See Luria v. 

Newman, 199 N. Y. S. 21. The rule prohibiting the substitution of an entirely different party applies as well to a party plaintiff as to party defendant. Suppose P sues as administrator to recover from D, the value of services rendered by P's deceased minor daughter. P should not be permitted on the trial to substitute himself individually as party P, since that would be asserting an entirely different cause of action. Doyle v. Carney, 190 N. Y. 386. The same would be true where P sues to recover the purchase price of goods sold and seeks to amend on the trial to substitute an entirely different P, the proof having shown ownership of the goods to be in the latter. Scofield v. Becker, 182 N. Y. S. 691.

But suppose A sells goods to B and sues to recover for same. By mistake, A's attorney names as parties plaintiff A and B, as co-partners. Striking out 

B's name under C.P.A. 105 is perfectly proper.

There is no substitution of a new party plaintiff with a new cause of action, since title to the goods was not intended to be asserted in the partnership (but was done so erroneously). Comeford v. Fahy 

Market, 198 N. Y. S. 353. As we shall see, similar questions arise where the application is under C. 

P.A. 192 to substitute a party. Pelzer v. United 

Dredging Co., 193 N. Y. S. 676.

CHAPTER IV. 

SERVICE OF THE SUMMONS 1. Generally: The Civil Practice Act speaks of three kinds of service: personal service, substituted service, and service by publication. What often confuses the student is that even in cases of non-personal service (i. e., service outside the state in lieu of publication, C.P.A. 233, or service outside the state without an order, C.P.A. 235, or service under V. & T. 

Law, Sec. 52, we speak of "personal" service. But the "personal" service contemplated by these enumerated methods of service is not per se personal service. There is only one form of personal service that will support an "in personam" judgment, in the Supreme Court against a nonresident, and that is personal service within the state; or, a voluntary, general appearance in the action, its equivalent. 

Jones v. Jones, 108 N. Y. 415. This is because the jurisdiction of any court is co-extensive with its territorial limits. 

In the ordinary, money-judgment action, should I serve D, a non-resident, outside the state and should D default in appearance, I could enter no default judgment against him. 

If I previously attached D's property in New York, so as to give the court jurisdiction "in rem," and D defaulted, I could enter a default judgment which could be satisfied only out of the attached property. I could not enforce such default judgment in D's state because the "full faith and credit" clause of the United States Constitution would not require the sister state to recognize a default judgment so obtained. 

Pennoyer v. Neff, 95 U. S. 714; Pope v. Hecksher, 266 N. Y. 

114. Notice, however, that parties may agree in advance to give our courts jurisdiction in personam, irrespective of the fact that process is served outside the territorial limits. 

Gilbert v. Burnstine, 255 N. Y. 348; In re Heyman Inc. 

242 A. D. 362. Such an agreement to submit to the jurisdiction of our courts will be given effect as a voluntary general appearance in the subsequent action. 

2. Personal Service: 

A. Who May Make It: Except where service is expressly required to be made by a marshal or sheriff, any person, eighteen years, or over, and not a party to the action,

may serve the summons. C.P.A. 220; R.C.P. 53, subd. 2. (1) An officer of plaintiff corporation may serve the summons, since, he, personally, is not a party to the action. Outdoor Supply Co. v. Westhome Supply 

Co., 249 N. Y. S. 571. (2) Where the summons is served by a party to the action, or by a person under the age of eighteen, the service is not void but irregular. Hess v. Smith, 37 N. Y. S. 635; Meyers v. Overton, 2 Abb. Pr. 344.

B. Where to Make it: As previously noted, the summons must be served within the territorial jurisdiction of the court out of which it is issued. 

(1) Supreme Court: Sêrvice of process may be made anywhere within the state. (2) County Court: Service of process may be made anywhere within the state, by virtue of statutory provision (C.P.A. 72) except that a domestic corporation or joint stock association must be served in the county of which it is deemed a resident. C.P.A. 68. (3) City Court: Service may be effected anywhere within New York City as it now exists. N. Y. Const. 

Art. VI, Sec. 15; City Ct. Act, Sec. 2; American 

Historical Society v. Glenn, 248 N. Y. 445. (4) Municipal Court: Service may be effected only within New York City as it now exists. Mun. Ct. 

Code, Sec. 21. 

C. When To Make It: Service of legal process, of any kind, on Sunday, is prohibited (Penal Law, Sec. 2148) but is valid on any other holiday, including Election Day. 

Scott Shoe Co. v. Dancel, 71 N. Y. S. 263. In addition, it would seem that Penal Law, Sec. 2150, in providing that whoever maliciously procures any process in a civil action to be served on Saturday upon a person who observes that day as holy time is guilty of a misdemeanor, impliedly renders invalid such service. Martin v. Goldstein, 46 N. Y. S. 961.

D. How to Make It: 

(1) Service on a Natural Adult Person: Service is made by delivering a copy of the summons to the defendant and leaving it with him. C.P.A. 225, subd. 3.

SERVICE 

53 (a) It is not necessary to "touch" D's person with the summons (a popular, lay belief). If the summons is handed to D and D refuses to accept it, the server should inform D of the nature of the paper, and of his purpose to serve it, and then put it down at any appropriate place in D's presence. Correll v. Granget, 34 N. Y. S. 24. (b) If D is not informed by the server of the nature of the paper, the service is good, nevertheless, if the circumstances are such as to make it reasonably certain that D will ascertain its nature. Heller v. Levinson, 152 

N. Y. S. 35 (summons placed by server in D's outer pocket). (c) Service through Intermediary: Such service has consistently been held invalid. In Ives v. Darling, 206 

N. Y. S. 493, the server went to D's home where he was met at the door by D's husband. The server stated his business to the husband, who, in turn, apprised D. 

The latter, in an adjoining room, called back telling her husband to "take them (papers) for me." 

The server, thereupon, surrendered the papers to the husband. The service was held invalid. To same effect, see Mecca v. 

Young, 233 N. Y. S. 169; O'Connell v. Gallagher, 93 

N. Y. S. 643. But compare Olsen v. Jacknowitz, 285 

N. Y. S. 698. 

(d) Designation by Adult Resident: 

This is important. 

Compliance with C.P.A. 227 may sometimes mean the difference between a collectible and a non-collectible judgment. Under C.P.A. 227, an adult resident may execute and acknowledge (in the same manner as a deed entitled to be recorded) a written designation of another resident, to accept service on his behalf, during the former's absence from the State. A written consent of the designee (similarly executed) and the designation itself, are filed in the County Clerk's office in the county where the prospective absentee resides. The designation is effective for the period specified therein; if no period is specified, then for three years after filing. It may be revoked earlier, however, by the death or incompetency of either of the parties or by the filing of a duly executed revocation, either of the designation or of the consent. While the designation is in force, process in any civil action or proceeding, may be served upon the designee, with like effect, as if it were served personally upon the absentee, notwithstanding the return of the latter to New York. We will see later, that although the section prescribes no penalties for failure to comply therewith, nevertheless, non-compliance will

make the absentee subject to service by publication (if he remains absent more than six months, C.P.A. 232) and his property subject to attachment in a moneyjudgment action against him of a certain type, C.P.A. 902-903. 

(2) Service On An Infant (C.P.A. 225, subd. 1): 

(a) Infant Fourteen Years or Over: The summons in such case, must be served, one copy to the infant, and one copy to his parent or guardian, if any within the state, and if not within the state, then, to the person having care or control of the infant, or with whom he resides, or to the infant's employer. 

(b) Infant Under Fourteen Years: Service is made as above except that the infant need not be served. (c) In all Cases of Infancy: The court, in its discretion, and in the defendant's interest, may make an order, in all cases of infancy, requiring an additional copy to be served on a person designated in the order (e. g. where both parents are drunkards, or where none of the persons mentioned in the section can be found).

In such case the designee must be served before service is complete. (He is not, however, a guardian ad litem to defend the action.) (1) Service pursuant to C.P.A. 225, subd. 1 must be made in strict conformity with the statute, else a judgment obtained is void ab initio. Jacobson v. 

Krekell, 223 A. D. 440; Potter v. Ogden, 136 N.

Y. 384. This is true even though the infant appears by attorney and does not question the defective service. Weiser v. Landess, 172 N. Y. S. 

337; Leahy v. Hardy, 232 N. Y. S. 543; Gruner v. 

Ruffner, 119 N. Y. S. 942. (As to "collateral attack" upon the service made upon an infant, see 

Murphy v. Shea, 143 N. Y. 78.) (3) Service on An Incompetent (C.P.A. 225, subd. 2): 

If defendant is a person judicially declared incompetent to manage his affairs, because of lunacy, idiocy, or habitual drunkenness, and for whom a committee has been appointed (for his person or property), service upon him is made in the ordi-

SERVICE 

55 nary way, except that an extra copy is served on the committee. (The court in its discretion may dispense with service on the incompetent, or, if it believes his committee to be hostile or unfit, may designate some other person to accept service. C.P.A. 

226.) If there has been no judicial adjudication of incompetency, the incompetent is served in the ordinary way. 

Grant v. Humbert, 100 N. Y. S. 44.

However, even in such case, the court, in its discretion, may order an additional copy of the summons to be served on a person designated in the order to protect the incompetent's interests. C.P.A. 226. See 

Wurlitzer Co. v. Williams, 206 N. Y. S. 145. 

(4) Service on the State of New York (C.P.A. 221): 

Service on the people of the state is made by serving the attorney-general; service in person on a deputy attorney-general at the office of the attorney-general is equivalent to personal service on the attorney-general. 

(5) Service on a Municipal Corporation (C.P.A. 228): 

(a) Action against City of New York: Service is made on the mayor, comptroller, treasurer or corporation counsel. (b) On any other City: Service is made on the mayor, comptroller, treasurer, or counsel, attorney or clerk; or, if the city lacks any of these officers, to the officer performing corresponding functions under another name. (1) The city clerk may be served even though the city charter provides that only the mayor may be served. Crawford v. City of 

Newburgh, 249 N. Y. S. 477. 

(c) On Town, Village, County, School District: If the action is against a county, service is made on the chairman or clerk of the board of supervisors, the county clerk or the county treasurer or attorney; if against a town, on the supervisor or town clerk; if against a village, on the mayor, clerk or any trustee; if against a school district, on any member of the board of education, clerk thereof, or any trustee; if against a public board

or commission on the chairman (or other presiding officer) secretary or clerk thereof. 

(6) Service on a Private, Domestic Corporation (C.P.A. 

228): Service is made on any one of the following officers, specified in the section itself: president (or other head), vice-president, secretary, ass't secretary, treasurer, ass't treasurer, clerk to the corporation, cashier, director or managing agent (subd. 3). (a) Who is a "Managing Agent": A "managing agent" must be some person invested with general powers involving the exercise of judgment and discretion. Kiely v. Utica Gas Co., 235 

N. Y. S. 288. Not title, but duties performed, control. The following test is applicable: "If the persons named are true agents of the corporation and their positions are such as would lead to a just presumption that notice properly given to them will be notice to the principal, the corporation must submit." Tauza v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 220 N. Y. 259; Barrett v. 

American Tel. Co., 138 N. Y. 491, (general superintendent of telephone company, held, “managing agent") Seldin v. Ins. Co., 201 N. Y. S. 

213, (local fire insurance agent, held, not to be "managing agent"), Taylor v. Granite Assn., 136 N. Y. 343, (attorney of corporation, held, not to be "managing agent"). In Olsen v. Jacknowitz, 285 N. Y. S. 698, it was held that service upon the corporation attorney at the direction of the corporation secretary was valid service under C.P.A. 228. (b) Service Upon Secretary of State (subd. 9): “If the defendant is a business corporation, to one of the persons designated in subd. 3 or to the 

Secretary of State when he shall have become the agent of the corporation for the service of process as provided in the Stock Corporation 

Law. If service be made upon the Secretary of

State, the time within which the corporation is required to appear shall be deemed to be extended ten days." This subdivision was added

SERVICE 

57 by L. 1934, ch. 908, in effect November 1, 1934. 

With it must be read Sections 5 (subd. 11), 24 and 25 of the N. Y. Stock Corp. Law, which were also added by L. 1934, ch. 908. These sections require that after November 1, 1934, certificates of incorporation of corporations organized after that date, designate the Secretary of State, as agent of the corporation to receive process, and in addition, designate the address within or without the state to which the Secretary shall mail a copy of the process by registered mail. Corporations organized before November 1, 1934 must file a similar designation in the Department of State, by separate certificate. Whether or not such designation is filed, as of January 1, 1935, the Secretary of State, nevertheless, automatically, becomes the designee. If there is no designation on file, and until such actual filing, the Secretary of State may send notice of service upon him to the corporation in care of any one of the directors named in the certificate of incorporation. Hence, neglect to file the designation may mean that such notice will never actually reach the responsible officers of the corporation if the director originally named in the certificate, (as is the case where he is the usual "dummy" incorporator) is no longer connected with the corporation, or perhaps cannot be found at the address given. 

S.C.L. Sec. 25, provides: "Service of process against a domestic business corporation upon the Secretary of State shall be made by personally delivering to and leaving with him or a deputy Secretary of State duplicate copies of such process at the office of the department of state in the city of Albany. The Secretary of State shall forthwith send one of such copies by registered mail to the corporation at the address fixed for that purpose, as herein provided. At the time of service the party making such service shall pay to the Secretary of State a fee of two

888 

dollars if the action is solely for the recovery of a sum of money not in excess of two hundred dollars and the process is so endorsed, and a fee of ten dollars in any other action or proceeding, which fee, in either case, shall be a taxable disbursement. (If such process is served upon behalf of a county, city, town or village or other political subdivision of the state, the amount of the fee is two dollars irrespective of the amount involved or the nature of the action.) If the action or proceeding is instituted in a court of limited jurisdiction, service of process may be made in the manner provided in this section if the office of the defendant corporation is in the county in which such court is located." See Durand v. Lipman, 299 N. Y. S. 771 (precept in summary proceedings to dispossess in Municipal Court held properly served under this section). 

It seems quite clear that the additional method of service provided by the new C.P.A. 228, subd. 

4 was intended by the revisers to enlarge plaintiff's rights, and to permit him to avail himself of service under subd. 3 of that section or under subd. 4. See Brooklyn Law Review, December, 1934. 

(7) Personal Service on a Foreign Corporation (C.P.A. 

229): Only those foreign corporations actually doing business within the state are subject to the pro visions of C.P.A. 229. Robert Dollar Co. v. Cana dian Car & Foundry Co., 220 N. Y. 270. Personal service within New York of a foreign corporation not doing business in New York, although upon a proper officer, cannot, constitutionally, give our courts jurisdiction. In re Heyman, 242 A. D. 362; 

Robert Dollar Co. v. Canadian Car & Foundry Co., supra (adopting the federal rule on the subject). (a) What Constitutes "Doing Business" within the 

State: To determine whether a corporation is doing business within the state, the question should be asked—“Are its activities within the state casual and occasional or are they system-

SERVICE 

59 atic and regular?" If the former, it is not doing business within the state, but if the latter, it is. 

Tauza v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 220 N. Y. 259; 

Holzer v. Dodge Bros., 233 N. Y. 216. The business done here by the foreign corporation must be some substantial part of its main business. Lillibridge Inc., v. Juanita Hosiery Mills, Inc., 247 N. Y. 592. (1′) Maintaining an office in New York, with a representative, only for the purpose of soliciting business and getting credit information is not "doing business" within the meaning of the rule. Rarold v. Los Angeles First Nat. Savings and Trust Bank, 233 N. Y. S. 301. (2′) Conducting research work and training employees in New York is not "doing business" there. Brocia v. Franklin Plan Co., 257 N. Y. S. 167. 

(3′) The mere soliciting of orders in New York to be filled from the foreign state is not doing business here. Halpern v. Pennsylvania Lumber Industries, 

244 N. Y. S. 372; Hamlin v. Barrett, 246 N. Y. 554. (4′) But where a foreign corporation maintains permanent buying offices here to which it sends its officers to buy merchandise and transact business, it is doing business here, and process may properly be served on an officer who comes to the state for the purpose of buying goods, although for a temporary visit only. Merchandise Rep. Co. v. L. Oransky & Sons, 234 N. Y. S. 83. (5′) A foreign insurance corporation that maintains a representative in New York to adjust claims is doing business here. Henriques v. Gauthrod Marine Ins. Co., 199 N. Y. S 131. 

(b) How is Service Made: Service under C.P.A. 229 is made by delivering a copy of the summons, within the state, to one of the following persons: (1') Subd. 1: President, vice-president, treasurer, ass't treasurer, secretary, ass't secretary, or, an officer performing corresponding corresponding under a different name. functions (2) Subd. 2: Person or public officer designated for the purpose pursuant to law by certificate filed in the departments of state, banks or insurance, whose designation is in force, or, if a designee other than a public officer has died, resigned,

or left the state, then, to the Secretary of State as provided by the Gen. Corp. Law. (3') Subd. 3: If service cannot be effected under subd. 2, or if an officer named in subd. 1, cannot, with due diligence, be found in New York, then, to the cashier, director or managing agent. (Subd. 3 impliedly provides that resort should first be had to subd. 1 and subd. 2 before the officers named in subd. 3 may be served. Gursky v. Blair, 218 N. Y. 41; Taplinger v. Montgomery Ward Co., 186 N. Y. S. 77.)

E. When is Personal Service Complete: Service is com plete upon delivering to and leaving with the person (or persons) required to be served, the copy of the summons. Proof of personal service is made by the affidavit of the person who served the summons. R.C.P. 53 subd. 

1; City Ct. Act Sec. 29; Mun. Ct. Code, Sec. 22. Subd. 

2 of R.C.P. 53 provides for a written admission signed and acknowledged by the defendant served, as a second method of proof of service. 

(1) Matrimonial Actions: In matrimonial actions the affidavit must also state what knowledge affiant had of the identity of the person served and how he acquired such knowledge. R.C.P. 53 subd. 9. Randall v. Randall, 60 N. Y. S. 718. 

F. Service by Fraud: Trickery, as distinguished from guile, in effecting personal service, is sufficient cause to set such service aside. But even guile, if accompanied by force, (as for example, in gaining admittance) makes the service improper. Olsen v. McOnihe, 105 N. Y. S. 386. 

One instance of trickery rendering the service subject to being set aside is found in Bulkley v. Bulkley, 6 Abb. 

Pr. 307, where P, husband, handed D, wife, a sealed package, containing a divorce summons, telling her it contained a gift, and requesting her not to open it until she reached her destination in California. (Compare, Jackson v. Schuylkill, 156 N. Y. S. 219, where service was held valid although the summons was enclosed in an envelope which was opened by D.) An instance of misrepresentation not amounting to trickery is found

SERVICE 

61 in a recent case, Gumpert v. Hofman, 283 N. Y. S. 823, where the process server in attempting to serve D, a 

Buenos Aires physician temporarily sojourning in New 

York, represented himself to D, to be a "Dr. Goldman with a letter from the president of the County Medical Society to be personally delivered." (How far the Appellate Division was motivated in holding the service good by the fact that D was a non-resident who might leave the jurisdiction at any moment, is, of course, conjectural.) 

(1) Enticement Within Jurisdiction: 

Where a nonresident is enticed to come within the state by trickery or scheming for the purpose of serving process on him, such service is improper and will be set aside. Metcalf v. Clark et al, 41 Barb. 45; Garabettian v. Garabettian, 206 A. D. 502. 

G. Immunity from Service; Non-Resident Witnesses: On the broad grounds of public policy in order to promote justice and encourage voluntary attendance in our courts, we grant immunity from service of process within the state to non-residents who come here voluntarily either as suitors or as witnesses. Person v. Grier, 66 

N. Y. 124; Petrova v. Roberts, 245 N. Y. 518. It has recently been held that the immunity would extend to a party attending the argument of an appeal in his case. 

Chase Nat'l Bank v. Turner, 269 N. Y. 397, overruling 

Sampson v. Graves, 203 N. Y. S. 729. (1) Such a witness (or suitor) is privileged from service while coming to the court, while here attending court, and while returning home, provided such return is made within a reasonable time after his services in court are terminated. 

Bunce v. Humphrey, 214 N. Y. 21. (2) If the non-resident comes here voluntarily to assist in the administration of justice and for a private purpose of his own, the immunity does not attach. The same is true where he comes here voluntarily to assist at the trial, but while here attends to private business of his own. Finucane v. 

Warner, 194 N. Y. 160. Of course if the non-resident (otherwise immune) while here commits a wrong, he can be served with process in an action with respect to such wrong. Bank v. Di Martino, 168 N. Y. S. 310. (3) Compulsory Attendance: Where a non-resident is brought here under legal compulsion, he has no privilege. Netograph

Mfg. Co. v. Scrughany, 197 N. Y. 377. Thus, where D is brought here by extradition to answer an indictment, he may be validly served here, unless the extradition was a ruse to get him into the state. Adriance v. Lagrave, 59 N. Y. 110.

In Bunce v. Humphrey, supra, D came from Germany, voluntarily, to testify in another case. While here he was served with a subpoena requiring him to remain for the duration of that case (a criminal one). The service in the civil case was made before the jury in the criminal case had rendered its verdict, HELD, the service was improper, since D was not deprived of his character of a voluntary witness by service of a subpoena after his appearance in New York. (See also Ginsberg v. Heller, 140 N. Y. S. 1013,-"the immunity accorded to appellant began, when, with the intention of testifying in a civil action in this state, he left his home in Chicago. * * * The fact that some one, either by design or super-serviceable zeal, had him served with a subpoena to appear in the same cause would not take from the voluntariness of his appearance".)

H. Immunity from Service; Other Persons: Resident foreign ambassadors, ministers, consuls and vice-consuls, duly accredited to the United States Government, are immune from service of civil process in state court actions brought against them. Holbrook v. Henderson, 6 N. Y. Super. Ct. 619; Valario v. Thompson, 7 N.

Y. 576; U. S. Const. Art. III, Sec. 2. But a foreign diplomatic attache, accredited to another foreign power, who is merely passing through the United States, is not immune, under the Federal Judicial Code, from service of civil process, although on principles of international law, he is immune from arrest or other interference with personal freedom. Carbone v. Carbone, 206 N. Y. S. 

41. Note that Art. I, Sec. 6 of the U. S. Constitution providing that members of Congress shall in all cases, "except treason, felony and breach of the peace," be privileged from arrest during their attendance at a session of their respective houses, does not include immunity from service of civil process. Long v. Ansell, 55 Sup. 

Ct. Rep. 21.

I. Filing of Summons: In the usual case, the summons (and pleadings) need not be filed in actions in the Supreme, County or City Courts, until entry of the judgment roll. C.P.A. 100 provides, however, that the sum-

SERVICE 

63 mons and each pleading in an action must be filed with the clerk by the party in whose behalf it is served within five days after notice from the adverse party requiring such filing. Upon failure to comply with such notice, the court, may, in its discretion, order such summons or pleading be deemed abandoned, either absolutely, or, upon failure to file within a time, if any, permitted by the order. 

(1) Art. VI, Sec. 2 of the State Constitution and R.C.P. 16 

empower each Appellate Division to make its own rules for the filing of papers. (2) In the Municipal Court, the original copy of the summons and complaint must be filed with the clerk, together with proof of service, within three days after service. Mun. Ct. 

Code, Sec. 22. See Rule 33 Mun. Ct. Rules as to filing of other papers in the action. 

3. Substituted Service: Although the expression "substituted service" is sometimes used to indicate any non-personal service, technically, it refers to the method of service authorized and defined by C.P.A. 230 and 231. These sections are constitutional in so far as they permit an "in personam" judgment founded on substituted service. Every sovereignty has the power to regulate procedure of its courts with respect to citizens of the jurisdiction. Cont. Nat. Bank of 

Boston v. Thurber, 26 N. Y. S. 956, aff'd. 143 N. Y. 648. 

Rawstone v. Maguire, 256 N. Y. 204. Since, however, they are derogatory of the common law, they are strictly construed and there must be full compliance to confer jurisdiction upon the court. Korn v. Lipman, 201 N. Y. 404. An order of the court (ex parte) is always necessary to authorize substituted service.

A. Where Applicable: C.P.A. 230 specifies against whom substituted service may be had. These are: (1) A natural person who is a resident. (a) "Residence" means "domicile" under C.P.A. 

230. Rawstone v. Maguire, 265 N. Y. 204. You cannot have substituted service under this section against a non-resident. Sweeney v. Nat'l 

Assets Corp., 246 N. Y. S. 315. (b) A domestic corporation, other than a municipal corporation. (c) A joint stock association, or other unincor-

porated association, having a president or treasurer residing within the state. (See Sec. 18 of

Gen. Ass. Law, as amended Feb. 10, 1938, on the necessity of a joint stock association or business trust doing business in this state filing a certificate designating the Secretary of State as agent to accept service of process.) 

B. Proof Necessary: Before an order for substituted service will be made, satisfactory proof (by affidavit) must be presented to the court or judge making the order "that the plaintiff has been or will be unable, with due diligence, to make personal service of the summons within the state." C.P.A. 230. Despite the liberal wording of the section it has been held special in its nature intended only as a means of reaching runaway debtors whose place of sojourn cannot be ascertained after proper and diligent effort and those who remain at home in the state but avoid service. The section should not be successfully invoked against a defendant whose place of sojourn can be ascertained or who does not avoid service. Matter of Bloss, 130 Misc. 786, citing Ottman v. 

Daly, 7 N. Y. S. 897. 

(1) Contents of Affidavit: The affidavit should contain: 

(a') a statement showing the character of the action and that the action is not a matrimonial one (Purvis v. Purvis, 152 N. Y. S. 269) nor one for partition. Toole v. Larkin, 201 N. Y. S. 952. (In these actions, while service could be made. by publication in a proper case, substituted service does not permit entry of default judgment and hence is not available. C.P.A. 1167, 1033, 1058.) (b') proof, by evidentiary facts, of the efforts made to serve D personally and the failure of such efforts. Nichols v. Emmett, 107 N. Y. S. 663. (c') a statement (required by R.C.P. 61 as to every ex parte application) that no previous application for the relief sought has been made, (or if made, the disposition of same).

C. Manner of Making Service: The order directs how ser-

SERVICE 

65 vice shall be made and follows the requirements of C. 

P.A. 231. If D is a domestic corporation, or joint stock association, having a resident president or treasurer, the order will direct that service be made by leaving a copy of the summons and order at its principal office or place of business, if admittance can be obtained, and a person of proper age found who will receive it. If D is a natural person (resident) the same procedure is followed except that service is made at his or her residence. If in either case, admittance cannot be obtained, upon reasonable application, or if a suitable person cannot be found to accept the papers, then, service may be made by affixing the papers to the outer or other door of D's place of business or office, or of his residence, and mailing another copy of the papers to D's place of business or office, or to D's residence, if D is a natural person. (1) Substituted service on a natural person must be made at his residence. Service at any other place is fatal to jurisdiction. Fisk v. Bennett, 23 N. Y. S. 471. But if his residence cannot be found, service of the summons may be made "in such manner as the court may direct." C.P.A. 231 subd. 3. (2) Affixing: The papers are usually "affixed" by means of a small tack or adhesive tape. It has been held that binding the papers to D's doorknob by means of a rubber band is a sufficient "affixing." Mechanics Bank v. Newman, 244 N. Y. S. 

901 rev'g 244 N. Y. S. 529. (3) Mailing: Affixing without mailing is insufficient. Fromer v. Tanger, 121 Misc. 550. The papers should be properly inclosed in a post-paid wrapper, addressed to D at its principal place of business or office, or to D's residence, if D is a natural person. Although C.P.A. 231 provides that mailing should be effected by depositing in a post office, C.P.A. 

164 (as amended in 1934) provides that "deposit in a branch post-office or in a mail box regularly maintained by the U. S. Government is equivalent to deposit in a general post office," in all cases under the Civil Practice Act. See Korn v. Lipman, supra. 

D. Papers to be Filed: Within twenty days after the order has been made, it, and the papers upon which it was granted, must be filed with the clerk. (If a court order, as distinguished from a judge's order, it will be filed when made.) Within the same period of twenty days, proof of service made under the order must also be filed. 

If the filing is not done, or the service is not made, with-

in the time permitted, the order becomes inoperative. 

Service is complete ten days after proof thereof is filed. 

C.P.A. 231 as amended, in effect September 1, 1935.

E. In City Court: Substituted service, in a money judgment action, may be had if made within New York City. 

F. In Municipal Court: Substituted service may be made only upon a defendant residing, or having its place of business (if a domestic corporation) within New York City. 

4. Service by Publication: We saw that no state can exercise direct jurisdiction over persons or property outside its territorial limits, except where jurisdiction in personam is conferred in advance by consent of the parties. Our courts can acquire jurisdiction of a non-resident defendant in two ways: first, against his person, by valid, personal service upon him within the state (or his general appearance in the action), in which case, an "in personam" judgment may be rendered, or, second, by proceeding against property of the defendant within the state, in which case only an “in rem” judgment may be rendered. In such latter case, we saw, the defendant is not personally bound by the judgment beyond the property in question, unless he appears in the action. Where the court acquires jurisdiction over a nonresident through neither of these two methods, it acquires no jurisdiction over him at all. Pennoyer v. Neff, 95 U. S. 

714. C.P.A. 232 provides for the cases in which service by publication may be had, but it is to be remembered that such service alone will not support an "in personam" default judgment against a non-resident. Like C.P.A. 230 and 231,

C.P.A. 232 is strictly construed even though C.P.A. 2 says that the Civil Practice Act is to be liberally construed. Korn v. Lipman, supra.

A. Where Applicable (C.P.A. 232): Service by publication may be had upon: (1) a foreign corporation (whether "doing business" here or not); or a dissolved foreign corporation (2) a joint stock association or other unincorporated association having a non-resident president or treasurer (if resident, substituted service could be had) (3) a domestic corporation (even though dissolved) if after diligent effort, service cannot be made within

SERVICE 

67 the state upon any of the officers mentioned in C.P.A. 228 subd. 3. (In such case, substituted service may also be had, C.P.A. 231) (4) a non-resident individual (5) a person who after diligent inquiry remains unknown to plaintiff, or where plaintiff is unable to ascertain after such inquiry whether such person is or is not a resident (see Karchman v. Karchman, 230 N. Y. S. 856) (6) a resident defendant who has departed from the state or concealed himself therein, with intent to defraud creditors, or avoid service (in such case substituted service may also be had, C.P.A. 231) (7) A resident adult defendant who has been continuously absent from the state more than six months before the granting of the order of publication, and who has made no designation of a person upon whom process is to be served, or, if such designation no longer remains in force, or, if service on the designee cannot be made within the state after diligent effort (see discussion of C.P.A. 227, supra) (8) an infant or incompetent, whether resident or nonresident, upon whom complete personal service cannot be made within the state after due diligence under C.P.A. 225, subd. 1 and 2 (1') C.P.A. 1358, in effect September 1, 1935, now makes it obligatory upon the committee of an incompetent to designate the clerk of the court (of his appointment) to accept service on the committee's behalf in case the committee cannot be found and served within the state after due diligence. (9) a defendant, irrespective of residence, where the complaint demands judgment annuling a marriage, or for a divorce or a separation (the matrimonial domicile within the state gives the court jurisdiction quasi in rem) (10) a defendant, where the complaint demands judgment that the defendant be excluded from a vested or contingent interest or lien upon specific real or

personal property within the state; or where it appears by affidavit that a warrant of attachment granted in the action has been levied upon defendant's property within the state (the property within the state is the "res" that gives the court in rem jurisdiction) (11) a resident, or domestic corporation defendant, where the summons was delivered to the sheriff (for service to toll the statute of limitations under C.P.A. 17) (12) defendant stockholders of a corporation or joint stock association in actions against them permitted by statute.

B. Papers Necessary for Service by Publication: C.P.A. 

232 requires (a) a verified complaint, (b) an affidavit, (c) an order. (1) The Complaint: To prevent fraud, and since a person served by publication may never get actual notice of the action against him, the law requires that an order for service by publication be based upon a verified complaint showing a sufficient cause of action against the defendant. C.P.A. 232. Foster v. Elec. Heat Reg. Co., 16 Misc. 147. (2) The Affidavit: The affidavit must show by evidentiary facts that the case falls within one of the enumerated subdivisions of C.P.A. 232, and it is advisable to incorporate a general allegation that the plaintiff has been and will be unable with due diligence to make personal service within the state. 

Rose v. Heller, 179 N. Y. S. 821. (a) If the defendant is an infant, the affidavit must also set forth the name and last known residence of the father, mother or guardian or person having control of him, or with whom he resides, or by whom he is employed, or, that the name and residence, or either of such person or persons, is unknown to the applicant. C.P.A. 232. (b) If the action relates to a specific subject matter within the state, the affidavit must so show. 

Bryan v. Univ. Pub. Co., 112 N. Y. 382.

SERVICE 

69 (c) Attachment and Levy: If the action is in personam against a foreign corporation or nonresident to recover a sum of money only, the affidavit must contain proof that a warrant of attachment granted in the action has been levied upon the non-resident defendant's property within the state. In such a case, the levy is a pre-requisite to the making of an order of publication. C.P.A. 232. Dimmerling v. Andrews, 236 N. Y. 43; C.P.A. 493. 

(1′) Note: A strict reading of C.P.A. 232 would ex tend the requirement of a previous attachment upon property within the state to money-judgment actions against residents and domestic corporations, as well as non-residents and foreign corporations, and it has so been contended. Carmody, Vol. II, Sec. 700, citing Karchman v. 

Karchman, 230 N. Y. S. 856. If the legislature so intended to repudiate the reasoning in Continental Bank v. Thurber, 143 N. Y. 648, it is hard to see why C.P.A. 493 permitting entry of default judgment against resident, or domestic corporation, defendants in money-judgment actions, without proof of prior attachment, was not made reconcilable. Moreover, such a strict reading would result in a vicious circle in a case where plaintiff sought a warrant of attachment against a resident defendant continuously absent from the state for more than six months next before the granting of an order of publication (C.P.A. 903). In such case, the order for publication must precede the warrant or be granted concurrently with it. Sonnak v. Walker, 180 N. Y. S. 848. 

(d) Attachment and Levy-Equity Actions: As a general rule, equity acquires no jurisdiction in rem where there is no property of the nonresident defendant within the state. Bryan v. 

University Pub. Co., supra. (1') In equity actions relating to realty within the state, the court has a specific subject matter upon which to act and no attachment is necessary to give the court jurisdiction in rem of a non-resident defendant.

This rule has been applied in an action for

mortgage foreclosure (Schwinger v. Hickok, 53 N. Y. 280), in an action for partition (Bergen v. Wyckoff, 84 N. Y. 659), and in an action for specific performance of a contract relating to realty within the state. Garfein v. McInnis, 248 N. Y. 261.

The same is true of an action involving specific personal property within the state. 

Holmes v. Camp, 219 N. Y. 359. (Cf. Hanna v. Stedman, 230 N. Y. 326, holding interpleader action to be in personam.) (a') But in foreclosure actions where the complaint demands a personal deficiency judgment against D, personal service on him within the state is necessary (unless he voluntarily appears in the action) to permit entry of such judgment. Schwinger v. Hickok, supra. (2′) In Matrimonial Actions: In such actions, the "res" which gives equity jurisdiction, is not property within the state, but the marital status of the parties within the state. 

If the parties have become domiciled within the state, the "res" is located within the state, and if the defendant unjustifiably abandons the domicile, he or she may validly be served by publication and the marital status dissolved or annulled. Everett v. Everett, 22 A. D. 473. Such a judgment will be enforceable in every state in the 

Union under the "full faith and credit" clause. Haddock v. Haddock, 201 U. S. 

562. What is the extent of such "in rem” jurisdiction? In a divorce or separation action against a non-resident, served by publication, our court acquiring jurisdiction only because the matrimonial domicile is within the state, our court may "fix the marital status or relations" of the plaintiff, "it may decree the divorce or the separation and it may fix the custody of the children, at least if they are within the state." Mat-

SERVICE 

71 thews v. Matthews, 247 N. Y. 32. 

(a') Alimony in Matrimonial Actions: Where ali mony or counsel fees are demanded, however, equity can act in personam against the person of the defendant, only if there is personal service within the state or defendant's general appearance. Rigney v. Rigney, 127 N. Y. 408.

Or it can act quasi in rem, by first acquiring jurisdiction of his property within the state by sequestration under enabling statutes. C.P.A. 

1171-a. Matthews v. Matthews, 240 N. Y. 28; 

247 N. Y. 32. 

(3) The Order: The order may be a judge's order or a court order (C.P.A. 234) and should follow the language of R.C.P. 50 almost verbatim. (A court order should be used, since R.C.P. 52 requires filing of all papers anyway.)

D. Manner of Making Service by Publication: There are two ways of making service under an order of publication: 

(1) Literal Compliance with the Order: The usual way of making the service is to do as the order directs, taking care to act within three months after the making of the order. R.C.P. 51. (a) File the papers with the clerk, i. e. the summons, complaint, affidavit and order. R.C.P. 52.

If the order was a court order, these papers will already be filed pursuant to the "enter" instruction contained at the foot of the order. Wilson v. Banque Francaise, 208 N. Y. S. 213. 

(b) Mail a copy of the summons, complaint, order and notice under R.C.P. 52 to the defendant at the address specified in the order (unless the order dispenses with mailing, for good cause shown, R.C.P. 50, 52). (1') The notice required by R.C.P. 52 is in the following form: "To (defendant): The foregoing summons is served upon you by publication pursuant to an order of (naming the judge and his official title) dated the day of 

19, and filed with the complaint in the office of the clerk off...……………………………………..

at ." If the action is brought to recover a judgment affecting the title to, or the possession, use or enjoyment of real property, the notice must also state the object of the action and briefly describe the property affected. (c) Begin publication of the summons and notice required by R.C.P. 52. The order of publication directs such publication in two designated newspapers, published in the English language, not less than once a week for six consecutive weeks. Service is complete on the forty-second day after the first publication. R.C.P. 51. (1') If publication is made in a newspaper not designated in the order, all other proceedings being otherwise regular, the irregularity may be corrected in the discretion of the court by approving the paper in which publication was made. Valz v. Sheepshead Bay Bungalow Corp., 249 N. Y. 122. (d) Make and file affidavit of mailing to defendant, and procure and file after publication, the two printers' affidavits of publication. R.C.P. 53, subd. 7 and 8. (2) Personal Service without the State in Lieu of Publication (C.P.A. 233): In all cases where publication of the summons is ordered, "personal" service of a copy of the summons and complaint and notice without the state is equivalent to notice by publication and mailing. Service must be made by either a resident or citizen of New York or a sheriff, undersheriff, deputy sheriff, constable, bailiff, etc. of the political subdivision in which the service is made, or by an officer authorized by New York laws (R. 

P.L. 299-301) to take acknowledgments of deeds to be recorded in New York, or a qualified attorney and/or counsellor at law, solicitor, advocate or barrister in the state or county where such service is made, or by a U. S. marshal or deputy marshal. (a) If service is made under C.P.A. 233, the notice under 

R.C.P. 52 is changed so that the words "without the state of New York" are substituted for the words "by publication." 

(b) Time Limitations: Under C.P.A. 233, (as last amended in September 1936), service without the state must be

SERVICE 

73 made, and proof thereof filed, within sixty days, after the order is granted, else, the order becomes inoperative. Service is complete ten days after such proof of service is filed. (The amendments to C.P.A. 233, and to 

C.P.A. 231, were designed to overcome the confusion caused by Winter v. Winter, 256 N. Y. 113, discussed in the introductory pages of this outline.) 

E. Service by Publication in City Court: Such service may be had. The provisions of the Civil Practice Act control. City Ct. Act, Sec. 51. Defendant, however, has ten days instead of six, to appear.

F. Service by Publication in Municipal Court: Such service may be had in certain cases. See Mun. Ct. Code, Sec. 21. 

5. "Personal Service" Outside of State Without An Order 

(C.P.A. 235): 

A. Where Permissible: Where the complaint demands judgment affecting an interest in or lien upon specific real or personal property within the state, or, where levy has been made under a warrant of attachment on property of the defendant within the state, or in a matrimonial action, the summons may be served outside the state without an order. The complaint must be served with the summons. In case D is a foreign corporation, it may be served under C.P.A. 235 by serving the same officers required to be served under C.P.A. 229. Howard Converters Inc. v. French Art Mills, 273 N. Y. 190. 

(1) When Complete: Personal service outside the state without an order is complete ten days after filing proof thereof. Such proof must be filed within thirty days after the service. C.P.A. 235 as amended 

September 1, 1935. (a) The Winter v. Winter case did not effect C.P.A. 235 as it formerly read, but the 1935 amendment added the provision making it incumbent upon plaintiff to file proof of service within thirty days after the service. (b) Who May Serve: Those persons who may serve under 

C.P.A. 233, may serve outside the state under C.P.A. 235. 

6. Service of Summons on Partnership. Using Name of De ceased Person: C.P.A. 233, reads: "In case any action or proceeding shall be brought, founded in whole or in part

upon any transaction growing out of a business conducted under the name of a deceased person, as provided by subdivision three, section eighty and section eighty-one of the Partnership Law, and the name of the deceased person is stated as a defendant, the process and papers therein may be served on any person or persons using such name with like effect as though such person or persons had been named as defendant by his or their own respective names, and with the same effect as though all such persons were served with process, and the process and all papers may be amended by substituting the name or names of the person or persons using the name of such deceased, and no action or proceeding shall fail, abate or in any manner be hindered by the name of such deceased being so used." 

7. Service under Vehicle and Traffic Law (Sec. 52-52a): Where an injury occurs on a public highway within New York, by reason of the negligent operation of a motor vehicle by a non-resident (or by a resident who has been absent more than thirty days) or by someone operating the vehicle with the express or implied consent of such non-resident or absentee resident, service of process may be made on the Secretary of State, with the same legal effect as if served upon the defendant personally within the state. The constitutionality of this statute rests upon the fact that the procedure as to service is calculated to make it reasonably certain that defendant will be apprised of the commencement of any action against him. Shusheraba v. Ames, 255 N. Y. 

490; Wuchter v. Pizzuti, 276 U. S. 13.

A. Procedure: Service is made by leaving a copy of the summons with a $2.00 filing fee with the Secretary of State and sending to the defendant, by registered mail, notice of such service and a copy of the summons. P's return receipt, affidavit of compliance with the statute, and a copy of the summons and complaint must be filed in the clerk's office in which the action is pending to complete service. If D refuses to sign a receipt for the registered letter containing the summons and notice, the service is insufficient. Dwyer v. Schalek, 248 N. Y. S. 355

However, the summons and complaint may be served personally on D outside the state (in lieu of mailing) after a copy and the $2.00 filing fee has been left with the Secretary of State. The persons who may make such personal service are substantially the same as those mentioned in C.P.A. 233 and C.P.A. 235. Such service is complete ten days after filing proof thereof.

SERVICE 

75 

B. In What Courts Permissible: The action contemplated by V. & 

T. Law, Sec. 52-52a, can be brought in any court of the state.

In courts of limited jurisdiction such as the City or Municipal Court, the Secretary of State (who is really made by the statute D's agent to accept service) cannot be personally served in Albany, because then the service would be invalid to confer jurisdiction. Heihs v. Reinberg, 243 N. Y. S. 284; Bischoff v. 

Schnepp, 249 N. Y. S. 249. Delivery of the Municipal or City

Court summons, however, in New York City to a deputy Secretary of State in the office of the State department in that city is sufficient. Stoibor v. Marinacci, 254 N. Y. S. 620; Teplitzky v. 

Lipman, 256 N. Y. S. 410; Salzman v. Atrean, 254 N. Y. S. 288. 

Again, in 'Bessan v. Public Service, 237 N. Y. S. 689 it was held that the City Court of New York had concurrent jurisdiction with the Supreme Court, within the City of New York, and since the Secretary of State is a state officer, he is deemed to be constructively present in every part of the state, and hence process mailed to him from New York City is service made within the territorial jurisdiction of the City Court. A similar result was reached in Marcus v. Day, 248 N. Y. S. 649, where it was held that personal service is not required on the Secretary of State, but merely that "process be left at his office" with the statutory fee; hence, without resorting to the fiction that the 

Secretary of State is constructively present in New York City, process mailed to the Secretary at Albany, from New York City gives the City Court jurisdiction. 

8. Right to Defend After Default Judgment: Where the sum mons is served other than personally within the state, and in any other manner than "personally" outside the state, and D defaults, he, or his representative, may be permitted to open up the default, on good cause shown, and defend on the merits, at any time before judgment. Except in a divorce case, or where the contrary is expressly prescribed by law, D may similarly open up his default and defend on the merits even after entry of judgment, and within one year after personal service upon him of written notice of such judgment, or, if such a notice has not been served then, within seven years after the filing of the judgment roll. 

C.P.A. 217. The section further provides: "If the defense is successful and the judgment or any part thereof has been collected or otherwise enforced, such restitution as the court directs may thereupon be compelled; but the title to property sold to a purchaser in good faith, pursuant to a direction contained in the judgment or by virtue of an execution issued upon the same, shall not be affected thereby. So

much of this section as requires the court to allow a defendant to defend an action, after final judgment, does not apply to an action for partition." C.P.A. 108 (which applies even in cases of personal service within the state) provides that the court in its discretion, and upon such terms as justice requires, at any time within one year thereof, may relieve a party from a judgment, order or other proceeding taken against him through "his mistake, inadvertance, surprise or other excusable neglect." It is questionable whether C.P.A. 

108 is to be considered a statute of limitation. 

Ladd v. 

Stevenson, 112 N. Y. 325; Schlimmer v. N. Y. O. & W. Rrd., 212 A. D. 782; Malicky v. Rosenberg, 273 N. Y. S. 818.

CHAPTER V. 

APPEARANCE IN THE ACTION 

1. Generally: Defendant "appears" in the action in response to service upon him of process. But the appearance may be voluntary, obviating the necessity of service, or curing defective service. "A voluntary, general appearance of the defendant is equivalent to personal service of the summons upon him." C.P.A. 237. It is to be noted however that the general appearance is deemed the equivalent of personal service, with regard to the running of the statute of limitations, only as of the date upon which the notice of appearance was served. Guilford v. Brody, 252 N. Y. S. 722. By virtue of C.P.A. 236 an adult defendant may appear in person, unless judicially declared incompetent. 

2. How Effected: A general appearance may be effected in the following ways:

A. By serving upon P's attorney, within twenty days after service of the summons, exclusive of the day of service, a notice of appearance. C.P.A. 237. (1) The notice of appearance (see "Forms," appendix) advises P's attorney that D appears in the action by the undersigned attorney and usually demands that a copy of the complaint and all papers in the action be served upon D's attorney at the office address given in the notice. (2) If a copy of the complaint is not served with the summons, a notice of appearance entitles D only to notice of the subsequent proceedings, unless with the notice he demands the service of a copy of the complaint (C.P.A. 238). The demand may be incorporated in the notice of appearance or served separately. C.P.A. 257. (3) But a mere demand for a copy of the complaint is not an appearance, either general or special.
