In order to make such a demand there must first be an appearance. Muslusky v. Lehigh Coal Co., 225 N. Y. 584. 

B. By serving a copy of the answer, if a copy of the complaint has been served with the summons. C.P.A. 237.

C. By serving a notice of motion raising an objection to the complaint in point of law. (1) Not every motion addressed to the complaint raises an objection in point of law. A motion for judgment dismissing the complaint under R.C.P. 106 or 

107 does raise such an objection and hence constitutes a general appearance under C.P.A. 237. Robinson v. Robinson, 204 N. Y. S. 329. But regulatory or corrective motions, such as to compel P to separately state and number causes of action (R.C.P. 

90) or to make the complaint more definite and certain (R.C.P. 102) are not motions raising objections to the complaint in point of law and do not constitute a general appearance. 225 Fifth Ave. Corp. v. 

Freeman, 199 N. Y. S. 519. (2) Other Acts by D as Constituting a General Appearance: D does not appear generally where he moves to set aside an attachment (Zobriskie v. Second 

Nat'l. Bank, 198 N. Y. S. 482) or to vacate an injunction. Regelmann v. So. Shore Traction Co., 123 

N. Y. S. 705. Cf. Bruman v. Bruman, 248 N. Y. S. 

181. He does appear generally, however, where he moves in a federal court to remove a state court action. Farmer v. Nat'l Life Ass'n., 138, N. Y. 265.

D. By D, or his attorney, coming into court and participating on the merits of the proceeding or action, (i.e. on issues, other than the court's jurisdiction) notwithstanding that such appearance is stated to be only special. Citizens Trust Co. v. Prescott, 223 N. Y. S. 191. (1) Thus D appears generally where he comes into court and opposes, on the merits, the granting of an injunction. Metal Shelter Co. v. Fosdick, 172 N. Y. S. 

272; Legler v. Legler, 278 N. Y. S. 804. (2) The same is true where D in a divorce action crossexamines P's witnesses as to the issue of recrimination. Henderson v. Henderson, 247 N. Y. 428. 

3. Effect of Voluntary General Appearance: We saw that such effect is to give the court jurisdiction in personam and to cure defects of service. In Reed v. Chilson, 142 N. Y. 152,

A commenced an action in New York against B and C,

APPEARANCE 

79 non-residents. The summons was served by publication after a warrant of attachment was issued, but no property was levied upon. Of course, at this stage of the proceedings, the New York court had no jurisdiction. B and C engaged an attorney in New York to protect their interests. 

The attorney served a general notice of appearance and later served an answer setting up as a defense that the court had no jurisdiction over the persons of the defendants. It was held that the general appearance gave the 

New York court jurisdiction over B and C, curing the defective service. See also A.B.C. Steel Equip. Co. v. Schreiber Co., 268 N. Y. S. 828; Meyers v. American Locomotive 

Co., 201 N. Y. 163. It must be remembered, however, that a voluntary, general appearance does not operate to waive any objection as to the court's jurisdiction over the subject matter of the action. C.P.A. 279. Gardner v. Condon, 190 

N. Y. S. 756; Goodale Real Est. Corp. v. Subridge Holding 

Corp., 248 N. Y. S. 245. 

4. Special Appearance: When a summons has been served on a defendant who does not intend to submit himself to the jurisdiction of the court over his person, he may elect to remain out of court; but he need not await until the entry of judgment against him by default. He may appear specially. Goldey v. Morning News, 156 U. S. 518; Reed v.

Chilson, supra. "The special appearance is recognized only for the purpose of raising the question whether the court has obtained jurisdiction over the defendant personally or through his property." Muslusky v. Lehigh Valley 

Coal Co., supra. 

A. Illustration: A commences an action against B.

A's process server serves X, and makes an affidavit that he served B. B may appear specially for the sole purpose of setting aside the service, although had default judgment been entered against him it could likewise have been set aside. 

5. Formal Notice of Appearance: Besides a means of subjecting the defendant to jurisdiction, the notice of appearance, when served generally by defendant's attorney, secures to the defendant the right of having notice of every step in the action served upon his attorney. The form of notice of ap-

pearance contains the same particulars as to the defendant's attorney's office, etc., as are prescribed with reference to the summons. C.P.A. 11. 

A. If defendant appears specially, the notice should so state and should also state the purpose of the special appearance.

B. Where a notice of appearance is served for a defendant, after the service of a summons upon him without a complaint, it should also contain a demand that the complaint be served. Plaintiff must then serve a copy of the complaint within twenty days or else the action may be dismissed. C.P.A. 257. (1) If this demand be omitted, defendant's attorney is entitled only to notice of subsequent proceedings. 

C.P.A 238. (2) If after appearing, defendant fails to plead by answer, judgment may be taken against him by default. C.P.A. 486, subd. 2. His notice of appearance, however, would still entitle him to receive notice of all subsequent proceedings. Martine v. 

Lowenstein, 68 N. Y. 456. 

6. Appearance and Waiver in Mortgage Foreclosure Action: 

Where P sues to foreclose a mortgage on real property and D (e. g. a subordinate lienor) has no defense on the merits, it is usual for such D to appear in the action but waive notice of all subsequent proceedings except notice of sale and surplus money proceedings. 

7. Appearance by Foreign Sovereignty: A representative or branch of a foreign government, in his or its official capacity, is immune from service of process in our state courts. 

Where he or it voluntarily appears, such appearance is regarded merely as a consent to be sued, and may be withdrawn under principles of international comity. De Simone v. Transporte Maritimes de Estado, 191 N. Y. S. 864. Cf.

Herman v. Apetz, 224 N. Y. S. 389; Carbone v. Carbone, 123 Misc. 656; Issaia v. Bank, 266 N. Y. 37. 

8. Appearance by Infant: An infant cannot voluntarily appear in a judicial proceeding. Weiser v. Landess, 172 N. Y. S. 337. 

9. Unauthorized Appearance: The general principles of

APPEARANCE 

81 agency do not obtain and even where the defendant does not authorize his attorney to appear in his behalf, a judgment entered thereon is binding and the injured defendant is left to his remedy against the attorney, unless (a) the attorney is pecuniarily irresponsible, or (b) the appearance is fraudulently entered, or (c) the defendant is a non-resident. Vilas v. Plattsburgh 

Co., 123 N. Y. 440. In these three cases the unauthorized appearance may be withdrawn by permission of the court upon a motion in the same action made for that purpose, but not otherwise. Equitable Trust Co. v. 

Halim, 234 N. Y. S. 37; Brown v. Nichols, 42 N. Y. 26. 

10. Time for Service: In Supreme Court actions, a notice of appearance must be served within twenty days after service of the summons is complete, exclusive of the day of service. 

C.P.A. 237. In the City Court, the time is six days, except in cases of service by publication when the time is ten days, exclusive of the day of service. City Court Act, Sec. 

49-50. In the Municipal Court, the time is five days, exclusive of the day of service. If the last day falls on a Saturday, Sunday or legal holiday, defendant may answer on the next business day. 

11. Authority for Appearance of Attorneys in Real Property 

Actions: Where a non-resident defendant appears by attorney in a real property action, the attorney must file and serve written authority from his client, duly acknowledged, and may require from plaintiff's attorney written evidence (informal) of his authority to bring the action. R.C.P. 55; 

Stock v. Mann, 230 N. Y. S. 107. 

12. Substitution, Removal and Suspension of Attorney: An at torney of record for an adult party or corporation may be changed by the filing with the clerk of the court in which the action or proceeding may be pending of a written stipulation for such change of attorney signed by the attorney and signed and acknowledged by the party. The making and filing of such a stipulation shall have the same effect as an order of substitution. An attorney may also be changed by order. R.C.P. 56. If an attorney dies, is removed or is suspended or otherwise becomes disabled before judgment in an action, no further proceedings can be taken against the

party for whom he appeared until thirty days after notice given to such party to appoint another attorney. C.P.A. 

240. Moreover, if an attorney dies, is removed or suspended within the time fixed by law to take an appeal, without having served a notice of appeal, such notice may be served within sixty days from the date of death, removal or suspension. C.P.A. 99 subd. 3 (added as of Sept. 1, 1938).

CHAPTER VI. 

DRAWING THE COMPLAINT AND FRAMING THE ACTION 

1. Generally: From the pleadings of the parties the court determines the issues of fact and law involved. C.P.A. 275 provides that pleadings are to be liberally construed with a view to substantial justice between the parties. While this rule of interpretation changes the common law rule of strict adherence to prescribed forms of action, it does not allow any such degree of liberality as would tend to ambiguity and confusion. The fundamental principal of pleading is that the party is required to make a plain and concise statement of the elemental or ultimate facts constituting his cause of action or defense, without regard to formal precedent, but avoiding, on the one hand, conclusions of law, and, upon the other, the statement of his evidence. C.P.A. 241, 255; Sherman v. Int. Pub., Inc., 214 A. D. 437; Joannes v. 

Lamborn, 237 N. Y. 207. 

2. Kinds of Pleadings in a Civil Action: There are only three permissable pleadings, to wit, the plaintiff's complaint, (C.P.A. 254), the defendant's answer, (C.P.A. 260) and plaintiff's reply to a counterclaim, if any, contained in the answer (C.P.A. 272). (In the Municipal Court, a counterclaim is deemed denied, so that plaintiff need not reply unless ordered to do so.) 

3. The Complaint: This is the first pleading on the plaintiff's part. It is required to contain "a statement of each cause of action" besides certain formal requisites that we will consider later. C.P.A. 255. The complaint is deemed to allege what by reasonable and fair intendment can be implied therein. Marie v. Garison, 83 N. Y. 14. Thus, where P's complaint set forth a copy of a note in full, bearing the words "Indorsed, Charles E. Monell," the complaint was deemed to allege that Monell was liable to P as an endorser. 

Moore v. Monell Co., 58 N. Y. S. 430. But an allegation that a note "was duly protested for non-payment" cannot be stretched to embrace the non-alleged fact that D indorsers were given due notice of dishonor, since one act does not necessarily assume the other. Cook v. Warren,

88 N. Y. 37. Again, an allegation by P assignee that the non-negotiable instrument in suit was assigned to him for value, cannot be stretched to include the non-alleged fact that the note was made for a valuable consideration. Spear v. Ass. Prod. & Ref. Corp., 199 N. Y. S. 84. It is to be noted, in the last case, that if the non-negotiable note were set forth, and on its face bore "for value received", or similar words, the complaint would have stated a valid cause of action. See Deyo v. Thompson, 65 N. Y. S. 459. 

A. Conclusions of Law; Illustrations: P may not allege generally that D "owes" him, or "is indebted" to him.

The facts showing the debt or obligation must be set forth. Sampson v. Furniture Co., 66 N. Y. S. 815. Similarly, P may not allege that he "is entitled" to certain rents and profits; he must allege his title and its sources. 

Sheridan v. Jackson, 72 N. Y. 170. Again, an allegation that D "wrongfully detains" a chattel sought to be recovered by P, in the absence of an allegation of ownership in P, is bad as a legal conclusion, and is not cured by D's denial, in the answer, of P's ownership. Scofield v. Whitelegge, 49 N. Y. 259. Where P, in an action to recover on a theft policy, alleges performance of the conditions of the policy, but in no way sets forth the policy or its material provisions, P is not alleging the facts of his cause of action and the complaint should be dismissed. Bandler v. Globe Ins. Co., 199 N. Y. S. 707. An allegation that a town supervisor (D) made "illegal payments" is a legal conclusion where P, taxpayer, states no facts in his complaint upon which the illegality may be predicated. Daly v. Haight, 148 N. Y. S. 46. That a party to an action is not a "real party in interest" (General Invest. Co., v. I. R. T. Co., 193 N. Y. S. 892) and that decedent "died intestate" (Velsor v. Freeman, 194 

N. Y. S. 191) are both conclusions of law.

B. Ultimate Facts; Illustrations: That a person was of 

"unsound mind" is a statement of an ultimate fact. Riggs v. American Tract Society, 84 N. Y. 330. The same is true of an allegation that D "committed adultery"; that a "valuable consideration" was paid or given for a nonnegotiable bill of exchange (California Packing Corp. v.

Kelly Storage Co., 228 N. Y. 49) or that D "converted to his own use" property held in common tenancy. Thayer v. Gile, 42-Hun. 268 (where the court said, "A lent B a dollar is the fact, B owes A a dollar is the law. A converted B's hay is a fact; B's liability to A, the law. But from a given state of facts the law will pronounce that A converted B's hay. Is the statement of the conversion, therefore, the statement of a conclusion of law? It is rather the statement of a fact, ascertained by the rules of law"). That P's injuries were caused by his own negligence and in no way through D's negligence, is also a statement of an ultimate fact (Robinson v. Ocean S.S. 

Co., 147 N. Y. S. 310) as is the statement that it was "mutually agreed". Grossman v. Schenker, 206 N. Y. 466. (1) Rule in Negligence Actions: Plaintiff in a negligence action must allege the ultimate facts showing D's negligence, or from which the latter may be inferred. Schweitzer v. Mindlin, 248 N. Y. 560. But plaintiff need not allege the ultimate facts showing his freedom from contributory negligence; he may state generally that he was free from contributory negligence. On the trial, however, in order to make out a prima facie case, P must prove D's negligence and his own freedom from contributory negligence. In an action for wrongful death and in an action by an injured employee against his employer the defense of contributory negligence of the deceased or the employee is a defense to be pleaded and proven by the defendant. Emp. Liab. Law Sec. 5. Hall v. Telephone 

Co., 220 N. Y. 299.

C. Effect of Evidentiary Matter: A complaint composed solely of evidentiary matter does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. De Cordova v. Sanville, 214 N. Y. 662. If evidence is commingled with the ultimate facts, a motion to strike out the evidentiary matter is proper. R.C.P. 103; Peabody v. Ins. Co., 200 N. Y. S. 612. 

D. Abbreviated Forms of Pleading: 

(1) Pleading Performance of Conditions Precedent 

(R.C.P. 92): Where a right of action in contract rests upon a party's performance thereunder, such performance would ordinarily have to be pleaded (by ultimate facts) in order to state a cause of action, or ultimate facts would have to be alleged

excusing the non-performance. On the first point, R.C.P. 92 permits the party suing on the contract to plead the conclusion "that he, or the person whom he represents, duly performed all the conditions of such contract on his part." (a) Since R.C.P. 92 is derogatory of the common law, strict compliance with its language is necessary, and the use of the word "duly" is one of substance and not of form. 

Clemens v. American Fire Ins. Co., 75 N. Y. S. 484.

Thus, an allegation that P "actually performed” is not equivalent to “duly performed." Marcus Co. v. Weinbros 

Co., 147 N. Y. S. 576. Similarly, where a complaint alleges that P “duly performed all the terms and obligations of his contract on his part”, there is no compliance with Rule 92. Berger v. Urban Mot. P. etc., Inc., 201 

N. Y. S. 489. (b) Nor may P plead D's specific breach by alleging that D failed to perform all the conditions on his part to be performed; P must set forth the ultimate facts showing such breach. Baby Show v. Crowell, 161 N. Y. S. 205. 

(2) Pleading Instrument for Payment of Money (R.C.P. 

94): A cause of action upon such an instrument may be pleaded by setting forth a copy of it with a statement that there is "due" thereon from the adverse party a stated sum. Tooker v. Arnoux, 76 

N. Y. 397. (a) However, where the liability of the adverse party is conditional and dependent upon extrinsic facts outside the instrument, those facts must be pleaded and the bare annexation of a copy of the instrument is insufficient. Tooker v. Arnoux, supra (where it was held that setting forth in a complaint an instrument reading "Pay to A the sum of $500.00 out of the money to be realized from the sale of X's house" is insufficient where the complaint fails to allege the sale of X's house and the realization by defendant of the proceeds thereof). See also Broome v. Taylor, 76 N. Y. 564. 

(3) Pleading Judgment or Determination (R.C.P. 95): 

Ordinarily, all the facts and circumstances showing the court's jurisdiction to render the judgment in question would have to be pleaded. R.C.P. 95 permits the pleader to set forth the conclusion that the judgment was "duly given," (not "duly entered"), but if this be controverted, complete proof

must be given. Peo. ex rel Bachelor v. Bacon, 55 

N. Y. S. 1045. The rule applies to the determinations of an officer or court of special jurisdiction. 

Cohen Iron Works v. Jaffe, 190 N. Y. S. 476 (arbi trators' award). 

(4) Pleading an Account (C.P.A. 246): Where a plead ing is drawn in an action on an account stated, the party may allege the account without setting forth the items thereof. C.P.A, 246. If the adverse party makes written demand therefor, the pleader must furnish an itemized account within ten days of such demand, unless within five days of receipt of the demand, he moves to vacate same in whole or in part. If he fails to furnish the items, he may be precluded from offering evidence on the trial as to the account. If the complaint is verified the copy of the account must be verified. R.C.P. 115, 117 as amended in 1936. 

(5) Pleading in Slander and Libel Actions (R.C.P. 96): 

At common law, and ordinarily, the complaint in a defamation action must allege that the words complained of were spoken, or written, concerning P, and the identical words must be pleaded. In case of oral defamation (slander), P must also allege that hearers understood the words spoken in the derogatory sense maintained by P. Where the words are ambiguous, or innuendo is used, the true sense of the words, as maintained by P, must be alleged and all extrinsic facts showing the words to be slanderous as to P must also be alleged. R.C.P. 96 provides a short form of statement on this last point. Under the rule, P may allege, generally, that the defamatory statements were spoken concerning him without going into the extrinsic facts leading to such conclusion. See Gross v. Cantor, 270 N. Y. 93. (a) However, P must still allege facts tending to explain doubtful expressions and must still set forth the actual defamatory words. Durante v. Contanti, 224 N. Y. S. 520. (b) R.C.P. 97 provides that when a woman brings an action for words imputing unchastity, she need not allege nor prove special damages. (The general rule of dam-

ages is that where P claims special damages the facts giving rise to same must be alleged in the complaint. 

Kurak v. Traiche, 226 N. Y. 266; Gumb v. 23rd St. 

Ry. Co., 114 N. Y. 411.) (c) C.P.A. 338 provides: "In an action for libel or slander, the defendant may prove mitigating circumstances including the sources of his information and the grounds for his belief, notwithstanding that he has pleaded or attempted to prove a justification." Were it not for this section, if a defendant failed in his defense of justification he could not set up mitigation. Haines v. 

N. Y. Evening Journal, 240 N. Y. S. 734. See also 

Fleckenstein v. Friedman, 266 N. Y. 19. 

(6) Pleading Private Statute (R.C.P. 98): In pleading a private statute, it need not be set forth in full but a mere reference to it by title, chapter number, and year of passage is sufficient. Of course, New 

York courts take judicial notice of the common law, public statutes of New York, and the constitutions of the United States and of the state of New York. 

Private statutes or those of a foreign state or nation must be pleaded and proved. C.P.A. 391; Lemye v. 

Sirker, 224 N. Y. S. 130.

E. Goods Sold and Delivered; Work, Labor and Services under C.P.A. 255a: In an action to recover for goods sold and delivered, or for work, labor and services performed, the plaintiff may annex to his verified complaint an itemized schedule of the goods sold or the work performed, and the reasonable value, or agreed price, of each item. The defendant in a verified answer must indicate specifically the items, if any, which he disputes in respect to delivery, performance, reasonable value, or agreed price. (1) Where the complaint is drawn in compliance with this section, a general denial in the answer does not raise any issue as to the delivery, reasonable value or agreed price of any item specified. The defendant in order to raise such issue must indicate specifically the item which he disputes in respect to those questions. Dairymen's League v. Levy 

Dairy Co., 233 N. Y. S. 433. (2) The purpose of the section is to narrow the field of controverted facts. Dynamo & Motor Exch., Inc. v. Brown 

Auto Hospital Inc., 250 N. Y. S. 468.

F. Pleading Where Corporation is Party (R.C.P. 93):

Where a corporation is a party, the complaint must state that it is a corporation, whether it is a domestic or foreign corporation, and, if a foreign corporation, under the laws of what state or government it was formed. 

Such allegation need not be proved on the trial unless the defendant by a verified answer specifically controverts it. Any misnomer in the corporate name is waived unless specifically pleaded.

G. Specific Performance: In an action brought to compel specific performance of a contract, plaintiff need not allege that he has no adequate remedy at law. Wasserman v. Manson, 233 N. Y. S. 80. But where there is a possibility that there may be an adequate remedy at law, the negative should be pleaded. Van Decar v. Streeter, 240 

N. Y. S. 492.

H. Construction of Allegations in Verified Pleadings 

(C.P.A. 276): The allegations or denials in a verified. pleading must, in form, be stated to be made by the party pleading. Unless stated to be made upon information and belief, they must be regarded for all purposes, including a criminal prosecution, as having been made upon the knowledge of the person verifying the pleading. An allegation that the party has not sufficient knowledge or information to form a belief with respect to a matter, must, for the same purposes, be regarded as an allegation that the person verifying the pleading has not such knowledge or information. See Penal Law Section 1620. (1) R.C.P. 93, providing that there must be a specific allegation that the party is a corporation is to be read with C.P.A. 

276, and the special allegation, like any other allegation, may be made on information and belief. Joint Stock Co., v. 

National City Bank, 240 N. Y. 368.

I. Formal Requisites of Complaint (C.P.A. 255): 

(1) Title of Action: The name of the court and the names of all the parties, plaintiff and defendant, must appear on the complaint; if the action be in the Supreme Court, the name of the county in which plaintiff desires the trial to be had must also be designated. (2) Demand for Judgment: The last formal element of a complaint is a demand for judgment, in which

the plaintiff asks for an award of the relief on the facts stated, to which he thinks himself entitled. 

C.P.A. 255. If there is no demand for judgment, and defendant does not answer, plaintiff cannot enter default judgment. C.P.A. 479. 

(a) Alternative Relief-Legal and Equitable: Whenever the facts show ground for both legal and equitable relief, both may be demanded; or alternative relief may be demanded, provided the types of relief are consistent. 

Hasberg v. Moses, 81 A. D. 199. Thus where P suspects that he has been dealing with an agent for an undisclosed principal he may plead against the agent individually, or as agent for an undisclosed principal. 

See note to Munn v. Cook, 24 Abb. N. C. 314. In Kass v. Garment Center Realty Co., 205 N. Y. S. 94, an action to reform a lease, P alleged alternatively that it was executed either through a mutual mistake or through P's mistake and D's fraud. It was held that

D was not entitled to compel P to definitely allege whether he would seek relief upon the first or the second grounds, P having a right to make these allegations in the alternative, and, upon the trial, to obtain relief in accordance with the proof. See also Johansson v. Kamp, 207 N. Y. S. 468. 

(b) Mistake in Remedy Demanded: C.P.A. 111 provides: 

"Whenever in any action or special proceeding it shall appear at any stage of the proceedings, or upon appeal that the appropriate remedy upon the facts pleaded or alleged, or proved, is different from that asked for in the pleadings or corresponding papers, the proceedings may be amended upon such terms as may be just, if jurisdiction exists to grant the proper remedy, and may be continued and determined by the court and at the term where then pending, or remitted to the proper term or court to be disposed of, in order that the relief may be finally granted which is appropriate to the facts, to the same extent as if the application had been in the first instance for the relief granted. The court may correct by amendment all defects and irregularities in matters of form or procedure and may bring in all parties necessary completely to determine the matter and award the appropriate relief upon the facts established." See Jackson v. Strong, 222 N. Y. 149; Kraemer v. World Wide Trading Co., Inc., 187 N. Y. S. 16; 

Moore v. Bonbright & Co., 195 N. Y. S. 854. 

(c) Recovery of Amount Stated in Complaint: Generally, a plaintiff cannot recover a larger sum than he asks for

in his complaint. If he finds that he has asked too little, he should move to amend his complaint. C.P.A. 

110-a, provides: "Whenever in any action or special proceeding it shall appear that the damages sustained are greater in amount than originally alleged, claimed or prayed for in any pleading, and the court in which such action or proceeding is pending is not empowered to award a judgment for such increased amount, the action or proceeding may be removed to a court having the proper jurisdiction." Morris v. Perelman, 260 N. Y. S. 

354. If plaintiff waits until after verdict, he must either content himself with the sum awarded or get a new trial. (1′) Effect of Answer: But if defendant answers, it is immaterial whether plaintiff has asked too much or too little relief, since, C.P.A. 479 provides that "where there is an answer, the court may permit the plaintiff to take any judgment consistent with the case made by the complaint and embraced within the issues. Murtha v. Curley, 90 N. Y. 372; 

Manhattan Beach Co. v. Bonner, 205 N. Y. S. 639. (2′) Negligence Actions: In an action to recover damages for personal injuries, the law implies general damages. But if the plaintiff seeks to recover special damages, we saw, he must affirmatively allege them in his complaint. Gumb v. 23rd St. 

Railway Co., 114 N. Y. 411. For similar rule in contract cases, see Barnes v. Brown, 130 N. Y. 372; 

Neverfail Lighter Co. v. Blum, 194 N. Y. S. 24. (d) Equitable actions: In equity actions, one should always add a prayer for general relief to that for specific relief: viz. "and for such other and further relief as to the Court shall seem just." This demand for general relief empowers the court in cases of default by the defendant to give relief differing in incidental matters from the relief demanded. (3) Subscription: The complaint must be subscribed with the name of the attorney for the plaintiff or with that of the plaintiff if he appears in person. 

R.C.P. 11, 91. 

4. Election of Remedies: A certain specified state of facts may offer a plaintiff his choice of remedies. Thus, where a contract to sell real estate has been broken, plaintiff may sue either at law for damages or, since in such case, damages do not constitute an adequate remedy at law, in equity, for specific performance. The plaintiff may take one course or the other, and if the remedies are inconsistent and the elec-

tion made with full knowledge of all the material facts, it is binding and final. The election is made when the complaint is served. Re Garver, 176 N. Y. 386. The rule is a harsh one and is not to be extended. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co. v. Childs Co., 230 N. Y. 285. Thus, where there is really but one remedy and not a choice between two, a fruitless recourse to a remedy withheld does not bar recourse thereafter to the remedy allowed. Schenck v. State Line Tele. 

Co., 238 N. Y. 308, (where a suit for rescission of a contract on the ground of fraud was allowed after an action at law for damages for fraud had been discontinued by plaintiff because defendant set up the statute of limitations). 

A. The Remedies Must Be Inconsistent: The doctrine of election of remedies is only applicable when a choice is made between remedies which proceed upon "irreconcilable claims of right." American Woolen Co. v. Samuelsohn, 226 N. Y. 61. (1) Illustrations: Specific performance (in affirmance) of a contract is inconsistent with rescission (disaffirmance) of the same contract. Conrow v. Little, 115 N. Y. 387. An action to rescind a contract for fraud is inconsistent with an action for damages for fraud. Clark v. Kirby, 243 N. Y. 295 (where the court said: "The plaintiffs, upon discovering the fraud of the defendants, could, according to our law, rescind the purchase and sue to get their money back; or they could sue to recover damages for fraud, in which instance they would keep their purchase and recover their loss. They could not do both, and, generally speaking, having elected to pursue one remedy, they could not thereafter resort to the other. Having made an election, it would be final”). 

Similarly, a party may not treat the possessor of his land both as tenant and as trespasser. Stuyvesant v. Davis, 9 

Paige (N. Y.) 427. If D converts a chattel belonging to P, bailor, the latter may sue in tort, for conversion, or in replevin, or may waive the tort and sue D for the value of the chattel. While the conversion action would not be consistent with the ex contractu action, since one assumes title in P and the other in D, (Terry v. Munger, 121 N. Y. 161), it would be consistent with the replevin action. Cramer v. 

Brownell, 222 N. Y. 705 (where it was held a judgment in the conversion action passes title to D as to the goods converted, only if he pays the judgment.) Cf. Oakland Mfg. Co. v. Linde Co., 162 A. D. 543. 

5. Joinder of Causes of Action: The New Law: C.P.A, 258, in effect September 1, 1935, provides: "Plaintiff may unite

in the same complaint two or more causes of action, whether they are such as were formerly denominated legal or equitable, provided that upon the application of any party the court, may, in its discretion, direct a severance of the action, or separate trials, whenever required in the interests of justice." It is to be noted that the amended section limits the court, in cases of improper joinder to directing a severance of the action or separate trials at any stage of the proceedings. The section does not authorize the court to strike out or compel plaintiff to drop causes of action improperly united or to serve a corrected amended complaint. 

The purpose of the amendment is to leave the question of proper joinder of causes of action entirely within the court's discretion in line with the legislative intent to encourage the adjudication of a maximum number of controversies between a maximum number of parties, with a minimum confusion of issues. Despite its liberal wording, it is doubtful whether under the new C.P.A. 258 mutually exclusive and inconsistent causes of action may be joined. (The old C.P.A. 

258, itself forbade such joinder.) There is authority that the new section does not permit such joinder, not having been intended to abrogate the doctrine of election of remedies. Dickler v. Nat'l City Bank, 160 Misc. 317, but dicta in 

Epp v. T. G. & T. Co., 160 Misc. 554 is to the contrary. 

At any rate, because of the Sept. 1st, 1936 amendment to

R.C.P. 102, even if such joinder of inconsistent causes of action is improper, the court seemingly has no power, today, to cure the misjoinder by ordering the service of an amended complaint with the non-elected cause of action omitted. See 

"Joinder of Inconsistent Causes of Action," N. Y. L. J. Edit. 

Oct. 9th, 1937. For the old law as to joinder of causes of action, see same title in Appendix. 

6. Splitting Causes of Action: If only a single, indivisible right of action exists in favor of a party, he has the right to bring a single action upon it, and may not split it up and bring separate actions for the component parts. If A loans B $100.00 and B refuses to repay it, A can bring only one action to recover the loan and may not bring ten separate actions to recover $10.00 in each. Thus, the damages accruing from a single wrongful act must be sued for in a single action, else should a partial recovery be had it bars any

further action upon the same right. Secor v. Sturgis, 16

N. Y. 548. So also, a party cannot split a single indivisible cause of action, such as an action for damages for the breach of a contract of employment by the master, although compensation under the contract is payable in instalments. Carvill v. Mirror Films, Inc., 165 N. Y. S. 676. Where the owner of a counterclaim sets it up in a Municipal Court action against him to the amount of the jurisdiction of that court, he cannot thereafter sue for the balance due thereon over and above the amount for which the Municipal Court could give judgment. Silberstein v. Begun, 232 N. V. 319.

But where the claims are separate and distinct, action may be brought on one without barring the others. Thus, where a tenant from year to year holds over for several years, each year's tenancy gives rise to a separate cause of action for the rent for that year. Kennedy v. City of New York, 196 

N. Y. 19. 

7. Action by Partial Assignee: If principles of substantive law, or statute, permit such assignment, an assignee of part of a chose in action, may sue in equity in his own name to recover his assigned part. Field v. City of New York, 6

N. Y. 179. Since the abolition of the distinction between legal and equitable forms of action (C.P.A. 8) the partial assignee may maintain his action at law, but, if the debtor objects (by making a timely motion under R.C.P. 102) the assignee must bring in all other persons owning interests in the claim so as to avoid harassing the debtor by a mul tiplicity of suits. Porter v. Lane Construction Co., 209 N. 

Y. S. 54. 

8. Parties to The Action: 

A. Real Party in Interest: C.P.A. 210 provides that every action must be prosecuted in the name of the real party in interest, but creates certain exceptions. The real party in interest may be a party with whom a contract was made, his assignee, or his personal representatives; or, it may be the party for whose benefit the contract was made. Atlantic Terra Cotta Co. v. Guthy Inc., 244 

N. Y. S. 331; Lawrence v. Fox, 20 N. Y. 268. (1) Assignee: If legal title to the right of action has been assigned to plaintiff, the latter is the real party in interest, even though he must account to the

assignor for the recovery and even though no consideration, or, a colorable one, was paid for the assignment. Sheridan v. Mayer, 68 N. Y. 30. A mere power of attorney given by A to B, authorizing B to sue in A's name, is not an assignment, and B is neither the real party in interest nor a trustee. 

Spencer v. Standard Chem. Co., 237 N. Y. 479. 

(2) Executor or Administrator: The real parties in interest in an action brought by an executor are the beneficiaries under the will, or, if by an administrator, the creditors and next of kin of the deceased. 

But C.P.A. 210 permits the executor or administrator to sue in his own name in his representative capacity. (a) Where an executor or administrator sells on credit the property of the estate and sues to recover the purchase price, the contract is made with him, and the debt does not belong to him in his representative capacity. He, as an individual, is the real party in interest since he is personally accountable for the assets which he has sold. Hence, in such an action the debtor cannot counterclaim a cause of action against the decedent's estate. 

Thompson v. Whitmarsh, 100 N. Y. 351. The rule would be different, of course, if the executor or administrator sued on a contract not made by himself but made by the deceased. (3) Trustee of an Express Trust: A trustee of an express trust is a real party in interest. C.P.A. 210. 

This exception in favor of a trustee extends not only to a statutory trustee but to any person with whom, or in whose name, a contract is made for the benefit of another. Symmers v. Carroll, 207 N. 

Y. 632. Mechanic's Bank v. Termini, 191 N. Y. S. 

334 (where it was held that N. I. L. 90 giving the holder of a negotiable instrument the right to sue thereon in his own name, makes a bank holding a check for collection such a trustee of the proceeds as to come within the above exception). 

(4) Person Expressly Authorized by Statute: This would include a receiver, trustee in bankruptcy, public official, etc. authorized by statute to maintain the action in his own name. 

B. Married Women: C.P.A. 200 does away with the com-

mon law rules concerning women as litigants, and a husband as such is an unnecessary and improper party in all actions wherein his wife is a party, unless he has some personal interest in the litigation. Bennett v. 

Bennett, 116 N. Y. 584.

C. Poor Persons: 

(1) As Plaintiffs: If a person desires to sue without liability for disbursements and costs, he may present a petition showing that he is a "poor person", that is, that he is not worth three hundred dollars in cash or available property, outside of his cause of action and necessary wearing apparel and household furniture for himself and family. C.P.A. 199. The petition must be supported by a certificate of counsel showing a meritorious cause of action. R.C.P. 35. The order permitting suit as a poor person designates an attorney for that purpose. The attorney must act without compensation except that if a recovery is had the court may allow such attorney a reasonable sum for his services and taxable disbursements. 

C.P.A. 196. A "poor person" is not barred from prosecuting his action by reason of his being liable for the costs of a former action or proceeding brought by him against the same defendant. C.P.A. 

197. Such leave to prosecute or defend as a "poor person" may be granted at any stage of the proceedings, including appeal, although no previous application was made prior to or at the commencement of the action. C.P.A. 198a, added in 1937. A "poor person" prosecuting or defending as such does not have to pay any fees to any officer, including clerks, stenographers or sheriffs (upon order of the court stenographic minutes furnished to the "poor person" may be charged to the county). C.P.A. 1493. 

Moreover, on appeal, a "poor person" may submit typewritten instead of printed records and briefs, and may not be required to furnish an undertaking to perfect his appeal. C.P.A. 558, as amended Sept. 

1, 1938. (a) It is no answer to a petition by an infant to sue as a poor person that the infant's guardian is a responsible person.

Feier v. Third Ave. R.R. Co., 41 N. Y. S. 821. (b) But where a guardian ad litem is appointed on an affidavit of financial responsibility and thereafter is ordered to file security for costs, the infant cannot subsequently petition to sue as a poor person without alleging that the guardian has become financially unable to file the necessary security. Rutkowsky v. Cohen, 77 N. Y. S. 546. (2) As Defendants: The same requisites apply where a person asks leave to defend as a "poor person", with this addition: that the action must involve his interest in or to real or personal property. C.P.A. 

198, R.C.P. 37. 

D. Infants: An infant is under a disability and may not prosecute or defend an action in his own name or through the employment of counsel. The infant must be represented by a "guardian ad litem" who is charged with the responsibility of the proper prosecution or defense of the action. An action to recover money or personal property belonging to an infant may be brought in the name of the infant, by his guardian ad litem, although the infant has a general guardian. While the latter has custody and management of the infant's personal estate, the beneficial interest is in the infant and he may maintain the action in his own name by a guardian ad litem. 

Segelken v. Meyer, 94 N. Y. 473; Perkins v. Stimmel, 114 N. Y. 359. Where the guardian ad litem is erroneously designated as plaintiff, instead of the infant, the defect is amendable under C.P.A. 105. Kaplan v. Biscuit 

Co., 38 N. Y. S. 1049. 

(1) Effect of Failure to Appoint Guardian Ad Litem: 

The failure to appoint a guardian ad litem for an infant plaintiff before service of the summons, does not defeat jurisdiction in the court to render judgment. The defect can be cured by amendment nunc pro tunc. Rima v. Rossie Iron Works, 120 N. Y. 

433. In the case of an infant defendant, appointment of the guardian ad litem is made after complete service of the summons (except where the infant is a non-resident or an absentee resident). Failure to make such appointment for an infant defendant renders the judgment voidable at the infant's elec-

tion and upon application seasonably made. Crouter v. Crouter, 133 N. Y. 55. See Grego v. Demario, 231 

N. Y. S. 130. (See also C.P.A. 492, prohibiting entry of a default judgment against an infant defendant until twenty days have expired after the appointment of a guardian ad litem for him.) 

(2) Qualifications of the Guardian Ad Litem: A guard ian ad litem must be capable of understanding and protecting the rights of his infant ward. He must have no interest adverse to that of the infant. He must not be connected in business with the attorney for any adverse party, nor be nominated by any such party. He must be of sufficient financial responsibility to answer in damages for negligence or misconduct. It is the duty of every attorney to act as the guardian of any infant whenever so appointed by order of the court. R.C.P. 40. The guardian ad litem is not responsible for costs unless specially charged therewith by order of the court. C.P.A. 205.

He is entitled to compensation, where, in the court's discretion, he has given thorough and able consideration to the protection of the infant's rights. R.C.P. 

43; Bartos v. Bartos, 244 N. Y. S. 713. (3) Guardian Ad Litem for Infant Plaintiff-How Appointed: The guardian ad litem for the infant plaintiff should be appointed before service. The appointment usually is made by the court, or a judge of the court in which the action is about to be brought, on a petition setting out the facts. (a) If the infant is fourteen years old, or older, he executes the petition himself. (b) If he is under fourteen years, the petition must be executed either by the infant's general or testamentary guardian, if he has one (C.P.A. 203) or by a relative or friend on notice to such guardian, if there be one; if not, then on notice to the person with whom the infant resides. C.P.A. 204. (c) Papers Necessary: The petition should state briefly the nature of the action to be brought, the age and address of the infant, and should nominate a person for appointment. The petition must be verified, and since the application is ex parte, it should comply with R.C.P. 61.

An acknowledged consent of the proposed guardian ad

litem, together with an affidavit by him of his qualifications (R.C.P. 40) must accompany the petition. C.P.A. 

202. The order of the court or judge making the appointment is superimposed and physically annexed to these papers and submitted with them. 

(4) Guardian Ad Litem for Infant Defendant-How Ap pointed: There are two methods of procuring such appointment. C.P.A. 203, 204, 206. Taylor v. Emmet, 122 N. Y. S. 66. (a) First Method: This method applies to all infant defendants. Under it no appointment can be made until the summons has been completely served. C.P.A. 225. 

If the defendant is fourteen years of age, or older, he may execute the petition himself and apply for the appointment within twenty days after the completion of service upon him. If he is under fourteen years, or over that age but fails to make personal application, his general or testamentary guardian, if he has one, or, a relative or friend, or, if these fail, any other party to the action, may make the application. Notice of the application is necessary where any one other than the infant, or his general or testamentary guardian makes the application, provided the infant or such guardian is within the state. C.P.A. 203, 204; R.C.P 39. Of course, where an adverse party applies for the appointment of a guardian ad litem for the infant defendant, he cannot nominate a proposed guardian. R.C.P. 40, subd. 2. Note, too, that the petition or affidavit must state whether anyone was designated under C.P.A. 225 to receive service on behalf of the infant. This is to prevent duplicity of representatives. (b) Second Method: Under this method, the guardian ad litem may be appointed before service of the summons, and if so appointed, the summons is served upon the guardian instead of upon the infant. C.P.A. 206, 224, 239; Taylor v. Emmet, supra. This second method applies only to non-resident, infant defendants or to temporarily absent, resident, infant defendants. C.P.A. 206.

Where the facts contemplated by C.P.A. 206 are shown, by petition or affidavit, to exist, the court will make an order (usually on plaintiff's application) designating a guardian ad litem to act, unless, within a time specified after service of a copy of the order, the infant or someone on his behalf, procures a guardian ad litem to be appointed under the first method. 

(5) Appointment of Guardian Ad Litem on Court's Own Motion: 

The Supreme Court may appoint a guardian ad litem or spe-

cial guardian for an infant or an incompetent at any stage in an action or proceeding for the proper protection of such infant or incompetent. C.P.A. 207. The same is true where the court feels that the committee for an adjudicated incompetent defendant is unfit or adverse to his interests. C.P.A. 208. 

F. Foreign Administrator or Executor: 

A foreign administrator or executor may neither sue nor be sued in the courts of this state if the subject matter of the suit is not within our jurisdiction. Helme v. Buckelew, 229 N. Y. 363; McMaster v. Gould, 240 N. Y. 379.

G. Foreign Corporation as Party: 

(1) As Party Plaintiff (a) G.C.L. 218: No foreign corporation may sue in 

New York on a contract made in New York, unless it is qualified to do business here under a certificate of authority (G.C.L. 218) granted before the making of the contract. This rule does not apply to a moneyed corporation, but does apply to any other foreign corporation that is conducting a systematic course of business here, unless the business is in the nature of interstate commerce. The rule, by its terms, would not apply to a contract made out of the State, nor would it apply to an action brought within New York, seeking tort damages or injunctive relief with respect to the tort. Hoevel 

Co. v. Hoevel, 167 A. D. 548; Wood & Selick v. 

Ball, 190 N. Y. 217; Alpha Portland Cement 

Co. v. Knapp, 230 N. Y. 55. The filing of a claim by a foreign corporation "doing business" here without a certificate, with the Court of Claims, on a contract made within this state would come within the interdiction of G.C.L. 218. Bridge's 

Sons, Inc. v. State, 231 N. Y. 532. Where the foreign corporation is sued in New York and its property attached here under a warrant of attachment, which is thereafter vacated, the foreign corporation may maintain an action on the attachment undertaking, even though it has no certificate of authority and is "doing business" 

here. Fairmount Film Corp. v. Casualty Co.,

178 N. Y. S. 525 (holding such undertaking not to be a contract within the meaning of G.C.L. 

218). Note, also, that a counterclaim arising out of the contract made here may be asserted by the foreign corporation when sued on the contract even though it is "doing business" here without a certificate of authority. G.C.L. 218 creates a condition precedent, performance of which must be alleged in the complaint. If such allegation is missing, the foreign corporation's complaint is dismissable (under R.C.P. 106) either on the ground of plaintiff's legal incapacity to sue, or on the ground that it does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. Wood & Selick v. Ball, supra. 

(b) Tax Law 181: The requirement of this section that within thirteen months after the foreign corporation has started to do business here it must obtain a receipt for the license fee imposed, else, it cannot maintain an action in our courts on any cause of action, was deleted in May 

1937. L. 1937 Ch. 496. See Matter of Scheftel, 275 N. Y. 135. Before the deletion it was held that plaintiff need not plead compliance with Tax Law 181, since it sets out a condition subsequent. But defendant may plead non-compliance by way of defense in the answer. Welsbach Co. v. Norwich Co., 180 N. Y. 533; Wood & Selick v. Ball, supra. The license tax provision cannot be made to apply to a foreign corporation engaged in interstate commerce. Int. Text Book 

Co. v. Tone, 220 N. Y. 313. 

(2) As Party Defendant: 

(a) G. C. L. 224: An action against a foreign corporation may be maintained by a New York resident, or by a domestic corporation, for any cause of action. Generally speaking, where a tort cause of action arises outside the state affecting a non-resident plaintiff and a foreign corporation (or non-resident) defendant, our courts, in the exercise of their discretion, may

refuse to take jurisdiction. Pietraroia v. Rry., 197 N. Y. 434; Burdick v. Freeman, 120 N. Y. 

420. But where the injured plaintiff becomes a resident of New York, bringing with him the transitory tort cause of action, our courts under 

G.C.L. 224 must take jurisdiction. Gregonis v. 

Philadelphia Coal Co., 235 N. Y. 152. (b) G.C.L. 225: An action against a foreign corporation may be maintained here by another foreign corporation or by a non-resident in one of the following cases only: where the action is for breach of a contract made in New York, or relating to property which was in New York at the time of the making of the contract; where the action is to recover real or personal property in New York; where the cause of action (not affecting foreign realty) arose in New York; where defendant foreign corporation is "doing business" in New York. (1') Where a non-resident P sues D foreign corporation in New York on a tort cause of action arising outside the state, our courts will refuse to take jurisdiction even though D is "doing business" in New York, unless there is some relation, between the tort and the "doing business" here. Waisikoski v. Philadelphia Coal Co., 159 N. Y. S. 906; aff'd 228 N. Y. 582. (2′) Although under G.C.L. 225, one foreign corporation cannot sue another foreign corporation in this state on a contract made and to be performed outside the state, where neither foreign corporation "does business" in this state, our courts do not refuse jurisdiction of such an action where it is brought by an assignee who is a bona fide resident of this state (G.C.L. 224) even though the purpose of the assignment is to circumvent G.C.L. 

225. McCauley v. George Rrd. Bank, 203 N. Y. S. 

550; aff'd. 204 N. Y. S. 935, 239 N. Y. 104; Re: 

Hamburg American Line, 238 N. Y. S. 331 (assignment by non-resident). Of course, such an assignment for the purpose of circumventing 

G.C.L. 218 would be ineffectual. 

9. Joinder of Parties: 

A. Necessary and Proper Parties: "Necessary" parties are

those without whom the court cannot proceed with the action, for example, partners suing at law on a partnership contract are all necessary parties. The same is true where they are sued on a partnership debt since their liability thereon is joint. Proper parties are those whose presence in the action is not necessary to permit the court to proceed, but whose rights in the action, if made parties, will be adjudicated. Thus, the same partners, being jointly and severally liable for a partnership tort may all be properly joined, in an action on such tort, but plaintiff need not join them all; he may sue one or several. Weidman v. Sible, 44 N. Y. S. 1057. In a taxpayer's action to vacate illegal audits of town accounts made by the town board of audit, the persons in whose favor the audits were made are necessary parties, since they will be prejudiced by the judgment. Osterhoudt v. 

Board of Supervisors, 98 N. Y. 239. Again, in a mortgage foreclosure, the rights of subordinate lienors cannot be disposed of unless they are made parties. Moulton v. Cornish, 138 N. Y. 133. In an action by a mortgagor on a fire insurance policy obtained by him, but payable to the mortgagee, under a mortgagee clause, the mortgagee is a necessary party to the action. If he refuses to join as party plaintiff, he may be made a party defendant. C.P.A. 194; Lewis v. Guardian Life Ins. Co., 181 N. Y. 392. 

B. Joinder of Parties (C.P.A. 194): Where two or more persons are jointly entitled or have a joint legal interest, they must be joined as plaintiffs or defendants. If the consent of any one who should be joined as plaintiff cannot be obtained, he may be made a defendant, the reason therefor being stated in the complaint. Thus, under C.P.A. 194, partners would all have to join as parties plaintiff in an action to recover a partnership debt. The same would be true of other joint contractual ventures. 

Natter v. 

Blanchard, 153 A. D. 814. If the parties' interests were several, they would be proper parties (joinable under C.P.A. 209) but not necessary parties under the section we are now considering. For example if D slanders A and B, who are partners, each plaintiff would own his cause of action separately, even though the slander is

with respect to the partnership business. (However, both may elect to sue as if on a joint wrong. Peacock v. 

Tata, 200 N. Y. $. 656.) In equity all parties entitled to have a deed or mortgage cancelled or reformed, or a contract rescinded, are necessary parties since there is a joint interest. Spier v. Robinson, 9 How. Pr. 325. (1) Joint and Joint and Several Liability: If the obligors in a contract action are jointly and severally liable, plaintiff may join all in one action or bring a separate action against one obligor, or sue two or more, each in a separate action. This same common law rule holds true in tort actions where the tort feasors are jointly and severally liable. Kaposky v. 

Berry, 207 N. Y. S. 719. Where the claim is against persons jointly liable on contract (as, for example, partners) although all must be made defendants (C.P.A. 194) the summons may be served on one only. In such case, execution may be directed against the individual property of the defendant served, and the joint personal property, but not against the separate property of the defendants not served. 

C.P.A. 1197, 1199; Mun. Ct. Code. Sec. 132. This procedural short cut does not apply where the action is against partners on a tort liability, since in such case the liability is joint and several; P cannot execute on the joint property unless all the partners are named in the tort action and served with process. Kittredge v. Grannis, 244 N. Y. 181. There are two more situations that require discussion because they are often confused by students. Suppose P sues A, B and 

C, copartners alleging a partnership debt. P names A, B and 

C, as defendants, but serves A only. Execution against 

A's property and the joint personal property, is returned partly or wholly unsatisfied. P may thereafter maintain an action against B and C to recover the sum remaining unpaid on the original judgment. C.P.A. 1185. The complaint in such action must be verified. C.PA. 1186. Now suppose P, on the same facts, originally names A and B only as defendants. Of course, there is a defect of parties (non-joinder) apparent on the face, but suppose A and B fail to object (by timely motion under R.C.P. 102) and waive the defect. recovers judgment, which is returned unsatisfied. May P thereafter sue C? At common law, the debt would have been merged in the judgment, and further recourse against C would have been impossible. C.P.A. 1201 abolishes the common law rule, and permits suit against C who was not originally joined. In such subsequent action the complaint need not be verified. (See Appendix for amendments.)

C. Joinder of Parties (C.P.A. 195): Where the question is one of a common or general interest to many persons,

or where the persons who might be made parties are very numerous and it may be impracticable to bring them all before the court, one, or more, may sue or defend for the benefit of all. It is to be noted that where several persons have a common interest in a matter, although not technically "united in interest", one, or more, may sue on behalf of all, even though the parties are not numerous and may all be made parties. Thus, one of several legatees, having separate legacies under a will, may sue on behalf of all to have the will established and land sold to pay the legacies. McKenzie v. 

L'Amoureux, 11 Barb. (N.Y.) 516. (1) Under C.P.A. 195, one of "many" (two, besides plaintiff) stockholders, or certificate holders, may sue on his own behalf and that of all others similarly situated who may come into the action and contribute to its expense. Until others are admitted as parties, the original plaintiff may settle or discontinue the action at will. Hilton Bridge Co. v. Foster, 59 

N. Y. S. 1106; Security Trust Co. v. Pritchard, 194 N. Y. S. 

486. (As to the trustee status of P in the representative action, see Manix v. Fantl, 205 N. Y. S. 174; Earl v. Brewer, 282 N. Y. S. 922.) (a) In the case of such representative suit, those for whose benefit it is brought may not lie by with knowledge of such action until their right to bring independent suits has expired under the statute of limitations and then seek to be made parties plaintiff. Mac Ardell v. 

Olcott, 70 N. Y. S. 930. (b) Where creditors who have joined in an action for a sequestration and distribution of their debtor's assets have been paid pro rata pursuant to an order in the action, such payment is a defense to a subsequent action by unpaid creditors who did not join in the action, although with knowledge thereof. Kerr v. Blodgett, 48 N. Y. 62. 

D. Joinder of Plaintiffs (C.P.A. 209): All persons may join as plaintiffs, in whom any right to relief in respect of, or arising out of, the same transaction or series of transactions is alleged to exist whether jointly, severally or in the alternative, where if such persons brought separate actions any common question of law or fact would arise. 

If such joinder may embarrass or delay the trial of the action, the court may order separate trials or make such other order as may be expedient. The causes of action

should be separately stated and numbered. (1) In Akely v. Kinnicutt, 238 N. Y. 466, one hundred and ninety three plaintiffs were permitted to join on separate causes of action ex delicto for fraud and deceit arising out of the issuance of a false prospectus. (2) Where plaintiff sustained an injury in the scope of his employment through the negligence of a third party, the insurance carrier for P's employer is properly joined as a party P against the third party, where the answer of the third party set up, as a defense, an election of P to accept an award of the Workmen's Compensation Bureau and the payment to P of such award, since if the defense is established the insurer would be subrogated to P's rights against 

D. Roecklin v. American Sugar Refining Co., Inc., 226 N. 

Y. S. 375. Cf. Lang v. B'n. City Rrd. 217 A. D. 501 aff'd. 247 

N. Y. 551. (3) Likewise, the joinder is proper where a wife and infant child claim damages for personal injuries, and the husband, damages for loss of their services and for medical expenses, all arising out of the same accident. Spetler v. Jogel Realty 

Co., 231 N. Y. S. 517. (4) The owner of land and the lessee thereof are properly joined as petitioners in mandamus in condemnation proceedings to have their damages determined and paid. Star Sand & Gravel 

Corp. v. Marsh, 232 N. Y. S. 235. (5) But where D bank paid out on a forged endorsement of a certified check, the maker and payee thereof cannot join as parties P under C.P.A. 209, since the claims of the maker and payee are not joint, several nor common, but mutually destructive. If one plaintiff proves his right to recover, the other's claim must be entirely extinguished. Olsen v. Banker's Trust Co., 199 N. Y. S. 700.

E. Joinder of Defendants (C.P.A. 211-212): C.P.A. 211 as amended in 1937 provides that all persons may be joined as defendants against whom the right to any relief is alleged to exist, whether jointly, severally or in the alternative; and judgment may be given against such one or more of the defendants as may be found to be liable, according to their respective liabilities upon all of the evidence without regard to the party by whom it has been introduced. The italicized material (added by the 

1937 amendment) changes the rule in Bopp v. N. Y. El. 

Rrd. Co. 177 N. Y. 33, that evidence of one defendant cannot be used for plaintiff against a joint tort-feasor co-defendant who withdrew from the trial at the end

of plaintiff's case. C.P.A. 212 provides that "it shall not be necessary that each defendant shall be interested as to all the relief prayed for or as to every cause of action included in the proceeding against him; but the court may make such order as may appear just to prevent any defendant from being embarrassed or put to expense by being required to attend any proceedings in which he may have no interest." Thus, where two insurance companies have each a pro rata liability on a burglary loss, it is proper to join them both as parties defendant in an action on both policies. Bossak v. Nat'l 

Surety Co., 200 N. Y. S. 148. (1) Joint Tort Feasors: As previously noted, joint tort feasors have a joint and several liability. Plaintiff may sue one, several, or all, even though all may not be liable on all the causes of action. Thus, in Sherlock v. Manwaren, 208 A. D. 538, plaintiff was permitted to join in a malpractice action, four physicians who successively set and reset plaintiff's broken arm, separately stating and numbering his cause of action against each. However, the jury may not apportion the damages against them on the theory of greater or lesser fault, since joint tort feasors are liable for the whole sum found as damage liability. 

Klepper v. Seymour, 246 N. Y. 85. C.P.A. 211a now permits contribution in favor of a joint tort feasor who has paid more than his pro-rata share of a judgment recovered by plaintiff.

F. Joinder of Defendants (C.P.A. 213): If plaintiff is in doubt as to the one from whom he is entitled to recover, he may join two or more so that the question of liability be determined on the trial. (1) Thus, where in an action for wages, plaintiff, because of similarity of names and identity of the officers of two corporations, is in doubt as to which corporation to sue, he may join both as parties defendant. Ellicott v. Archibald Mc- 

Neill & Co., 201 N. Y. S. 500. (2) Where plaintiff is in doubt as to which of two tortfeasors caused his injury, he may join both. Jacobs v. Barron, 215

A. D. 560. Thus, P may join two landowners who in separately excavating on different sides of P's building caused it to collapse.

Cowles v. Eidlitz, 201 N. Y. S. 254

aff'd. 206 N. Y. S. 895. Cf. Klein v. Betzold, 197 N. Y. S. 501. (3) Similarly, where plaintiff is in doubt whether an agent acted with proper authority, he may join both principal and agent in one action. Jamison v. Lamborn Co. 202 N. Y. S. 113. (a) But P having made a complete contract with X in the latter's name, cannot sue X and Y on the theory that he thinks Y may be an undisclosed principal. Since he has a certain cause of action against X, there cannot be any uncertainty as to who is liable. Garrett v. Mc- 

Allister, 244 N. Y. S. 283. 

G. Joinder of Defendants (C.P.A. 216): If two or more persons are severally liable on different causes of action, but their respective liabilities arise from the same written instrument, they may be joined as parties defendant. 

H. Non-Joinder and Mis-Joinder of Parties: C.P.A. 192 

provides that no action or special proceeding shall be defeated by the non-joinder, or mis-joinder, of parties and that new parties may be added or substituted and parties misjoined may be dropped, by order of the court, at any stage of the cause as the ends of justice may require. A substitution of parties under this section may be had only where the substituted party has lawfully succeeded to the title or interest of the party for whom he is substituted. Pelzer v. United Dredging Co., 193 

N. Y. S. 676. Where the substitution of the party or parties would also change or substitute the cause of action, it cannot be had. New York State Monitor Assn. v. Remington, 89 N. Y. 21. See also Boyd v. U. S. Mtge. 

Co., 187 N. Y. 262; Kelly v. Carson Petroleum Co., 206 

N. Y. S. 590.

I. Adding New Parties (C.P.A. 193): 

(1) Subd. I: In equitable actions where the court cannot make a complete determination of the controversy without the presence of other parties, the court must direct them to be brought in. First Nat. Bank v. Shuler, 153 N. Y. 163. In any action, at law or in equity, any person who could have been joined as a party at the commencement of the action, may, by order of the court, be brought in as a party defendant upon plaintiff's application, on good cause shown. C.P.A. 105; Gittleman v. Feltman, 191 N. Y. 205.

(2) Subd. 2: Where any party to an action shows that some third person, not then a party to the action, is or will be liable to such party wholly or in part, for the claim made against such party in the action, the court, on application of such party may order such person to be brought in as a party to the action and direct that a supplemental summons and pleading be served upon such person and that such person plead thereto, so that the claim against such person may be determined in the action. The order is obtained on notice to the parties in the action, but no notice need be given to the person sought to be impleaded. Harris v. Simpson, 243 N. Y. S. 457.

Where the defendant obtains the order, he (and not plaintiff) prepares and serves upon the impleaded party a supplemental summons, a copy of the order, and an answer which answers plaintiff's claim and sets out a claim against the impleaded party. Hailfinger v. Meyer, 212 N. Y. S. 746; Mun. Service Co. v. D. B. & M. Corp., 257 N. Y. 423. (a) Where P sues D for negligence in taking out insurance for P in pounds sterling instead of in dollars, D may bring in, under subd. 2, M, its agent, who took out the insurance. Travlos v. Commercial Union, 217 N. 

Y. S. 459. (b) On the same theory where the master is sued for the employee's negligence in the scope of the master's business, the master may bring in the employee. Fedden v. Terminal, 199 N. Y. S. 9. (It is to be noted that in such case the parties are not joint tort-feasors in pari delicto, but that the master's liability is a derivative one and exists only because of the primary wrongdoing of the employee. The master seeks indemnification and not contribution). (c) C.P.A. 211a, which abolishes the common law rule prohibiting contribution between joint tort feasors, does not authorize D in a personal injury action to bring in an alleged joint tort-feasor not sued, since the right to contribution exists only if one joint tort-feasor pays more than his pro rata share of a judgment recovered. Fox v. 

Western Film Co., 257 N. Y. 305. (But where the tort-feasors are not in pari delicto, the rule would be different. Hailfinger v. Meyer, 215 A. D. 35; Birchall v. 

Clemons Realty Co. Inc., 241 A. D. 286.)

(d) In an action for personal injuries, where D is insured against the payment of a judgment, he cannot bring in the insurance company as a party D since the latter's liability would arise only if D paid the judgment obtained against him. Kromback v. Killian, 213 N. Y. S. 

138. Cf. 125 W. 45th St. Corp. v. Framax Realty Co., 293 N. Y. S. 216; aff'd. 273 N. Y. 536. (e) Subd. 2 does not give a party to an action the right to bring in another person allegedly indebted to him on the claim for which the action is brought where it will become necessary to try in the one action two, wholly unrelated, claims. Neuss Hesslin & Co. v. Nat. Amline 

Co. 197 N. Y. S. 808. (f) Nor does it permit P to bring in as a party D any party who might ultimately be liable to P in the event that the original D was successful in the defense of the action. Stern v. Ide & Co. 209 N. Y. S. 473. (g) For a so-called "workable rule" under subd. 2, see, May Co. v. Mott, 121 Misc. 398. (3) Subd. 3: Where a person not a party to the action has an interest in the subject thereof, or, in real property, the title to which may in any manner be affected by the judgment, or in real property, for injury to which the complaint demands relief, and makes application to the court to be made a party, it must direct him to be brought in by proper amendment. This provision is mandatory and the court cannot impose terms. Uhlfelder v. Tamsen, 44 N. Y. S. 484. (a) The interest contemplated under subd. 3 is a property interest or some duty or right devolving on the party who is to be brought in. Monell v. B'n Union Gas Co., 231 N. Y. 405 (where the City of New York was refused permission to intervene in an action brought to restrain the collection of an increased gas rate fixed by the Public 

Service Commission). (b) An assignee for the benefit of creditors has such an interest in an action against the debtor by an outside creditor, since a judgment obtained in such an action will materially affect the disposition of the debtor's assets. Merchants' Bank v. Hagemeyer, 38 N. Y. S. 626. (c) Where a third party claims to be the owner of a pending cause of action and that the plaintiff therein is an imposter, the former may become a party to the action, on his own application, and litigate the question of ownership. Bulova v. S. S. Corp. 194 A. D. 418.

(d) But subd. 3 does not give the court the power to compel a plaintiff to bring in as a party defendant, upon the latter's application, a third party, in an action for brokerage commissions where the third party's only interest in the subject of the action is an agreement with plaintiff's assignor under which there was allegedly due to the third party part of the said commissions. 

Bauer v. 

Dewey, 166 N. Y. 402. (4) Subd. 4: When the ownership of realty is divided into possessory and future interests, and an action. for injury to realty is pending in which less than all the owners are plaintiffs, defendant may apply to have all living persons who have a present or future interest made parties to the action. See 

R.P.L. 538 as to distribution of damages. (5) Subd. 5: The controversy between the defendants shall not delay a judgment to which the plaintiff is entitled, unless the court otherwise directs: 

10. Res Adjudicata: 

Should a defendant succeed in defeating plaintiff's claim, he gets a judgment dismissing the claim. If the judgment is reached after litigation and trial upon the merits, it is res adjudicata between the parties and settles conclusively all issues raised by the pleadings. If, however, the judgment is reached merely because of some defect in plaintiff's proceedings and not upon the merits, it is no bar to a subsequent action for the same cause. C.P.A. 482 provides that a dismissal of a complaint at the close of plaintiff's evidence, or later, is a final determination on the merits, unless stated to be a dismissal without prejudice. A prior judgment is not res adjudicata unless the same issues were involved, the same parties or their privies were involved, and such judgment was final. Rudd v. Cornell, 171 N. Y. 114; Donahue v. Ins. Co., 259 N. Y. 98. In Wolf v. Kenyon, 242 A. D. 

116, it was held that since the master's liability exists only because of the servant's negligence, if plaintiff does not succeed in his negligence action against the employee, the judgment in such action is res adjudicata in a subsequent action against the master. Where the issues litigated in the first action are different from those of the second or subsequent action, there has been no adjudication on the merits. Silberstein v. Silberstein, 218 N. Y. 525. Thus,

a judicial determination and construction of a contract in a former action will be binding on the parties to a subsequent action on the same contract. Schykell v. Nuberg 

Realty Co., 250 N. Y. 304. A judgment rendered on the merits, whether right or wrong, is a bar to another action brought for the same cause unless adjudged defects present in the first action are corrected or supplied by the pleadings in the second. Joannes Bros. Co. v. Lamborn, 237 N. Y. 

207. An equity decree barring a plaintiff from equitable relief is not a bar to the bringing of a subsequent law action. 

Carmen v. Fox Film Corp. 204 A. D. 776. But in such law action, the findings of fact in the equity action are binding on the parties. Pac. Southwest Tr. & Ser. Bk. v. Hyman, 222 N. Y. S. 756. An action for breach of warranty in a contract for the sale of goods survives the recovery by the vendor of a judgment for the purchase price where the vendee's counterclaim for breach of warranty in the action for the purchase price was dismissed because of default on the trial. Honsinger v. Union Carriage & Gear Co., 175 N. Y. 

229. 

11. Verification of Complaint: 

A. What is a Verification?: A verification is an affidavit at tached to a pleading (or petition) and made by a party, or some other person authorized by statute, in which affidavit the party swears that the contents of the pleading (or petition) are true to deponent's knowledge, except as to matters therein stated to be alleged on information and belief, and that as to those matters he believes it to be true. R.C.P. 100; C.P.A. 276.

B. When Complaint Must be Verified: Although it is op tional with plaintiff whether or not to verify his complaint, he must do so, where: (1) the summons is to be served by publication (C.P.A. 232), (2) the action is on a judgment against joint debtors not served with the summons in the original action in which the judgment was obtained (C.P.A. 1186), (3) it is necessary to file a lis pendens in an action affecting real property (C.P.A. 120), (4) the complaint is under C.P.A. 255-a, (5) the complaint is in the Municipal Court in an action

to establish a mechanic's lien. Mun. Ct. Code, sec. 78.

C. Subsequent Pleadings: Where a complaint is verified, each subsequent pleading must be verified. C.P.A. 248. 

There are some exceptions to this rule which will be considered in the next chapter. 

D. Who May Make Verification: The party himself, or where there are several parties plaintiff united in interest, one of them, usually verifies the complaint. The same applies to an answer or other pleading where the parties are united in interest. Hannevig v. Frankel, 201 

N. Y. S. 762. (1) Corporation: If the party be a domestic corporation, the verification must be made by an officer. If a foreign corporation, the attorney or an agent may verify. R.C.P. 99. 

(2) Verification by Agent or Attorney (R.C.P. 99): This is permissible: (a) if the party is a foreign corporation, (b) if there are two or more parties united in interest and pleading together, and none of them acquainted with the facts is within the county where the attorney has his office, (c) if the party is not within the county where the attorney has his office, (d) if the action or defense be founded on a written instrument for the payment of money only, which is in the possession of the agent or attorney, (e) if all the material allegations of the pleading be within the personal knowledge of the agent or attorney.

E. Additions to Verification where made by Agent or 

Attorney (R.C.P. 100): 

Where the agent or attorney verifies, he must state: (1) why the affidavit is not made by the party, and (2) the grounds of his belief as to all matters not stated on his knowledge.

F. Verifying Allegations on Information and Belief: In drawing a complaint, the attorney should be particu-

larly careful to see that every allegation in respect to which the person verifying is not willing to take full responsibility should be made "upon information and belief" (C.P.A. 276) else the party who verifies will be held to strict statutory liability for the truth of the allegations of the pleading. 

12. Consolidation and Severance of Actions: Although under 

C.P.A. 258, plaintiff may join in one action several causes of action, he is not compelled to do so and may bring as many actions as he has causes of action. Where substantially the same question is involved in the several actions, the court, in its discretion, upon motion of one of the parties consolidates the different actions into one action wherever it can be done without prejudice to a material right of the parties in order to obviate unnecessary costs, expenses and litigation. C.P.A. 96, 97. It is no longer necessary that the actions must be by the same plaintiffs and against the same defendants. Goldey v. Bierman, 194 N. Y. S. 373.

Actions by one plaintiff against separate defendants for libels of similar import may be consolidated on motion of the defendants. C.P.A. 97-a. C.P.A. 96, as amended in 1935 now permits special proceedings, as well as actions to be consolidated or severed. C.P.A. 96a, added in 1937, provides that the court may order two or more actions or special proceedings growing out of the same set of facts to be tried together without consolidation. The motion for consolidation or severance should be made after issue joined, but before trial.

A. In What Courts Permissible: The Supreme Court may remove to itself an action pending in another court and consolidate it with one in the Supreme Court. But the
