Supreme Court may not make an order consolidating two actions pending in two different courts of an inferior jurisdiction. Curry v. Earll, 203 N. Y. S. 750. The

County Court has power to consolidate an action brought in a Justice's Court with one pending in the County 

Court. C.P.A. 97. The Municipal Court has power to consolidate actions pending in different Municipal Court districts. Melker v. Guarino, 238 N. Y. S. 569. The City 

Court may remove to itself and consolidate with an

action pending therein a Municipal Court action. City 

Ct. Act 18a.

B. Costs: The successful party in a consolidated action is entitled to only one bill of costs, unless the right to tax costs in the individual actions is reserved in the order of consolidation. Hilmire v. Fitzgerald Bros., 247 N. Y. S. 

255. (The consolidation order may fix the rights as to opening and closing to the jury. Brinks v. Burns, 245 

N. Y. S. 649.)

C. Severance: Whenever in the court's discretion and in the interest of justice it is necessary, the court may order the severance of an action in the same way as a consolidation, where no substantial rights of the parties will be prejudiced. C.P.A. 96. Thus, where the proceeds of an insurance policy were payable to the surviving wife and children, and the wife settled with the insurer, the action on the policy was properly severed. Kellian v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 251 N. Y. 44.

CHAPTER VII. 

1. The Answer: The answer is the defendant's only pleading (C.P.A. 260) and since “each material allegation not controverted by the answer must be taken as true for the purposes of the action" (C.P.A. 243), the primary function of the answer, it can be seen, is to develop issues of fact. When the complaint is served upon defendant, he may either move with respect to it, or answer it. We now consider some of the questions which arise where defendant answers the complaint.

A. Contents of the Answer. The answer may consist of denials, either general or specific, or new matter as a defense or counterclaim, or both. C.P.A. 261. Defendant should deny whatever he does not admit.

B. Forms of Denial: 

(1) General Denial: A general denial embraces all the allegations constituting a cause of action and denies them all, as distinguished from a specific denial which singles out and specifies the particular allegations in the complaint which defendant denies. (a) Thus, to controvert the allegations of a complaint, a general or specific denial is necessary, and a denial which expressly excepts from its operation all allegations in the complaint, qualified by previous statements in the answer, does not deny either generally or specifically such allegations as are qualified, and hence amounts to an admission of the same. Clark v. Dillion, 97 N. Y. 

371. Cf. Griffin v. Rrd., 101 N. Y. 348. (b) A denial of every "material" allegation of a complaint is bad because it does not show what defendant deems material. N. Y. Coach Co. v. Brown, 143 N. Y. S. 100. (2) Unqualified Denial: This form is used where the party verifying is willing to take the responsibility of denying absolutely and under oath the allegations of the complaint. Defendant "states that he denies" is not good form, but is effective as a denial. Kirschbaum v. Eschmann, 205 N. Y. 127. 

(3) Denial Upon Information and Belief: It is proper for a defendant to deny upon information and belief where he has insufficient personal knowledge but

has information sufficient to give him a belief in the truth of his denial. Bennett v. Leeds Mfg. Co., 110 N. Y. 150. (4) Denial of Knowledge or Information Sufficient to Form a Belief: This form may be used where the defendant wishes to put in issue certain allegations of the complaint but has neither knowledge nor information sufficient to form a belief as to the truth of the allegations. C.P.A. 261. The denial must be in the express phraseology of the statute, viz. "of any knowledge or information thereof sufficient to form a belief." (a) A denial merely of information sufficient to form a belief is insufficient to put in issue the allegations to which it is directed. Edwards v. MacArtney, 183 N. Y. S. 851; 

Steinback v. Drepenbrock, 65 N. Y. S. 118. (b) The word "thereof" after the word "information" must be used else the denial is invalid. Jurgens v. Wichmann, 108 N. Y. S. 881. The omission of the word "any," however, does not invalidate the denial. Hidden v. Godfrey, 85 N. Y. S. 197. (See Baum v. Elias, 117 N. Y. S. 

935, pointing out the inconsistency of these cases.) (c) Where defendant denies any knowledge or information sufficient to form a belief as to facts necessarily within his personal knowledge, (e. g. the termination of a law suit brought by him, or the execution by him of a note) such a denial may be struck out on motion (either before or at trial) as sham or frivolous, or disregarded in case plaintiff moves for judgment on the pleadings. 

Dahlstrom v. Gemunder, 198 N. Y. 449; Royal Bank of 

Canada v. Williams, 222 N. Y. S. 425. (d) Where this form of denial is used as to a fact only presumptively in D's knowledge, D should be given a chance to show his good faith. Kirschbaum v. Eschmann, supra, where the court said: "We think a different rule applies, however, where this form of denial is used to meet allegations of fact which are only presumptively within the knowledge of the person making the denial; or where the defect in the pleading is such that the court may upon proper terms and conditions, permit it to be changed or amended. In such cases, it is obviously the correct practice for the party who attacks the pleading to make the proper motion before trial, as the attacked pleader then has the opportunity to establish his good faith, if he can, and to prove that he cannot honestly go further than to deny knowledge or information,

or make his pleading more definite or certain than it is. A single familiar instance will serve to illustrate this point. When an action is brought against a corporation or corpartnership, the complaint usually charges many things which are presumptively within the knowledge of the officers or the partners, and yet their only knowledge may have been derived wholly from their agents, or employees."

C. Improper Forms of Denial: The denial, no matter what form is used, must be express and direct and not left to inference. Mayer v. Chamberlain, 164 N. Y. S. 806. It must be directed to a material allegation of fact in the complaint (Wall v. Buffalo Water Works, 18 N. Y. 119) 

and is insufficient if it denies merely a conclusion of law. 

Emery v. Baltz, 94 N. Y. 408 (where D specifically denied an allegation in the complaint "that the defendant is indebted to the plaintiff"). See also Lion Brewery v. 

Loughran, 229 N. Y. S. 216. Where the answer denies only immaterial allegations, plaintiff's motion for judgment on the pleadings should be granted. Smith v. Coe, 170 N. Y. 162; Commercial Bank v. Woodward, 191 

N. Y. S. 51. 

(1) Argumentative Denial: A denial implies a direct contradic tion of an averment made in the complaint and not a mere inconsistent statement of a different version from which a denial of plaintiff's version may be argued. Fleishmann v. 

Stern, 90 N. Y. 110. Cf. Hull & Lyon Co. v. Torrey, 188 

N. Y. S. 486. (2) Negative Pregnant: Where defendant denies plaintiff's allegation in the same words of the allegation, we have a "negative pregnant," since defendant's denial may be canstrued to apply to an immaterial point in the allegation. (a) Thus, where P alleges that "D, on December 18, commited an assault from which death resulted on December 25," and D denies "that on December 18, he committed an assault from which death resulted on December 25," we have a case of negative pregnant. Baker v. 

Bailey, 16 Bart. (N. Y.) 54. (b) If P does not attack the negative pregnant by motion before trial, it will, ordinarily, be disregarded. Stone v. 

Auerbach, 117 N. Y. S. 734.

D. Evidence Admissible Under General Denial: Under a general denial, defendant may controvert by evidence anything which the plaintiff is bound or permitted to prove in the first instance to make out his cause of

action. Molloy v. Village of Briarcliff, 217 N. Y. 577; 

Norris v. McMecham, 236 N. Y. S. 486. (1) Thus, where P alleges that he duly performed all the conditions by him to be performed, D, under a general denial, may show non-performance of any of the conditions. Adams v. Lawson, 188 N. Y. 460. (2) So also in an action on a note alleged to have been executed jointly by a corporate defendant and an individual defendant, under a general denial interposed by the latter, evidence is admissible to show that the note was not a joint note but that of the corporate defendant only. Hoffstaedter v. Carlton, 196 N. Y. S. 577. (3) In a replevin action, since P must prove title in himself, D, under a general denial may show title in a third person from whom he claims. Griffin v. Rrd. Co., 101 N. Y. 348.

The same rule would apply to an ejectment action. (4) Where P sues D for wrongful breach of a contract of employment and alleges that he was discharged without cause, D, under a general denial, cannot show justification, since, the allegation by P that he was discharged without cause was unnecessary and need not have been proved by him to make a prima facie case. Linton v. Unexcelled Fire Works 

Co., 124 N. Y. 533. Cf. Milbank v. Jones, 141 N. Y. 340. (5) Where P's equity complaint alleges that he has no adequate remedy at law, D is deemed to accept the equitable issue presented, unless in his answer he affirmatively pleads such adequate legal remedy. Farrington v. Steel Co., 194 N. Y. S. aff'd, 235 N. Y. 581. See also Chadbourne v. Ritz-Carlton, 207 A. D. 754, holding that the complaint in equity must show on its face that there is no adequate remedy at law, else a motion to dismiss for insufficiency (R.C.P. 106) will be granted. Cf. Jackson v. Strong, 222 N. Y. 149.

E. Defenses on New Matter: A defense founded upon new matter may not be proved under a general denial. Generally, defenses which assume or admit the original cause of action alleged, but are based upon subsequent facts or transactions which go to qualify or defeat it, must be specifically pleaded and proved by defendant. Seifert v. Commercial Travelers, 263 N. Y. 496. This is intended to obviate surprise at the trial. C.P.A. 242 provides that all such grounds of defense or reply, which if not raised would be likely to take the opposite side by surprise, or, would raise issues of fact not arising out of the preceding pleadings, as, for instance, fraud, statute of limitations, statute of frauds, release, payment, facts

showing illegality, etc., should be affirmatively pleaded. 

Seemingly, this section changes the rule in Murray v. 

Narwood, 192 N. Y. 172, that negative defenses such as conspiracy or duress pertaining to the res gestae of the execution of the contract in suit, may be proved under a general denial, since the burden is on P in such case to show the contract to be a good and valid one having a legal inception. The defendant may use as defensive matter anything which has occurred up to the service of the answer. Wormser v. Met. Ry., 98 A. D. 29. (1) The following defenses should be affirmatively pleaded: fraud, usury, special license, statute of limitations, statute of frauds, discharge in bankruptcy, accord and satisfaction, general release, ultra vires, res adjudicata, and all matter pertaining to confession and avoidance, such as justifiable cause for discharge of an employee. Linton v. Unexcelled Fire 

Works Co., supra. (2) Contributory negligence in actions for wrongful death (D.E.L. 130) or in actions by an injured employee against his employer (Employers Liab. Law, sec. 5) is a defense to be pleaded and proved by the defendant. C.P.A. 265. (3) To join issue on the allegation that P is a corporation, D must affirmatively plead, by verified answer (R.C.P. 93) that 

P is not a corporation. Such a denial must be specific, but may be made upon information and belief. Joint Stock Co. v. Nat'l City Bank, 240 N. Y. 368. (4) Generally speaking, payment cannot be shown under a general denial but must be affirmatively pleaded by D, although P must allege non-payment. Acharan v. Samuel Bros., 128 

N. Y. S. 943. However, where the action is not upon contract for the payment of money, but is upon an obligation created by operation of law, or is to enforce a lien, P must both plead and prove non-payment. Conkling v. Weatherwax, 181 

N. Y. 258. (But see C.P.A. 242.)

F. Dilatory, Partial and Inconsistent Defenses: 

(1) Dilatory Defenses: These defenses, (e. g. infancy or other disability of the plaintiff, lack of jurisdiction in the court over defendant, another action pending) do not attack the merits of the cause of action, and were known at common law as pleas in abatement or dilatory pleas. These dilatory defenses are deemed waived unless taken by motion, if the defects appear on the face of the complaint. C.P.A. 278, R.C.P. 106.

If the defects do not appear on the face, defendant

may either move under R.C.P. 107 to dismiss the complaint or plead the defects by way of defense in his answer. Gentilala v. Fay Taxicabs, 214 A. D. 255. 

(2) Partial Defenses: These defenses tend to mitigate or reduce damages and may be pleaded as such if they are labelled as partial defenses. C.P.A. 262. If not so labelled, they will be regarded as complete defenses, and if insufficient as such, they may be stricken out. Schieffelin v. Hylan, 199 N. Y. S. 691 (where a plea of justification was interposed in a libel action, but was not pleaded as a partial defense; it was tested for sufficiency as a complete defense, found insufficient and stricken out). See also Fleckenstein v. Friedman, 266 N. Y. 19. 

(3) Inconsistent Defenses: A defendant may plead in consistent defenses, but where only one can be true, defendant will be compelled to elect at the trial upon which he intends to rely. Breunich v. Weselman, 100 N. Y. 609. (a) In a slander action D may deny speaking the defamatory words and at the same time set up their truth. 

Buhler v. Wentworth, 17 Barb. (N. Y.) 649; See C.P.A. 338. (b) In a malicious prosecution action, D may set up a general denial together with a defense of probable cause. 

Raddle v. Ruckgaber, 3 Duer (N. Y.) 684. (c) In an action for damages for breach of a contract of employment D may deny making the contract and defend that he had been induced to enter into same by 

P's fraud. Wendling v. Pierce, 50 N. Y. S. 509. (d) D may deny that title to goods, alleged to have been bought by him, is in D, but may at the same time, claim the proceeds of a re-sale by P, in the event that title is found to have passed to D. Carter Co. v. Matthews, 222 N. Y. S. 472. 

E. Counterclaims: As of September 1, 1936, the Civil Practice Act was amended with respect to counterclaims so as to liberalize the practice in conformity with the already liberalized practice as to joinder of causes of action (C.P.A. 258). For the technical requirements of the old law as to counterclaims see the same title in the Appendix. Under the new law, a counterclaim may be any

cause of action, legal or equitable, in favor of defendants, or some of them, against plaintiffs, or some of them, a person whom plaintiff represents, or a plaintiff and another person or persons alleged to be liable. C.P.A. 

266. However, the court, in its discretion, may, whenever the interests of justice require, order a severance of the action or separate trials, or strike out the counterclaim without prejudice to the bringing of another action. 

C.P.A. 262. The counterclaim must still be set out and labelled as such in the answer. Deeves v. Manhattan 

Life Ins. Co., 195 N. Y. 324. If it is not labelled, plaintiff need not reply thereto (Equitable Life Ass'n v. Cuyler, 75 N. Y. 511) but it may be sufficient as a defense.

Notice that while it is no longer requisite that the counterclaim tend in some way to diminish or defeat plaintiff's recovery, it is still required that defendant, if he deems himself entitled to affirmative judgment on his counterclaim, demand same in his answer. C.P.A. 262.

The former requirement of C.P.A. 266 that a counterclaim, otherwise proper, is not interposable (R.C.P. 109) if a separate judgment thereon cannot be entered, has not been retained by the amended section. Moreover, C.P.A. 271 has not been changed. Thus where defendant sets up any counterclaim which raises questions between himself and plaintiff along with any other persons, if such other persons are not already in the action, they may be brought in, by service upon them of a copy of the answer containing the counterclaim against them. Such service is the equivalent of service of process. C.P.A. 

271; Youngman v. N. Y. Indemnity Co., 199 N. Y. S. 420. (1) Thus, if A sues B, and B has a claim against A, X and Y, severally, or, jointly, but not in the alternative, B may set up the counterclaim in his answer and bring in X and Y under C.P.A. 271. Williams v. Tompkins, Inc., 208 A. D. 574. (a) In Gettinger v. Glasser, 199 N. Y. S. 41, Ps sued for work, labor and services rendered on raw materials supplied by Ds to be manufactured by Ps into dresses. The goods were alleged by Ps to have been stolen while on Ps premises. Ds counterclaimed against Ps for conversion of the goods, or, in the event that Ps recovered judgment against Ds, that Ds recover against X Surety

Co. on a burglary insurance policy issued to Ds. The counterclaim was held good, although seemingly in the alternative, because it raised questions between Ds and 

Ps "along with" the insurance company. See also 

Cramerton Mills v. Nathan Co., 231 A. D. 28.

F. Counterclaims where Plaintiff is Assignee (C.P.A. 267): 

A plaintiff assignee of a cause of action takes it subject to defenses existing between the original parties. He also takes it subject to any counterclaim that exists in favor of the defendant against the plaintiff's assignor, provided: (a) the cause of action is on a contract. (b) the cause of action counterclaimed had accrued at the time of the assignment, (Michigan Sav. Bk. v. Miller, 96 N. Y. S. 568; Stafford v. Kremer, 258 

N. Y. 1), (c) the cause of action counterclaimed belonged to defendant in good faith before he received notice of the assignment; (d) it is then good as a counterclaim but only to defeat plaintiff's claim. (e) If the action is upon a negotiable promissory note or bill of exchange which has been assigned to plaintiff after maturity, a demand existing against the assignor after such maturity may be counterclaimed to the amount of plaintiff's demand, if it might have been allowed against the assignor while the note or bill belonged to him.

(f) Equitable Recoupment: A sells and delivers goods to D in installments, to be paid for, separately, within a time specified after. each delivery. A assigns to P the money due on one installment. sues D on the assigned cause of action. D may counterclaim a breach of warranty growing out of a subsequent delivery. This was permitted in equity under the principle of equitable recoupment and is continued under the Civil Practice Act, although seemingly contradictory of C.P.A. 267. Seibert v. 

Dunn, 216 N. Y. 237.

G. Demand for Relief on Counterclaim: The counter claim must show a right to relief against the plaintiff

other than the mere dismissal of the complaint, (C.P.A. 

477), and where the defendant deems himself entitled to affirmative judgment by reason of a counterclaim interposed by him, he must demand such judgment in his answer. C.P.A. 262. 

H. Controversy between Defendants: C.P.A. 264 provides, in effect, that where a judgment may determine the ultimate rights of two or more defendants, as between themselves, a defendant who requires such determination must demand it in his answer, and at least twenty days before the trial must serve a copy of his answer upon the attorney for each of the defendants to be affected by the determination, and personally, or, as the court may direct, upon the defendants so to be affected who have not duly appeared therein by attorney. The controversy between the defendants shall not delay a judgment to which the plaintiff is entitled unless the court otherwise directs. 

C.P.A. 474 further provides that the judgment "may determine the ultimate rights of the parties on the same side as between themselves"; the purpose being to finally determine all of the issues between all of the parties in the one action. (1) Under C.P.A. 264, D cannot bring in a named co-D who was not served with process by P and who has not appeared in the action, merely by serving him with a copy of the answer. Bennett v. Bird, 261 N. Y. S. 540. (2) The remedy under C.P.A. 264 is distinguishable from that under C.P.A. 211a (contribution between tort feasors after judgment). Thus one D, under C.P.A. 264, can claim against a co-D, that as between the two of them, the latter's primary wrongdoing makes him completely responsible to D, if the latter is held secondarily liable to P. Birchall v. Clemons Realty Co., 271 N. Y. S. 54. (3) But co-Ds cannot have litigated between themselves, under C.P.A. 264, a controversy totally unconnected with the cause of action stated in P's complaint. Nauss v. Nauss Bros. Co., 187 N. Y. S. 165. (4) A judgment in favor of one co-D cannot be entered by default against another co-D unless he has had notice and opportunity to defend. Ostrander v. Hart, 130 

N. Y. 406; New Netherland 

Co., 203 N. Y. S. 766. 

Bank, etc. v. Boucheron (5) However, under C.P.A. 264, even if a co-D is not served

with a copy of the answer of another co-D, he is, nevertheless, bound as to all issues litigated and adjudicated in the action. Pratt v. Johnson, 69 N. Y. S. 86.

G. Verification of Answer: In the following cases, the de fendant must verify his answer even though the complaint is unverified: (1) Where the answer contains a dilatory defense (C.P.A. 251), (2) Where the answer puts in issue an allegation of incorporation of a party (R.C.P. 93), (3) Where the action is against a corporation for nonpayment of an instrument for the absolute payment of money (C.P.A. 252), (4) Where the complaint charges defendant with having suffered judgment, conveyed or transferred his property with intent to delay or defraud his creditors, or with being a party or privy to such a transaction by another person with like intent towards the creditors of that person, or with any fraud (not criminal), affecting a right or the property of another (C.P.A. 250; Travelers Ins. Co. v. Mulligan, 247 N. Y. S. 85), (5) Where the complaint is verified (C.P.A. 248); except, (a) Where the answer is that of an infant by his guardian ad litem (C.P.A. 248), (b) Where the defendant could claim his constitutional privilege against self-incrimination (C.P.A. 248), (1) Thus, where defendant is charged with criminal libel, his answer need not be verified. Martin v. 

Bernheim, 34 N. Y. S. 784. (2′) The same applies to a charge of adultery in a divorce action; but if defendant counterclaims charging plaintiff with adultery, the counterclaim must be verified, if the complaint was verified. C.P.A. 1148. (3') Where defendant is charged with fraud, not constituting a crime, he is not excused from verifying his answer. C.P.A. 250, supra. 

(6) Verification of Counterclaim: If the complaint is verified, the counterclaim must be verified; if the complaint is not verified, the answer may contain unverified defenses or denials, and a verified counterclaim (C.P.A. 249), in which latter case plaintiff's

reply must be verified. C.P.A. 248. 

(7) Remedy for Defective Verification (C.P.A. 253): 

A defectively verified pleading may be treated as though unverified, and if served unverified, where it must be verified, it may be regarded as a nullity. 

But the defect is waived, unless, it is returned within a reasonable time with a notice pointing out the defect and stating the election of the party returning it to regard it as a nullity. In such case, the other party has a reasonable time to correct the error, even though his time to serve the paper has expired; or, if he believes there is no error, he may move to compel an acceptance of the pleading. 

2. The Reply: 

A. Generally: Usually, the pleadings in an action end with the answer. But a reply may be a necessary pleading in two classes of cases. 

B. Reply to Counterclaim: The reply is plaintiff's answer to defendant's counterclaim. It must not be inconsistent with the complaint. MacHen Realty Corp. v. Mayser, 221 N. Y. S. 857. It may contain denials permitted in an answer or may set up new matter in avoidance. C.P.A. 272. 

(1) When Necessary: Where a counterclaim is inter posed by answer and plaintiff can raise no objection to it by motion, he must perforce reply to it if he would avoid a default judgment against him. on the counterclaim. C.P.A. 272, 494. (a) However, where matter which might have been pleaded as a counterclaim is set up as a defense only, no reply is necessary. American Guild v. Damon, 186 N. Y. 

360; Susquehanna S. S. Co. v. Anderson & Co., Inc., 239 N. Y. 284. The same applies where the counterclaim is not expressly labelled as such. Hume v. Woodruff, 197 A. D. 510; National Bank of Rochester v. 

Erion-Haines Realty Co., 209 N. Y. S. 522. 

C. Reply by Order: The court has the reserved power to order the plaintiff to reply to an affirmative defense contained in defendant's answer, even though no counterclaim is set up. The power is discretionary and may be invoked only on defendant's motion in order to prevent surprise at the trial and to narrow the issues. Hungarian

Bank v. Titus, 175. A. D. 504. The court should not order a reply to affirmative matter which could be introduced under a general denial. Fragner v. Fischel, 126 

N. Y. S. 478. (1) Although the order of the court is a discretionary one, it is reviewable on appeal. Sizee v. Frankfort Gen. Ins. Co., 162 

A. D. 282. (2) Where the defendant pleads the statute of frauds in bar of an action, a motion to compel plaintiff to reply thereto should be granted. Guinzburg v. Joseph, 126 N. Y. S. 324. (3) However, where defendant sets up new matter in his answer by way of defense, which new matter is wholly and peculiarly within his own knowledge, a reply should not be ordered. 

Masters v. DeZavala, 62 N. Y. S. 791. 

(4) A reply should be ordered to an affirmative defense where it would permit D to dispose of the action by motion for judgment on the pleadings. Bonizon v. N. Y. United Hotels, 260 N. Y. S. 408. (5) Effect of Failure to Reply: Where the plaintiff fails to reply to a defense although ordered to do so, the allegations of the defense will be deemed admitted. C.P.A. 274; Humboldt Explor. Co. v. Fritsch, 150 A. D. 90. 

D. Effect of Reply: Denials in a reply put in issue the allegations of the defense or counterclaim to which they are directed; the new matter contained in the reply is put in issue by operation of law. C.P.A. 243. In the 

Municipal Court, a reply to a counterclaim or affirmative defense is unnecessary, unless ordered by the court. The allegations of the counterclaim or defense are deemed denied by operation of law. Mun Ct. Code, Sec. 87.

E. Verification of Reply: A reply is verified the same way as an answer. 

3. Amended and Supplemental Pleadings: 

A. Amendments as of Course: A party has the right to amend his pleading, once, as a matter of course, if he acts in good faith and "within twenty days after a pleading, or the answer or reply thereto, is served, or, at any time before the period for answering it expires, or within twenty days after the service of a notice of a motion addressed to the pleading." C.P.A. 244. (1) This right may not be exercised simply to delay proceedings. 

Naylor v. Loomis, 79 N. Y. S. 1011. (2) Nor can an amended pleading be served changing the parties named in the summons; although any other change may be

made even to the extent of partly or completely changing a cause of action or defense. Brown v. Leigh, 49 N. Y. 78. 

Cf. Dietz v. Harris, 221 A. D. 581. (3) A party may not amend an amended pleading as a matter of course. Town of Hancock v. Delaware & E. R. R. Co., 

113 N. Y. S. 80.

B. Amendments by Leave of Court: The court in its dis cretion (C.P.A. 105) may permit either party to serve an amended pleading except where the other side will be prejudiced thereby. This may be done either before trial or even after judgment is rendered. Hatch v. Central National Bank, 78 N. Y. 487 (where, after a judgment had been entered and satisfied, the court permitted it to be cancelled upon repayment of the money received by plaintiff, and permitted plaintiff to serve an amended complaint adding a new cause of action which had been left out in the original complaint by an obvious error). (1) The court in its discretion may allow the plaintiff to so amend his complaint as to substitute an entirely new cause of action. Deyo v. Morse, 144 N. Y. 216. (a) Where the new, proposed pleading introduces a cause of action which is barred by the statute of limitations since the beginning of the original action, it is the rule that such an amendment should not be allowed, except with leave to plead the statute as though the action had been brought at the time of the amendment. Dietz v. Harris, supra. (2) Where an action to recover for personal injuries caused by a defective stairway was on the calendar for two years and the statute of limitations had run in favor of the real owner, D, who had been sued as the owner, should not be permitted to serve an amended answer withdrawing a previous admission of ownership and control, which he .alleged had been included in his answer under a mistake on his part as to its legal import, since to do so, would substantially prejudice P. Drescher v. Mirkus, 207 N. Y. S. 739. (3) Where the amended complaint has been duly served and accepted, the original complaint has no further existence as a pleading. Millard v. Del. L. & W. R. R. Co., 197 N. Y. S. 747. (4) Thus, the service of an amended pleading, as of right, after the cause has been noticed for trial and a notice of issue filed, destroys the issue raised by the original pleadings and creates a new issue, and P must serve a new notice of trial. 

Pelzer v. United Dredging Co., 196 N. Y. S. 342. (a) The fact that plaintiff has already served a notice of

trial does not bar him from serving an amended complaint as of right. Clifton v. Brown, 27 Hun. (N. Y.) 231. (b) The adverse party has twenty days to plead or otherwise move with respect to the amended pleading. O'Hara v. Dersching, 276 N. Y. S. 201.

C. Supplemental Pleadings: 

(1) Generally: The difference between an amended pleading and a supplemental pleading is that while the former takes the place of the original pleading, the latter adds to, or supplements, the original, and they are used together. A supplemental pleading is most generally used to set out new facts arising after the commencement of the action and affecting the rights of the parties in the original cause of action. C.P.A. 245; Galm v. Sullivan, 101 N. Y. S. 

1060. C.P.A. 245a, as amended in 1936, provides that the court may permit a party, on application, to serve a supplemental pleading alleging any new and additional cause or causes of action or counterclaims, even though such new causes of action or counterclaim arose after the commencement of the action (and, apparently, even though unconnected or unrelated to the transaction out of which the original cause of action or counterclaim arose). The supplemental pleading may perfect a deficient cause of action. If so, the opponent may get permission to withdraw his defense and satisfy the claim without payment of costs. C.P.A. 245b. (a) In a divorce action, P may be permitted to serve a supplemental complaint setting out adulterous acts committed by D since the commencement of the action, of which acts P had no knowledge when she served her original complaint. Otto v. Otto, 220 A. D. 130. (b) Where facts have occurred subsequent to the commencement of an action tending to affect P's measure of damages in the action, P may serve a supplemental complaint embodying such facts. Brimberg v. Oliver Typewriter Co., 161 N. Y. S. 226 (where after P served his complaint in an action for personal injuries arising out of D's negligence, his condition became such that he was forced to give up his business at great loss). See also Park & Sons v. Hubbard, 198 N. Y. 136. 

(2) How Effected: A supplemental pleading may be in terposed only on an order made on a motion on

notice to adversely interested parties. A supplemental complaint like an amended complaint must be answered. 

4. Service of Pleadings: 

A. Time within which Pleadings Must be Served: In the 

Supreme Court, each party has twenty days in which to answer the preceding pleading of his opponent. (1) Notice of Appearance: If a summons only is served, defendant has twenty days to appear and to demand a copy of the complaint (C.P.A. 237, 257). (2) Answer: Where a complaint is served, defendant has twenty days to answer (C.P.A. 263). (3) Reply: If the answer contains a counterclaim, plaintiff has twenty days to reply thereto (C.P.A. 273). 

(4) Motions: 

(a) Defendant may within twenty days after service of the complaint, object thereto by motion under R.C.P. 102, 103, 104, 106 and 107. If his motion is granted, the complaint will either be dismissed or an amended complaint will be permitted; in the latter case the defendant will have the usual twenty days to answer. (1') If the motion attacking the complaint in point of law is denied, D has ten days to serve an answer after proper service upon him of a copy of the order. denying his motion with notice of entry. C.P.A. 283. (b) Plaintiff may within twenty days raise an objection to the answer by motion under R.C.P. 102, 103 and 104; or, if he wishes to dismiss a counterclaim under R.C.P. 

109 and 110, within ten days. (1') If his motion, attacking the answer in point of law is denied, he has ten days after service upon him of a copy of the order denying his motion, with notice of entry thereof, to serve a reply. C.P.A. 283. (c) In all cases, if the last day on which to serve a pleading falls out on a Sunday, or legal holiday, service may be made on the first business day thereafter. C.P.A. 

164 provides: "Where it is prescribed by statute or in the rules of civil practice that a notice must be given or a paper must be served, within a specified time before an act is to be done; or that the adverse party has a specified time after notice or service within which to do an act; if service is made through the post-office, pursuant to statute or rule, three days shall be added to the time specified, except that service of a copy of

a note of issue may be made through the post-office not less than fourteen days before the day of trial, including the day of service. Deposit in a branch postoffice or in a mail box regularly maintained by the government of the U. S. is equivalent to deposit in a general post-office."

B. Extensions of Time to Serve Pleadings: 

(1) By Stipulation: Attorneys for the respective parties to an action, to suit their own reasonable convenience, may stipulate to enlarge the time of one to serve a pleading. Such an agreement should be in writing (R.C.P. 4) and should be worded expressly to accomplish the purpose for which it was intended. (2) By Order: The time to plead may be extended by order of the court. (a) Ex parte: Where a party's time to plead has not expired, or has expired, but no default judgment has been entered against him, he may apply to the court for an ex parte order, (i. e. without notice to his adversary) for an extension of time (C.P.A. 98). The order is usually granted on an affidavit of merits setting forth a good defense and the party's good faith (R. 

C.P. 88). A copy of the order (if obtained ex parte) and the affidavits upon which it was granted must be served on the opposite side (R.C.P. 85). It would seem, however, that where the extension desired is for time to plead after the statutory period has expired, and there is danger of default judgment being entered, the party should move on notice (by order to show cause) to open the default and extend the time to plead with a stay of proceedings in the meantime. Ornstein v. Goldberg, 226 A. D. 

746; Paddock v. Palmer, 66 N. Y. S. 743. 

(b) On Two Days' Notice: An ex parte order, how ever, will not be made in the following cases, and the party must apply for an extension of his time to plead on two days notice to his adversary: (1') Where the party's time to plead has already

been once extended by stipulation or order (R.C.P. 87), (2′) In an action on a promissory note or other instrument for the unconditional payment of money (R.C.P. 86), (3') Where the application is for an extension of time to make a motion (under R.C.P. 

102, 103, 104) addressed to a pleading (R.C.P. 105).

CHAPTER VIII. 

MOTIONS 1. Motion Practice Generally: A motion is an application to a court or judge for an order usually granting incidental relief to a party to an action. C.P.A. 113. C.P.A. 115 and 116 tell us when the application is to be made to the court and when to a judge thereof. A motion may be ex parte, or on notice (by notice of motion or by order to show cause). 

R.C.P. 60.

A. Ex Parte: Where the Civil Practice Act requires notice of the application, notice must be given. Where the Act says that the application may be made ex parte, no notice need be given. Where the Act is silent, notice must be given to all parties who have appeared, where the order sought might in any way prejudice their rights. As in the case of a contested motion (i. e. one on notice) the affidavits in the ex parte application set forth the evidentiary facts, upon which relief is sought, but unlike the case of the contested motion, the order granting the relief is physically attached to the moving papers. The affidavits on the ex parte application must comply with 

R.C.P. 61. 

(1) Where An Ex Parte Order is Made: Any order in a County 

Court which a county judge may make out of court, may be made ex parte (except an order to stay proceedings which must be made upon notice) by a Supreme Court justice or by a county judge of any other county. Where an order, in any other court, may be made by a judge of the court, out of court, and without notice, and the particular judge is not specially designated by law, it may be made by any judge of the court in any part of the state; or, (except to stay proceedings after verdict, report or decision) by a

Supreme Court justice, or by the county judge of the county where the action is triable or in which the attorney for the applicant resides. C.P.A. 130. The court may make any ex parte order authorized by statute to be made by a judge. 

C.P.A. 128. An order made by a Supreme Court justice, out of court, is not void, because a statute or rule requires the order to be made only by such Court, unless the order is made outside the county or judicial district in which an application to the Court for such order is authorized. C.P.A. 129.

(2) How Vacated or Modified: Where the ex parte order grants a provisional remedy it can be vacated only as prescribed by law; in any other case, it may be vacated or modified, without notice, by the judge who made it, or upon notice, by him, or by the court. C.P.A. 131. An ex parte order made by the Supreme Court, or by a justice thereof, may be vacated or modified, ex parte, by the Appellate Division or presiding justice thereof. The Appellate Term, (or any justice thereof) has similar power with respect to an ex parte order made by a justice of the City or Municipal Courts of the city of New York. C.P.A. 132.

B. Contested Motions: Only those papers, copies of which are served on his adversary by the moving party, or those papers referred to therein or filed papers, can be used on the hearing of the contested motion, unless permission of the court is granted for the filing of additional affidavits. When a motion is made in an action the pleadings therein are deemed before the court. R.C.P. 

64, 65. (1) Where Made: R.C.P. 63 tells us where a contested motion should be made returnable. For our purposes, only the following is important: (a) A motion on notice in an action in the Supreme Court must be made within the judicial district in which the action is triable or in a county adjoining the county in which it is triable. (b) Where the action is triable in the first or the eighth judicial districts, the motion must be made in the district where the action is triable; and a motion on notice cannot be made in the first district in an action triable elsewhere. (c) The last two subdivisions do not apply to a case where it is especially prescribed by law that a motion may be made in the county where the applicant or other person to be affected thereby or the attorney resides. (d) In the first judicial district, all motions must be noticed to be heard at, and all orders to show cause must be returnable, at the special term for hearing of litigated motions, except in cases where the special rules of the first judicial district require such motion to be made at some other term of the court. (e) Except in the first judicial district, a motion may be made in any county in the district in which is situated the county where the action is triable, or in a county in any other district adjoining the county in which it is so triable.

MOTIONS 

135 (f) In populous counties such as New York and Kings, there is a Special Term of the Supreme Court (Parts 1 and 

III) for the hearing of contested motions and a Special

Term, Part II, for ex parte business. The rules of the court should be consulted in each case. 

(2) Requisites of Notice of Motion: 

(a) Title: The notice of motion is entitled in the name of the action or proceeding. (See "Forms,"

Appendix.) 

(b) Body: The body of the notice of motion contains a notice of the nature of the motion and the time and place where the hearing will be brought on and must specify the papers to be used in support of the motion. R.C.P. 62, 63, 64, 65. (1′) It should include in its request for relief the words "and for such other relief as may be just”, or similar phraseology. See Smith v. Fleischman, 45 

N. Y. S. 553. (2′) Under C.P.A. 117, the moving party may make a so-called "omnibus motion", asking for several or alternative kinds of relief which must be specified in the notice of motion. Thus, a party may move for a bill of particulars, or, in the alternative, to have the adversary's pleading made more definite and certain; or, move to vacate defendant's notice of examination before trial on the ground of insufficiency of the answer with respect to the issues upon which the examination is sought and at the same time move for summary judgment under 

R.C.P. 113. General Invest. Co. v. I. R. T. Co., 

193 N. Y. S. 892. (3′) Under the same section (C.P.A. 117) at least three days before the return date of the motion, the adverse party may serve affidavits and notice of motion asking for specified relief to which he may deem himself entitled. 

(3) Time Table for Service of Motion Papers: 

(a) Except in the case of an order to show cause, or, a cross motion under C.P.A. 117, a party must give his adversary eight days' notice of motion, but where the attorneys have their offices in the same city or village, five days' notice is sufficient. R.C.P. 60.

(b) By giving eight days' notice, a moving party becomes entitled to service of copies of answering affidavits one day before the hearing. C.P.A. 117. (c) By giving ten days' notice of motion, and including in the notice of motion a demand that answering affidavits be served five days before hearing, and such answering affidavits are served, the moving party may serve replying affidavits at least two days before the hearing.

R.C.P. 64. 

(d) In all cases where service of motion papers is made by mail, three days additional should be allowed. C.P.A. 164. (4) Order to Show Cause: It sometimes becomes necessary to bring on the hearing of a motion in less than the required time; as, for example, where the moving party seeks to vacate a default judgment against him upon which execution has been issued and levy made or threatened. In order to stay the levy, or sale under the levy, and to get quick action, the judgment debtor should bring on his motion by an order to show cause. (a) Purpose: The purpose of an order to show cause is, as its name implies, to compel a party to show cause why the relief demanded by the moving party should not be granted. The order adjudicates nothing and merely preserves the status quo of the action until a determination of the motion which it brings on. (1′) However, a stay of proceedings on an ex parte order may not usually be had for longer than twenty days. C.P.A. 169. (b) How Made: The order to show cause may be either a court order or a judge's order, except that an order to show cause initiating contempt proceedings for failure to pay alimony, under C.P.A. 1172, should be a court order. Generally, in practice, the order to show cause is a judge's order and does not have to be filed until the hearing of the motion. 

(c) Contents: The order to show cause contains the same statements as to the papers to be used on the motion and the time and place of the hearing thereof as are commonly contained in a notice of motion. It must also contain a special provision directing the time and

MOTIONS 

137 manner of its service. R.C.P. 60. (d) Moving Affidavit: The affidavit on which the order to show cause is based, besides containing the evidentiary facts contained in the affidavit where the motion is brought on by an ordinary notice of motion, must also contain a statement showing why shorter notice than usually required is necessary, a statement showing the status of the action and the statement required under 

R.C.P. 61. 

(5) Proceedings on Return Day: Having thus briefly considered how a motion is generally brought on, we now turn to what happens upon the return day. 

In Kings County, a note of issue of motion must be filed two days before the return date with the clerk of Special Term, Part I. In New York county, the moving papers, with proof of service thereof, are filed. In either event, the motion will appear on the motion calendar which will be published in the New 

York Law Journal for the specified day. (a) Where the moving party appears on the call of the calendar in court and answers for the motion, and no one answers in opposition, the motion will be granted by default, if the moving papers are sufficient on their face and upon their being filed by the moving party, if they have not already been filed. Likewise, if the moving party fails to appear, his adversary may enter an order denying the motion by default. (b) If both parties appear and if there are no preliminary objections to the regularity of the motion papers or their service, or if such objections are waived or overruled, argument proper of the motion begins. The attorney for the moving party states the facts supported by the moving affidavits and argues for the motion. His adversary then follows in an argument in opposition to the motion based on the answering affidavits, to which argument the attorney for the moving party may then reply. (c) After the argument, the court in its discretion may adjourn the motion to permit further proof by affidavit or before a referee (e. g. under R.C.P. 108) or it may grant or deny the motion with or without conditions or costs, or it may, and generally does, reserve decision thereon. The court may ask counsel to submit memoranda of law to sustain their contentions and aid it in its decisions. Memoranda of law should not appear in affidavits.

C. The Order: Let us assume now that the court has decided in favor of the moving party. The decision on the motion will appear in the New York Law Journal under "Decisions" for the respective counties covered by the Law Journal. It now becomes necessary for the moving party to prepare an order for the court to sign and have entered. C.P.A. 127. 

(1) Settling the Order: An order on a contested motion is usually a court order, and where the decision so directs, (as a matter of fact, it is the usual and safe practice even where there is no such direction), it must be "settled" on two days' notice to the opposite side. This means that the successful party serves on the adverse party a copy of the proposed order with a statement that on two days' notice at a certain designated time and place, (usually, the clerk's office of the motion part), the order will be submitted for signature to the justice who decided the motion. Should the adverse party deem it to be not in accordance with the court's decision, or otherwise defective, he may submit a counter order and the court will sign the order deemed proper. If a defective order is entered without notice of settlement, the other side may move to have it "re-settled" and corrected. 

(2) Entering the Order: If the order is deemed proper by it, the court will sign it, and if a court order, it will be filed in the office of the clerk of the court, pursuant to the word "Enter" contained at the foot of the order. R.C.P. 71. When the order is signed and entered, the successful party should conform a copy and serve it, together with notice of entry, upon his adversary, whose time to appeal therefrom will run from the date of such service. C.P.A. 612. 

(3) "Short Form Order" (R.C.P. 70): In the First De partment, a short form of order on a motion is used as permitted by R.C.P. 70, especially in case of default on the motion. This consists of an informal memorandum attached to the motion papers indicating by numbers the papers used on the motion and the determination of the motion, and is signed

MOTIONS 

139 by the court or judge with his name or initials, like any other court order. (a) But a mere indorsement "motion denied", without a recital of the papers used, is a nullity. Traub v. Arrow 

Mfg. Corp., 202 N. Y. S. 121. (b) A short form order signed by the justice determining the motion, which directs the winning party to "submit order" is not an order, but merely a notation of the determination, the submitted long form order being the order entered on the motion. In re Friedman, 267 

N. Y. S. 523. 

2. Motions Addressed to the Complaint: R.C.P. 62 requires that a notice of motion specify the mistake, omission, defect or irregularity, where a motion is based on the same. C.P.A. 

280 tells us that in certain cases the particulars showing the objection to a pleading must be specifically set forth in the notice of motion. We will see what these cases are as we come to them.

A. To Correct and Compel Amendments: 

(1) Generally: These motions are corrective or regulatory; they are: to compel P to separately state and number (R.C.P. 90); to compel P to make the wording of his complaint more definite and certain (R.C.P. 102); to compel P to drop mis-joined parties, or to add parties (R.C.P. 102); and to strike out improper matter in the complaint (R.C.P. 103). 

The motion under R.C.P. 90 should be made before answering, else the defect is waived. Duffy v. 

Henkel, 211 A. D. 342. The motion under R.C.P. 

102 should be noticed within twenty days of the service of the complaint, else the defects are waived (except a defect of parties not apparent on the face of the complaint). R.C.P. 105. The motion under 

R.C.P. 103 should likewise be noticed within twenty days of the service of the complaint else the defects are waived. R.C.P. 105. (Impropriety of joinder of causes of action under C.P.A. 258 must now be challenged under that section itself and not under 

R.C.P. 102. The defect need not be taken promptly. 

C.P.A. 278; R.C.P. 102 as amended in 1936.) The moving affidavit, if used at all, on the corrective motion, is only to show the status of the action and

the timeliness of the motion, since the defect is on the face of the pleading. The affidavit may, however, go into the facts on the merits, where the motion is to strike out sham matter, or to cure misjoinder or non-joinder of parties if such latter defect does not appear on the face. C.P.A. 283 does not apply to the corrective motions. 225 Fifth Ave. 

Corp. v. Freeman, 199 N. Y. S. 519. 

(2) Motion to Strike Out Improper Matter (R.C.P. 

103): If any allegation in any pleading (complaint, answer or reply) be sham, frivolous, irrelevant, redundant, unnecessary, repetitious, scandalous, or prejudicial to a fair trial, the court may order such matter stricken out and the pleading amended accordingly. (a) A "sham" allegation is one which is good in form but which can be demonstrated to be false in fact. Affidavits should be permitted to prove such falsity. Wayland v. Tysen, 45 N. Y. 281. (b) A "frivolous" allegation is one indicative of bad faith; it is patently insufficient and cannot be supported by plausible argument, or needs no argument to show its defectiveness, (as where D denies knowledge or information sufficient to form a belief as to the execution by him of a guaranty agreement upon which he is sued). Royal Bank of Canada v. Williams, 222 N. Y. S. 425. (c) An "irrelevant" allegation is one that has no real relation to the issues raised by the pleadings and which cannot affect the decision of the court. The test is whether evidence of the facts pleaded can be admitted upon the trial as relevant. Hall v. U. S. Casualty Co., 211 N. Y. S. 741. (d) A repetition of denials is surplusage, even though made in separate, affirmative defenses, and may be stricken out as being "redundant, repetitious and unnecessary" under R.C.P. 103. Blackwell v. Columbia Trust Co., 192 

N. Y. S. 226. (e) Similarly, evidentiary facts contained in a pleading may be stricken out as unnecessary under R.C.P. 103. Peabody v. Travelers Ins. Co., 206 A. D. 206. (f) But matters pleaded in justification of an alleged assault, under a general denial in an answer, should not be stricken out as being irrelevant and redundant although they should be pleaded specially. Fisk v. Hobern, 198 

N. Y. S. 654.

MOTIONS 

141 

(3) Motion to Compel P to Separately State and Number Causes of Action (R.C.P. 90): If recovery on one of the alleged causes of action would not bar a recovery upon others, there is more than one cause of action stated, and the motion under R.C.P. 90 should be granted. (a) Thus where P sues to recover money earned under a contract of employment and also damages by reason of D's breach of the contract, there are two causes of action which should be separately stated and numbered. 

Dailey v. Standard, 167 N. Y. S. 38. (b) The same is true where P seeks to recover the price of goods delivered under a contract and damages for D's breach in refusing to accept further deliveries under the contract. Sampson v. Pels Co., 186 N. Y. S. 815. (c) But where P alleges that six persons saw the libel by D, only one cause of action and not six are stated. Fried v. Halstead, 203 A. D. 113. Where three Ps join under

C.P.A. 209 in an action for libel on the same libelous publication, they must separately state and number their causes of action. Peacock v. Tata Sons, 200 N. Y. S. 656. (d) On the same theory where P in a negligence action to recover for personal injuries alleges that D's act was actionable at common law, under a state employer's liability law and under a federal law, he cannot be compelled to separately state and number, because he is setting out only one cause of action predicated on different grounds of liability. Payne v. N. Y. Ry. Co., 201 N. Y. 436. (e) Nor can you compel P to make separate causes of action out of different items of damages. Chalmers v. 

Murphy, 144 N. Y. S. 825. (f) Causes of action in the alternative should be separately stated and numbered. Michie v. Slayback, 148 N. Y. S. 

890 (money had and received, conversion, fraud and deceit). (g) Where P alleges that Ds negligently prescribed, prepared and compounded a certain medicine resulting in personal injuries to P, he should separately state and number his causes of action against D physician who prescribed and D pharmacist who compounded. Cohen v. Jarecky, 35 N. Y. S. 935. 

(4) Motion to Make More Definite and Certain (R.C.P. 

102) Where an allegation of a pleading is so indefinite and uncertain that its meaning is obscure,

or where the facts are so general that the cause of action cannot be inferred, a motion to make more definite and certain under R.C.P. 102 will lie. (a) Thus, where a complaint is defective in alleging only a legal conclusion as to the legal effect of various agreements, none of which are set forth in the complaint, a motion to make more definite and certain is the proper remedy and not a motion for a bill of particulars. U. S. 

Printing Co. v. Powers, 157 N. Y. S. 440. (1') But where the defect in a pleading is not that its allegations are indefinite in their statement, but that the pleading lacks facts necessary to constitute a cause of action, a motion to dismiss is the proper remedy and not a motion to make more definite and certain or a motion for a bill of particulars. U. S. 

Printing Co. v. Powers, 170 N. Y. S. 314. (b) Ordinarily, matters as to time are brought out by a motion for a bill of particulars, which although usually made after joinder of issue, may be made before. 

Bracken v. Toland, 137 N. Y. S. 1043. But where a date is a material part of a pleading, a motion to make more definite and certain is proper, especially where its presence in the complaint would enable defendant to determine whether to plead the statute of limitations or not. Bennett v. Lawrence, 75 N. Y. S. 902. (1′) Thus, where a complaint for goods sold and delivered did not allege the dates of sale or delivery, a motion to make more definite and certain was granted. Royle v. McLaughlin, 186 N. Y. S. 356. 

Cf. Pigone v. Lauria, 100 N. Y. S. 976. (c) It has been held that where a cause of action stated is on a simple contract, a motion to make more definite and certain will not lie where the only indefiniteness is the failure to state whether the contract was oral or in writing. Swartmore Textile Co. v. Bernhard Co., 172

N. Y. S. 15. The better rule is that the motion should be granted in such case. First Presbyterian Church v. 

Kennedy, 76 N. Y. S. 264; Royle v. McLaughlin, supra. 

(5) Motion to Compel Addition or Dropping of Parties: 

Under C.P.A. 192 no action or special proceeding may be defeated by non-joinder or mis-joinder of parties. If there is a defect of parties, as for example, where only one of two joint obligors is sued, or where a partial assignee sues alone at law, D may waive the defect as to himself by failing to move promptly under R.C.P. 102 if the defect appears on ?

MOTIONS 

143 1 the face. (But refer to discussion, infra, of C.P.A. 

193, subd. 1; C.P.A. 105 and Gitelman v. Feltman, 191 N. Y. 205.) The notice of motion on a motion made under this subdivision of R.C.P. 102, must specifically set out the defects complained of. C.P.A. 280. 

3. Motions for Judgment Dismissing the Complaint: These motions are divided into two classes; for defects appearing on the face of the complaint (R.C.P. 106) and for defects not appearing on the face thereof (R.C.P. 107). In both cases the motion must be noticed within twenty days from service of the complaint or the defects (except those hereinafter mentioned) will be waived. C.P.A. 278, 279; Gordon v. Bank, 205 N. Y. S. 522. An affidavit, if used at all, on the motion under R.C.P. 106, is only to show the status of the action and the timeliness of the motion. If the motion is under R.C.P. 107, the affidavit must show the facts indicating that the objection exists. If defendant's motion is granted the complaint is dismissed or an amended complaint is permitted to be served. If the motion is denied defendant may answer, as a matter of right, within ten days after service upon him of a copy of the order denying his motion with notice of entry thereof. C.P.A. 283. If the motion is under R.C.P. 107, any question of fact arising thereon may be tried by a jury or referee. R.C.P. 108.

A. For Defects on Face (R.C.P. 106): These defects are five: (1) That the court has no jurisdiction of defendant's person: (a) This does not mean that D was not served or improperly served. It refers to cases where the defendant is not subject to the court's jurisdiction, e. g., a foreign executor, ambassador, minister, consul, or branch of a foreign sovereignty (De Simone v. Transportes, etc., 192 N. Y. S. 815) or, in the case of a money judgment action in the county court, where the complaint shows that D is a non-resident of the country. Meyers v. Locomotive Co., 201 N. Y. 163. Cf. Gilbert v. York, 111 N. Y. 544. (2) That the court has no jurisdiction of the subject of the action: (a) An action to abate a nuisance outside the state of New York, would be illustrative of this defect (Peo. v. Cen-

tral Rrd. of N. J., 42 N. Y. 283), or a divorce action brought in the Municipal Court. (b) This defect is not waived by failure to move promptly. 

C.P.A. 279. (3) That plaintiff has no legal capacity to sue: (a) Such a defect would appear on the face of a complaint where an alleged infant sues without the appointment of a guardian ad litem on his behalf, or where P sues as administrator, executor, receiver, trustee, etc. and does not allege facts showing his right to act in such representative capacity. Secor v. Pendelton, 47 Hun. 

281. See also Ward v. Petrie, 157 N. Y. 301. (b) The failure of a foreign business corporation, doing business in this state and suing on a contract made in this state, to allege that it was authorized to do business within the state under G.C.L. 218 gives grounds for a motion to dismiss the complaint for failure to state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action (not waived under C.P.A. 279) as well as for lack of legal capacity in P to sue. Wood & Selick v. Ball, 190 N. Y. 217. (c) The notice of motion should be specific as to the defect. 

C.P.A. 280. (4) That there is another action pending between the same parties for the same cause in a New York court. (a) Thus, where D in an action sets up a counterclaim, he cannot bring a new action on the matter constituting his counterclaim as long as the first action is pending.

But where the later action seeks greater relief, the earlier action cannot be a bar. Cornell v. Bonsall, 163 

N. Y. S. 384. (b) Another action pending between the same parties for the same cause in a federal court cannot be pleaded as a bar to an action in a state court. Oneida County Bank v. Herendon, 101 N. Y. 173. (5) That the complaint does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. (a) This defect is not waived by failure to move within twenty days after service (C.P.A. 279) and may be raised at the trial (Seamon v. Borentsen, 180 N. Y. 333), or even on appeal for the first time. Klippel v. Weil, 198 N. Y. S. 13. (b) The test applicable in making this motion is fundamentally the same as under the old form of demurrer and is: assuming everything that P alleges to be true, is he entitled to judgment as against D? If yes, the motion to dismiss must be denied even though the alle-

MOTIONS 

145 gations of the cause of action in the complaint are obscure, imperfect, or argumentative, (Marie v. Garrison, 83 N. Y. 14) or even though the relief demanded is partly inappropriate. Milliken v. Western Union Tel. 

Co., 110 N. Y. 403. If no, the motion to dismiss should be granted unless P is permitted to amend. (c) A complaint drawn in equity and asking only equitable relief may be dismissed on this motion, if it shows that P has only a cause of action at law (Kraemer v. World 

Wide Trading Co., 187 N. Y. S. 16; Chadbourne v. Ritz 

Carlton Restaurant & Hotel Co. Inc., 202 N. Y. S. 805) or, that he has an adequate remedy at law. Robinson v. Whitaker, 199 N. Y. S. 680; Sevin v. Jones, 244 

N. Y. S. 541. See also Jackson v. Strong, 222 N. Y. 

149, C.P.A. 111.

B. For Defects not appearing on Face (R.C.P. 107): These defects are: (1) no jurisdiction of defendant's person, (2) no jurisdiction of the subject of action, (3) plaintiff's legal incapacity to sue, (4) another action pending between the same parties for the same cause in a state court of New York, (5) res adjudicata, (6) statute of limitations, (7) general release, (8) statute of frauds, (9) infancy or other disability of defendant. (a) In cases 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, and 9, the notice of motion should be specific as to the defect. C.P.A. 280. (b) In case 2, the defect is not waived by failing to move promptly. C.P.A. 279. (c) In all other cases (except case 1) the defect is waived unless taken promptly by motion or set up in the answer by way of defense. Gentilala v. Fay Taxicab Co., 243 N. Y. 397. The defect in case 1 should be taken by special notice of appearance; if taken by answer, the answer itself may subject the defendant to the court's jurisdiction. 

4. Motions Addressed to the Answer: 

A. The Corrective Motions: These are: to compel defendant to separately state and number defenses and/or causes of action in the counterclaim (R.C.P. 90); to compel D to make the wording of his answer more definite and certain (R.C.P. 102); to compel D to add or drop parties

in the counterclaim (R.C.P. 102); and to strike out improper matter (R.C.P. 103). Generally, the same rules discussed with respect to these motions when addressed to the complaint apply where they are addressed to the answer. 

B. To Strike Out the Answer for Sham or Frivolity and for Judgment; (R.C.P. 104): If the whole answer of defendant be sham or frivolous the court may treat the pleading as a nullity and give judgment accordingly. 

R.C.P. 104. That a pleading is sham (i.e. good in form but false in fact) can be shown only by affidavits, but that a pleading is frivolous must be patent on its face and no affidavits or arguments should be necessary to establish it. Horvath v. Brettschneider, 227 N. Y. S. 

109, Cook v. Warren, 88 N. Y. 37. The motion must be noticed within twenty days of the service of the answer (R.C.P. 105) but the same result can be obtained, in case of a frivolous answer, by a later motion for judgment on the pleadings. R.C.P. 112. (1) In Wayland v. Tysen, 45 N. Y. 281, it was held that a verified general denial cannot be stricken out as sham thus depriving D of his constitutional right to a jury trial, even though the court was convinced of its falsity. This is questionable law today especially where the motion is for summary judgment under R.C.P. 113 and the affidavits clearly indicate the falsity of the denial. Dwan v. Massarene, 192 

N. Y. S. 577. Cf. Fleisher v. Terker, 259 N. Y. 60. (2) An answer which consists merely of denials of knowledge or information sufficient to form a belief of a personal transaction necessarily, or presumptively, within D's knowledge, may be stricken out under R.C.P. 104. Harley v. Plant, 210 N. Y. 405; Dahlstrom v. Gemunder, 198 N. Y. 449. See also Kirschbaum v. Eschmann, 205 N. Y. 127.

C. To Dismiss Counterclaim or Strike Out Defense: 

A. For Defects Appearing on the Face (R.C.P. 109): 

These defects are: (1) no jurisdiction of the subject matter of the counterclaim, (2) defendant has no legal capacity to recover on the counterclaim, (3) another action pending between the parties, etc., (4) the counterclaim is not properly interposable,

MOTIONS 

147 (5) the counterclaim does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action, (6) the defense of new matter is insufficient in law (a demurrer motion that "searches the record" back to the complaint). (a) The defects above, except in cases 1, 5 and 6 are waived unless taken by motion within ten days of the service of the answer. C.P.A. 279. (b) In cases 2 and 4 the notice of motion should designate specifically the defects complained of. C.P.A. 280. (c) The affidavit, if used at all, should concern itself only with the status of the action and the timeliness of the motion. (d) If the motion is denied and a reply is made necessary because of the presence of a counterclaim in the answer, C.P.A. 283 (supra) applies.

D. For Defects Not Appearing on Face (R.C.P. 110): These defects are: (1) no jurisdiction of the subject matter of the counterclaim, (2) another action pending, etc. (3) res adjudicata, (4) general release, (5) statute of frauds. (a) The defects above, except in case 1, are waived unless taken by motion within ten days of the service of the answer. C.P.A. 279. (b) In cases 3, 4, and 5 the notice of motion should designate specifically the defects complained of. C.P.A. 280. (c) Since the defects do not appear on the face, affidavits should be used to show their existence. (d) If the motion is denied, C.P.A. 283 applies. (e) Note that only the original plaintiff may move to dismiss the counterclaim under R.C.P. 109 or 110, and that a defendant impleaded under C.P.A. 271 may not make such a motion. Stokes v. Ottoman etc. Co., 228 

N. Y. S. 728. 

5. Motions Addressed to the Reply: These motions are: 

A. The Corrective Motions: To compel P to separately state and number defenses (R.C.P. 90); to compel P to make his wording more definite and certain (R.C.P. 102); to strike out improper matter (R.C.P. 103); and to correct a defect of parties. R.C.P. 102. (It is difficult to see how this last applies since a reply can add no parties.)

B. To Treat the Entire Reply as a Nullity for Frivolity or 

Sham. R.C.P. 104.

C. To Strike Out the Reply or Separate Defense Therein for 

Insufficiency in Law (a demurrer motion that searches the record back to the counterclaim). Security Finance 

Co. v. Stuart, 224 N. Y. S. 257. (1) The motions under A and B must be noticed within twenty days after service of the reply. R.C.P. 105. The motion under C must be noticed within ten days of that time. 

R.C.P. 111. (In this last respect R.C.P. 111 is clearly repugnant to C.P.A. 279 which provides that legal insufficiency of a defense is not waived by failure to take promptly by motion. See Hale v. Hirsch, 199 N. Y. S. 514.) 

6. Motion for Judgment on the Pleadings (R.C.P. 112): “If either party be entitled to judgment on the pleadings, the court may, on motion, give judgment accordingly and without regard to which party makes the motion." R.C.P. 112. 

See Mun. Ct. Code Sec. 91. 

A. Application: This motion can be made only after the joinder of issue, (it may be made at trial). Therefore, the only objections which it can raise to a complaint, are that the complaint is insufficient to state a cause of action and that the court has no jurisdiction over the subject matter. All other objections to the complaint in point of law are waived by answering. C.P.A. 278. If the answer contains no counterclaim, its sufficiency in law may be tested by this motion. Where there is a counterclaim (or a complete affirmative defense) there must first be a reply thereto before this motion can be availed of to attack those objections not waived by the reply. C.P.A. 

278, 279. The sufficiency of a reply may also be tested by this motion.

B. Necessary Papers: These consist of the notice of motion, the bill of particulars, if any, and the pleadings. (1) Affidavits on the merits may not be considered by the court, nor may evidence be taken. Emanuel v. Walter, 123 N. Y. S. 

491; Donnelly v. Bauder, 216 N. Y. S. 188. Admissions in writing intended to be treated as part of the pleadings, may be used. C.P.A. 476; Lloyd v. R. M. S. Corp., 251 N. Y. 318.

C. Principles Governing Determination of Motion: The facts stated in the pleadings are deemed admitted for the purposes of the motion. Walter v. Walter, 123 N.

MOTIONS 

149 Y. S. 491. If the pleadings raise any material issue of fact, the motion must be denied. Kidder v. Hesselman, 196 N. Y. S. 837. The motion searches the record, the court being empowered to render judgment for either party without regard to which party makes the motion. 

(1) Bill of Particulars: The bill of particulars, if resorted to on the motion, cannot make the complaint bad nor the answer good. Thus where P sues on a contract which on the face of the complaint satisfies the statute of frauds, and D interposes only a general denial, a bill of particulars thereafter served by P showing his contract to be unenforcible under the statute of frauds, will not be read with the complaint so as to make the latter bad and the answer good. The same would be true where the statute of limitations is involved. Harmon v. 

Peats Co., 243 N. Y. 473. But where P sues upon a contract alleged in his complaint to be founded upon a valuable consideration, D interposing only a general denial, and thereafter P serves a bill of particulars showing the contract to be without consideration, the bill of particulars may be read upon D's motion for judgment on the pleadings. In such case, the bill of particulars would not make a bad answer good, since D's answer was always good. Richardson v. 

Gregory, 219 N. Y. S. 397. (2) A power to amend, as of course, by serving an amended pleading, cannot be invoked to defeat the effect of the granting of P's motion for judgment on the pleadings, unless the amended answer is served before the motion is heard. 

Snedecor v. Chapel, 183 N. Y. S. 86. 

7. Motion For Summary Judgment: 

A. Generally: R.C.P. 113, taken from the English rules of practice, provided prior to 1932 that at any time after the answer is served in an action to recover a debt or liquidated demand arising on a contract, or on a judgment, the answer may be struck out on plaintiff's motion made on affidavits showing the existence of his cause of action and his belief that there is no defense, unless the defendant by answering affidavits, or other proof, satisfies the court that he is bringing forward, in good faith, an issue to be tried involving an arguable defense. The motion may be made only after joinder of issue, or, at the trial. As amended in 1932, and as further amended in 1933, R.C.P. 113 now reads: "When an answer is served in an action, 1. To recover a debt or liquidated demand arising on a contract express or implied in fact or in law, sealed or not sealed; or,

2. To recover a debt or liquidated demand arising on a judgment for a stated sum; or, 3. On a statute where the sum sought to be recovered is a sum of money other than a penalty; or, 4. To recover an unliquidated debt or demand for a sum of money only arising on a contract express or implied in fact or in law, sealed or not sealed, other than for breach of promise to marry; or, 5. To recover possession of a specific chattel or chattels with or without a claim for the hire thereof or for damages for the taking or detention thereof; or, 6. To enforce or foreclose a lien or mortgage; or, 7. For specific performance of a contract in writing for the sale or purchase of property, including such alternative and incidental relief as the case may require; or, 8. For an accounting arising on a written contract, sealed or not sealed. 

The complaint may be dismissed or answer may be struck out and judgment entered in favor of either party on motion upon the affidavit of a party or of any other person having knowledge of the facts, setting forth such evidentiary facts as shall, if the motion is made on behalf of the plaintiff, establish the cause of action sufficiently to entitle plaintiff to judgment, and if the motion is made on behalf of the defendant, such evidentiary facts, including copies of all documents, as shall fully disclose defendant's contentions and show that his denials or defenses are sufficient to defeat plaintiff, together with the belief of the moving party either that there is no defense to the action or that the action has no merit, as the case may be, unless the other party, by affidavit or other proof, shall show such facts as may be deemed by the judge hearing the motion sufficient to entitle him to a trial of the issues. If upon such motion made on behalf of a defendant, it shall appear that the plaintiff is entitled to judgment, the judge hearing the motion may award judgment to the plaintiff even though the plaintiff has not made a cross-motion therefor.

If the plaintiff or defendant in any action set forth in subdivisions 3, 4, or 5 hereunder shall fail to show such facts as may be deemed by the judge hearing the motion, to present any triable issue of fact other than the question of the amount of damages for which judgment should be granted, an assessment to determine such amount shall forthwith be ordered for immediate hearing to be tried by a referee, by the court alone, or, by the court and a jury, whichever shall be appropriate. Upon the rendering of the assessment, judgment in the action shall be rendered forthwith.

When in any actions in cases set forth in subdivisions 6, 7

MOTIONS 

151 and 8 hereunder the judge hearing the motion has been convinced that there is no preliminary, triable issue of fact, the court shall forthwith render an appropriate judgment or order, and thenceforth the action shall proceed in the ordinary course. 

Where an answer is served in any action setting forth a defense which is sufficient as a matter of law, where the defense is founded upon facts established prima facie by documentary evidence or official record, the complaint may be dismissed on motion unless the plaintiff by affidavit, or other proof, shall show such facts as may be deemed by the judge hearing the motion sufficient to raise an issue with respect to the verity and conclusiveness of such documentary evidence or official record. (It has finally been decided by the 

Court of Appeals, settling a divergence of opinion between the 

First and Second Departments, that the word "any" in this paragraph is to be literally construed and that it does not refer only to the eight permitted types of action first mentioned by R.C.P. 113. Lederer v. Wise Shoe Co., 276 N. Y. 459.)

This rule shall be applicable to counterclaims, so that either party may move with respect to the same as though the counterclaim were an independent action. The court in its discretion may provide for the withholding of entry of judgment until the disposition of the issue in the main case. 

This rule shall be applicable to all pending actions."

B. Necessary Papers: These are the notice of motion, the pleadings, affidavit and exhibits, if any, substantiating 

P's cause of action or D's defense. Either P or D may make the motion. If P moves, his affidavit, made by himself or by some other person who knows the facts, sets forth evidentiary matter sufficient to establish his cause of action, and states deponent's belief that there is no defense to the action. Tompkins Hauling Corp. v. 

Roberts, 249 N. Y. S. 22. If D moves, independently, or, by way of cross-motion, his affidavit should set out evidentiary facts, including copies of all documents, showing that D's denials and defenses are sufficient to defeat P, together with a statement that in deponent's belief P's action has no merit. It is interesting to note that under the amended R.C.P. 113, the court may grant summary judgment in favor of P, where D has moved for same, even though P has not cross-moved for such relief. Where P moves, D may cross-move, and obtain such relief. If P moves, and D does not crossmove, D's opposing affidavits should show real and sub-

stantial facts augmenting his answer and entitling him to defend on the merits. Smith v. McCullough, 255 N.
