Y. S. 497. These facts may be stated in the affidavit even though they could not be proved at the trial without an amendment of the answer. Curry v. MacKenzie, 239 N. Y. 267. A mere technical defense raised in D's answer is not a bar to summary judgment (H. C. King 

Motor Sales Corp. v. Allen, 204 N. Y. S. 555) but if D has an arguable defense on the record before the court, he cannot be deprived of his day in court. Barrett v. 

Jacobs, 255 N. Y. 520. If D moves, P's affidavit in opposition should show the merits of his cause of action. 

C. Principles Governing Determination: Since the obvious purpose of R.C.P. 113 is to prevent the delay of judgment by setting up defenses without merit (and to prevent the prosecution of groundless claims) the test of its application is whether there is a triable issue established by the pleadings and the affidavits submitted on the motion. If the judge hearing the motion under subd. 3, 4, or 5 finds that there is no triable issue other than the question of the amount of damages, he must order a hearing to assess same. Such hearing must be had before the court and jury where D has not waived his constitutional right to a jury trial, as for example, where P sues to recover the reasonable value of professional services rendered and D duly demands trial by jury. Livingston v. Blumenthal, 289 N. Y. S. 5. The court must grant or deny the motion unconditionally. 

Gibson v. Standard Casualty Co., 203 N. Y. S. 53; cf. 

Greenberg v. Rudnick, 258 N. Y. S. 679. Either party may appeal from an adverse ruling. Dwan v. Massarene, 192 N. Y. S. 577.

D. Partial Summary Judgment (Rule 114): Where a mo tion for summary judgment is made under R.C.P. 113 and it is found that D is entitled to defend as to only one cause of action out of several, P may have partial summary judgment on those causes of action as to which D has no defense or which he admits in whole or in part. The rule applies as well where D moves for summary judgment as to part of P's complaint. (1) But in order that P be able to sever an action and enter

MOTIONS 

153 judgment against D for a part of a cause of action admitted in the answer, under R.C.P. 114, such admission must be explicit, positive and definite. Doctors Service Corps. v. 

Russell, 201 N. Y. S. 764 (where P suing for $1,300 for professional services moved to enter partial summary judgment for $400 on D's admission in his answer that P's services "were not worth an amount exceeding $400," and where P's motion was denied). See also Hilbring v. Mooney, 233 

N. Y. S. 303. (2) Nor should P be allowed to enter partial judgment against D for an amount incidentally admitted by the answer to be due upon a certain cause of action, where the action against D is on an altogether different cause of action for an altogether different amount. Lyons v. Sam & Lee Shubert, Inc., 197 N. Y. S. 253. 

8. Motion For Bill of Particulars: The purpose of a bill of particulars is to amplify a pleading and to restrict the proofs thereunder and it is neither given nor required for the purpose of disclosing to an adverse party the case relied on nor the proof to substantiate the same. 

Higenbotom v. Green, 25 Hun. 214. The courts are averse to permitting a "fishing expedition," under the guise of a bill of particulars, for the purpose of unduly disclosing the evidence of the opposite side. Hobbie v. 

Ryan, 223 N. Y. S. 654. Matters of time, place and circumstances, unless they constitute material parts of a cause of action or defense, are strictly within the province of a bill of particulars and must be obtained by that method and not by a motion to make more definite and certain. Tilton v. Beecher, 59 N. Y. 176. Although an order granting or denying a motion for a bill of particulars is a discretionary order, it is reviewable on appeal if there has been an abuse of discretion. Witkowski v. 

Paramore, 93 N. Y. 467.

A. What "Particulars" May be Granted; Instances: 

(1) In an action for damages arising to the husband from criminal conversation with his wife, a bill of particulars should be granted to D as to the times and places when the alleged acts of sexual intercourse took place. 

Tilton v. 

Beecher, supra. (2) Particulars will be ordered in negligence cases of the general respects in which P claims D was negligent (unless the rule of res ipsa loquitur applies to the situation). Dwyer v. 

Slattery, 103 N. Y. S. 433; R.C.P. 116.

(3) Extent of injuries may also be found out by a bill of particulars; but, where there is no allegation of permanent injury and P claims only general damages, the bill should not be allowed as to items of pecuniary loss. Renz v. Lugt, 132 

N. Y. S. 522. R.C.P. 116. (4) P's age is properly ascertainable by a bill of particulars in a negligence case as bearing upon the extent of the injuries received. Steinleger v. Frankel, 192 N. Y. S. 74. (5) But in an action for personal injuries or for property damages, while D is entitled to a bill as to P's medical expenses or repair expenses, he is not entitled to the names and addresses of those (doctors, druggists, nurses, mechanics, etc.) rendering the bills. Hobbie v. Ryan, supra; R.C.P. 116. (6) Bills of particulars may be required in reference to affirmative defenses. Thus, if D has pleaded affirmatively defensive matter which P would have to negative as part of his own case, D will be required to serve upon P a bill of particulars as to his special defenses. Goldberg v. National Surety Co., 174 N. Y. S. 562. (7) In the First Department, a defendant ordinarily is not required to give particulars as to a defense of payment, unless for very special reasons. Eben v. Equitable Life Assur. 

Soc. of U. S., 164 N. Y. S. 284. (8) Where P alleges a breach of contract of employment, D may properly require, in his demand for bill of particulars, a statement of the exact and complete terms and conditions of the contract and whether it was oral or in writing, since such a requirement is directed only at ultimate facts and not evidence. Gaudry v. Todd Shipyards Corp., 197 N. Y. S. 742. (9) But in Wolf v. Hubert, 192 N. Y. S. 801, an objection was sustained to the following requirement in a demand for a bill of particulars: "If oral, state the substance of what was said by each party herein on each of the dates mentioned, etc." It was there held that the requirement obviously called for the evidence of the opposing side. 

B. When Motion For Bill should be Made: The motion may be made at any time before trial if the moving party is not guilty of laches which may prejudice the rights of his opponent. Ordinarily, the motion is premature if made before joinder of issue, but where the bill of particulars is necessary to enable the defendant to plead properly, the court will direct the plaintiff to serve a bill of particulars before the answer is served. Bracken v. 

Toland, 137 N. Y. S. 1043.

MOTIONS 

155 

C. Effect of New R.C.P. 115, 116, 117: 

Effective September 1, 1936, C.P.A. 247 was repealed and a new C.P.A. 247 substituted which reads: "the justices of the Appellate Division of the Supreme Court shall have the power to adopt, amend and rescind rules regulating the practice pertaining to bills of particulars and copies of the items of an account." Pursuant to such legislative authority, the old R.C.P. 115 was rescinded in 1936 and a new R.C.P. 115 substituted which latter (as further amended in 1937) reads as follows: "(a) Any party may require any other party to give a bill of particulars of his claim, or a copy of the items of the account alleged in a pleading, by serving a written notice stating the items concerning which such particulars are desired. If the party upon whom such notice is served is unwilling to give such particulars, in whole or in part, he may move to vacate or modify such notice by serving his motion papers within five days after receipt thereof. Such motion may be brought on by notice of motion and accompanying affidavits, or, in a proper case, by notice of motion alone, in which event, however, the notice must specify clearly the objections and the grounds therefor. If no such motion is made, the bill of particulars shall be served within ten days after the demand therefor, unless the court or judge shall otherwise direct. (b) Instead of proceeding by demand, the party may move for a bill of particulars, or a copy of the items of account, upon notice of motion in the first instance. (c) In the event that a party fails to furnish a bill of particulars, or copy of the items of an account, pursuant to the demand, or order modifying such demand, or other order for a bill of particulars, the court or a judge authorized to make an order in the action, upon notice to said party may preclude him from giving evidence at the trial of the items of which particulars have not been delivered. (d) Where a bill of particulars, or copy of the items of an account, served pursuant to demand or order, is regarded as defective or insufficient by the party upon.

whom it is served, such party shall make an application, upon notice, for an order of preclusion or directing the service of a further bill. In the absence of special circumstances, motion papers for such relief shall be served within ten days after the receipt of the bill claimed to be insufficient. (e) A preclusion order may provide that the same be effective unless a proper bill is served within a specified time thereafter. (f) Affidavits to be used in support of or in opposition to the motions hereinbefore described may be made by the party or the attorney. (g) Upon any motion, except under subdivision (b) hereof, costs may be imposed." 

(1) In Personal Injury Actions: A new R.C.P. 116, added as of September 1, 1936 provides that in such actions the following particulars may be required: (a) The date and approximate time of day of the occurrence. (b) Its approximate location. (c) General statement of the acts or omissions constituting the negligence claimed. (d) Where notice of a condition is a prerequisite, whether actual or constructive notice is claimed. (e) If actual notice is claimed, a statement of when and to whom same was given. (f) Statement of the injuries and description of those claimed to be permanent. (g) Length of time confined to bed and to house. (h) Length of time incapacitated from employment. (i) Total amounts claimed as special damages for (1) physicians' services and medical supplies; (2) loss of earnings; (3) hospital expenses; (4) nurses' services. (j) Nothing contained in the foregoing shall be deemed to limit any court or judge in denying in a proper case any one or more of the foregoing particulars, or, in a proper case, in granting other, further or different particulars."

MOTIONS 

157 

(2) Verification of Bill of Particulars: If a pleading be verified, a bill of particulars or a copy of the items of an account also must be verified in the manner provided by R.C.P. 99 and 100. R.C.P. 117 as amended January 15, 1937. This means, as a general rule that a party need not serve a verified bill unless the pleading which the bill particularizes is verified. Carmody, Vol. 3, p. 2441. Some Special

Term decisions have required a verified bill by the plaintiff, however, where the defendant has served a verified answer to an unverified complaint (Olwell v. Central Park Catering Co., N. Y. L. J. 1/4/37, Sup. Ct. N. Y. Co. p. 31 col. 4.) This is on the theory that a bill of particulars is a pleading and under C.P.A. 248 "where a pleading is verified each subsequent pleading must also be verified." These holdings are questionable.

D. Improper or Inadequate Bill: If an improper or in adequate bill is furnished, a motion on notice for a further bill or for a preclusion order must be made within ten days after receipt of the bill claimed to be insufficient. 

R.C.P. 115. (a) Under the old law where a party was unable to give the particulars ordered and stated under oath the facts indicating such inability, the bill was proper. Hartog v. Stein, 193 

N. Y. S. 411. For a time, the new law (R.C.P. 117) expressly provided for such sworn statement of lack of knowledge, but this provision was shortly thereafter deleted in January, 1937. Does this mean that if a party is unable to give the particulars demanded because of lack of knowledge thereof, the burden is on him, in the first instance, to move to vacate the demand on that ground? (b) In any event, evidence in variance or in addition to the facts and circumstances stated in a bill of particulars may be validly produced on the trial of the action, unless an order to preclude such evidence has been made. Rees v. Post, 170 N. Y. S. 610.

E. Renewal Or Rehearing Of Motion: Once a valid order has been entered on a motion by a court having jurisdiction to do so, a reconsideration thereof may be had only by a direct appeal and a second motion for the same relief may not be had. In Steuben County Bank v. Alberger, 83 N. Y. 274, it was held that a second motion to vacate

an attachment so far as it affected real estate, on the ground that it was a cloud upon the alleged title of the moving party, was not precluded by the fact that the moving party made a prior motion to vacate the attachment upon the ground that it was an obstruction to the enforcement of a judgment and was defeated thereon. 

Where a motion has been once denied, it cannot be renewed on new papers (so as to get relief by reason of additional facts which existed at the time of the original motion), without leave of the court, unless made on new facts arising since the denial of the motion. De Lacy v. 

Kelly, 131 N. Y. S. 702. (Where a motion is denied because of some deficiency of proofs or irregularity in practice, it is usual to provide in the order denying the motion that the denial is without prejudice to a renewal of the motion upon new papers.) If a motion has been once determined, a motion for its re-argument may be granted, in which case the motion must be based on the original papers and must be heard by the same court that decided the original motion. Thus, an application on new papers for a re-determination of a previous motion is not a motion for the re-argument of the previous motion but for leave to renew the original motion. 

Seletsky v. Third Ave. RR. Co., 60 N. Y. S. 405. A ruling denying a motion for leave to renew, as distinguished from one denying a re-argument, is appealable. Tucker v. Dudley, 93 N. Y. S. 335.

CHAPTER IX. 

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 1. Service of Interlocutory Papers. 

A. Generally: Any paper or process beginning a special proceeding, except a proceeding to punish for contempt of court, must be served in the same way as a summons. 

All subsequent papers of an interlocutory nature are served on the attorneys for the respective parties. However, even where the parties have appeared by attorneys, where it is desired to subject either party to a personal proceeding requiring the party's personal attendance, or where it is desired to bring a party into contempt, the party must be personally served. C.P.A. 163; R.C.P. 20, 21. Johnson v. Ackerman, 181 N. Y. S. 772. 

(1) Service on Attorney of Orders to Show Cause to Punish for 

Contempt: An order to show cause to punish for contempt is not a paper "bringing a party into contempt", but is an order bringing him into court to be punished for a contempt already committed. The original order for the non-compliance of which the individual is sought to be punished is the paper "bringing the party into contempt" and which is required to be personally served. As far as the order to show cause to punish for contempt is concerned, it may be served upon the attorney for the delinquent party even though the action has already been terminated by final judgment, as long as the attorney continues to represent the party. But it is to be noted that after the entry of final judgment there is no presumption that such representation continues. Rosenthal v. Rosenthal, 201 A. D. 27, and cases cited therein.

B. Service of Notice or Other Paper upon Party or Attor ney (C.P.A. 163): Where a party has appeared, a notice or other paper required to be served in an action must be served upon his attorney. If a defendant has not appeared, service of a notice or other interlocutory paper in the ordinary proceedings in an action need not be made upon him, unless he is actually confined in jail for want of bail. C.P.A. 163. 

(1) How Service Made on Attorney (R.C.P. 20): The methods of service of interlocutory papers on an attorney are as follows: (a) By delivering a copy to him in person, (b) By mail, by depositing a copy, properly enclosed

in a post-paid wrapper, in a post office, or in any post office box regularly maintained by the government of the U. S. in the city, village or town of the party or the attorney serving it, directed to the attorney upon whom service is to be made at the address designated by him upon the last paper previously served by him in the action or proceeding (subd. 1), (c) By leaving a copy at his office with his partner or clerk or a person having charge of the office (subd. 2), (d) By leaving a copy in a conspicuous place in his office if it be found open and no person in charge, between 6 A.M. and 9 P.M. (subd. 2), (e) If the office is not open, by enclosing a copy in a sealed wrapper addressed to the attorney and depositing it in his office letter-box or letterdrop, accessible from without his office, or by leaving it at his residence within the state with a person of suitable age and discretion (subd. 3), (f) By leaving a copy with the clerk of the court where the action or proceeding is pending, if service cannot be made under the three previous subdivisions (subd. 4), (g) If the attorney is a non-resident of the state, but a practicing attorney thereof, and his office be not open and there is no letter-box or letterdrop, by mailing a copy of the paper to him at his office (subd. 4). A service so made is equivalent to personal service on the attorney. 

(2) How Service Made on Party (R.C.P. 20): Where a party appears in person, service of interlocutory papers may be made as follows: (a) By delivering a copy to him in person, (b) By mail, as on an attorney (subd. 1), (c) By delivering a copy at his residence within the state to a person of suitable age and discretion between 6 A.M. and 9 P.M. (subd. 5), (d) If a non-resident, or where his residence cannot be ascertained with reasonable diligence and where he has designated no address within the

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

161 state on preceding papers, the paper may be served by delivering a copy to the clerk of the court (subd. 5). (3) Irregularities in Service: These are waived if the paper is received and not promptly returned (within twenty-four hours by custom), with a notice of irregularity. (a) Thus, where the addressee pays the amount due for short postage, he waives the defect. Kuh v. Goldman, 104 N. Y. S. 285. (b) But where the addressee refuses to receipt for a registered letter containing a copy of a paper so served by mail, and the receipt card is returned to the sender bearing the word "Refused" endorsed thereon by the postman, there is no valid service. Saffold v. Fellows, 220 

N. Y. S. 200. (c) Service by mail may be made by depositing in the mail, properly wrapped, the paper to be served, at any hour of the last day on which the service may be made, and the paper is deemed served when so deposited although the paper is lost or delayed in the mail. Auto Lighter 

Co. v. Wickes Co., 99 N. Y. S. 611. 

(4) Additional Time where Service Is by Mail: Where service is made by mail and the time within which a party may take action is limited to a certain period after such service, three days additional must be added to the time specified, except in the case of a note of issue, which by express provision may be served by mail not less than fourteen days before the day of trial, including the day of service. C.P.A. 164. (a) Under C.P.A. 164, D has twenty-three days to answer where the complaint was served upon him by mail, and the same applies where he moves under R.C.P. 107 to dismiss the complaint. Thurman v. Gordon Co., 213 

N. Y. S. 249. See also N. Y. Cent. R. R. Co. v. Gugino, 221 N. Y. S. 608. (b) Eight or eleven days notice of motion is necessary if the moving papers are served by mail, instead of the usual five or eight. (See Motions, Chap. VIII.) (5) Proof of Service: Where an interlocutory paper has been served in compliance with the provisions of 

R.C.P. 20, and C.P.A. 163-4, an affidavit to that effect is made by the server, giving all the facts and

circumstances tending to show that a proper service was made under the method followed. However, no affidavit is necessary where the attorney (or party), endorses upon the original an admission of service of the copy. This is customarily done, and legal "backs" invariably contain a printed form for such admissions. 

2. Place of Trial and Change of Venue: The plaintiff's choice of the tribunal for the trial must be governed by the type of action involved.

A. Local Actions: These are: 

(1) Real property actions such as ejectment, partition, actions for dower, foreclosure, waste, nuisance, etc. 

C.P.A. 183. (a) An action to rescind an executed contract for the sale of land and to recover the consideration paid thereunder is such an action affecting the right, title or interest in real property, which, under C.P.A. 183, must be brought in the county where the real property is situated. Birmingham v. Squires, 123 N. Y. S. 906. (2) Actions under C.P.A. 184 which are specifically made triable in the county where the cause of action arose. These actions include: (a) Actions to recover a statutory penalty (with some minor exceptions), (b) Actions against a public official to recover damages for misfeasance or nonfeasance, (c) Actions to recover a chattel distrained or damages for the distraining. (3) Actions against a city or any of its officers, boards or departments are triable in the county in which the city is situated, except as otherwise provided with respect to cities of the first and second class. 

C.P.A. 182a.

B. Transitory Actions: These include the great mass of litigation and are subject only in the choice of venue of trial to the limitation that the county selected must be the county of residence of one of the parties at the time of the commencement of the action. C.P.A. 182. (1) An executor or administrator is deemed a resident of the county of his appointment as well as of his actual residence. 

C.P.A. 182.

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

163 (2) If no party resides in the state, P may designate any county. 

A party having, or maintaining, a residence in more than one county is deemed a resident of either county. C.P.A. 182. (a) Where P is a foreign corporation, the action must be brought in a county in which one of the defendants resides, unless the action is one specified in C.P.A. 183-4. 

Mills & Gibbs v. Starin, 104 N. Y. S. 230. A domestic corporation (not a railroad corporation), is deemed to be a resident of the county named in its certificate of incorporation. Ajax Rubber Co. v. J. P. Devine Co., 214 N. Y. S. 311. (3) A railroad corporation is deemed to have a residence in every county through which its roadbed extends. Poland v. United Traction Co., 85 N. Y. S. 7; Gaboury v. Vt. Central 

Ry. Co., 231 N. Y. S. 630.

C. Change of Venue: 

(1) How Affected (C.P.A. 186): Ordinarily, if plaintiff does not name the proper county for trial and if defendant does not object, the defect will be waived and the action will be tried in the county named. 

Hull v. Trainor, 252 N. Y. S. 845. But if defendant has an objection, he must make it promptly. Denbitz v. Orange County Traction Co., 132 N. Y. S. 593.

He makes such objection by serving, either before or with his answer, a written demand upon the plaintiff's attorney to correct the county of trial to the proper one, naming it. If plaintiff's attorney does not consent to this demand within five days after service thereof, defendant may move within ten days thereafter to compel such change. C.P.A. 

186; R.C.P. 146. Peerless Motor Co. v. Hambleton, 219 N. Y. S. 641. (a) However, failure to move to change the place of trial on the ground that the county named is not the proper county until the time to make such motion has expired does not preclude the making of a motion, at any time thereafter, to change the place of trial on the ground of convenience of witnesses and in the interests of justice. C.P.A. 187; Marcus v. Greenbaum, 230 N. Y. S. 478. 

(2) Grounds for Change of Venue (C.P.A. 187): The court may order a change, in three classes of cases: (a) Where an Improper County is Designated, (b) Where There is Reason to Believe That an Im-

partial Trial Cannot be Had in the County Named: Such cases are few, and a motion on this ground will not be granted unless facts are shown indicating that local prejudice or excitement concerning the outcome of the action will render a fair trial in the designated county practically impossible. Jacob v. Town of Oyster 

Bay, 104 N. Y. S. 275 (where the whole town was arrayed against one man in a contention whether the town, or the individual, owned a valuable piece of land). 

(c) For Convenience of Witnesses: The motion to change the designated venue to suit the convenience of witnesses may be made by either party, and like a similar motion on the ground of the impossibility of an impartial trial (supra), is made after the answer and before trial, no prior demand being necessary. Page Belting 

Co. v. Joseph, 226 N. Y. S. 723. The moving party submits affidavits containing the names and residences of each of his witnesses, and stating the materiality and necessity of the witnesses' testimony, according to the advice of counsel. This last, if omitted, is a fatal defect. 

Rieger v. Pulaski Glove Co., 99 N. Y. S. 558. (1′) On a motion to change the venue designated, on the ground of inconvenience of witnesses, the court will balance the inconvenience to the witnesses of both parties and will decide in favor of the one whose witnesses would be most inconvenienced by a change. Viertels v. N. Y. O. & 

W. Ry. Co., 169 N. Y. S. 497; Rogers v. Blum, 154 N. Y. S. 336. (2') The convenience of resident witnesses alone is considered; that of non-residents, or of the parties to the action and their employees, is not considered. 

(3) Effect of Order Changing Venue: When an order is entered granting a motion for change of venue, all subsequent proceedings take place in the new county designated and the clerk of the court in the county where the action was originally designated for trial must transfer all the papers to the new county.

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

165 

C.P.A. 188-9; R.C.P. 15. But for the purpose of an appeal from the order granting the change, the place of trial is deemed unchanged. R.C.P. 147. 

3. Tender and Offer: 

A. Tender: 

(1) Generally: At common law, in actions upon a liquidated money demand, if defendant tendered payment to plaintiff before action was begun, it had the effect of stopping interest on plaintiff's claim from the time the tender was made and entitling defendant to costs, if the plaintiff recovered on the trial no greater amount than that tendered. The tender passed absolute title to plaintiff to the money deposited. Taylor v. Brooklyn Elevated RR., 119 N. Y. 

561. Under the Civil Practice Act, the law is not changed as to tender before action (Burrows v. 

Maritime Warehouse Co., 175 N. Y. S. 698), but defendant's remedy is extended as to tender made after action brought. It is only with this latter remedy that the Civil Practice Act concerns itself. C.P.A. 171-174. (2) What is Tender: Tender is the amount plus interest which defendant thinks is due to plaintiff plus accrued costs up to the time the tender is made. 

C.P.A. 171. 

(3) Where Permissible (C.P.A. 171): Under this sec tion, it may be made after any action brought to recover a sum of money for liquidated damages or damages for negligence for casual or involuntary injuries to person or property. C.P.A. 171. (See

R.P.L. 333 as to tender of payment of mortgage on real property; also, C.P.A. 1077, subd. 1 as to dismissal of mortgage foreclosure action upon payment by defendant of all taxable costs and disbursements and remedying of any default other than non-payment of principal.) (4) How Tender Must be Made: A tender after action begun has no effect unless accepted or paid into court (Osterman v. Goldstein, 66 N. Y. S. 506), and written notice of such payment served on plaintiff's attorney before trial and within ten days after the

tender (C.P.A. 172). In making such payment, defendant must file a memorandum designating the portion thereof deposited on account of costs. C.P.A. 

172. But whether made before or after action brought, in order to he effectual, the tender must comply with the following requirements: (a) The tender must be absolutely unconditional and "without strings," (1) Thus a "tender" of payment of a mortgage debt on condition that the lien of the mortgage be extinguished is bad, since there is a condition annexed to the tender. Noyse v. Wyckoff, 114 N. Y. 204. (2') But a tender of the amount due on a mortgage on land is proper even though a demand is made by the mortgagor for a satisfaction piece. Halpin v. Phenix Ins. Co., 118 N. Y. 165. (3') A tender made for the purpose of discharging a lien, and not to extinguish the debt, may be conditional and need not be kept good, (as in the case of a tender made on condition that diamonds pledged as security for the loan be returned). Cass v. Higenbottom, 100 N. Y. S. 248. (b) It must be made by the debtor or his agent, (c) It must be in legal tender, (1') The tender of a check is good unless it is objected to upon that ground. Duffy v. O'Donovan, 46 N. Y. 223. (d) It must tender costs. (1′) In Stadler & Stadler v. Long, 241 N. Y. S. 637, where no tender of costs was made, the tender was held to be a common law tender, passing absolute title to P, and P was permitted to withdraw the money and continue the action. (5) Legal Effect of a Tender: Where defendant makes a tender before action begun, the validity of the plaintiff's claim up to the sum tendered is deemed to be admitted by the defendant. But the defendant may defend against any claim beyond the sum tendered upon any ground consistent with an admission of the original cause of action. Wilson v. 

Doran, 110 N. Y. 101. Under C.P.A. 173, plaintiff accepts the tender by withdrawing the same at any time before trial and within ten days after the notice

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

167 of tender. If plaintiff accepts the tender made after action brought, it is a satisfaction of the cause of action and defendant may have the action dismissed without costs. C.P.A. 173.

If the money so paid into court is not accepted: (a) If defendant recovers judgment, the money is paid to him and he gets his costs in the action besides. C.P.A. 173, subd. 1. (b) If plaintiff recovers judgment, the money is applied upon his judgment and the surplus, if any, is paid to defendant. C.P.A. 173, subd. 2. (c) If plaintiff's judgment, exclusive of costs, is not in excess of the tender, exclusive of the portion thereof deposited on account of costs, plaintiff cannot recover costs or interest from the time of the tender but must pay defendant's costs from that time. C.P.A. 173, subd. 3. (d) Plaintiff's right to costs is determined by the excess of his judgment, exclusive of costs, over the amount of the tender exclusive of the amount thereof deposited on account of costs. 

C.P.A. 173, subd. 4. (6) Tender on Counterclaim (C.P.A. 174): A tender on a counterclaim may be made by plaintiff in the same cases and manner and with the same effect as in case of a tender by defendant after suit brought. 

(7) Tender under Gen. Bus. L. Sec. 376: This is made to avoid forfeiture for usury and is not to be confused with, and has none of the effects of, a tender under the Civil Practice Act. Jefferson T. & M. 

Corp. v. Dempsey, 266 N. Y. 190. 

B. Offers: (1) Offer to Compromise (C.P.A. 177): In any action, either legal or equitable (except matrimonial actions), the defendant may serve upon the plaintiff, before trial, a notice containing an offer in plain, concise language to permit plaintiff to take judgment for a designated sum, or for certain property, or specific relief, with costs. (a) Form of Offer: The notice must be signed by

the defendant personally, or by his attorney, but, if by the latter, he must attach an affidavit to the effect that he is duly authorized to make the offer on behalf of his client. C.P.A. 179. (1') Failure to annex such an affidavit renders the notice fatally defective beyond an amendment nunc pro tunc. Riggs v. Waydell, 17 Hun 515. (b) When to be Made: The offer, in order to be valid, must be served not less than ten days. before trial, since the opposite side has ten days, too, in which to accept the offer. Herman v. 

Lyons, 10 Hun 111. (c) Effect of Acceptance: If the offer is accepted, (by written notice of acceptance within ten days) the action is ended and the plaintiff may have the clerk enter judgment according to the terms of the accepted offer. Such a judgment concludes the plaintiff from bringing a new action for any part of his claim embraced in his complaint. Robinson v. Marks, 19 Hun 325, (holding that where, by the acceptance of an offer of compromise, a note is extinguished, the liability of an endorser as well as that of the maker is also extinguished). (1') So also, where a complaint sets forth two causes of action one of which defendant admits in his answer, an offer of judgment made generally, if accepted, will settle both causes of action and will preclude plaintiff from severing the action and prosecuting the cause of action not admitted. 

Walsh v. Empire Brick & Supply Co., 85 N. Y. S. 528. (2′) A defendant cannot move to compel a plaintiff to accept defendant's offer of judgment where plaintiff demands both legal, (money damages), and equitable (easement) relief, and the offer extends only to the equitable relief. Margulies v. Solomon & Berck Co., 229 N. Y. S. 157. (d) Effect of Refusal: If the offer is not accepted, it cannot be given in evidence on the trial, and if the plaintiff fails to recover a more favorable judgment than that offered, he becomes liable to the defendant for costs accruing subsequent

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

169 to the offer and his recovery will be lessened by the amount of such costs. C.P.A. 177; Smith v. N. Y. RR. Co., 196 N. Y. S. 521. (1') To determine whether the judgment is "more favorable," the amount of the judgment obtained without costs, should be compared with the amount offered, without costs. Singleton v. Home Ins. 

Co., 121 N. Y. 644. 

(2) Offer to Liquidate Damages Conditionally (C.P.A. 

175-6): Unlike the offer to compromise, just considered, an offer to liquidate damages conditionally is confined to actions for damages for breach of contract. 

(a) How Made; When Made: The defendant may serve with his answer a written offer that if he fails in his defense the plaintiff may assess his damages at a specified sum. If the plaintiff, by a notice served with or before the note of issue, accepts the offer, the question of the amount of damages is not disputed, and if the plaintiff is entitled to recover anything, the sum awarded will be the amount specified in the offer. C.P.A. 175. (b) Effect of Refusal: If the plaintiff fails to accept the offer in the manner prescribed, it has no effect, unless on the trial he does not succeed in recovering a greater sum than that offered, in which case the defendant is entitled to recover from the plaintiff the amount of his expenses incurred in preparation for trial of the question of damages; such amount to be ascertained by the judge or referee before whom the case was tried. C.P.A. 176. 

4. Abatement and Continuance of Actions: 

A. Generally: An action or special proceeding does not abate, by any event, if the cause of action survives or continues. C.P.A. 82. Hence, in the case of a sole plaintiff and sole defendant, no matter what happens to either party, disability, insanity, bankruptcy or death, the action does not die unless the cause of action itself dies. 

B. Effect of C.P.A. 84: In the case discussed above, if the

cause of action is one which survives or continues, the court, must, upon motion, allow or compel the action to be continued by or against the representative or successor in interest. (1) However, laches in application may be ground for a denial of a motion to continue an action after death of a party, although C.P.A. 84 states that the court must allow or compel the action to be continued. Pringle v. L. I. RR. Co., 157 

N. Y. 100.

C. What Causes of Action Survive: Previous to the 1935 

amendments, generally, only causes of action based upon property rights, whether in contract or tort, survived as against the dead defendant's representatives or in favor of the dead plaintiff's representatives; causes of action for damages for purely personal wrongs did not survive the death either of the party wronged or of the tortfeasor. Since September 1, 1935, D.E.L. Secs. 118, 119, applicable to causes of action arising after September 1, 1935 (In re Miller's Estate, 158 Misc. 775) now provide that no cause of action for injury to person or property shall abate because of the death of either party; the action can be begun or continued against the executor or administrator; or if the injured party dies, can be begun or continued by his executor or administrator. 

Causes of action for wrongfully causing death do not abate because of the wrongdoer's death. D.E.L. 120 provides that where the injury causes death, the damages shall be limited to those accruing before death and shall ⚫ form part of the deceased's estate and that an action to recover such damages shall be separate from an action by the next of kin under D.E.L. 130, for the wrongful death, although both may be prosecuted to judgment in a single action, with separate verdicts for each. See 

Kwiatkoski v. Lowry Inc., 276 N. Y. 126. Where an action is begun to recover damages for personal injuries, and plaintiff dies as a result of the injuries before judgment, his executor or administrator may enlarge the complaint to include a cause of action under D.E.L. 130.

The new laws provide, however, that where the action is one for a personal injury, although it survives, no punitive damages, may be awarded nor penalties ad-

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

171 judged. It is thus necessary to retain the definition of "personal injury" actions not only for causes of actions which arose before September 1, 1935, but for those arising thereafter, in order to determine whether punitive damages can be awarded. 

(1) Purely Personal Wrongs: Under Gen. Const. Law, 

Sec. 37-a, a personal injury includes libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assualt, battery, false imprisonment or "other actionable injury" to the person of the plaintiff or of another. (a) A cause of action to recover for loss of services of P's wife injured through D's negligence did not survive D's death under the old law, since it comes under "other actionable injury to the person of another." Gorlitzer v. Wolfberg, 208 N. Y. 475. (b) An action to recover damages for breach of promise to marry does not survive against D's executors. Wade v. 

Kalbfleisch, 58 N. Y. 282. (c) Also, actions to recover penalties under statute, including those authorized to be brought against directors and trustees of corporations for omissions of duty, are actions which die with the person. Bank v. Griswald, 104 

N. Y. 613. (1') But where one is a director of a corporation when he starts an action against another director for misconduct in office and is removed as director after the commencement of his action, the action does not abate by reason of such removal. 

Manix v. 

Fantl, 205 N. Y. S. 174. (d) Where an action is commenced by a widow, as administratrix, for the wrongful death of her husband and she dies pending the action, the action survives but only to the extent of the pecuniary damages suffered by her to the time of her death. Sider v. Gen. Elec. Co., 238 N. Y. 64. (e) An action for accrued alimony survives the death of the wife in favor of her personal representative and as against the personal representatives of the deceased husband. Van Ness v. Ransom, 215 N. Y. 557. 

(2) Continuance of Actions by or Against Several Par ties: (a) Joint Plaintiffs: Where the right survives, and one of the plaintiffs dies, the case proceeds without the necessity of bringing in the dead plain-

tiff's representatives, but the surviving plaintiffs are individually liable to account to the estate of the dead plaintiff for its share of any recovery (as in the case of co-partners). C.P.A. 

85, 86. In its discretion, the court may, however, require the successor in interest to be brought in as a party. 

(b) Joint Defendants: If a contractual liability is joint, the cause of action survives as against the representative of a deceased defendant; the court may revive the action and may allow it to continue as against the surviving and added defendants together. But it is necessary to prove inability to collect from the surviving joint defendants in order to permit the joinder of the deceased's representatives. Central Hanover Bank v. Vernon Estates, 265 N. Y. S. 527. (c) Joint and Several Defendants: The court may revive the action and allow it to continue as against the surviving and added defendants together, on their joint liability; or, may sever the action so that one action may proceed against the surviving defendant and one against the personal representative of the deceased defendant, on their several liability. On the death of one of several defendant tortfeasors, (who are jointly and severally liable), the action surviving, the estate is liable only on the several liability, and therefore, the action, if revived against the representative of the decedent, must be severed so as to continue separately against the surviving tortfeasors and the representative of the deceased defendant. Union Bank v. Mott, 27 N. Y. 633. 

(d) Defendants Liable Only Severally: The action must be severed. (3) Continuance of Actions upon Transfer of Interest, or Devolution of Liability (C.P.A. 83): Events other than death may occasion a transfer of interest or devolution of liability during the action. C.P.A.

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

173 83 provides that in case of a transfer of interest, the action may be continued by or against the original party, unless the court directs the person to whom the interest has been transferred or upon whom the liability has devolved, to be substituted in the action or joined with the original party, as the case requires. (a) Thus, where P was sold certain property by D, with an express easement and D thereafter commenced to obstruct said easement, P could continue his original action for injunctive relief even though during the pendency of the action he sold the property to X. 

Phillips v. West Rockaway Land Co., 196 N. Y. S. 

723; Hardee v. Karman, 266 N. Y. S. 601. (4) Effect of Death Occurring After Verdict, Report or 

Decision: After verdict, report or decision in an action to recover damages upon a cause of action not surviving, there is no abatement because of the death of either party, and the subsequent proceedings are the same as in a case where the cause of action survives. If there is a reversal, on appeal, there is no abatement because of the death of either party, but no punitive damages or penalties may be awarded on any subsequent, new trial. C.P.A. 89, as amended, September 1, 1935. Compare with C.P.A. 23. 

5. Statutory Interpleader: If A owes a debt or is in possession of property, which he desires to pay or deliver to the rightful owner, but both B and C assert claims to this same debt or property, A can protect himself from the danger of paying to the wrong claimant, by using the statutory remedy of interpleader. Under C.P.A. 284-287, interpleader may be had by action or by order, but each applies to a different situation.

A. Action of Interpleader: In the case above cited, no action was commenced or pending against A by B or C. 

Hence, A, in order to avail himself of the remedy of interpleader, would have to bring an action of interpleader making B and C parties defendant. 

(1) Who May Bring Action; Requisites (C.P.A. 285): 

The action may be brought by a debtor who owes money on a contract, where the amount due is not

in dispute (Empire Eng. Corp. v. Mack, 217 N. Y. 

85) and where fifty dollars or more, is claimed by adverse claimants between whom the plaintiff stands indifferent and is uncertain to whom he should pay the money. Crane v. McDonald, 118 N. Y. 648. (a) Since the action is essentially one in personam, service by publication on a non-resident under C.P.A. 232 on the ground that the action relates to specific personal property within the state, is invalid. Hanna v. Stedman, 230 N. Y. 326. (Cf. Morgan v. Ins. Co., 189 N. Y. 

447; Crozier v. U. S. Steel Corp., 259 N. Y. S. 241.) (2) Requisites: The adverse claims must be to the same debt. Clark v. Childs, 256 N. Y. S. 69. The amount claimed by B and C cannot be disputed by A. C.P.A. 

286; Empire Eng. Corp. v. Mack, supra. A should also allege that there is no collusion between the parties, and sufficient facts to indicate that there is a reasonable basis for the adverse claims (Sulzberger v. Sekler, 138 N. Y. S. 691), so as to justify a reasonable doubt in his mind as to which claim is valid. 

White v. Bank of Angola, 233 N. Y. S. 508. 

(3) Procedure in Action (C.P.A. 286): After the sum mons and complaint have been served plaintiff moves upon at least five days notice and not more than fifteen days, for an order permitting him to pay the amount of the debt into court. Upon such payment, he is discharged from any further liability to the defendants, and the latter are enjoined from further proceedings against the plaintiff with reference to the subject matter of the action of interpleader. Each defendant should serve a copy of his answer on the other defendant. New Netherland 

Bank v. Boucheron Co., 203 N. Y. S. 766.

B. Order of Interpleader in Pending Action (C.P.A. 287): 

If in the case considered before, A is already being sued by B, and C asserts his claim against A, the latter may apply for an order of interpleader in the pending action. 

(1) Requisites (C.P.A. 287): The remedy is restricted to actions to recover money upon a contract, or ejectment or to recover a chattel. Defendant makes his application for an order of interpleader either before

INCIDENTAL PRACTICE 

175 or after answer, and upon affidavit that a person not a party to the action is making a demand against him for the same debt or property. The affidavit should further allege the same requisites pointed out before in the case of an action of interpleader. 

(2) Procedure and Effect of Order: The application for the order is made on notice to the plaintiff and to the outside adverse claimant. The motion papers must be served personally within the state on the adverse claimant sought to be interpleaded. Devon v. Neeles, 189 N. Y. S. 492. If defendant's application is granted and he does not dispute the amount due, the adverse claimant is brought in as a substituted party defendant. The original defendant is discharged from further liability upon bringing the money or property disputed into court. (a) However, if defendant disputes any part of the claim asserted against him or if defendant asserts his own interest in the subject matter of the action (as he may under C.P.A. 287), the outside adverse claimant is brought in only as a co-defendant with the original defendant, and is not substituted in the place and stead of the latter. C.P.A. 287. This may have the effect of changing the original action, which may have been one at law, to one in equity in which the entire controversy between all the parties is adjudicated. Clark v. Mosher, 107 N. Y. 118; Zies v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 261 N. Y. S. 709. 

(3) Supplemental Complaint after Order of Interpleader : 

To present the issues for trial by the court after an order of interpleader has been made, the plaintiff should serve (and the court will usually so order) a supplemental complaint upon the added defendant, showing besides the cause of action contained in plaintiff's original complaint, the substitution or addition of the new defendant, and the discharge of the original defendant by the payment into court of the money or property in dispute. To this supplemental complaint, the added defendant answers.

CHAPTER X PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 1. Generally: The Civil Practice Act provides for certain rem edial instrumentalities, of a provisional or temporary nature, to be used during the pendency of an action and before the rights and liabilities of the parties are adjudicated, in order to render the ultimate judgment less likely to be ineffective. 

These remedies are arrest, injunction (by order, as distinguished from action), attachment and receivership. Another similar remedy is replevin. 

2. Arrest (C.P.A. 826, 7): 

A. History: At old common law, a defendant was arrested and held to bail as the first step in the action against him, and if admitted to bail, and a judgment subsequently recovered against him, he was again arrested on an execution if he failed to pay, and imprisoned indefinitely.

This method of indiscriminate imprisonment for debt, whether contracted honestly or fraudently, aroused reformative measures and in the United States, commencing with the Stillwell Act of 1831, legislation has been enacted tending to limit arrest as a remedy to actions sounding in tort or such contract actions in which the liability was fraudulently incurred. C.P.A. 826 can be traced in origin to this old practice of imprisonment for debt in actions at law. C.P.A. 827, however, is a remnant of the old chancery writ of "ne exeat" by which a defendant in equity was forcibly restrained from leaving the jurisdiction and defeating the purpose and efficacy of the chancellor's decree. The writ of "ne exeat" is expressly abolished (Civil Rights Law, sec. 23) but C.P.A. 

827 has, in effect, taken its place. Sturges v. Sturges, 114

Misc. 475. C.P.A. 826 can be invoked only in law actions; thus, an order of arrest under that section in an equity action for an accounting and a reconveyance is improper. 

Fenton v. Duckworth, 115 N. Y. S. 686.

B. Arrest under C.P.A. 826: The actions enumerated under 

C.P.A. 826, in which an order of arrest may be had, are: (1) To recover a fine or penalty: In Staub v. Henry, 44

N. Y. S. 954, by a divided court, it was held that a

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

177 

statute providing that any person purchasing a share in an illegal lottery may recover double the sum paid therefor does not prescribe a penalty and therefore does not authorize an order of arrest. See also 

Hutchinson v. Young, 87 N. Y. S. 678. (2) To recover damages for a personal injury: "Personal injury" includes libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault, battery, false imprisonment, or other actionable injury to the person of the plaintiff or of another. Gen. 

Const. Law, 37-a; Gelles v. Rosenbaum, 252 N. Y. 

S. 827. (a) It has been held that an action for a separation based on the defendant's cruel and inhuman treatment is not within the scope of this subdivision (Blair v. Blair, 145 

N. Y. S. 397, overruling Schwartz v. Schwartz, 73 N.

Y. S. 935) although, in such case, an arrest might be had under C.P.A. 827 upon proof of extrinsic facts. (b) Under this subdivision, the principal may be arrested for the tortious act of his agent in the scope of his employment. Davids v. Brooklyn Heights RR. Co., 93 N. 

Y. S. 285, aff'd 182 N. Y. 526. See also Dracup v. Lutgen, 211 N. Y. S. 305. (3) To recover damages for injury to property: "Injury to property is an actionable act whereby the estate of another is lessened, other than a personal injury, or the breach of a contract." Gen. Const. Law, 25-a. (a) An action for trespass on realty within the state comes under this section (Losaw v. Smith, 69 N. Y. S. 191); also, where the land is in a foreign state. Real Prop. 

Law 536. (b) A wrongful act causing the death of P's intestate comes under this section. People ex rel Harris v. Gill, 83 N. Y. S. 135, aff'd 176 N. Y. 606. (c) Note: Under a strict reading of C.P.A. 826, subd. 2 and 3, all other conditions being satisfied, an order of arrest may be had in ordinary, negligence actions to recover for personal or property injuries. But "may" in the statute gives the court discretion. As a matter of practice, the order of arrest under the two subdivisions named is usually granted only in actions of assault and battery, libel or slander, or in extreme cases of very outrageous battery, or, where it appears that defendant is a non-resident, or, that there is good reason to believe that he is about to, or may, depart from the state.

Gelles v. Rosenbaum, 252 N. Y. S. 827. (4) To recover damages for breach of promise to marry: (a) This by exclusion applies only to a promise made by a male defendant, since, under C.P.A. 829, a woman cannot be arrested unless the action is brought against her to recover for a wilful injury to person, character or property. A breach of promise action does not come under this category. (5) To recover damages for misconduct or neglect in office or in a professional employment: See Van 

Schenck v. Siegel, 9 Daly 313. (6) To recover damages for fraud or deceit: (a) This refers only to tort actions for fraud, and not to actions on contract or in equity based on fraud. Walker v. Sanford, 215 N. Y. S. 202. Under this subdivision, as distinguished from subd. 10 (post), a principal may be arrested for the fraud of his agent in the scope of the latter's employment. Under subd. 6, fraud is the "gist of the action”; under subdivision 10, it is “the gist of the arrest only." Novotny v. Kosloff, 214 N. Y. 12. (7) To recover a chattel where it is alleged in the complaint that the chattel has been "concealed, removed or disposed of so that it cannot be found or taken, with intent to do so or to deprive the plaintiff of the benefit thereof." (8) To recover money received or specific property, or damages for the conversion of property, where it is alleged in the complaint that the money was received or the property fraudulently misapplied by a person in a fiduciary capacity. (a) The relationship between the parties must be more than contractual merely; it must involve personal trust and confidence. Asano Bussan Co. v. Skinner, 202 N. Y. S. 510. (b) A public officer, an attorney, officer, or agent of a corporation or banking association in the course of his employment, a broker, factor, agent or other person acting as a fiduciary, all come within this subdivision. 

Sherman v. Grinnell, 159 N. Y. 50. (c) But in ordinary transactions between dealers or merchants, no such element of trust and good faith exists. 

See Britton v. Ferrin, 171 N. Y. 235. (d) A deposit of money in a bank creates only a relation of debtor and creditor which is not a fiduciary one unless

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

179 the money is received for a specific use. German Bank v. Edwards, 53 N. Y. 541. (9) To recover for the misappropriation of public money or property. (10) In an action on contract (other than to marry) where it is alleged in the complaint that the defendant was guilty of fraud in contracting or has disposed of or is about to dispose of his property with intent to defraud creditors. (a) Under this subdivision, as well as under subd. 7 and 8, P cannot recover unless he proves at the trial the extraneous allegation required to be made in the complaint. But a judgment for D (by reason of such failure of proof) is not a bar to a new action brought by P to recover on a complaint which omits the unproved allegation. (b) Under this subdivision, as distinguished from subd. 6, the fraud upon which the action is founded must be personal to D; hence in an action for goods sold and delivered, an order of arrest under C.P.A. 826 subd. 10 will not be granted against the principal where the agent only was guilty of fraudulently procuring the making of the contract. Hathaway v. Johnson, 55 N. Y. 93. (c) Fraud charged: P must allege and prove an intent on the part of D to defraud. (1') Thus, the mere fact that a vendee had knowledge of his insolvency but omitted to disclose his condition to the vendor at the time of the sale is insufficient proof of intent to defraud. 

Morris v. 

Talcott, 96 N. Y. 100. (Where the fraud is not passive the rule would be otherwise. Hoboken 

Beef Co. v. Loeffel, 4 N. Y. S. 798.) (2′) The fraud in making the contract must be related to the breach thereof. Thus, a defendant may not be arrested in an action for wrongful discharge even though the complaint alleged fraud in making the contract of employment. Novotny v. Kosloff, supra. (3′) Subd. 10 has its most frequent application in cases where credit is obtained by fraudulent representations or false financial statements and suit is brought to recover on the contract. Price v. Levy, 87 N. Y. S. 740.

C. Arrest under C.P.A. 827: A judgment or decree in equity is in personam and usually directs the defendant to do or not to do a certain act; hence, in order for the judg-

ment to be effective, the person against whom it is directed must be within the jurisdiction. C.P.A. 827 provides a remedy to keep the defendant within the jurisdiction by arresting him. 

(1) Where Applicable: An order of arrest under C.P.A. 

827 may be had in equity actions where defendant is a non-resident, or being a resident is about to depart from the state, and where the judgment demanded requires the performance of an act, neglect or refusal to perform which would be punishable by the court as a contempt and which judgment would be rendered ineffectual by reason of such non-residence or departure. (a) Thus, an order of arrest under C.P.A. 827 may never be had in an action to recover a sum of money only. (b) But it may be had in an action for divorce or separation where alimony is sought. Sturges v. Sturges, 114 

Misc. 475; Lichstrahl v. Lichstrahl, 77 N. Y. S. 900. 

D. Restrictions on Issuance of Arrest Orders: 

(1) Effect of Reducing Cause of Action to Judgment: 

If a cause of action on which defendant could have been arrested has been reduced by plaintiff to a domestic judgment, the plaintiff in a subsequent suit upon that judgment cannot have the defendant arrested; but if plaintiff's cause of action was reduced to judgment in the courts of another state, defendant may be arrested in a subsequent action on such judgment, if an order of arrest would have been allowed in the original action. C.P.A. 828, Baxter v. Drake, 85 N. Y. 502; See also Boyle v. Semenoff, 194 N. Y. 

S. 603. 

(2) Waiver by Joinder of Causes of Action: Where plaintiff's complaint joins two causes of action one of which authorizes an order of arrest and the other does not, such joinder operates as a waiver of the right to arrest. Bowen v. True, 53 N. Y. 640. 

(3) Persons Exempt: 

(a) Women: A woman cannot be arrested in actions at law (under C.P.A. 826) except for wilful injury to person, character or property. 

C.P.A. 829; Allen v. Frome, 195 N. Y. 404. 11

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

181 (1') Where a female warehouse-keeper is sued for conversion in asserting a lien for charges upon P's property, the act charged is not a wilful injury to property, since a wrongful intent is not necessarily an element of conversion. Allen V. 

Frome, supra. (2') But where a widow of a mortgagor with knowledge of the existence of the mortgage, and after demand by the mortgagee, openly sells the mortgaged property, her act is within the meaning of the word "wilful." Delbon v. Krautwald, 165 N. 

Y. S. 534. (3′) Similarly, where a female defendant induces her employer's clerk to steal gold certificates from the employer and turn them over to her for her use, an order of arrest will issue. Duncan v. Katen, 64 N. Y. 625. (b) Insane Persons; Infants under 14: An idiot, lunatic, or an infant under fourteen years may be discharged from arrest, in the court's discretion, as a privileged person. C.P.A. 830. 

(c) Persons Sued in Representative Capacity: A 

person sued in a representative capacity, such as an administrator, executor, trustee, assignee, heir, devisee, cannot be arrested except for his personal act. C.P.A. 831. Although once arrested and discharged, a privileged person may be re-arrested after his privilege has ceased. 

C.P.A. 841. 

(d) Other Privileged Persons: On the broad grounds of public policy, certain persons exercising some public function or duty are immune from arrest under civil process. (1′) Such persons include foreign ministers and representatives, members of their families and their servants (Holbrook v. Henderson, 7 N. Y. 576), congressmen (U. S. Const. Art. I, sec. 6), mem bers and officers of the state legislature (Leg. 

Law sec. 2), soldiers and sailors in service (Rev. 

Fed. Stat. sec. 1237), post office officials in New 

York City, postmen, firemen, policemen and militiamen, while on duty in any city of the second class (Sec. Cl. Cities, L., 139), prisoners being conveyed to jail through another county, officers of courts of record during the term of the court, non-

resident witnesses or parties while in court, going to, or, returning from court, and resident witnesses attending under subpoena or order. Civil Rights 

Law, 22, 24, 25. (e) Submitted Controversies: An order of arrest, temporary injunction, or warrant of attachment may not be had on a submitted controversy on an agreed statement of facts. C.P.A. 548.

E. Necessary Papers for Order of Arrest under C.P.A. 826: 

(1) Affidavit: A party who applies for an order of arrest must present the evidentiary facts in affidavits made by a person, or persons, acquainted with the facts, and showing a strict right to the statutory relief asked. Facts should be stated on the knowledge of the affiant (Price v. Levy, supra), but where that is impossible, the facts may be stated on information and belief, provided the sources of the affiant's information are given and the grounds of his belief plus reasonable explanation why the affidavit of the party transmitting the information is not offered. Golub v. Baruchin, 197 N. Y. S. 120.

Hearsay evidence, in such case, is not per se objectionable. Buell v. Van Camp, 119 N. Y. 160. (a) But where there is an utter absence of knowledge on the part of the affiant of the facts necessary to confer jurisdiction in the granting of the order of arrest, it will not be granted. Banque Agricole v. Ungureanu, 65 N. Y. S. 892, where in an action to recover money converted, affiant, in asking for an order of arrest, gave as the sole source of his information as to D's alleged conversion, a cablegram from a foreign consul, advising of D's embezzlement and absconding but not stating that the sender had any knowledge of the facts. (b) A supporting affidavit may be dispensed with where P's chances of getting it are highly improbable. Valentine v. Gonzales, 179 N. Y. S. 711. (c) Additional requirements: The affidavit must show facts and circumstances from which the amount of bail to be required of defendant may be determined (R.C.P. 81), and where the order of arrest is ex parte, the affidavit must comply with R.C.P. 61. (2) Complaint: The usual practice is to include the complaint in the papers to obtain the order of arrest, but under C.P.A. 826, subdivisions 7, 8 and 10, the

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

183 complaint is a necessary paper and must be pro- 'duced. C.P.A. 833. (3) Undertaking: Before the order of arrest will be granted against a defendant, a plaintiff must provide a written undertaking with one or more sureties to the effect that if the defendant recovers judgment, or, if it is finally decided that the plaintiff was not entitled to the order of arrest, the plaintiff will pay all costs and damages which the defendant may sustain by reason of the arrest, not exceeding the sum specified in the undertaking. (a) Amount: The amount of the undertaking must be at least one tenth of the bail required and in no case less than $250. C.P.A. 835. (b) Form: The form of the undertaking is prescribed by 

R.C.P. 25 and in all cases where an authorized fidelity or surety company executes an undertaking, such execution is equivalent to the execution of the undertaking by two individual sureties. C.P.A. 156. 

(4) Order: The order of arrest under C.P.A. 826 may be granted either by the court in which the action is brought, or by a judge thereof, or by any county judge. C.P.A. 817. It must be subscribed by plaintiff's attorney and may be directed either to the sheriff of a particular county in which the defendant resides, or to the sheriff of any county in which the defendant may be found. The order must briefly state the grounds on which it is made (C.P.A. 821), which grounds should not be stated in the alternative. Cronin v. Crooks, 143 N. Y. 352. (a) But an order of arrest will not be vacated for such an alternative statement where it recites the grounds upon which it was granted, as the embezzlement or fraudulent misapplication by D of P's funds, since the terms really mean the same thing. Quail v. Nelson, 56 N. Y.

S. 865. (b) Direction to Sheriff: The order must require the sheriff to forthwith arrest the defendant, if found within his county, to hold him to bail (in a sum specified in the order), and to return the order with his proceedings thereunder as prescribed by law. R.C.P. 82.

F. Necessary Papers for Order of Arrest under C.P.A. 827. 

(1) Affidavit:

In the case of an application for an order

of arrest under C.P.A. 827, the affidavit must contain not only facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action in equity, as specified under C.P.A. 827, but it must also contain a statement of the extrinsic facts required by that section. (a) Where an order of arrest is made under C.P.A. 827 fixing bail at a specified sum, it will be vacated where the affidavits upon which the order was granted show no facts upon which bail can properly be fixed. Gordon v. Fox, 11 N. Y. S. 5. (2) Complaint: Although it is the usual practice to submit the complaint along with the other papers in an application for an order of arrest under C.P.A. 827, it is not absolutely necessary to do so. C.P.A. 816, 833. The complaint, if submitted, however, best shows the character of the judgment demanded. (a) Thus, the complaint is held to be a necessary paper where the action is for divorce. Lichstrahl v. Lichstrahl, 77 N. Y. S. 900, in which case the court refused to accept the statement on the summons that the action was one for divorce as a sufficient statement of the character of the judgment demanded. (3) Undertaking: This may be dispensed with in the discretion of the court. C.P.A. 836. (4) Order: The order under C.P.A. 827 is substantially the same as that under C.P.A. 826, in all respects. except this-it must be a court order. C.P.A. 827. 

G. The Arrest; Executing the Order: It is the duty of the attorney to file the petition or affidavit on which the order was granted within ten days of such granting otherwise the order may be vacated on motion. R.C.P. 80 (as amended in 1936). The original order, or a certified copy thereof, if a court order, and all the papers upon which the order was made, including the undertaking, are delivered to the sheriff. When the latter makes the arrest he serves the defendant with copies of all the papers (C.P.A. 839), and takes and keeps the defendant in custody until discharged by law. C.P.A. 840. (1) Return by Sheriff: Within ten days after the defendant is arrested, if he does not give bail, or, if he gives bail, within ten days after the justification of the bail, the sheriff must file with the clerk of the

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

185 court the order of arrest (or the certified copy, if a court order), with his return thereon endorsed, the papers upon which the order of arrest was granted, and the undertaking given by the plaintiff. C.P.A. 864. (2) Imprisonment: The defendant arrested, if he does not give bail or vacate the order of arrest, will be confined during the usual course of the action and pending its outcome. He cannot avail himself of 

Civil Rights Law, sec. 72, limiting the time of such confinement, since that section applies only to final process in an action. Levy v. Salomon, 105 N. Y. 

529. See also Esporo v. Mervis, 201 N. Y. S. 264. (a) In a Municipal Court action, a defendant arrested on a civil order of arrest may not be detained more than two days, exclusive of Sundays and legal holidays, unless the trial of the action in which he is defendant, has commenced and continued without unnecessary delay or is adjourned at defendant's request. Mun. Ct. Code, sec. 32.

H. Remedies of Defendant Arrested: At any time before judgment, and in any case, within twenty days after his arrest, a defendant arrested under C.P.A. 826 or 827 may move to do any of the following or all of them: to vacate the order of arrest; to reduce the bail or to increase the security given by plaintiff. R.C.P. 83. 

(1) Motion to Vacate the Order: 

(a) On Original Papers: An application to vacate on the original papers may be made either ex parte to the judge who signed the order, or, upon notice, if made to any other judge or to the court. C.P.A. 844. The motion is based upon the insufficiency of the papers upon which the order of arrest was made, and if based solely upon the insufficiency of the complaint, it may be made at any time before judgment. C.P.A. 

842, R.C.P. 83; New Haven Web. Co. v. Ferris, 125 N. Y. 364. The court may, in furtherance of justice, allow plaintiff to submit additional proof to supply defects in his original papers where the defect is not jurisdictional. C.P.A. 822.

(b) On New Facts by Affidavit: If a motion to vacate the order of arrest is based upon new facts submitted by defendant in new affidavits, it must be made upon notice. In such case, the plaintiff is allowed, as a matter of course, to oppose the motion with new affidavits tending to support the original papers upon which the arrest was made. C.P.A. 822, 844. Such new proof on the part of the plaintiff may take the form of an amended complaint. Ensign v. Nelson, 1 N. Y. S. 

685; aff'd 112 N. Y. 674. (c) Conditional Vacating: Since the court has power to vacate wholly, it may vacate conditionally, as where the court vacates an order of arrest on condition that the defendant arrested shall not bring action against the plaintiff for false imprisonment, where it appears that in procuring such order, the plaintiff acted without malice and upon probable cause. But where defendant has an absolute right to have the order vacated, the court cannot annex such conditions. (2) Motion to Reduce Bail: This motion may be made at any time before final judgment or at any time within twenty days after arrest. R.C.P. 83. 

(3) Motion to Increase Plaintiff's Security: This motion may be made at any time before judgment whether the arrest was made under C.P.A. 826 or under 

C.P.A. 827. R.C.P. 83; Hull v. Cohen, 252 N. Y. S. 162. 

I. Bail: Suppose that the defendant who has been arrested has been unsuccessful in vacating the order of arrest and that he does not wish to remain in custody during the usual course of the action. He may obtain his liberty by (a) giving bail, or (b) making a deposit of money in lieu thereof, or (c) giving an undertaking for the jail liberties. (1) By Giving Bail: The defendant arrested under civil process must be allowed to give bail, or make a cash deposit in lieu thereof, at any hour of the day or night. If arrested under C.P.A. 826, he may give

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

187 bail at any time before execution is issued against his person. If arrested under C.P.A. 827, he may give bail at any time before he is in contempt of court. C.P.A. 847. (a) Form of Bail Undertaking: This must be made in the statutory form (C.P.A. 849) : (1') If the arrest be made under C.P.A. 827, the bail undertake that the defendant will obey the direction of the court (or of an appellate court), or render himself amenable to process to punish him for disobedience. (2′) If the arrest be made in an action to recover a chattel, the undertaking is to the effect that the defendant will deliver it if ordered to do so and pay the recovery if any. (3′) In any other case, the bail undertake that the defendant will at all times render himself amenable to any mandate which may be issued to enforce final judgment. (4') It is to be noted that only in the isolated case of the action to recover a chattel, do the bail undertake to pay any judgment recovered. (b) Amount: The bail undertaking must be for the amount named as bail in the order of arrest. 

(c) Qualifications of Bail: The bail undertaking must be executed by two or more sufficient bail who must state their places of residence and occupations. They must be residents of the state, and either householders or freeholders (renters or owners of real estate). Each bail must be worth the amount named as bail exclusive of property exempt from execution, but to make up the required amount more than two sureties may be used. C.P.A. 853. (d) Justification of Bail: Although the sheriff may be satisfied with the form and sufficiency of the bail tendered by the arrested defendant (and he has an opportunity to so satisfy himself under C.P.A. 850, which gives him the power to examine the sureties under oath concerning their property and circumstances), the plantiff is also given the opportunity to satisfy himself as to the sureties, and if he neglects to do so, the sheriff

is relieved from responsibility in the event the bail prove to be worthless. (1′) Method of Justification: Within three days after the bail is given, the sheriff must deliver to the plaintiff's attorney certified copies of the order of arrest, his return, and the bail undertaking. Within ten days after such delivery, if the bail is not acceptable, the plaintiff's attorney must serve a notice upon the sheriff to that effect. C.P.A. 851. Upon service of such notice, and within ten days thereafter, the sheriff or the defendant must serve a notice of justification before a judge of the court, or a county judge, at a specified time and place not less than five nor more than ten days thereafter. 

At such time and place the bail attend and submit to an examination, under oath, as to their sufficiency. If the judge finds the bail sufficient, he endorses his allowance on the bail undertaking and, thereupon, the sheriff is released from all further liability. C.P.A. 852, 855. 

J. Cash Deposit in Lieu of Bail: The defendant arrested may deposit cash to the amount named as bail, and the sheriff must accept such cash, give a receipt therefor, and discharge the defendant. C.P.A. 847, 856, 860. Unless the money is withdrawn before judgment, it will by direction of the court, be applied in satisfaction of the judgment. C.P.A. 859; Wesenberg v. McCormack, 198 

N. Y. S. 340.

K. Jail Liberties: Each county has certain territory adjacent to the jail building known as the jail limits, which, in cases of principal cities, usually coincide with the bounds of the city. A prisoner under civil process may be admitted to such "jail liberties" by giving a special undertaking required by Prison Law, Sec. 364. In New

York City the jail limits coincide with the bounds of the counties. This undertaking is in an amount double the bail specified and is conditioned that the defendant shall remain a prisoner and shall not at any time escape or go beyond the liberties of the jail until discharged from arrest. (Under the ordinary bail undertaking, the defendant is at full liberty to go anywhere he pleases.) (1) Where the undertaking is given for the jail limits only, it is sufficient that it comply with Prison Law, Sec. 364; it need not conform to C.P.A. 849. Horowitz v. Olenick, 70

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

189 

N. Y. S. 1116. (2) Where cash is deposited in lieu of an undertaking for the jail limits, the prisoner, or his representative, is entitled to a refund of the deposit on final judgment, and it should not be applied to plaintiff's judgment. Wesenberg v. McCormack, supra. But if the prisoner escapes, the cash must be applied to any judgment for escape. C.P.A. 856.

L. Enforcement of Plaintiff's Liability on Undertaking given for Order of Arrest: Should the order of arrest be vacated, an immediate cause of action on the undertaking to procure the order accrues to the party arrested. 

His damages, under such undertaking, are limited to actual legal expenses, costs and disbursements sustained in the vacating of the order and does not include damages properly recoverable in an action for false imprisonment. Krause v. Rutherford, 60 N. Y. S. 1047. 

3. Attachment: The remedy of attachment is radically different from that of arrest, or injunction, in that attachment is "in rem" while the latter are "in personam." The use of the remedy of attachment depends upon the presence of property of the defendant within the jurisdiction, and its purpose is to seize such property and hold it as security for any judgment subsequently recovered against the defendant. 

A. Actions in Which Available: A warrant of attachment may not be had except in an action to recover a sum of money as a tax or as damages. C.P.A. 902. This has the necessary effect of restricting attachment to actions at law. An action in equity will not lay the foundation for a warrant of attachment. Smith v. Mayer, 174 N. Y.

S. 197. But even in actions at law, a warrant of attachment may be had only in four classes of cases enumerated by C.P.A. 902: (1) Breach of contract, express or implied, other than a contract to marry. (a) An action on a foreign judgment although obtained on a tort cause of action is considered as coming under this class of cases. Gutta Percha etc. v. Mayor of Houston, 

108 N. Y. 276. (2) Wrongful conversion of personal property, (3) An injury to person or property in consequence of negligence, fraud or other wrongful act of the defendant,

(4) A wrongul act, neglect or default by which the decedent's death was caused, when the cause of action arose in this state before or after the passage of this act and the action is brought by an executor or administrator against a natural person, who, or a corporation which, would have been liable to an action in favor of the decedent by reason thereof if death had not ensued. 

B. Against Whom Available (C.P.A. 903): Attachment may be had against non-resident defendants, including foreign corporations. C.P.A. 903, subd. 3. It may be used against a resident but in that case it must be shown, by affidavit; (1) That defendant, a natural person, has departed from the state with intent to defraud creditors or to avoid service, or, keeps himself concealed therein with like intent. (a) "Residence" under C.P.A. 902 means place of abode rather than legal domicile. Zenatello v. Pons, 256 N. Y. 

S. 763. (b) Where only one member of a partnership has left the state with intent to defraud his creditors and the other partner remains within the state, there is no jurisdiction for the granting of a warrant of attachment against the co-partnership property. Bogert v. Dart, 25 Hun 395.
