(2) That defendant, if a natural person, or domestic corporation, has removed or is about to remove property from the state with intent to defraud creditors, or has assigned, disposed of, or secreted such property, or is about to do so with like intent, or that defendant, a domestic corporation, has no officer within the state upon whom, after diligent effort, a summons can be personally served. (3) That the defendant has made a false statement in writing as to his financial responsibility. (a) But where P creditor advances credit without knowledge of the false statement, the fact of D's making it does not deceive P and the latter is not entitled to an attachment under this subdivision. Penoyer v. Kelsey, 150 

N. Y. 77. (4) That the defendant (an adult resident), has been continuously absent from the state for more than six months, next before the granting of an order for

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

191 service by publication, without designating a person on whom to serve a summons in an action against him, or where said designation is ineffective. 

The order for publication must precede the warrant or be granted concurrently with it. Sonnak v. 

Walker, 180 N. Y. S. 848. (5) C.P.A. 904 provides, in brief, that a warrant of attachment may be had where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only against a defendant for public funds, or in an action to recover for peculation and deceit.

C. Papers Necessary to Obtain Warrant: These are the affidavit, undertaking and warrant of attachment; the complaint is not necessary, although if submitted and verified it will be considered as a supporting affidavit. (1) Affidavit: The affidavit, or affidavits should contain: (a) Evidentiary facts showing the existence of a cause of action for substantial damages in plaintiff's favor as specified in C.P.A. 902, (1′) It is necessary for the plaintiff to set out the facts which he claims prove his damages in order that the court may judge as to whether he has evidence of damages, and that the allegations of damage are not mere matters of speculation. Abdun-Nur v. Arbeed, 191 N. Y. S. 38; Carnera v. Schmeling, 260 N. Y. S. 82. (b) In contract cases, facts showing that plaintiff is entitled to recover a specified sum "over and above all counterclaims" known to plaintiff (C. 

P.A. 903; McGinley v. Gildersleeve, 108 N. Y. S. 888.), (c) Facts showing that the defendant is a non-resident (or a foreign corporation); or else has evaded service, or concealed himself or disposed of his or its property with intent to defraud creditors, or made, in writing, a false statement as to his financial responsibility, or has been absent six months without designating a person to receive service of a summons, etc. (C.P.A. 903), (1′) The affidavit may validly state in the alternative that the defendant has departed from the state with the intent to defraud his creditors or keeps him-

self concealed therein with like intent. Stewart v. 

Lyman, 70 N. Y. S. 936. (d) A statement as to any prior application for the order, if the application is ex parte. R.C.P. 61. 

(e) Requisites of Allegations of Fact in Affidavits: 

A mere averment of a fact upon information and belief, without more, is not sufficient; the sources of information and grounds of belief must be set forth (Crowns v. Vail, 51 N. Y. 

204), so that the court to which the affidavit is presented may have evidentiary facts from which to conclude that the plaintiff is entitled to the remedy of attachment. Ladenburg v. 

Comm. Bank of Newfoundland, 33 N. Y. S. 821 aff'd 146 N. Y. 406. (1') But where a warrant of attachment is issued on affidavits made on information and belief, and these affidavits give as the source of affiant's information affidavits of third parties made in other actions, the warrant is validly issued. Buell v. Van Camp, 119 

N. Y. 160. (2′) If the affiant's information comes through letters or telegrams in the affiant's possession or control, the originals or copies should be annexed to the affidavit (Gumbes v. Hicks, 101 N. Y. S. 741, aff'd 

190 N. Y. 532), and if they are not in English, translations should be included and the affidavit should recite by whom the message was sent and received and its connection with defendant. Friedman v. 

Prescetti, 192 N. Y. S. 55. (2) Undertaking: The undertaking must be to the effect that if the defendant recovers judgment, or, if the warrant is vacated, the plaintiff will pay defendant's costs and damages sustained by reason of the attachment, not exceeding the sum specified in the undertaking which must be at least $250. C.P.A. 819, 907. (a) Liability on Undertaking: Generally, the fact that the warrant was improperly granted is no defense to the sureties on P's undertaking. C.P.A. 911. (1') But, where a warrant of attachment had been granted in a specific performance action on a contract, in which action such a warrant was illegal and subject to being vacated as a matter of law,

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

193 D, who went to trial without moving to vacate, is not entitled to recover from P's sureties expenses incurred in successfully defending the action, since, it was incumbent upon D to make the motion in order to minimize his damages. Olsen v. U. S. Fid. & Guar. Co., 230 N. Y. 31. 

(3) Warrant (C.P.A. 910): The warrant must be directed to the sheriff of a particular county or, generally, to the sheriff of any county. It must briefly recite the grounds of attachment, which must not be stated in the alternative. Cronin v. Crooks, 143 N. Y. 352 ("that D has assigned or is about to assign his property", held bad). It must require the sheriff to attach and safely keep so much of the defendant's property within his county, or which the defendant may have before final judgment as will satisfy the plaintiff's demand with costs. The amount of the demand must be stated in the warrant and must be consistent with that stated in the affidavit. 

(a) Subscription: The warrant must be subscribed by plain tiff's attorney and by the judge who granted it. But, if granted by the court, it may either be subscribed by the judge holding the term, or issued out of the court by the clerk under the court's seal. R.C.P. 84. (b) Filing of Papers: The papers upon which the warrant was issued must be filed by plaintiff's attorney with the clerk within ten days after the warrant was granted.

In case of failure so to file, the warrant may be vacated, unless the court extends the time for filing. R.C.P. 80. 

D. Service of Summons: If the warrant is issued prior to service of the summons, personal service, or if that be impracticable, commencement of service by publication, or actual service outside the state, must be effected within thirty days after the granting of the warrant. 

C.P.A. 905. 

(1) Effect of Voluntary Appearance: A voluntary gen eral appearance by the defendant before the expiration of the thirty days will obviate the necessity of service of the summons, but if made after the thirty days, it will not save the attachment. Blossom v. Estes, 94 N. Y. 614. 

(2) Effect of Personal Service: If the non-resident de fendant is served personally (or appears generally),

and the plaintiff recovers, the latter gets a personal judgment against the former, whereas, if the service be made by publication and the defendant defaults in appearance, the nature of the judgment is in rem and can be enforced only against the attached property, and beyond that, as for a deficiency, there is no personal liability on the defendant. Pennoyer v. 

Neff, 95 U. S. 714.

E. Execution of Warrant: 

(1) Time for Execution: The warrant may be executed at any time before final judgment, but not afterward. The sheriff may levy from time to time until the amount for which the warrant was issued has been secured. C.P.A. 912. 

(2) What Property is Attachable and How Attached 

(C.P.A. 917): (a) Generally: Only legal assets, as distinguished from equitable assets, may be attached. (b) Real Property: Defendant's interest in realty, whether vested or not, may be attached. This is done by the plaintiff filing and recording in the county clerk's office in the county where the real estate is located a notice, subscribed by plaintiff's attorney, of the attachment, containing a statement of the amount of the plaintiff's claim and a description of the real property sought to be affected. C.P.A. 917. (1′) But where a transfer of real property has been effected in good faith, before the filing of a notice of attachment against it, such notice must be vacated as against the transferee. Fidelity & Dep. Co. v. 

Heller, 198 N. Y. S. 499. (2′) The sheriff must take into custody all books of account, vouchers, etc., relating to the personal property attached, and all evidences of defendants' title to the real property attached. C.P.A. 912. (c) Personal Property: All goods and chattels not exempt from levy under an execution may be attached, even in the hands of a fraudulent assignee (Hess v. Hess, 117 N. Y. 306), but not in the hands of a pledgee or consignee who has made advances thereon. Clements v. Doblin, 204 N.

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

195 

Y. S. 413, aff'd 239 N. Y. 526. Choses in action (even though intangible), to which defendant has title may be attached; for example, ordinary debts, bank accounts, bonds, promissory notes, an interest in the estate of a decedent, salaries or wages due defendant and not exempt. Courtney v. 18th Ward Bank, 154 N. Y. 688. Generally, alimony for the support of a wife or children is not subject to attachment. Maurice Baskin & Co., Inc. v. Howe, 233 N. Y. S. 648. Levy is made upon goods and chattels capable of manual delivery by the sheriff taking them into actual possession. This includes bonds and other instruments for the payment of money. C.P.A. 

917. See Roth v. Wells, 29 N. Y. 471. The court may aid the sheriff in making attachment by making an order permitting him to open the debtor's safe deposit box. Carples v. Cumberland Coal & Ice Co., 240 N. Y. 187. Personal property not capable of manual delivery (such as a bank account) is levied upon by leaving a certified copy of the warrant with a notice showing the property attached with the person holding the same. 

(1') Shares of Stock: Although Personal Property Law, 

Sec. 174 provides that no attachment upon shares of stock shall be valid unless the certificate is actually seized or surrendered or its transfer enjoined, C.P.A. 917, subd. 3 includes shares of stock as property not capable of manual delivery to be levied upon as such. See American Surety Co. v. 

Kasco Mills Inc., 149 Misc. 10, aff'd 237 A. D. 880, aff'd 262 N. Y. 585. (2′) Stock of a foreign corporation, owned by a nonresident D, and not physically present in New York, is not subject to attachment here, even though the foreign corporation has an office here. 

Plimpton v. Bigelow, 93 N. Y. 592. If, however, the certificates of stock of such foreign corporations are actually in New York, they may be levied upon, although the foreign corporation is not doing business here. Peo. ex rel Wynn v. Griffenhagen, 167 A. D. 572; Gen. Motors Corp. v. Linden, 192

N. Y. S. 28. Where D, a non-resident, pledges with a New York resident certificates of stock of a foreign corporation, they may be attached in New 

York. Simpson v. Jersey Const. Co., 165 N. Y. 193. (3′) Debts: The general rule is that the situs of the debt is where the debtor resides and it may be impounded only in the jurisdiction of such situs. 

Nat'l Bank v. Sampson, 179 N. Y. 213. C.P.A. 916, as amended in 1936, now permits levying of attachment upon any indebtedness due or to become due from a non-resident or foreign corporation upon whom or which service of process may be made within New York, to any person whether a nonresident or foreign corporation. (4') Judgments: A judgment is attached by serving the warrant of attachment upon the person against whom the judgment was recovered and who owes the payment of the judgment. Shipman v. Delaware & Hudson Co., 245 N. Y. 567.

F. Motions to Vacate Warrant: The defendant, or any other person having an interest in the attached property, and this includes a subsequent (junior) attaching creditor, may, at any time before the application of the attached property to the payment of the judgment, move to either vacate or modify the warrant or to increase plaintiff's security, or for one or more of these forms of relief. C.P.A. 948. Where the amount of plaintiff's undertaking is insufficient to protect defendant against costs, disbursements and damages, a motion should be granted to increase the security. Fuerstenberg v. American Soda Co., 21 A. D. 501. (1) How Made: As in the case of arrest, a motion to vacate the attachment on the ground of insufficiency may be made either on the original papers, or on new proofs, and the same rules as to the giving of notice are applicable as in the case of arrest. (a) If the motion is based on the original papers, the court or judge may, in furtherance of justice, permit plaintiff to offer new proof to supply amendable, but not jurisdictional, defects in his original proof. C.P.A. 822; 

Wade v. Gates Rubber Co., 199 N. Y. S. 16; Cutler v. 

Allavena, 150 N. Y. S. 790. (b) If founded on new proof, plaintiff may oppose by new proof a matter of course. C.P.A. 949.

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

197 (2) Test Applicable: If defendant is attacking the warrant of attachment on the ground of the insufficiency of the affidavits upon which it was granted, the question resolving itself for the court's determination is whether the affidavits set forth evidentiary facts sufficient to clearly establish all the facts necessary to constitute the cause of action under C.P.A. 

902, and the extrinsic facts required by C.P.A. 903. 

However, where the motion to vacate is being made by a junior attaching creditor, the question is only one of jurisdiction and the sufficiency of the evidence or the exercise of the court's discretion is not in question. Haebler v. Bernharth, 115 N. Y. 459. (a) Thus, a junior attaching creditor has no grounds upon which to vacate a prior warrant of attachment in the absence of fraud, collusion or jurisdictional defects in the prior warrant. Colcord v. Banco de Tamaulipas, 180 N. Y. S. 579. (b) Defendant may move to set aside a levy under the warrant on the ground of lack of jurisdiction where the property levied upon does not in fact belong to the defendant. Premier Malt Corp. v. Tullack, 197 N. Y. 

S. 579. See C.P.A. 924, 925, 926. 

G. Discharge of Attachment: A defendant may secure the release of his attached property by substituting security in its place. The application for such discharge is made to the court, or judge, that made the warrant. The defendant must furnish an undertaking, with suitable sureties, to the effect that he will pay any judgment up to the amount of plaintiff's demand. Plaintiff may demand an examination of the sureties as to their responsibility and they will have to justify much in the same way as sureties on a bail undertaking. C.P.A. 952-959. Green 

River District Co. v. Mass. Bonding & Ins. Co., 234 

N. Y. 109. 

4. Injunction:

A. Generally: The remedy of injunction by order is a provisional remedy controlled by C.P.A. sections 876 to 

901. It is not to be confused with permanent injunctions rendered in equity actions brought under the general equity power of the court, which injunctions are

obtained only upon proper pleadings after trial. The court has no inherent power to grant a "temporary injunction" covering the period between the commencement of the action and entry of judgment, and it has such power only by virtue of those sections of the Civil Practice Act which expressly provide for the provisional remedy of injunction. Bachman v. Harrington, 184 N. 

Y. 458. The old writ of injunction has been expressly abolished. C.P.A. 876.

B. Where Applicable: The provisional remedy of injunc tion is applicable in two classes of cases: (1) In actions where the complaint seeks a permanent injunction, where it further appears that the commission or continuance during the pendency of the action of the act sought to be enjoined would produce injury to the plaintiff. C.P.A. 877. (a) This class of cases is described and referred to as cases where the right to an injunction depends upon the nature of the action, which must be equitable. (b) In such cases, the complaint (not merely the moving affidavits), must contain averments showing the plaintiff to be entitled to final relief by injunction and facts showing the necessity for injunctive relief pending the action. Aetna v. Bassick, 220 N. Y. 767. (2) In all other actions, where the defendant is about to procure or suffer to be done, or threatens to do, in violation of the plaintiff's rights, an act respecting the subject of the action tending to render a judgment ineffectual; or, where the defendant is about to dispose of his property with intent to defraud the plaintiff. C.P.A. 878. Under C.P.A. 878, the equitable provisional remedy of injunction is thus applicable to actions at law. (a) The term "subject of the action" does not include a common law action for money damages, and a temporary injunction may not be had therein unless D is about to remove or dispose of his property with fraudulent intent. C.P.A. 878, subd. 2. Campbell v. Ernest, 19 N. Y. S. 123. (b) Thus, in a foreclosure action, an injunction pendente lite may not be had restraining D from selling personal property, without fraudulent intent, so that P may reach it on a deficiency judgment, since the personal

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

199 property is not the "subject of the action." Broadfoot v. Miller, 174 N. Y. S. 497. (c) Similarly, in an action at law for fraud and deceit for misrepresentations made by D in inducing P to buy D's business, P may not enjoin D from negotiating notes given as the purchase price, since the notes are not the "subject of the action." Golden v. Touster, 125 N. Y. 

S. 83. See also Weber v. Freifield, 198 N. Y. S. 122.

C. General Principles Controlling: Since the purpose of the temporary injunction is to preserve the rights of the parties during the pendency of the action, it will not be granted unless shown to be necessary to prevent irreparable injury to plaintiff and to keep matters in status quo until an adjudication upon the merits. (1) Thus, where the final relief sought will in effect be granted by the temporary injunction, the latter should not be granted. 

Van Veghten v. Howland, 12 Abb. Pr. N. S. 461. (2) A mandatory injunction pendente lite (as distinguished from a restraining one) will be granted only where P makes out a clear and positive right to such affirmative relief, and where a denial thereof will cause irreparable injury. West Side 

Elec. Co. v. Consol. Tel. Co., 84 N. Y. S. 1052; Stampp v. 

Board of Supervisors, 252 N. Y. S. 303. This is especially so where title is in dispute. City of Syracuse v. Hogan, 234 

N. Y. 457. (3) It appearing that D is about to transfer stock rightfully belonging to P, a temporary injunction should issue restraining such transfer, where P's action is expressly brought to compel the return of the stock and for an accounting. Weston v. Goldstein, 56 N. Y. S. 755.

D. Types of Injunction Actions in which Injunction Pendente Lite Will Be Granted: (1) An actor may be restrained from performing for another employer in violation of his contract where it is shown that the actor has such special merit or reputation as to make his loss a matter of serious consequence to plaintiff. Shubert 

Theatrical Co. v. Gallagher, 193 N. Y. S. 401. 

(2) The board of estimate or apportionment (or any other public board or official), may be restrained from committing an illegal act e.g. making an illegal payment. Schieffelin v. 

Berry, 216 N. Y. S. 367. (3) A wife may enjoin a husband from prosecuting a suit for divorce in a foreign state on the basis of a fictitious legal residence therein. Gwathmey v. Gwathmey, 190 N. Y. S. 

199. See also Richman v. Richman, 266 N. Y. S. 513. (4) A person may be enjoined from breaching his agreement not

to engage in a certain business. Wallace v. Kranz, 253 N. 

Y. S. 30. (5) A person may be enjoined from making unfair use of a trade name (Longs Hat Stores Corp. v. Longs Clothes, Inc., 231 

N. Y. S. 107), or of customers' lists wrongfully obtained (Conviser v. Brownstone & Co., 204 N. Y. S. 82), or other trade secrets. Davey Tree Co. v. Back, 244 N. Y. S. 239. (6) A nuisance will be enjoined. Keenly v. McCarty, 244 N. Y. S. 63. (7) Unlawful means or unlawful purposes in labor disputes (e.g. unlawful picketing), may be enjoined. Picadilly Laundry Service, Inc. v. Broader, 252 N. Y. 539 aff'g 233 N. Y. 

862. (For what constitutes lawful picketing, see Wise Shoe 

Co. v. Leventhal, 266 N. Y. 264.)

E. Necessary Papers: If an application is made for a temporary injunction in a case where the right to the injunction depends upon the nature of the action, the necessary papers are the summons, complaint, affidavit, undertaking and order of injunction. Where the right to the injunction depends upon extrinsic facts, the complaint may be omitted. C.P.A. 877, 878. (1) Affidavit: As in the case of arrest and attachment, previously considered, the affidavit or affidavits for the order of injunction must set out evidentiary facts by one who knows them. Information will not suffice for knowledge, except where the sources and other facts are set forth in detail, and it is shown to be impracticable to get the latter. Gambull Mfg. 

Co. v. Sherburne Co., 194 A. D. 425. (2) Complaint: Where a verified complaint is submitted, it may take the place of an affidavit, but only to the extent of facts contained therein. Clark v. Booth King Co., 40 A. D. 405. (3) Undertaking: The undertaking is executed either by the party, or by one or more sureties, as the court or judge directs, and is to the effect that the plaintiff will pay to the party enjoined, such damages, to the amount named in the undertaking, as he may sustain if it be finally decided that the plaintiff was not entitled to the injunction. C.P.A. 893. Such decision must be on the merits and where the injunction is dissolved on some other, extraneous ground, arising subsequent to the commencement of

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

201 the action, there is no liability on the undertaking.

Appollinaris Co. v. Venable, 136 N. Y. 46 (dissolu tion of temporary injunction as punishment for contempt of court). 

(a) Summary Remedy against Sureties: Where it is finally decided that plaintiff was not entitled to the temporary injunction and it is dissolved, the defendant may move for a reference, on notice to all parties, in order to ascertain the amount of damages suffered by the defendant through the injunction. C.P.A. 894. On such reference, the defendant may properly prove as part of his "damages", counsel fees paid in the vacating of the injunction. Rochester Yacht Club v. Rochester Boat

Works, 230 N. Y. S. 731. After the amount is thus ascertained, the defendant may bring an ordinary action at law for such damages and the amount thereof will be indisputable. C.P.A. 896. (The court on the reference may not direct entry of judgment in defendant's favor for the amount of such ascertained damages. Kelly v. Myreck, 200 N. Y. S. 90.) 

(4) The Injunction Order: 

(a) How Obtained: Ordinarily, the injunction order may be either a court or judge's order. Notice of application for the order must be given (C. 

P.A. 882, as amended in 1930). The notice contemplated is a formal notice of the application, if the action is pending, or if not pending, bearing the title of the action to be brought, either with or without the moving papers, returnable forthwith or at such other time as the court or judge may direct. Geo. F. Stuhmer & Co. v. 

Korman, 257 N. Y. S. 140. If an order to show cause is used to bring on the motion, the order cannot be made to contain an ex parte interim injunction. Hunt v. Stirone, 249 N. Y. S. 591. (b) When Obtained: The order may be made at any time before final judgment, after commencement of the action, or it may be made to accompany the summons, in which case it must be served with the summons, and if the summons is not served, the order loses its force and effect. C.P.A. 817, 818, 879-881; People ex rel 

Cauffman v. Van Buren, 136 N. Y. 252.

(c) Contents: The order must briefly recite the grounds upon which it is made and should specifically and clearly show on its face all that the defendant is required to do or to refrain from doing. C.P.A. 821; Lyon v. Botchford, 25

Hun. 57. The order may be directed to the defendant and his agents, servants and all persons "acting in aid, combination or collusion with the defendant." But it may not be directed to bind "all other persons having knowledge" of the order. Rigas v. Livingston, 178 N. Y. 20, Daly v. Amberg, 126 N. Y. 490. (1′) Where an injunction order is broader in its terms than the prayer for relief in the complaint, such variance is an irregularity for which it may be set aside; but until so set aside, a wilful disobedience thereof is punishable as a contempt. City of N. Y. v. N. Y. & Staten Is. Ferry Co., 64 N. Y. 622. See also Cohn v. Meinhart, 183 N. Y. S. 713. (d) Manner of Service of Order: This depends upon the form of the order. If a judge's order, service is made by delivering a copy and at the same time exhibiting the original with the judge's signature; if a court order, a certified copy is served. In both cases, copies of the papers upon which the order was made must be served with it, and the original papers must be filed in the clerk's office within ten days after granting of the order. C.P.A. 883; R.C.P. 80.

F. Motions to Vacate Injunction Order: Much as in the cases of arrest and attachment, a motion to vacate an ex parte injunction order may be made ex parte. Such motion must be made on the original papers and to the same judge in or out of court or to the Appellate Division. See Godfrey v. Matzene, 158 N. Y. S. 530. If the motion is made on new proof, it must be made on notice and may be made to the same judge who granted the order or to another judge or to the court. In disposing of the motion, the court, may require security from either side as a condition. C.P.A. 822, 897, 900. 

G. The Labor Injunction: C.P.A. 876a, in effect, since April

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

203 25, 1935, provides that no temporary or permanent injunction in a "labor dispute" shall be granted except after a hearing which must show: that unlawful acts or a breach of contract have been threatened or committed and will continue unless restrained, that substantial, irreparable injury is threatened; that refusal to grant an injunction will inflict greater injury upon P than will result to D by the granting thereof; that there is no adequate remedy at law; that police cannot furnish P adequate protection. The following acts, done by either a single person or group, cannot be prohibited by the decree; the right to refuse to perform any work; to join any labor organization regardless of any agreement; the payment or withholding of strike benefits; aiding, by lawful means, any party to the litigation; publicizing the facts of any dispute by speaking, picketing, or by any other method not involving fraud, violence, or breach of the peace; ceasing to patronize or to employ any person; peaceable assembly to do any of the above acts; agreeing with other persons to do or not to do any of the above acts; advising and inducing others without fraud, violence or threat thereof, to do the above acts. 

Doing of any of these acts in concert shall not constitute an unlawful combination or conspiracy. Hearings shall be held only after a verified complaint and a verified bill of particulars have been served and after due personal notice, as the court directs, has been given to all known persons against whom relief is sought, and also to the police charged with the duty of protecting P's property. 

The hearing shall be based on testimony and cross-examination, and not upon affidavits. No temporary injunction shall be granted unless P shall file a minimum undertaking of $1000. If more than one D is involved, $20 additional is to be secured for each D enjoined (maximum is $10,000). This is to recompense for any loss, including attorney's fees. No injunctive relief is to be granted if P has not made every reasonable effort to settle such dispute by negotiation or arbitration. The injunction shall be binding only upon the parties to the suit, their agents, employees or those in active partici-

pation with them and who shall, by personal service or otherwise, have received actual notice of the same. No officer or member of an association, or an association, shall be civilly or criminally liable for the unlawful acts of any members except upon proof by the weight of evidence, without aid of any presumptions, of the doing of such acts by members and actual participation or authorization of such acts, or ratification thereof after actual knowledge. (C.P.A. 882a, in effect Sept. 1, 1935, provides that no person shall be punished by fine or imprisonment for any alleged contempt, except those committed in presence of the court, arising out of any failure to obey a labor injunction, except after trial by jury to which D shall be entitled as a matter of right.) 

Temporary injunctions are not to exceed ten days unless

P is ready at the end of the period to proceed to trial. 

Permanent injunctions are limited to six months, subject to continuance on a further hearing. 

(1) What is a "Labor Dispute": The term "labor dis pute" includes any controversy concerning terms or conditions of employment, or concerning the association or representation of persons in negotiating, fixing, maintaining, changing or seeking to arrange terms, conditions or relations of employment, or any other controversy arising out of the respective interests of employer and employee regardless of whether or not the disputants stand in the relation of employer and employee. C.P.A. 876a, subd. 10. (a) In Thompson v. Boekhout, 273 N. Y. 390, it was held that where an owner "of a small business seeks to avoid 'labor disputes', as defined in the statute, by running his business without any employees, an attempt to induce or coerce him to hire an employee or employees, upon terms and conditions satisfactory to persons associated in such attempted inducement or coercion is not a 'labor dispute' within the letter or spirit of the statutory definition." (b) In Goldfinger v. Feintuch, 276 N. Y. 281, the 

Court of Appeals held that although a retailer 1

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

205 has no employees, as was the case in Thompson v. Boekhout, the statute applies "if he is in unity of interest with the manufacturer," and that a union "may follow non-union goods and seek by peaceful picketing to persuade the consuming public from purchasing the non-union product, whether that is at the plant of the manufacturer or at the store of the retailer in the same line of business and in unity of interest with the manufacturer."

H. When Arrest, Injunction and Attachment not to be 

Granted Together (C.P.A. 823): Where an application is made for an order of arrest, an injunction order and a warrant of attachment, or two of them in the same action, against the same defendant, and it satisfactorily appears that under the circumstances of the case two of them, or all of them, are not necessary for the plaintiff's security, the court or judge, in its or his discretion, may require the plaintiff to elect between them. Thus, in the ordinary case, the plaintiff should be compelled to elect as between an attachment and order of arrest. 

Todd-Buick Inc. v. Smith, 192 N. Y. S. 459, aff'd 194

N. Y. S. 985. A motion to obtain, vacate, or modify an order of arrest, injunction or warrant of attachment must be decided by the court or judge, within twenty days of its submission. C.P.A. 823. 

4. Receivers: 

A. Equity and Statutory: There are two classes of receivers; equity and statutory. Equity receivers are mere custodians of property of which they take possession, control and manage as agents and officers of the equity courts which appoint them. They have no legal title to such property. Equity receivers only are contemplated under the provisional remedy of receivership provided for in the Civil Practice Act. A statutory receiver is one in whom title vests. Such a receiver is always specially provided for by statute; e. g. receiver in supplementary proceedings, or receiver in proceedings to dissolve a corporation. A receiver pendente lite is an equity receiver in whom title does not vest. He is a person

appointed merely as the representative of the court to take charge of the fund or property to which the receivership extends during the pendency of the action. 

Decker v. Gardner, 124 N. Y. 334.

B. Receiver in Foreclosure: The most usual form of equity receiver is the receiver of rents and profits of mortgaged premises in an action to foreclose a mortgage thereon. 

Such a receiver is appointed upon plaintiff's application if he shows to the court's satisfaction that the security is inadequate and the rents of the mortgaged premises will likely be necessary to pay the indebtedness. Hollenbeck v. Donnell, 94 N. Y. 342. Eidlitz v. Lancaster, 59 N. Y. S. 54. (But see R.P.L. 254, sub. 10.) The receiver may be appointed ex parte, if the mortgage so provides. C.P.A. 975.

C. Application for Receiver: 

(1) In Pending Action (C.P.A. 974, subd. 1): An appli cation for a receiver pendente lite may be made only to the court and only by a party to an action already pending in the Supreme or County Courts, on proof by affidavit: (a) that he has an apparent interest in specific property which is the subject of the action (Mack v. Stanley, 74 A. D. 145), (b) that the said property is in the possession of an adverse party, and, (c) that there is danger of its removal, loss or injury during the action. Cafadaris v. Bulow, 244 

N. Y. S. 600. (2) After Judgment: After final judgment and in order to carry it into effect, a receiver may be appointed to supersede the receiver pendente lite (C.P.A. 974, subd. 2); or, after final judgment, a receiver may be appointed to preserve the property during the pendency of an appeal. C.P.A. 974, subd. 3. 

(3) How Application Made: Ordinarily, the adverse party, unless he is in default in appearance, or is being served by publication, must be given notice of application for the appointment of the receiver. 

C.P.A. 975.

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

207 

D. Powers and Duties of Receiver: The first duty of the receiver when appointed is to file a bond in the amount fixed by the order appointing him. C.P.A. 976. The order appointing him should also order the adverse party to deliver the property to the receiver. If the adverse party fails to do this, after service upon him of a certified copy of the order, the receiver's only remedy is by contempt proceedings. The receiver should not act without an order of the court directing him, and except to collect debts or rents, he may neither begin nor defend an action without permission of the court. He may employ counsel and is entitled to compensation and reimbursement for his expenses. He must render and file a full account on notice to all the parties and the sureties on his bond. After he is discharged, his liability can be enforced only by action against him individually. 

6. Replevin: Although replevin is not a provisional remedy, it has some of the characteristics of attachment. 

It is a common law action to recover a chattel during the pendency of which the chattel may be seized to await the outcome of the action.

A. When Maintainable: Where plaintiff's goods have been wrongfully taken or detained from him, he may either bring an action for damages for conversion, or, may bring replevin. (The two remedies are consistent.) 

The owner usually brings the action unless his right to possession is suspended; but any person having a temporary or special property interest in the chattel, entitling him to possession, such as a lienee or bailee, may likewise maintain the action. The object of the action is the recovery of the specific chattel, although as an incident to the main relief, the plaintiff may recover damages for the value of the chattel, if it cannot be redelivered, or for any injury to it or for both. Replevin may not be used where the chattel has been seized under legal process, such as a valid tax warrant, execution or attachment. C.P.A. 1089. Where a defendant has been civilly arrested in respect to the chattel sought to be replevied, replevin will not lie. If an order of arrest has been issued, but not executed, a subsequent replevin of the chattel supersedes the order. C.P.A. 1112.

B. Replevin Pending the Action: With the service of the summons in the replevin action, or at any time thereafter before entry of judgment plaintiff may avail himself of the summary remedy of replevin. C.P.A. 1094. 

(1) Papers Necessary: The only necessary papers are an affidavit and an undertaking. The plaintiff's attorney annexes to or endorses upon the affidavit a requisition addressed to the sheriff directing him to replevy the chattel described therein, and this requisition is deemed the mandate of the court. C.P.A. 1094-5. (a) Affidavit: The contents of the affidavit are prescribed in C.P.A. 1096. The affidavit must show: (1′) the ownership of the plaintiff in the chattel, or facts showing his special interest (Tuthill v. Skidmore, 124 N. Y. 148), (2′) a wrongful detention by defendant, (3') the alleged cause of detention (according to plaintiff's belief), (4') that it was not taken or seized under valid legal process, (5') the present state of the action, (6') the actual value of the chattel, (7′) a clear and particular description of the specific chattel or chattels sought to be replevied so that the sheriff will have no difficulty in making identification. Nat.

Enamel and Stamp Co. v. Kaplan, 65 N. 

Y. S. 732. (b) Undertaking: The undertaking must be executed by two sureties and must be approved by the sheriff. It must be to the effect that the sureties are bound in a sum, not less than twice the value of the chattels, for the prosecution of the action, for the return of the chattel to the defendant if that be awarded (or if the action abates or is discontinued before the chattel is returned to defendant), and for the payment to

PROVISIONAL REMEDIES 

209 the defendant of any judgment recovered by him. C.P.A. 1099. (2) How Replevy Executed: The replevy is made by the sheriff taking possession of the chattel if he finds the chattel in the possession of the defendant or his agent. He may not take it from a third person not a party. Otis v. Williams, 70 N. Y. 208.

Personal service of copies of the affidavit, undertaking and requisition must be made upon the defendant by the sheriff, or, where such personal service is impracticable, service may be made by other methods provided for in C.P.A. 1100. 

(3) Defendant's Remedies: Upon the seizure of the chattel, the defendant may require a justification of the plaintiff's sureties or may reclaim the chattel. (a) Justification: If the defendant requires justification, he must within three days after receipt of copies of the affidavit and undertaking, serve a notice of exception to the plaintiff's sureties on the sheriff and within ten days thereafter, the sureties must justify in the usual way. 

C.P.A. 1102, 1104. If they fail to justify, the defendant gets his goods back. (b) Reclaiming the Chattel: However, if the defendant desires to reclaim the chattel, he must institute a cross-replevin proceeding by serving a notice, affidavit and undertaking on the sheriff within three days after the goods have been taken. With his undertaking, the defendant must give notice of justification of his sureties. C.P.A. 1103. 

(1') Effect of Reclaiming: If the defendant reclaims and justifies his sureties, the sheriff must return the goods to him. If no reclamation is made and the plaintiff justifies his sureties (or if the justification is waived by the defendant), the sheriff must deliver the goods to the plaintiff. (2′) Third Party Claimant: If a claim to the goods while in the sheriff's possession is made by a third party claimant adversely to the defendant and the plaintiff, the sheriff in his discretion, before he delivers the chattel to the plaintiff, may, in order

to avoid personal liability, serve upon plaintiff's attorney a notice requiring indemnity against the third party's claim, and if the indemnity is not furnished within a reasonable time, the sheriff may deliver the chattel to the third party. C.P.A. 11071109. 

C. Right to Examination of Adverse Party: C.P.A. 1094a, added as of September 1st, 1937, provides that plaintiff in a replevin action, may obtain an order to take the deposition of an adverse party for the purpose of locating the chattel where he needs such information. The order may be granted to accompany the summons and verified complaint or at any time after the service of a verified complaint and before entry of final judgment. 

The order may be ex parte and may enjoin the adverse party from removing, selling, pledging, assigning, etc. the said chattel until further order of the court, provided plaintiff furnishes an undertaking in an amount of at least $250 for the obtaining of such an injunction.

CHAPTER XI. 

: 

DEPOSITIONS: 

ADMISSIONS: DISCOVERY AND INSPECTION 1. Examination Before Trial. 

A. Generally: At common law, a party could not compel his adversary to submit to an examination either before or at trial. Equity, however, provided a form of relief by the bill of discovery. A plaintiff by such a compulsory mandate of chancery could compel a defendant, in certain cases, to "make discovery" under oath. Although the bill of discovery was expressly abolished by the Code of Civil Procedure, its substance was preserved under another heading. The Civil Practice Act has continued the right to examine one's adversary before trial.

At present, such examination is the usual rather than the exceptional procedure, and the burden of applying to the court is ordinarily upon such party who opposes the right of his adversary seeking the examination. 

Buehler v. Busch, 191 N. Y. S. 848. Article 29 of the 

Civil Practice Act, beginning with Sec. 288, provides for examination before trial in a variety of cases which we now consider. (1) Although C.P.A. 308 provides that testimony may be taken by deposition under this article, (i. e. Art. 29), in a special proceeding, or for use in such a proceeding about to be brought as though the proceeding were an action, this section does not permit an examination before trial in summary proceedings to recover possession of real property. Dubowsky v. Goldsmith, 195 N. Y. S. 67.

B. Examination Before Action Commenced: Under C.P.A. 

295 examination is permitted of a witness, or of a person expected to be a party in a future action to be commenced in a court of record. The applicant for such examination must submit an affidavit to the court in which the expected action is to be brought, setting forth the nature of the issues involved therein and the facts and circumstances which make it necessary for the applicant's protection that the witness' testimony be taken. 

R.C.P. 123. The application, would, of necessity, be ex parte. This remedy was obviously intended for the mere

perpetuation of testimony and there is conflict in authority as to whether an examination for the purpose of discovering against whom the applicant has a cause of action, is proper. (1) Thus, the proper application of the remedy seems to be in a case such as In re Stevens, 199 N. Y. S. 698, where an examination before action commenced was allowed to perpetuate the testimony of a witness on the ground that he was dangerously ill and was about to go to Arizona to live. 

(2) But in the Fourth Department, in Lauffer v. Eastern Star 

Temple, 207 N. Y. S. 292, an examination of members of a committee was allowed in order to determine the proper party defendant. The applicant was an architect who had rendered services at the request of the committee but did not know what organization the committee represented. See also In re Silverberg, 273 N. Y. S. 831. (3) However, in the First Department, a rigid rule is observed that an examination before suit, in order to determine the parties defendant and for no other purpose will not be ordered. Re Hufstutler, 222 N. Y. S. 43. (4) The Second and Third Departments have followed the more liberal rule but in exceptional cases only. Matter of Weil, 25 A. D. 173. (5) This examination cannot be had under R.C.P. 122 to enable an expected plaintiff to frame his complaint, since there is no necessity to frame until after the commencement of the action (In re Titanium Alloy Mfg. Co., 198 N. Y. S. 503), nor may the applicant have the examination to find out whether or not he has a cause of action. Beikirch v. Loebs, 278 N. Y. S. 606.

C. Examination After Action Commenced: 

(1) To Enable Plaintiff to Frame Complaint: In order to be able to properly frame a good complaint, a plaintiff may examine either a witness or an adverse party (R.C.P. 122). The general purpose of the remedy is to enable an accurate formulation of the issues to be tried. Lockwood v. Merchants Dispatch 

Co., 254 N. Y. S. 573. (a) Thus, such an examination is allowed where, in an action against a fiduciary, P is unable to determine whether he should frame his complaint in law for fraud and deceit, or in equity for rescission or accounting. Teall v. 

Roeser, 201 N. Y. S. 280. (b) But, the examination is not allowed where its sole purpose is to enable P to plead damages precisely, for a general statement of damages is sufficient. Newman v.

Potter, 194 N. Y. S. 207. (c) The examination may be had only on an order made on notice and on proof by affidavit that P has a specific, meritorious cause of action against D, that certain relevant and necessary facts unknown to P, are within the knowledge of the person sought to be examined and that without such examination P will be unable to draw his complaint. 

(2) To Frame Answer or Other Pleading: Wherever necessary, either party to an action may examine an adverse party, or a witness, to enable him to frame. any other pleading, or a bill of particulars, or to enable him to comply with an order to make his pleading more definite and certain. This would give a defendant a right to an examination after action commenced; but where the defendant has sufficient facts to properly plead, the examination should be denied. Southman v. Ward, 154 N. Y. S. 449.

D. Examination After Issue Joined: 

(1) Of Parties: After the issues are determined in an action, each party should have the benefit of knowing those facts known to the adverse party which would tend to establish his own cause of action or affirmative defense. But such testimony must be demanded in good faith. (a) What Constitutes "Parties": 

(1') Original Owner of Claim: 

The original owner of a claim which constitutes the basis of a cause of action or counterclaim may be examined after the action is commenced under the same circumstances and to the same extent as an adverse party. 

C.P.A. 288. Thus, wherever defendant can examine the plaintiff, he can also examine the plaintiff's assignor, indorser or grantor. (a') However, if P has such an interest in the claim that he is not suing as assignee, grantee, or in a similar capacity, no non-party to the action, although interested in the litigation, can be examined as an "original owner." Thus where an agent sues in his own name on a contract which he signed for an undisclosed principal, the latter may not be examined as

an adverse party. Redfield v. Nat. Pet. Corp., 211 A. D. 152. (b') Likewise, a sole stockholder of a previous corporate owner of P's claim may not be examined before trial as "an original owner" under C.P.A. 288, where such stockholder is not an adverse party in the action. Wappler v. Woodbury Co., 210 N. Y. S. 503. (2′) Corporations or Unincorporated Associations: Corporations or unincorporated associations may be examined as parties or original owners through an officer, director, managing agent or employee. Not all employees may be examined but only those who have "some degree of management" or who personally managed or supervised the activities or instrumentalities which gave rise to the opponent's claim. C.P.A. 289. (a) Thus P suing for injuries received when struck by an elevator door was allowed to examine defendant bank by its elevator operator before trial to prove denied allegations as to circumstances of injury, defendant's operation and control and failure to take reasonable precautions. Loring v. Dime Sav. 

Bank, 286 N. Y. S. 334. Cf. Friedman v. N. Y. 

Cent. R.R. Co., 200 N. Y. S. 337; Enequist v. 

B'klyn City Rrd. Co., 214 N. Y. S. 450. 

(b) On What Matters Examination Granted: Gen erally, the applicant is allowed the examination to prove his own case only, that is, the examination is confined to those matters upon which the applicant has the affirmative (Oshinsky v. 

Gumberg, 176 N. Y. S. 406) but the court has discretionary power to enlarge the scope of the examination. Public Nat'l Bank v. City Nat'l 

Bank, 261 N. Y. 316. However, an examination should not be ordered upon those matters which are properly the subject of a bill of particulars. 

Pfeifer v. McNally, 163 N. Y. S. 577. 

(1') Affirmative Defenses: There are several exceptions to the general rule that an examination is confined to those matters upon

which the applicant has the affirmative. In these exceptions an examination will be allowed as to an affirmative defense of the adverse party in: (a') Cases involving a fiduciary relationship, (b') Cases where the facts are peculiarly within the knowledge of the adverse party. The fact that such matters may also be proved by subpoenaing other witnesses on the trial does not bar the right to the examination. Terry v. 

Ross Mfg. Co., 167 N. Y. S. 747. See

Marine Trust Co. v. Neway Devices, 

Inc., 198 N. Y. S. 715, where it was held that in an action on a note a defendant maker or endorser may be examined by the plaintiff holder before trial as to presentment and protest where the defense is lack of due presentment and protest. See also Griffin Mfg. Co. v. 

Gold Dust Corp., 282 N. Y. S. 931, where in an action to restrain alleged unlawful competition based on D's pirating P's secret formulae, it was held P may not require D to divulge the formulae it used without first establishing the communication of P's secret processes and formulae to D. (c') Where an affirmative defense is pleaded which if unanswered and established would destroy P's cause of action, as where in an action on a contract D interposes as a defense the cancellation of the contract sued upon. Gramatan 

Bank v. Sagamore Apartments, 271 N. 

Y. S. 201. 

(2') Examination in Accounting Actions: Or dinarily, in an action in equity for an accounting, an examination of defendant as to the account is not allowed until after it has

been determined that the defendant should account, since the testimony would deal only with the measure of liability and would therefore not be material or necessary on the trial of the issue as to whether the defendant ought to account. Moffat v. Phoenix 

Brewery, 288 N. Y. S. 281. (a') But it seems that where a fiduciary relationship existed between the parties, P, in the accounting action, may examine D before trial, even before establishing the right to the accounting. Wertheim v. Grombecker, 240 N. Y. 

S. 623. (b') And where in an action for, an accounting and to set aside a release P attacks the release for fraud, he may examine D concerning representations made by D about partnership accounts, even before entry of an interlocutory judgment. The accounting will have a direct bearing on the right to set aside the release. Struckler v. Teitz, 201 N. Y. S. 394. (c') Also, in an action for an accounting, an examination before trial may be had on the question of receipts and disbursements made by D on an alleged previous accounting, since such information is necessary and material to the issues. Griffin v. Davis, 90 N. Y. S. 419. (d') Second Department Rule: It has been decided in the Second Department that where the examination would involve an accounting it should not be allowed. Pomerantz v. Naomi 

Realty Corp., 240 N. Y. S. 480. 

(3') Examination in Negligence Actions: In the 

Second Department there is no limitation on the right to an examination before trial in a negligence action. Samols v. Mayer, 199 N. Y. S. 754. The Appellate Division in the First Department has permitted a limited right of examination before trial in negligence actions, but except in unusual circumstances, a general examination before trial of either party upon the issues will not be permitted in the said Department. Shaw v. Lamley Realty Co., 194 N. Y. S. 531. (a') Even in the First Department P may examine

D specifically as to ownership and control where these have been denied in the answer. 

Shaw v. Samley Realty Co., supra. (b') In cases such as these the rule has been somewhat relaxed and an examination permitted: where P was without personal knowledge of the circumstances surrounding the accident that caused the death of the deceased (Werner v. Haas, 285 N. Y. S. 327); where P had to prove scienter as to vicious propensities of a dog that bit him. Palmer v. Hampton, 220 N. Y. S. 768. 

(c') Physical Examination (C.P.A. 306): In an action to recover damages for personal injuries

P may be ordered to submit to a physical examination of his injuries. The order is made on notice on D's affidavit showing ignorance of the nature and extent of P's alleged injuries. The order designates a physician or surgeon to make the examination. It may not require a report to be made before trial to the court or to either party. Kelman v. Union 

Ry. Co., 195 N. Y. S. 313. If P is a woman she is entitled to have such examination by a female physician and before her own physician and any relative that the court may direct. 

Under this section the court may require P to submit to X-ray photographs. Gimenez v. 

Great Atlantic and Pac. Tea Co., 259 N. Y. S. 

597. (In an annulment action, P is entitled to a physical examination only where D is unwilling to waive his statutory privilege and to allow his own physician to testify. Yelin v. 

Yelin, 255 N. Y. S. 708.) 

(d') Blood Grouping Test (C.P.A. 306a): Wherever relevant, the court may order any party or the child of any such party v. the person involved in the controversy, to submit to one or more blood grouping tests, the specimen to be collected v. the tests made by duly qualified doctors. The results are receivable in evidence only where definite exclusion is established. 

The testimony of the experts and the persons examined may be taken by deposition. 

(4') Examination in Divorce Cases: 

An ex- amination before trial of an adverse party may be had in divorce actions along the same lines and subject to the same rules as

previously considered. (a') But the examination will not be permitted in an attempt to support a conjectural defense of consent, connivance and procurement, where such defense is alleged in D's answer only on information and belief. Rosenwasser v. 

Rosenwasser, 182 N. Y. S. 27. (b') Nor may an examination before trial be had of D in order to ascertain his financial condition with reference to ability to pay alimony since such information would be immaterial and unnecessary should P fail in her action.

Von Valkenburgh v. Von Valkenburgh, 133 

N. Y. S. 942. Cf. Altman v. Altman, 289 

N. Y. S. 937. 

(2) Of Witnesses: Material and necessary testimony of a witness may be taken after action begun, but before trial, in the following cases (C.P.A. 288, 290, 292; R.C.P. 123, 138): (a) Where the witness is about to depart from the state or is without the state, (1') But where the witness is a non-resident who has a place of business within the state, he does not come under this subdivision. Warner v. Dockendorff, 167 N. Y. S. 247. (b) Where he resides more than a hundred miles from the place of trial, (c) Where he is so sick or infirm as to justify reasonable belief that he will not be able to attend the trial, (1') The party applying to examine the witness must clearly show that the witness' testimony will be material and necessary and that the witness is so sick or infirm that he will not be able to attend the trial; the mere fact that at one time the witness was seriously ill is not sufficient. Johnson v. New Home Sewing Machine Co., 70 N. Y. S. 875. (d) Where other special circumstances justify the examination, (1′) Thus, where a hostile witness is completely under the control of the adverse party, he may be examined before trial under this subdivision. Auto 

Club v. Canavan, 112 N. Y. S. 785. (2′) But a party does not show "special circumstances” warranting the examination of a witness before

trial where he merely shows that the witness and the adverse party are "friendly" and no more. 

Harburger v. Westchester Fire Ins. Co., 152 N. Y. 

S. 272. (e) In any case, parties and witnesses may not be examined pursuant to the same order. Diefendorf v. Fenn, 110 N. Y. S. 68. 

2. Examination During Trial or After Judgment: An examina tion during trial or after judgment in order to carry the judgment into effect, whether of a party or a witness, may be had on an order made on notice and on proof by affidavit showing the necessity therefor. C.P.A. 293. 

3. Examination for Purposes of Motion: C.P.A. 307 and R.C. 

P. 120 give a party intending to make a motion for some relief, the right to take the testimony of a person not a party who is possessed of some information necessary to the moving party. The party examined merely makes an affidavit as to what he knows and he cannot be compelled to take any means to inform himself or to produce anything which contains such information either by a subpoena duces tecum or otherwise. Wallace v. Baring, 37 N. Y. S. 1078. 

4. Practice and Procedure in Examining Party or Witness. 

A. Generally: There are two methods to secure the examination. The first is by notice (C.P.A. 290; R.C.P. 

121), and the second is by order made on notice (C.P.A. 

292). Either method may be used except in the following cases in which an order on notice must be used: (1) During trial or after judgment (C.P.A. 293), (2) Where the production of books or papers is desired for other purposes than identification or to refresh the recollection of the witnesses (C.P.A. 296), (3) Where the person to be examined is imprisoned within the state under sentence of a felony (C.P.A. 297), (4) Where the purpose of the examination is to enable a party to draw a complaint (R.C.P. 122), (5) Where a physical examination of plaintiff is sought under C.P.A. 306.

B. Procedure by Notice: 

(1) Contents of Notice: The party desiring the examina tion must serve upon the adverse party or his attor-

ney, at least five days before the date fixed for the examination, a notice, entitled in the action and subscribed by the party, or his attorney, stating the time and place of the examination and the person before whom it is to be taken, the names of the persons to be examined and the matters upon which they will be examined. C.P.A. 290; R.C.P. 121. (a) Where the full five days' notice of examination has not been given, under C.P.A. 294, the court may cure the defect on a motion to vacate the notice on that ground, by setting the date of the examination ahead. Isenburger v. Arrowhead Mills, Inc., 208 N. Y. S. 323. See

Eagle-Picher Lead Co. v. Mansfield Paint Co. Inc., 

194 N. Y. S. 386. (b) It is not "stating the matters" on which the person is to be examined by merely repeating the allegations of the complaint. The notice must specify material facts which the party seeking the examination must prove in order to prima facie make out his cause of action or defense. Rogers v. Gould, 201 N. Y. S. 535. 

(2) Motion to Vacate or Modify Notice: Where the notice for his examination has been served upon him as above, the opposing party may move to vacate it. On such motion, the use of affidavits is optional, but the notice of motion must state the grounds upon which the motion to vacate will be made. The court by order, in its discretion, may deny the motion, or vacate the notice, or modify it by limiting the scope of the examination or by imposing other just conditions. C.P.A. 291, 294; R.C.P. 124. (a) Burden of Proof: On such motion to vacate, the moving party has not the burden of proof to show the examination sought is improper, but it is incumbent upon the party seeking it to prove its propriety. Lovasz v. 

Fowler, 204 N. Y. S. 308. 

(b) Stay: Service of notice of motion to vacate or modify the first notice stays the taking of testimony until the decision of the motion, provided the motion is promptly made. C.P.A. 291. Use of an order to show cause would be the safer practice. See Hand v. Haiduck, 204 N. Y. S. 214. 

(c) Where Made: A motion to vacate a notice of examina tion should be made in the judicial district or in the county where the action is triable. Cohen v. Mann, 193 

N. Y. S. 199.

C. Procedure by Order: A party seeking to examine a person, may do so by order obtained on notice to all parties who have appeared after proof by affidavit of the facts and circumstances that make the examination proper, and which indicates that the testimony sought is material and necessary to the party seeking it. C.P.A. 292; 

R.C.P. 122. The applicant, in this case, unlike the case where the examination is sought by notice, must show, in the first instance, the validity of the examination sought. The order itself need not, as in the case of notice, specify the matters as to which the person is to be examined (Eagle-Picher Co. v. Mansfield Paint Co., supra) although it is the better practice to do so. Bamberger v. Cooke, 169 N. Y. S. 227.

D. Scope and Incidents of Examination-Either Method: 

(1) Compelling Attendance of Person to be Examined: 

(a) Witness: A witness may be compelled to attend only by personal service upon him of a subpoena or an order. In either case, he must be paid, or at least tendered, the statutory witness fees and mileage fees. If the witness fails to attend, he may be punished in the same way as upon failure to obey a subpoena to attend a trial. C.P.A. 299, 405. (b) Party: A party may be compelled to attend for examination by a subpoena if witness and mileage fees are paid, or tendered to him, (he may be punished for failure to obey in like manner as a witness) or by order. Ditomasso v. 

Loverro, 242 A. D. 190. Further, where a party duly served with such subpoena or order wilfully fails to attend for examination, his pleadings may be ordered stricken out and judgment taken against him by default. If the party has appeared in the action, the order for his examination may be served on his attorney, and for a wilful failure to appear his pleading may be stricken out upon application to the court on notice. C.P.A. 299; Levine v. Moskowitz, 200 N. Y. S. 597. In Levy v. Emerson Steuben 

Mills, Inc., 284 N. Y. S. 532, plaintiff's reply

was stricken out for his failure to appear for examination in support of the defendant's counterclaim, but his complaint was properly not dismissed. (See Cone v. Bernanke, 278 N. 

Y. S. 566.) (1') However, if only a notice of examination has been served on a party who refuses to attend, or on his attorney, the court has no power to strike out the delinquent party's pleading. Levine v. Moskowitz, supra. In re Pipski, 274 N. Y. S. 256. (2′) But it has power to stay all proceedings on his part until he submits to the examination. Graziano v. Eagle Pencil Co., 201 N. Y. S. 757. (3′) In all cases where a witness or party is duly served personally with an order requiring his submission to examination, and wilfully defaults, he may be punished for contempt of court. Peters v. Berkeley, 219 N. Y. S. 709. 

(2) Production of Books and Papers: 

Production of books and papers for use on examination of a party or witness may be compelled either by a subpoena duces tecum or by an order, but not by notice alone. 

Ritzwoller v. Lurie, 204 A. D. 768. If the subpoena is served, the books and papers produced on the examination may be used only for identification and to refresh the recollection of the witness. New York 

City Car Advert. Co. v. Regensburg, 200 N. Y. S. 

152. However it is possible to join an application for an examination before trial of an adverse party with an application for an inspection of books and papers (C.P.A. 296), and both forms of relief may be united in one order. Fey v. Wisser, 202 N. Y. S. 

30; in re Smathers, 274 N. Y. S. 109. Where the books and papers are produced pursuant to order the relevant parts thereof may be inspected by the applicant and offered in evidence. See Zeltner v. 

Fidelity & Deposit Co., 220 A. D. 21. Cf. C.P.A. 324. 

(3) Place of Examination (C.P.A. 300): 

(a) Resident: The testimony of a resident of the state may be taken by deposition within the state either in the county in which he resides or in which he has an office for the regular transaction of business. Hollister v. Robert-

son, 203 N. Y. S. 514. (b) Non-Resident: A non-resident may be required to attend in any county in which he is served with a subpoena, but if an order is served instead of a notice and subpoena, such non-resident may be required to attend anywhere in the state specified in the order. (c) Where an order for the examination of a party is served on his attorney, he cannot be required to attend in any county other than that in which he resides or where he has an office for the régular transaction of business in person, or in the county where the action is pending. C.P.A. 300. (4) Before Whom Taken: (a) Within the state: Testimony within the state may be taken before any impartial and disinterested judicial officer, notary public, commissioner of deeds or attorney. C.P.A. 301. (b) Without the State: Testimony without the state may be taken before any such officer or attorney residing in the state or territory where the witness is, or before the person to whom a commission has been issued for the purpose. Affinity or consanguinity to a party, or interest in the event, is a valid objection to the disinterestedness of the officer named to take testimony. 

C.P.A. 301. 

(5) Objections to Questions on Examination: An ex ception to a ruling on the admissibility of evidence made on an examination before trial should be duly noted, but the examination itself should not be held up for the purpose of entering an order on such ruling and then appealing from the order. Harrison v. 

Miller, 179 N. Y. S. 331. All objections raised on an examination before trial are reserved for the trial, except objections as to form, which if not taken at the examination are deemed waived. During the course of the examination the court should not rule on competency or materiality but only on relevancy. 

Guenther v. Ridgway Co., 143 N. Y. S. 961.

(a) Incriminatory Replies (C.P.A. 351-355): A wit ness cannot be compelled to incriminate himself or expose himself to a penalty, or forfeiture, or to disclose professional communications made to him as a clergyman, attorney or physician, nor to disclose a legitimate trade secret. 

Solar Baking Powder Co. v. Royal Baking Pow der Co., 112 N. Y. S. 1013. Ordinarily, the witness must answer all proper questions and claim his constitutional privilege against self-incrimination with each question involving it; but where substantally all of the items on which a party's examination is sought relate to matters concerning which he would be entitled to claim his constitutional privilege, he may take objection to the entire examination and he will not be forced to claim his privilege as to specific questions. Baar v. U. S. Fid. & Guar. Co., 201 N. Y. S. 391. 

5. Depositions Outside State For Use Within State: Testi mony taken within the state is oral unless the parties stipulate otherwise. Where testimony is to be taken outside the state for use within, the court may order that such testimony be taken wholly or partly upon written interrogatories. 

The court, in its order, directs the testimony to be taken before an officer or attorney, as mentioned before, but if not it must direct the issuance of a "commission" to some specified person, and if it does, the settled interrogatories are attached to the "commission" and sent to the person designated. C. 

P.A. 302; R.C.P. 130.

A. Taking the Testimony by Commission: 

(1) Order: The order for a commission is usually issued as a matter of course upon notice, and quite often, upon consent of the parties. In granting the order, the court in its discretion may prescribe terms and conditions. Dayton v. Farmer, 194 N. Y. S. 620.

The order is a court order and should direct the issuance of a commission to a designated person as commissioner for the examination of specified witnesses at certain places. (2) Settlement of Interrogatories: After the order has

been made, the attorney for the party on whose motion it has been made, draws and serves proposed interrogatories to be annexed to the commission. 

The attorney for the adverse party, when served with the proposed interrogatories, prepares and serves cross-interrogatories. Both the interrogatories and cross-interrogatories are then settled on notice before a judge, unless they are settled between the parties themselves on consent. R.C.P. 126. 

(3) Commission; Issuance and Execution: 

The com- mission, under authority of the order, is signed and sealed by the clerk of the court and the interrogatories are annexed thereto; then, all the papers are sent to the commissioner. The commission is in the name of the state and directed to the commissioner named in the order and sets forth the title of the action, the names of the witnesses to be examined, and the statutory directions as to the execution of the commission. R.C.P. 128. Upon receipt of the commission, the commissioner has the specified witnesses attend before him, swears them, and records in writing the witnesses' answers to the questions contained in the interrogatories. The commission is then returned, with the depositions of the witnesses annexed and certified, to the clerk, who files them for inspection by the parties. R.C.P. 130-132. 

B. Letters Rogatory: Where there is a probability that the person to be examined outside the state will refuse to testify, the court in which the action or proceeding is pending, may, on an order made on notice of motion, issue letters rogatory. This remedy is a formal request to a court in the state or country where the witness is, to summon him and take his testimony upon the written interrogatories which are attached to the letters rogatory. C.P.A. 309. See Union Sq. Bank v. Reichmann, 9 A. D. 596.

C. Suppression of Deposition: When a deposition taken without the state is returned, it may be "suppressed" on motion of either party on notice, provided such notice is made promptly and before trial. Union Sq. Bank v.

Reichmann, supra.

The following are grounds for suppressions: (1) fraud or unfair conduct in taking the deposition, (2) irregularity in the taking or return of the deposition, (3) deliberate refusal by the witness to answer questions fully and fairly (Calhoun v. Commonwealth Trust 

Co., 124 A. D. 633), (4) where it appears that a resident, whose testimony was taken without the state, resides within one hundred miles of the place of trial and could have been subpoenaed to attend the trial. R.C.P. 133. 

6. Reading Depositions in Evidence: 

A. Generally: Either party may read upon the trial any part of a deposition made on an examination before trial and may not object to the reading of any other part by his adversary except on the ground of competency. 

Schupp v. Barnett, 206 N. Y. S. 553. Depositions may not be used against a party who has appeared, unless he, or his predecessor in interest, had an opportunity to cross-examine the deponent. C.P.A. 303.

B. Of Parties: The depositions of a party taken at the re quest of the adversary may be read in evidence as of course, even if the party is in the court room. C.P.A. 304. 

C. Of Witnesses: The deposition of a witness taken by stipulation between the parties may be read in evidence similarly as above, and the same applies to the deposition of a witness taken without the state, unless it has been suppressed. (1) But the deposition of a witness taken within the state can be used only upon proof that the witness examined is still residing more than one hundred miles from the place of trial or other facts showing the impossibility of bringing him to court by subpoena. C.P.A. 304; Curtis v. Searles, 200 N. Y. S. 602. 

7. Depositions Within State for Use Without: A party to a pending action or proceeding in another state or country may procure the taking of testimony of a person in New York. 

In such case, the Supreme Court, or a County Court, or a judge of either court, may issue a subpoena, commanding the witness to appear and testify. The witness may be

punished in like manner as a delinquent witness in an action in our court. C.P.A. 311; R.C.P. 136-137. The court will compel the taking and transmission of the deposition so taken to the proper foreign tribunal. C.P.A. 310-312. 

8. Admissions: 

A party may be called upon to make two types of admissions in order to expedite the trial of an action.

A. Of Facts: By a notice in writing served not later than ten days before trial, a party may demand from his adversary an admission of certain specified facts. A failure to so admit within six days, or for any longer period granted by the court, imposes upon the refusing party the liability to pay the expenses of proving the same unless there was a good reason for the refusal. C.P.A. 323. (1) If the party upon whom the demand has been made decides not to make the admission, he may ignore the demand and the court will pass upon the merits of the refusal at the trial, but he should not move to set aside the demand, prior to the trial, since such practice is not sanctioned by the section. Banca Nazionale v. Equitable Trust Co., 224 N. Y. S. 617. 

B. Of Genuineness of Paper: At any time before trial, the attorney for a party may exhibit to the opposing attorney a paper material to the action and demand a written admission of its genuineness. If the admission is not given within four days after the demand and the paper is admitted or proved on the trial, the expense incurred to prove its genuineness is ascertained at the trial and paid by the party refusing the admission, unless it appears that there was some good reason for the refusal. C.P.A. 322. (1) Under this section, an admission by counsel, at a trial, of the genuineness of a paper offered by the other side, entered on the record, is binding as to subsequent trials on the same cause unless specially restricted when made to the trial upon which it was made. Voisin v. Commercial Mut. Ins. Co., 22 N. Y. S. 348. 

9. Discovery and Inspection: 

A. Discovery and Inspection on Order (C.P.A. 324): Where a party shows by affidavit that a specific book, paper, machine, or other article in the possession or control of an adverse party, materially relates to the merits of the action or defense, a court of record will order the ad-

verse party to show cause why he should not produce the thing specified for inspection before trial. R.C.P. 

140. The affidavit for the inspection and discovery must be made by the party and not by his attorney, unless it be clearly shown that it is impossible to do so within a reasonable time. Breyer v. Bloch, 199 N. Y. S. 664. (1) Where Permissible: Such an inspection will be granted only to enable a party to obtain evidence which is material and necessary to support his cause. 

In re Smathers, supra; Marx v. Penn. Fire Ins. Co., 59 N. Y. S. 693. (a) Thus, where issue has not been joined and the pleadings are not before the court, P's motion for an inspection of an allegedly spurious deed should not be granted in an action for ejectment where there is no indication that D will defend under claim of the deed or otherwise set it up in support of his title. Rhoades v. Schwartz, 65 

N. Y. S. 111. (b) And an inspection should not be allowed in a negligence action of D's private records and reports of the accident, since they are in no sense evidence. Falco v. N. Y. N., H. & H. RR. Co., 146 N. Y. S. 1024. (c) But in a stockholder's action for an accounting, an inspection will be ordered even before P has first established his right to an accounting by interlocutory decree, where P seeks to discover acts of waste and misconduct relating to the merits of his cause of action. Bell v. 

Frank Gilbert Paper Co., 193 N. Y. S. 26. (d) A discovery and inspection of D's books will be allowed where they relate wholly to the subject matter of the litigation between the parties, even though D is a foreign corporation. Sullivan v. Ryan Parker Const. Co., 132 

N. Y. S. 344. (e) Discovery and inspection of an article of personal property is authorized under C.P.A. 324. Donoghue v. 

Callanan, 136 N. Y. S. 657, where P who sustained personal injuries as a result of the explosion of an allegedly, defectively filled, soda-water bottle was permitted to inspect a "fountain" belonging to defendant, which filled, charged and tested the bottles. (f) Under this power to allow discovery and inspection the court cannot order an examination and dissection of a dead body. Bernstein v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 255 N. Y. S. 591. (g) In all cases, the granting of discovery and inspection is

PREPARI 

229 within the sound discretion of the court. Union Trust 

Co. v. Francis, 277 N. Y. S. 332. 

(2) Effect of Disobedience of Orders: Wilful and de liberate disobedience of an order for discovery and inspection by a party, shown to be able to obey, may be punished as a contempt, or by striking out that part of the delinquent party's pleading to which the discovery would be relevant. Such procedure is not a denial of due process of law. Feingold v. Walworth Co., 238 N. Y. 446.

B. Discovery and Inspection on Notice (C.P.A. 327): A 

document mentioned in an affidavit or pleading of a party may be subjected to a written demand for inspection by the adverse party, or for the purpose of making a copy. 

If the demand is refused, the refusing party cannot put such document in evidence unless he satisfies the court that his refusal was justified, or that the document “relates only to his own title, he being a defendant." (1) If the adverse party has good reason for not yielding the document to inspection, he should move before trial to have the notice vacated or modified. Wile v. Nassau Smelt. & 

Ref. Works, 200 N. Y. S. 154.

C. Disclosure in Aid of Discovery (C.P.A. 328): Where a party has reasonable grounds to believe that the adverse party has, or had, control of a specified document relating to any matter at issue, he may move upon affidavit showing such grounds of belief for an order requiring the adverse party to state by affidavit whether the document is, or has been, in his possession or control, when he parted with it and what has become thereof. Rosenthal v. Magazine Repeating Razor Co., 246 N. Y. S. 286.
