(1) A party cannot by notice (without an order) compel production of documents not referred to or relied on in an adversary's pleadings even after compelling the adversary to admit by affidavit his possession of the documents. Schmall 

Fils Asso. v. Baltic Amer. Line, 231 A. D. 231.

CHAPTER XII THE TRIAL AND ITS INCIDENTS 1. Motion For Security For Costs: Where a non-resident plaintiff brings an action against a defendant in our courts, the latter is not compelled to follow such non-resident into a foreign state for indemnity in case the action is unsuccessful and the defendant recovers a judgment for his costs and disbursements. Application for security may be made at any stage of the action. Snyder v. Griswold, 250 N. Y.

S. 26. The defendant in an action in a court record may require security for possible costs to be given by the plaintiff (except where plaintiff sues as a poor person) in the following cases (C.P.A. 1522): 

A. Where the plaintiff, at the time the action was commenced, was, (Subd. 1 to 4): (1) A non-resident, (a) In such case, the right of D to security is absolute and not discretionary with the court, even though D is a non-resident or a foreign corporation. Craig v. Du Cross, Ltd., 117 N. Y. S. 384. (b) If there are several Ps, D is not entitled to security for costs unless he is entitled to it as against each. C.P.A. 

1522, subd. 8. But, where a non-resident P joins with a resident P in suing on separate causes of action, D is entitled to security for costs from the non-resident P. 

Akely v. Kinnicut, 238 N. Y. 466. (c) A resident guardian ad litem for a non-resident infant 

P cannot be required to give security for costs. Blumenthal v. Alexander, 162 N. Y. S. 403. (d) But where the non-resident next of kin in a death action are represented by a non-resident administrator and there is no property of the decedent within the state from which costs could be collected, D's motion for security for costs should be granted in, the discretion of the court. C.P.A. 1523. McKeaggan v. Post & Mc- 

Cord, 102 N. Y. S. 276. (2) Residing outside the county in which action was brought, provided it is a county court action (except county courts of Albany, Kings, Queens, Rensselaer and Richmond), (3) Residing outside the city of New York, city of Yon-

THE TRIAL 

231 kers or Albany, provided the action be brought in the city courts of these cities, (4) A foreign corporation, (5) Imprisoned for a crime, or if plaintiff is the assignee of such person, (6) The official assignee or official trustee of a debtor, (7) The assignee of a bankrupt, provided that the cause of action arose before the assignment, the appointment of the trustee, or the adjudication in bankruptcy.

B. Where the plaintiff, after the action was commenced, (Subd. 

5 to 7): (1) Ceased to be a New York resident, or ceased to be a resident of the county or city as specified in A, (2) and (3) above, (2) Is adjudicated a bankrupt or discharged from his debts, (3) Is sentenced to state prison for a term less than life. 

C. C.P.A. 1523: The court within its discretion may require the plaintiff to give security for costs in an action by or against an executor or administrator, the trustee of an express trust, a person expressly authorized by statute to sue or to be sued, an official assignee, the assignee of a receiver, or the committee of a judicially declared incompetent.

D. Liability of Attorney: C.P.A. 1530 provides that where a defendant is entitled to require security for costs, as prescribed in subdivisions 1 to 4 of C.P.A. 1522, the plaintiff's attorney is liable for the defendant's costs to an amount not exceeding $100 until security is given. 

The plaintiff's attorney may relieve himself from that liability, although the defendant has not required security for costs to be given, by filing and procuring the allowance of an undertaking for costs as if an order had been made. 

2. Noticing the Case for Trial: A "notice of trial" and a "cross notice of trial" are no longer necessary. C.P.A. 433, as amended in 1936, provides that after a note of issue has been filed and copies thereof have been served, any party to the action may bring the issue to trial, and proceed with the

cause, in the absence of the adverse party, and take a dismissal of the complaint, or a verdict, decision or judgment as the case requires. See C.P.A. 494a.

A. The Note of Issue: C.P.A. 433 and R.C.P. 150, as last amended, now provide that at any time after issue is first joined or subsequent to forty days after service of a summons initiating the action has been completed irrespective of the joinder of issue, and at least twelve days before the commencement of any term, any party may file a note of issue with the clerk, stating the title of the action, the names and addresses of the attorneys, the time when the then last pleading was served, whether the action is triable by a jury or by the court without a jury, the term at which a trial of the action is desired, the specific nature and object of the action, and, if such cause be entitled to a preference under C.P.A. 138 or 

139, a demand therefor. The clerk thereupon enters the cause upon the calendar as of the date of the filing of the note of issue. The party filing the note of issue therewith files proof that a copy of the note of issue has been duly served personally or by mail (under R.C.P. 20) upon the attorney for each party who has appeared in the action. Such note of issue takes the place of the former notice of trial and no other or further notice of trial in the action is necessary. No new or further note of issue is necessary for any subsequent or adjourned term. A party who brings in a new party must within five days thereafter serve such new party or his attorney, if he has one, with a copy of the note of issue and must also, within the same five day period file a statement with the clerk advising him of the bringing in of such new party and of any change in the title of the action, together with proof of due service of the note of issue upon the new party, or his attorney and of the statement upon all parties who have appeared. The cause retains its place upon the calendar unless the court otherwise directs. If it appears that prejudice will result, no cause is to be tried if time to appear or plead or time to amend a pleading, as of course, or time to move with respect to the pleadings shall not have expired. The required note of issue fees must be paid upon filing.

THE TRIAL 

233 C.P.A. 164 provides that service of a copy of a note of issue may be made through the post office not less than fourteen days before the day of trial, including day of service. It was held under the former practice, that if an amended pleading is served as of right, after a case has been noticed for trial, the joinder of issue is destroyed, and it is necessary to serve a new notice of trial (Pelzer v. Perry, 196 N. Y. S. 342) but the court may direct that the cause retain its place on the calendar in spite of the amendment. Kelly v. Hilbert, 193 N. Y. S. 

263. The same rule would appear to apply with respect to the new note of issue.

B. Dismissal For Failure to Prosecute: Whenever an issue of fact in any action pending in any court has been first joined and (1) the plaintiff therein shall fail to serve and file a note of issue for a term within six months thereafter; or (2) if the plaintiff therein shall have failed to serve and file a note of issue at a time when later cases have been reached, in regular order, on the calendar of a term for which a note of issue could have been served and filed, the defendant, at any time after the expiration of either such event, may move for the dismissal of the complaint, with costs for the failure on the part of the plaintiff to diligently prosecute such action. If it be made to appear to the court on the hearing of such motion that the neglect of the plaintiff to bring the action to trial has not been unreasonable, the court may permit the plaintiff, on such terms as may be just, to bring the said action to trial at a future term; otherwise the complaint shall be dismissed. R.C.P. 156.

C. Calendar Practice: Most trial courts of record at the present time, especially in populous counties as in New York City, have woefully congested calendars, and suitors must perforce wait many months before a cause appears for trial. Each court has its own calendar rules, and it is the duty of an attorney to keep himself posted on the progress of the business in the courts in which he has cases pending. Mandel v. Donohue, 208 N. Y. S. 

807. Briefly, when a note of issue is filed in a pending action in the Supreme or City Courts, a calendar number is assigned to the case and it then becomes incum-

bent upon the parties to watch the progress of the court calendars, (in New York City, through use of the official New York Law Journal), until the cause is reached. It will first appear upon the reserve or day calendar of the court, so as to give the parties time to prepare for trial, before it appears upon the actual, "ready" calendar for trial. 

(1) When Case Appears for Trial: When the case is called for trial and both parties are present and ready, the trial proceeds in the regular way, to be discussed later. (a) If neither party appears, the case may be marked "off" the calendar; in which case, it may be restored on proper notice to restore or by motion. (b) If D appears and P does not, D may move to dismiss the complaint. C.P.A. 433, supra. Such a dismissal is not a judgment on the merits and the defaulting P may move to open such default on good cause shown (mistake, surprise or excusable neglect. C.P.A. 108). (c) If P appears and D does not, P may take default judgment. Similarly D may take default judgment on his counterclaim if P fails to appear. (See discussion of new C.P.A. 494a under default judgments in Chap. XIII.) 

3. Compelling Witnesses To Appear Or Papers To Be Pro duced.

A. Subpoena: 

(1) Contents: The subpoena is a judicial writ running in the name of the people of the state, attested in the name of any judge of the court in which the action is pending, addressed to the person whose attendance is required, and containing directions as to the time and place of the required attendance. In practice, the attorney draws the subpoena himself and writes in the name of the clerk of the court, although, in form, the subpoena is supposed to be issued out of the clerk's office, authenticated by the clerk's signature and the seal of the court.

THE TRIAL 

235 

(2) Service: The service of the subpoena must be made within the state; the original subpoena must be exhibited to the witness, a copy must be left with the witness, and the witness' legal fee and mileage fee must be paid or tendered to him. C.P.A. 404. (a) Fees C.P.A. 1539: A witness in an action or special proceeding in a court of record is entitled to the sum of fifty cents for each day's attendance; and, if he resides more than three miles from the place of attendance, he must be paid eight cents for each mile going to the place of attendance. Such fees are taxable disbursements. (1′) For the purposes of computing mileage, the witness' residence may be either temporary or permanent. Smith v. Hutton, 134 A. D. 445. (2') A witness residing out of the state is entitled to fees for the number of miles he travels from the boundary line within the state to the place of trial, the distance being estimated by the nearest usually travelled route. Hinds v. Schenectady 

Ins. Co., 7 How. Pr. R. 142. 

(3′) A physician subpoenaed as a witness, in the absence of a special agreement, has no claim for fees against attorneys for the party in whose behalf he is subpoenaed and is entitled only to the statutory fee. Potter v. Austin, 190 N. Y. S. 712. (4') In a personal injury action, the testimony of a physician, surgeon or registered nurse attached to a hospital, dispensary or other charitable institution must be taken before a referee appointed in the court in which the action is pending, with respect to information acquired while professionally attending a patient in said institutions, provided that any judge of such court, may, at any time, in his discretion, notwithstanding such deposition, order a subpoena to issue for said physician, surgeon or nurse to compel attendance at the trial of the action. In such a case a copy of the order must be served with the subpoena. C.P.A. 296a. 

(3) Effect of Disobedience: Only absolute necessity is excuse for disobedience of a valid subpoena regular ly served. People v. Davis, 15 Wend. 602. A de linquent witness who wilfully disobeys the subpoena

may be punished for contempt under a warrant of attachment against his person (Re Bradner, 87 N. 

Y. 171) or he may be subjected to a civil liability either for damages sustained by the party who subpoenaed him, or for $50 penalty. People ex rel 

Duffins v. Weir, 46 Hun. 320.

B. Subpoena Duces Tecum: Should a party desire a wit ness to attend a trial and "bring with him" a book, paper, record or document in his possession, a subpoena duces tecum is used, specifying the books and papers to be produced. 

(1) Restrictions on Use: The use of the subpoena duces tecum is restricted in certain cases. (a) Thus, certified transcripts of public records may be used in evidence in place of originals, and such records may not generally be removed from the place where they are kept without a court order specifying the reason why a certified copy cannot be used instead of the original. C.P.A. 410. If the record is with the Register of Kings, Queens, Bronx, or New York county, two days' notice of the application must be given. (b) Similarly, the records of a hospital or city department or bureau, may be produced on a subpoena duces tecum by transmitting a certified transcript of the material parts of such records. C.P.A. 412. (1) The removal of original books or papers of a library or a municipal corporation or public officer is likewise restricted. R.C.P. 162. (c) Public officers, or officers of a corporation, on a subpoena duces tecum may comply with the subpoena by sending the required documents to court with a subordinate who possesses the requisite knowledge to identify the record and testify on it, unless an ordinary subpoena is also served upon the particular officer, in which case he must personally attend. C.P.A. 414. (d) Books of account are to be produced only on special order of the court unless the subpoena duces tecum be served at least five days before the return date therein, in which case, the person subpoenaed is given an opportunity to apply to the court for an order vacating or modifying the subpoena. C.P.A. 411. 

C. Notice to Produce: Either party may serve upon the adverse party a written notice, at any reasonable time before trial, demanding the production, upon the trial, of a specified paper or document in the possession of the ad-

THE TRIAL 

237 verse party, and stating that upon the failure to produce such original, secondary evidence of its contents will be given. If the adverse party fails to comply with the notice, he loses his right to object to his opponent's evidence as not being the "best" evidence. However, if from the pleadings it is patent that a certain paper will be material on the trial of the issues, or if the paper is actually in the possession of the adverse party at the trial, a notice to produce, previously given, is not necessary to support the opponent's demand at the trial for the production of the original. Whelan v. Gorton, 37 N. Y. S. 

344. Note that the service of a subpoena duces tecum will not lay the foundation for the introduction of secondary evidence.

D. Order to Bring up Prisoner as Witness: The attendance of a prisoner who is desired to be used as a witness may be compelled only by a court order made by a court of record or a judge of the Supreme Court or by a county judge. If the prisoner has been sentenced for a felony, the order must be made by a Supreme Court justice on notice to the district attorney of the county where the prisoner was convicted. R.C.P. 163; C.P.A. 415, 416, 418. A prisoner sentenced to death cannot be brought into court by this method. 

4. Right To Jury Trial. 

A. Constitutional Right: "The trial by jury in all cases in which it has been heretofore used shall remain inviolate forever, but a jury trial may be waived by the parties in all civil cases in the manner prescribed by law." Art 1, Sec. 2 of New York Const. This constitutional provision preserves the common law institution of trial by jury in common law actions, but since trial by jury is not an element of equity jurisdiction and since the Supreme Court combines both legal and equitable jurisdiction, we find trial by jury on the law side of the court (Trial Term) and trial by the court alone on the equity side (Special Term). An issue of fact in the following actions must be tried by a jury unless such jury trial is waived or a reference directed: an action in which the complaint demands a money judgment only, an action of ejectment, for dower, for waste, for a nuisance or to

recover a chattel. C.P.A. 425. Whether an action is for legal or equitable relief is ordinarily determined from the complaint. But where the real issue in dispute is title to real property, the prayer for judgment in the equity complaint (e. g. for a mandatory injunction), is not conclusive on the court, and defendant is entitled to a jury trial as if the action were one in ejectment. City of Syracuse v. Hogan, 234 N. Y. 457. Issues of fact arising on a counterclaim are triable by jury whenever so triable in an action by defendant against plaintiff for the same relief. C.P.A. 424. Defendant waives this right where he notices the cause for trial at a Special Term. Mackellar v. Rogers, 109 N. Y. 468. (See also Maag v. Maag Gear 

Co., 184 N. Y. S. 630.) (1) If an action be equitable in character although the judgment demanded is for a sum of money only, there is no trial by jury as of right. Bell v. Merrifield, 109 N. Y. 202. The same applies where the main relief sought is equitable (to enjoin a nuisance) and incidental money damages is demanded concurrently. Miller v. Edison Elec. Illum. Co., 80 N. Y. S. 319. (2) So also where a party brings one action for both legal and equitable relief in respect to the same cause of action, he waives his right to jury. Cogswell v. N. Y., N. H. & H. 

R.R. Co., 105 N. Y. 319. But he cannot, by so doing, deprive defendant of his right to a jury trial. Di Menna v. Cooper, 220 N. Y. 391. (3) On the other hand, D by setting up an equitable defense to a legal cause of action cannot deprive P of his right to a jury trial. Hudson v. Caryl, 44 N. Y. 533. Similarly, in an equity action, if D interposes a legal counterclaim for a sum of money only he is entitled to a jury trial on the counterclaim. Di Menna v. Cooper, supra; Fout v. Wolfe, 245 N. Y. 

S. 505; contra, Manhattan Life Ins. Co. v. Hammerstein, 171 N. Y. S. 678. Cf. Mackelar v. Rogers, 109 N. Y. 468. (4) If P in an action to abate a nuisance shows only a right to abatement and damages, either party has a right to a jury trial; but if he adds facts entitling him to injunctive relief which he also demands, he waives his right to a jury trial although he cannot deprive D of a jury trial as to the law side of the action. Cogswell v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R.R. Co., supra; McNulty v. Mount Morris Light Co., 172 N. Y. 410.

B. Statutory Right in Other Cases: Before the adoption of the State Constitution, the right to a jury trial was provided by statute in certain cases, and upon the adoption of the Constitution such right became a constitutional

THE TRIAL 

239 one. These cases are, notably : 

(1) quo warranto actions (C.P.A. 1221) and actions to dissolve a corporation (Gen. Corp. Law, Sec. 94), (2) issue of adultery in divorce actions (C.P.A. 1149); 

Conderman v. Conderman, 44 Hun 181), (3) actions for annulment of marriage, unless based upon physical incapacity (C.P.A. 1142), (4) partition actions (C.P.A. 1023), (5) issues of fact in mandamus proceedings.

C. Discretionary Right: Even in equity, the court may direct, in its discretion, that certain issues be "framed" to be tried before a jury. C.P.A. 430. In such case, the procedure is only for the convenience of the court and the verdict of the jury is not binding upon the court but merely advisory. Di Menna v. Cooper, supra; Carroll v. Deimel, 95 N. Y. 252.

D. Waiving the Jury Trial (C.P.A. 426): A party may waive his right to a jury trial: (1) by failing to appear at the trial, (2) by filing a written waiver signed by the attorney for the party, (3) by an oral consent in open court entered in the minutes, (4) by moving the trial of the action, without a jury, or, if the adverse party so moves it, by failing to claim a jury trial before the production of any evidence upon the trial, (5) by failing to make written demand therefor, or failing to pay a jury fee where prescribed by a statute or rules of practice. 

Note: In the actions specified in C.P.A. 425 (supra) pending in the Supreme Court, in the counties of New York City, a jury trial is waived by failing to serve with the note of issue, a written notice demanding such trial by jury, and failing to file a copy of the said notice with the county clerk, (who is by law clerk of the Supreme Court), within three days after service thereof. If plaintiff fails to serve and file such notice, any other party may

within six days after the service of the note of issue on him, serve upon the attorneys for all other parties a notice demanding a jury trial and file a copy with the county clerk within three days thereafter. The party first demanding a jury trial must pay the fee therefor as prescribed by C.P.A. 1557-a. See also City Ct. Act, Sec. 16-a; Mun. Ct. Code, Secs. 118; 173. (As to effect of failure to serve a jury demand with the first notice of trial, see Goldstein v. Langenceux, 245 N. Y. 

S. 222.) (a) A provision in a contract that if an action at law is brought thereon, a jury be waived, is given effect. Waterside Holding Co. v. Lask, 25 N. Y. S. 183. (b) If a party desires to have an issue of fact framed for jury trial, in the discretion of the court, he must move therefor within twenty days after issue joined, else the right is waived. 

R.C.P. 157. (c) Where an action is ordered removed to another court, and is one triable by jury, unless a reference is directed, or jury trial waived, the issues of fact must be tried by a jury in the court into which the action is removed. C.P.A. 110a. 

5. Selecting The Jury. 

A. Generally: Ordinarily, the attorney has nothing to do with the enrolling and drafting of citizens (of either sex) for jury service. The procedure is statutory, is prescribed by the Judiciary Law and the Penal Law, and is carried out by special officers, clerks, sheriffs or commissioners authorized to do so.

B. Special or Struck Jury: Where a special jury of more than ordinary intelligence and ability was required in the Supreme Court, C.P.A. 449 formerly provided for a "struck" jury by order of the court made on notice. The section was repealed as of September 1, 1937.

C. Impanelling the Jury: At every Trial Term, there are jurors summoned by "jury notices" to serve as jurors for the term. When a case is called for trial, ballots containing the names of all the jurors present are placed by the clerk in a wheel or box and the clerk then draws

THE TRIAL 

241 the ballots one at a time. "The first twelve persons who appear as their names are drawn and called and who are approved as indifferent between the parties and not discharged or excused, must be sworn, and constitute the jury to try the issue." C.P.A. 448. C.P.A. 449-a, now provides for one or two additional, alternate jurors in protracted civil trials in the discretion of the trial justice. These alternate jurors are drawn from the same source as the regular jurors and have the same qualifications. They are examined and sworn, like the regular jurors, and listen to the evidence along with the latter, but upon final submission to the regular jury they are discharged. If before such submission, however, an original juror becomes incapacitated the alternate replaces him in the jury box. (2) Examining and Challenging the Jury: It is customary, in the ordinary civil case, for counsel in examining the proposed jurors to inquire whether any of them are acquainted with the facts of the case or with any of the parties or their attorneys. However, the right to challenge exists in other cases and may be exercised where further examination of the jurors reveals grounds therefor. (a) Challenge to the Array: This is a seldom used challenge and is directed to the entire body of jurors in attendance upon the grounds of some irregularity in drawing the panel. C.P.A. 453 subd. 2. Only the court may decide. C.P.A. 450. (b) Challenge to the Polls: This challenge is directed to the individual juror and may be peremptory or for cause. 

(1') Peremptory: 

A peremptory challenge is one without statement of any ground or reason. Each party in a civil action has the right to six peremptory challenges in a court of record and to three in a court not of record. C.P.A. 451. But "each party" does not have to be each litigant; nonantagonistic defendants having a substantially common defense are treated as one party and permitted only six peremptory

challenges between them. Downey V. 

Finucane, 205 N. Y. 251. 

(2') For Cause or Favor: A challenge to the polls may be made for cause or favor. A challenge for principal cause may be made for relationship within the sixth degree of consanguinity or affinity (C.P.A. 455), for business association of a juror with a party or counsel, or where the juror has formed or expressed an opinion upon the controversy to be tried. Such a challenge raises a question of law for the court to decide as to the competency of the juror to act and the court has no discretion. Hildreth v. 

City of Troy, 101 N. Y. 324. A challenge for favor is made upon any circumstance revealed upon examination tending to show, though not conclusively, that the juror is prejudiced or biased in favor of a party. 

Such a challenge raises a question of fact for the court to decide in its discretion. 

Butler v. Glens Falls, 121 N. Y. 112. 

(a') Grounds for Challenge for Favor: That a juror is in the employ of a party to the action; or, if a party to the action is a corporation, that he is an employee thereof or a shareholder or a stockholder therein; or, in actions for damages for injuries to person or property, that he is a shareholder, stockholder, director, officer or employee, or in any manner interested, in any insurance company issuing policies for protection against liability for damages for injury to person or property, are good grounds for a challenge to the favor as to such juror. C.P.A. 452. (b') But that a juror is a taxpayer in a city or town or county which is a party to the action is not a good cause of challenge for cause or favor. C.P.A. 454. 1

THE TRIAL 

243 (c') In conducting the examination of the jurors, challenges should first be made for cause or favor, and if these are overruled the juror may nevertheless be excluded by peremptory challenge. In conducting the examination, the attorney may not convey to the jury, by innuendo or intimation, information not admissible in evidence. Thus, while a stockholder or employee in a liability insurance company in actions for injury to person or property may be challenged for favor (C.P.A. 452, supra), it is reversible error to so question the jury as to unnecessarily disclose that the defendant in the action is insured or insured in any designated company. 

O'Brien v. Houcken, 72 A. D. 142; Butler v. Glens Falls Co., supra. 

6. The Trial Proper. 

A. Motions at Beginning of Trial: After the jury has been accepted and sworn in, certain motions may be made on the pleadings. (1) Defendant may move for judgment dismissing the complaint upon the ground that it does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action or that the court has no jurisdiction of the subject matter of the action. He may also move to strike out a defense to a counterclaim as insufficient in law. 

C.P.A. 278, 279. (2) Defendant may move to compel plaintiff to elect between two inconsistent causes of action in the complaint but the court may properly reserve decision on this motion until the evidence is in. Tuthill v. 

Skidmore, 124 N. Y. 148. (3) Plaintiff may move for judgment in his favor where the answer on its face states no defense to plaintiff's claim or to some part of it. R.C.P. 112, 113, 114. (4) Either party may move to correct or amend defects in title or form of papers that have not been waived

by failure to take them promptly by motion. C.P.A. 

105, 192; R.C.P. 102. 

B. Opening to the Jury. 

(1) Right to Open and Close: The party moving the cause for trial should furnish the court with a copy of the pleadings so marked as to clearly indicate which allegations are admitted and which denied. 

To impart similar information and the substance of the evidence to be adduced to the jury, the party holding the affirmative of the first material issue of fact has the right to make the opening address to the jury. R.C.P. 160, 161. Usually, this party is the plaintiff, but where the defendant's answer admits all the allegations in the complaint necessary to be established to make out a cause of action and sets up an affirmative defense only, the defendant has the affirmative of the issue and the corresponding right to open and close. Murray v. N. Y. Life Ins. 

Co., 85 N. Y. 236. After the party holding the affirmative has opened to the jury, the other side follows with its opening address before the introduction of any evidence. R.C.P. 161. Either party or both may waive the opening. 

(2) Motion to Dismiss after Plaintiff's Opening: It sometimes happens that although the complaint is sufficient, plaintiff's attorney, in his opening address, may admit or assert some fact tending to destroy plaintiff's cause of action. If such opening address is made part of the trial record by being taken down by the official stenographer, the defendant may move for a dismissal thereon. This rather sharp practice is not in very high esteem, as sometimes counsel may make an unguarded admission; "but when the facts lie within a narrow compass, sharply defined, and not giving rise to conflicting inferences, there is no reason why the court in absence of protest by counsel, may not dispose of the case upon the assumption that the facts stated will be proved and thus avoid loss of time in hearing the evidence." 

Garrison v. McCullough, 51 N. Y. S. 128.

C. Plaintiff Puts in His Case: Plaintiff produces his wit-

THE TRIAL 

245 nesses who testify and are cross-examined. He also puts in evidence his documentary proof. Objections to competency, relevancy and materiality and the rulings thereon come under the scope of the subject of evidence, with which we are not here concerned. C.P.A. 445, as amended in 1936, currently provides that where an issue of fact is tried by a referee or by a court without a jury an exception shall be deemed to have been taken by the party adversely affected to the granting or refusal of every request to find, and to every ruling upon a question of law made after the cause is finally submitted. 

In any other case, a formal exception, reduced to writing or entered in the minutes, must be taken at the time when the ruling is made, unless it is taken to the charge given to the jury, in which case it must be taken before the jury have rendered their verdict. C.P.A. 446, as amended in 1936. But the appellate court in the interests of justice may review a prejudicial ruling made by the trial court during the trial, and not acquiesced in affirmatively by the party aggrieved, even though no formal exception was taken, provided the ruling was pointed out at the trial by objection or was a refusal or failure by the court to grant a motion or request by such party. C.P.A. 583 subd. 2, as amended in 1936. (Effective September 1, 1938, C.P.A. 445 will also provide for an automatic exception in favor of an aggrieved party as to every ruling either before or after the cause is finally submitted, where such party, at the time when the ruling is sought or made, makes known to the court, judge or referee his position thereon by objection or otherwise. On the other hand, a new C.P.A. 446 as of the same date, will require a formal exception, noted before the rendition of the verdict, as to any part of a charge or to any refusal to charge. See Fourth Annual 

Report of Judicial Council, p. 56.) Generally, if improper testimony gets into the record without prompt objection being made, the aggrieved party may not subsequently move to strike it out. After plaintiff has put in all his evidence, he rests his case.

D. Motions at Close of Plaintiff's Case: 

(1) Motion to Dismiss (Non-Suit): After the plaintiff

has rested, defendant may move for a dismissal of the complaint upon the ground that the plaintiff has failed to prima facie establish, by evidence, a cause of action against the defendant. Such a motion must indicate specific defects or failure of proof, except where the objection to plaintiff's recovery could not have been obviated even if the defect had been specifically pointed out. Gerding v. Hasking, 141 

N. Y. 514. Mere variance of proof from the pleadings, as distinguished from failure of proof, may be cured by amendment. C.P.A. 434; R.C.P. 166. 

(a) Effect of Motion Being Granted: A judgment dismissing the complaint before the close of plaintiff's evidence is not a bar to another action for the same cause, unless it is expressly stated to be on the merits. But a dismissal of the complaint after plaintiff's evidence is in, or at the close of the whole case, is final, unless it is expressly stated that the judgment of dismissal is without prejudice. C.P.A. 482; Ziegler v. International Ry. Co., 248 N. Y. S. 375 (overruling Caruso v. Metropolitan, 212 N. Y. S. 

199). Where the judgment of dismissal has been erroneously entered on the merits, plaintiff's remedy is to either move to amend it so as to recite that the dismissal was without prejudice, or to appeal from the judgment; but he cannot attack the judgment collaterally in a second action for the same relief. Hollenbeck v. Aetna Cas. & Sur. Co., 214 N. Y. S. 402.

E. Motions at Close of Whole Case: 

(1) Renewal of Motion to Dismiss by Defendant: Fail ure of defendant to move for a nonsuit or a directed verdict in his favor at the end of the whole case, constitutes a waiver of his motion to dismiss at the close of plaintiff's case, since such a failure to move is deemed an admission that plaintiff is entitled to go to the jury on all the evidence. Thomas v. Mass. 

Elec. Co., 172 N. Y. S. 817. (a) The right to move for a non-suit does not exist after verdict (Bail v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R.R. Co., 201 N. Y.

THE TRIAL 

247 355), but if the motion is made before verdict, the court may reserve decision until after verdict of the jury as to the amount of damages or as to any special questions arising upon the pleadings (C.P.A. 459) and may dismiss the plaintiff's complaint even after a jury verdict in his favor, but such dismissal may not be on the merits. 

Blythe v. J. Quincy & Co., 133 N. Y. S. 602; Fitzgerald v. Colt-Stewart, 246 N. Y. S. 535. 

(2) Motions for Directed Verdict: 

(a) Theory of Motion: It is the duty of the court to apply the law to the undisputed facts. It is the duty of the jury to decide the disputed facts. But if there is no substantial conflict in the evidence adduced, there is nothing for the jury to decide and it is incumbent upon the judge to declare the law applicable to the facts established and to direct the jury to find accordingly. 

(b) By Whom Made: Either party may make the motion or both. If at the close of all the evidence plaintiff thinks that upon the undisputed facts he has made out his cause of action, and that the defendant has failed to establish a sufficient defense, he moves for a directed verdict in his favor; if the defendant believes that upon the whole case the plaintiff is not entitled to recover, he may move in the same manner for a directed verdict in his favor. In either case, if the motion is denied, the moving party must then affirmatively move to go to the jury if he wishes to review the decision of the court. 

Niagara Co. v. Eagle Co., 242 N. Y. S. 273, aff'd 

254 N. Y. 626. 

(1') Both Parties Join in Motion: Where both sides make a motion for a directed verdict, it amounts to a virtual agreement that there are no questions of fact for the jury. Thompson v. Simpson, 128 N. Y. 270. In such case, where both parties join in the motion, the court must explicitly deny one, so that the party whose motion is denied may move to go to the jury on all the evidence.

O'Brien v. Tilden, 240 N. Y. S. 355; Happel v. Lehigh Valley R. R. Co., 206 N. Y. S. 

726. However, it is not necessary to state the specific questions that the party desires submitted to the jury unless the court directs such statement. Brown Paint Co. v. 

Reinhardt, 210 N. Y. 162. 

(c) Test Applicable: The test of the right to direct a verdict is whether the court would be bound to set aside a verdict as against the evidence if rendered against the party in whose favor it was directed. C.P.A. 457-a. Cf. McDonald v. 

Metropolitan St. Ry. Co., 167 N. Y. 66; Bank of 

U. S. v. Mannheim, 264 N. Y. 45. As to whether a fact is established by evidence so conclusively that a court and jury may not find against it, may be determined, in part, by the rule laid down in Kavanagh v. Wilson, 70 N. Y. 177: "it is undoubtedly a general rule that when a disinterested witness, who is in no way discredited, testifies to a fact within his own knowledge, which is not of itself improbable, the witness is to be believed and the fact is to be taken as legally established so that it cannot be disregarded by court or jury." See also Hull v. 

Littauer, 182 N. Y. 569; Dwight v. Germania, 103 N. Y. 341.

F. Amendments on Trial-Withdrawal of Juror: 

(1) Amendments: During the trial, the court, in its discretion, may allow such corrections or additions to be made in the pleadings of the parties as might have been allowed at Special Term. It may also allow similar corrections or additions in the proofs of the parties. See C.P.A. 105, 434; R.C.P. 166. Such discretion will not be overruled on appeal unless flagrantly abused. Feizi v. Second Russian Ins. Co., 192 N. Y. S. 118; Goldstein v. Schleifer, 205 N. Y. S. 399. (a) Thus, where an action is brought against several Ds jointly, and one of them admits his liability on the trial individually, the action may be severed and judgment

THE TRIAL 

249 rendered against him individually. Snedeker v. Bernard, 102 N. Y. 327. (b) When some ground of equitable jurisdiction is alleged in a complaint but fails of proof in its entire scope on the trial, and it appears that there never was any substantial ground for equitable relief, the court will not retain the action and grant purely legal relief but will dismiss the complaint. Poth v. Wash. Sq. Church, 201 

N. Y. S. 776. See C.P.A. 111. (c) An amendment on the trial substantially changing the claim made in the complaint is improper, especially where the amendment is permitted at the close of all the evidence to D's surprise and in abrogation of his right to meet by proof the new allegations. Walrath v. Hanover Fire Ins. Co., 216 N. Y. 220. (d) In Berkshire Cotton Mfg. Co. v. Cohen, 236 N. Y. 364, the complaint alleged the sale and delivery of goods. 

On the trial P was properly permitted to prove delivery by appropriation under the Sales Act, since D could have asked for a bill of particulars of the times and manner of the alleged delivery and thus prepared to meet P's claim. (e) In Gordon v. Anderson, 200 A. D. 616, P alleged a written agreement of employment, but on the trial was permitted to prove an oral agreement. This was held prejudicial error, since the proof did not conform to the pleadings. (f) In Finch v. Foster Co., 233 N. Y. 619, P alleged an agreement made on May 19, but on the trial was permitted to prove that the agreement was made on May 7. This was held proper since it made no substantial change in the cause of action, and corrected merely an immaterial variance. (g) In Neverfail Lighter Co., Inc. v. Blum, 201 A. D. 153, it was held that where a complaint alleges general damages only, it is error to permit recovery of special damages. (2) Withdrawal of Juror: During the trial, some contingency may arise whereby the attorney for one of the parties finds his proofs in such bad shape that he would like to discontinue the trial in order to avoid defeat. In such case, or, where some other grounds for declaring a mistrial exist, the court in its discretion, and on motion of either party, or upon its own motion may withdraw a juror and thus end the trial, necessitating a new one, de novo, at some

later day (Note that after final submission to the jury the right to submit to a voluntary non-suit is lost. C.P.A. 457.) (a) Where counsel in opening and summing up to the jury utterly disregards the rights of the adverse party and makes remarks not within the issues and of a highly prejudicial and inflammatory character, there are sufficient grounds to declare a mistrial. Cherry Creek Nat'l 

Bank v. Fid. & Cas. Co. of N. Y., 202 N. Y. S. 611. 

G. Summation: Should the case be sent to the jury, one counsel for each side has the right to make an address to the jury, limited to one hour, unless the court otherwise directs. R.C.P. 161. The trial judge must preside over the summation to rule on objections; the official stenographer must also be present to take down the record. Ciccolini v. Vocafilm Corp., 236 N. Y. S. 577. (1) Scope: Attorneys may use in summation, argument, appeal or comparison. But they should confine themselves to the facts in evidence and comment upon them and not upon the law, which is properly for the court. Neither should they avail themselves of prejudicial remarks tending to impassion or bias the jury. Williams v. Brooklyn El. R. Co., 126 N. Y. 

96; Cherry Creek Nat'l Bank v. Fid. & Cas. Co., supra. (a) Where the summation of counsel passes beyond the bounds of legal propriety, it is the duty of counsel for the adversary to object specifically and point out the language deemed objectionable, requesting the court to rule thereon. Dimon v. N. Y. Central & Hart. R.R. Co., 173 N. Y. 356.

H. Charges to Jury: After the attorneys for the parties have completed their summations, the judge charges the jury. The judge's charge must apply the pertinent law to the facts as the jury may find them. Haefeli v. Woodrich Co., 255 N. Y. 442. The court should not encroach upon the domain of the jury by passing upon the facts. 

An opinion or commentary upon questions of fact by the court is error where it is not accompanied with explicit instructions to the jury that it is their duty to consider the evidence and decide as to whether the truth is this or that. Whether or not the illegal comment by the

THE TRIAL 

251 trial court justice influenced the jury is immaterial, since, in reversing, the appellate court will not speculate whether such error did or did not affect the result. Allis v. Leonard, 58 N. Y. 288. Once the jurors have retired for deliberation, the court cannot further communicate with them in the absence of counsel. (1) Requests to Charge: Counsel have the right to submit propositions of law bearing upon the matters in dispute and it is the duty of the court to instruct the jury as to each proposition. These requests may be oral or written and may be made after the court's charge but before the retirement of the jury. (a) Thus, where after the court charged the jurors and they rose from their seats and were about to retire, and counsel for one party requested that they wait a moment, and the trial court justice stated that he would not add to his charge and directed the jury to go on, and counsel then stated that he had a request to make for a further charge, the refusal of the trial court judge to listen to the request sought to be offered was held to constitute reversible error. Chapman v. McCormick, 86 N. Y. S. 479. (b) But where the trial judge fully instructed the jury on the law of the case and counsel offered several requests and stated that those were all the charges they wished, the refusal of the judge to receive any more requests to charge was held not to be an abuse of discretion. 

O'Neill v. Dry Dock Co., 129 N. Y. 125. (2) Exceptions to Charge: An exception to a charge must be taken at any time before the jury renders a verdict. C.P.A. 446; Hunt v. Becker, 173 A. D. 9.

The exception may be taken to any part of the charge deemed erroneous or prejudicial, picking out and explicitly stating the specific part of the charge deemed objectionable. (For the necessity of making objections in a succinct, audible manner, see Utica 

Bank v. Nickel, 219 N. Y. S. 556.) Likewise, an exception must be taken and noted to the granting of the adversary's requests to charge. C.P.A. 446.

I. Verdict: After deliberation, the jury returns its verdict, which may be general or special. (1) General Verdict: A general verdict is one in which the jury finds for the plaintiff, assessing his dam-

ages, or, for the defendant. C.P.A. 458. (2) Special Verdict: A special verdict is one in which the jury reports its decision on specific questions of fact submitted to it by the court; as where, pending the decision of a motion for a non-suit or a directed verdict, the court submits a question of fact raised by the pleadings to the jury and then upon such special verdict of the jury directs a general verdict for either party who is entitled to such verdict. 

Stevenson v. Hartman, 184 N. Y. 634; C.P.A. 458-9.

Where a special and general verdict are inconsistent, the former controls. C.P.A. 459. (a) The decision of a jury on an issue of adultery in a divorce action is not a special verdict, but is in the nature of a finding on a framed issue. Anderson v. 

Anderson, 170 N. Y. S. 612. (3) Sealed Verdict: Sometimes a case goes to the jury in late afternoon, in which case the court may direct a sealed verdict. The judge and parties may then go home, and if the jurors agree upon a verdict they state it in writing, sign it, enclose it in an envelope, and deliver it to the clerk. On the following day, at the opening of court, the clerk opens the envelope and reads and records the verdict. (4) Polling the Jury: When a verdict is announced, either party may have the jury polled; that is, have the clerk call the roll and ask each juror as called- "Is that your verdict?" (5) "Quotient" Verdict: One of the commonest mistakes lay jurors make in arriving at a verdict is to arbitrarily fix the amount of recovery at the average of the amounts deemed proper by the individual jurors. Such a verdict is improper and may be set aside. However, where the jurors compromise on the damages at a sum all are willing to approve, the verdict is proper. Thomas v. Dickinson, 12 N. Y. 364. 

(6) Unanimous Verdicts Not Required: C.P.A. 463a, added in September, 1937, now provides: "A verdict may be rendered by not less than five-sixths of the jurors constituting a jury in any civil case and no

THE TRIAL 

253 jury shall be discharged by any court because of its inability to agree upon a verdict unless the court is satisfied that agreement among such proportion of jurors is impossible of attainment. This section shall apply to every civil case tried by a court and jury in any court of the state, whether a court of record or not of record, and whether the provisions of the Civil Practice Act are or are not applicable to such court or to trials by jury held therein." 

(7) Disagreement By Jury: C.P.A. 463 as amended in 

September, 1937, now provides: "Where five-sixths of the jurors constituting a jury empaneled to try one or more issues of fact, to make an inquiry or assess damages, cannot agree after being kept together for such a time as is deemed reasonable by the court before which, or the officer before whom, they were empaneled, the court or officer may discharge them and may by precept or order, as the case requires, direct that another jury be drawn or may direct retrial before a jury regularly drawn for another term. If the jury disagreement related to one or more but not to all of the issues submitted to the jury, the court or officer may, by order, direct the retrial only of the issues as to which the jury disagreed, or may, in its discretion, direct the retrial of such issues and of any other issue as to which the jury rendered a verdict either by direction or otherwise. Such order may direct the entry and record of the verdict of the jury on such issues as are not ordered resubmitted to another jury. Such order and the proceedings on which it is based must be incorporated in and shall form a part of the record upon which the final judgment, order or decree is entered which determines all the issues in the action or proceeding. Such order shall be deemed to affect a substantial right and may be reviewed on a separate appeal therefrom or on appeal from the final judgment, order or decree. On the retrial of the issues resubmitted to another jury, the same proceedings must be had in respect of such issues as if such new jury were the jury first empaneled to

try such issues." The amendment conforms C.P.A. 

463 with the new C.P.A. 463a, supra, and also changes the rule in Matter of MacKenzie, 272 N. Y. 

403, requiring a retrial of all issues, including those on which the first jury agreed, in the second trial. 

7. Trial By Judge Without Jury. 

A. Generally: Trial by the court alone is had where the case is not triable by jury, or where the right to a jury has been waived, and a reference has not been directed. 

C.P.A. 428.

B. Trial of Issues of Fact by Judge without Jury: Although there is no jury, the case is opened and summed up and witnesses examined in the same way as before a jury, but there is more laxity in admission of evidence, since a judge is less likely to be influenced by irrelevant evidence than a jury. There is no motion for a directed verdict, no requests to charge, no charge or exceptions thereto. 

However, motions for judgment and for amendments may be made in the same way, at the same time, and subject to the same rules as in jury trials. 

C. Form of Decisions: Where the court alone is the trier of the facts, there is no verdict. C.P.A. 439, as amended in 1936, provides: "Upon a trial by the court without a jury, each party shall be afforded reasonable opportunity, before the cause is finally submitted to the court, to submit requests to find the essential facts which he deems established by the evidence. Each request must be numbered and so prepared with respect to its length and the subject and phraseology thereof that the court may conveniently pass upon it. If the court fails to grant a request either specifically or by embodying it in the decision it shall be deemed to have been refused." 

Each party is deemed to have made a motion for judgment in his favor. The decision of the court is oral or in writing and, except where a non-suit is granted, states the essential facts found. So far as practicable the court by its decision must grant the proper motion for judgment. In an action where costs are discretionary, the court, in its decision, must award or deny same. The decision is part of the record. C.P.A. 440 as amended in

THE TRIAL 

255 1936. (C.P.A. 440a, added in 1934, and giving the court, in a law action where a jury trial has been waived, the right to make a decision in the same form as a general jury verdict was repealed in September, 1936.) On appeal, C.P.A. 584, as amended in 1936, authorizes the appellate court, unless it affirms the judgment, so far as practicable, to grant the motion for judgment which the court below ought to have granted.

D. Time for Rendering Decision: The decision of the court, in a case tried without a jury must be rendered within sixty days after the final adjournment of the term where the issue was tried. If it is not so rendered, either party may move at Special Term for a new trial upon that ground. If the decision has not been rendered when the motion is heard, the court must make an order for a new trial, either absolutely or unless it is rendered within a time specified in such order. C.P.A. 442 as amended in 1936. 

8. References. 

A. Referee to Hear and Determine: (1) On Consent: The parties to any action may have such person as they agree upon, by written stipulation, appointed by order of the court to try the case; or, they may agree to have the court designate the referee. C.P.A. 464. (a) Exceptions: In the following three classes of cases, the court in its discretion may refuse a reference even though the parties consent (C. 

P.A. 465): (1') Matrimonial actions (divorce, separation, annulment) Schapley v. Schapley, 205 N. Y.

S. 577, (2') Where there is an infant defendant, (3) Actions to liquidate a corporation (unless brought by the attorney-general). 

(2) Compulsory Reference: An action may be ordered referred, regardless of consent, where the trial will require the examination of a long account, but not the decision of difficult questions of law. Spence v. 

Semis, 137 N. Y. 616. If the action be at law, and

thus upon general principles triable by a jury, it may not be referred without consent, unless the action be on a contract in which a long account is involved. 

C.P.A. 466; Assets Realization Co. v. Lockwood, 245 

N. Y. 520. Since such actions were referable before the adoption of the Constitution in 1846, C.P.A. 466 is constitutional and does not deprive the parties of their absolute rights to a jury trial.

B. Incidental References: C.P.A. 467 provides that the court, of its own motion, or upon the application of either party, without the consent of the other, likewise may direct a reference to take an account and report to the court thereon, either with or without the testimony, after interlocutory or final judgment, or where it is necessary to do so for the information of the court; and also to determine and report upon a question of fact arising in any stage of the action, upon a motion or otherwise, except upon pleadings. (1) Under this section, a reference is proper to determine whether a foreign corporation defendant is doing business in New 

York, on a motion to dismiss for want of jurisdiction. Davis v. Julius Kessler & Co., Inc., 194 N. Y. S. 9. See also 

R.C.P. 108. (2) Similarly, the court may, in a separation case, after judgment for the plaintiff, appoint a referee for the purpose of determining the amount of alimony, although this practice is not to be extended. Le Page v. Le Page, 204 N. Y. S. 260. 

C. Form of Referee's Decision: The referee makes his decision in the form of a "report" to the court. C.P.A. 471, as amended in 1936, provides: "Before the cause is finally submitted to the referee the attorney for either party may submit in writing a statement of the essential facts which he deems established by the evidence and of the rulings upon questions of law which he desires the referee to make. The statement must be in the form of distinct propositions of law or of fact, or both, separately stated; each of which must be numbered and so prepared with respect to its length and the subject and phraseology thereof that the referee may conveniently pass upon it. 

At or before the time when the report is rendered, the referee must note in the margin of the statement the manner in which each proposition has been disposed of,

THE TRIAL 

257 and must file the statement thus noted; but an omission so to do does not affect the validity of his report." 

9. Motions for New Trial. 

A. On Record (C.P.A. 549): The judge presiding at a jury trial may grant a motion, made upon his minutes and at the same term, to set aside the verdict, or a direction dismissing the complaint, and grant a new trial, upon the exceptions taken at the trial; or, because the verdict is excessive, insufficient, or otherwise contrary to the evidence, or contrary to law. C.P.A. 549. The stenographer's minutes may be written out and used as the judge's minutes. R.C.P. 223. Hanburg v. Metropolitan 

Sec. Co., 213 N. Y. S. 555. C.P.A. 550 provides that upon the application of a party who has taken one or more exceptions, the judge presiding at a jury trial, in his discretion, at any time during the same term, may order that the exceptions so taken be heard in the first instance by the court to which an appeal could be taken from a final judgment in the action, and that judgment be suspended in the meantime. C.P.A. 551 provides that where an interlocutory judgment has been entered and further proceedings are to be taken before entry of final judgment, a motion for a new trial upon one or more exceptions may be made at a term of the court to which an appeal could be taken from a final judgment in the action, after the entry of the interlocutory judgment and before the commencement of the hearing directed therein. 

B. Outside Record: Motions for a new trial may also be made by the unsuccessful party on grounds not disclosed by the record. Such motions are not made before the trial court but at Special Term, and must be made upon a "case" if made on the ground of newly discovered evidence. C.P.A. 552; Weaver v. Scripture, 211 N. Y. S. 

593. These grounds are: (1) Surprise: Where something has occurred in the course of a trial which the moving party had no reason to expect, and which, through no fault on his part, he could not reasonably anticipate, or, with due diligence, prepare to meet, and which resulted in an injustice to him, he may move at Special Term for a new trial on the ground of surprise.

(a) The surprise to sustain such motion must relate to a matter of fact and not to a question of law. Helwig v 

Second Ave. R. R. Co., 9 Misc. 61, where a witness became insane during the trial and gave unexpected damaging testimony, HELD motion for new trial should be granted. (b) A party who is surprised by evidence which he is not prepared to rebut should not proceed without intimating his surprise. If he does, he cannot thereafter move, under C.P.A. 552, to set aside an unfavorable verdict on the ground of surprise. Weaver v. Scripture, supra. (c) What he should do in such contingency, in order to be entitled to a new trial on the ground of surprise, is to move for an adjournment or for a mistrial. Suken v. 

Metcalfe, 205 N. Y. S. 360. 

(2) Irregularity: Misconduct by, or disqualification of, a juror, or tampering with the jury by outsiders, afford ground for a new trial when properly established. (a) The jurors' affidavits may be used to show misconduct by outsiders or to sustain the verdict as not being influenced, but their affidavits cannot be used to show misconduct or mistake on the part of any juror tending to impeach the verdict. In re Brasher, 113 Misc. 48. 

See also Miller v. Gerard, 192 N. Y. S. 884; Dennis v. 

Tishman, 253 N. Y. S. 482. 

(3) Newly Discovered Evidence: Where an unsuccess ful party has discovered new evidence in his favor since the trial, the court, in its discretion, may grant a new trial. (a) Requisites: The unsuccessful party in moving for such a new trial must show in his moving affidavits what the evidence is; that it was discovered since the trial (Collins v. Central Trust 

Co., 238 N. Y. S. 226), that it could not have been discovered before the trial with reasonable diligence (Rockowitz Corset Co. v. Madam X 

Co., Inc., 225 N. Y. S. 360), that it is not merely cumulative or impeaching (Collins v. Central 

Trust Co., supra), but that if produced at the trial a different result would likely have oc curred. New Amsterdam Casualty Co. v. Beards ley, 205 N. Y. S. 771.

THE TRIAL 

259 (1) In an action to recover damages for personal injuries, newly discovered evidence that P after the accident stated in an application by her for insurance that she was in good physical condition, had sustained no injury and had not been confined to bed by illness, warrants a new trial. Nevins v. 

Tiffen Products, 190 N. Y. S. 746.

CHAPTER XIII. 

JUDGMENTS: SPECIAL PROCEEDINGS 1. Classification Of Judgments: Judgments may be by default or after trial, in personam or in rem, declaratory, or coercive, interlocutory or final. A final judgment is one which finally and completely determines the rights of the parties in the action. An interlocutory judgment is an intermediate judgment which settles the rights of the parties but leaves something to be done, as where there is an accounting or a reference to be had. C.P.A. 472; R.C.P. 187-8. A decree foreclosing a mortgage, fixing the amount due and the liability of the defendant, and ordering a sale of the premises and the application of the proceeds is a final decree, although further directions may be necessary with reference to its execution, such as entry of a deficiency judgment. Morris v. 

Morange, 38 N. Y. 172. 

2. Judgment By Default. 

A. Entering Default Judgment by Clerk: If the defendant defaults in an action on a contract, express or implied, where the sum recoverable is liquidated or capable of being ascertained by computation, and where the summons and complaint were personally served on defendant, or the summons was served with a notice under 

R.C.P. 46, or where defendant appeared in the action but defaulted in pleading, default judgment may be taken without application to the court. In every other case, legal or equitable, application to the court and proof of damages must be had. C.P.A. 485 to 487. Where a complaint joins two causes of action, one in contract and the other in tort, arising out of the same transaction, application for default judgment must be made to the court. 

Bouker v. Neale, 146 N. Y. S. 894. 

(1) Papers Necessary: 

(a) A verified complaint, (where the complaint is unverified the clerk may take proof under oath to ascertain the amount due, C.P.A. 487), (b) Summons and complaint, (c) Affidavit of regularity showing default,

(d) Proposed judgment to be signed by the clerk, (R.C.P. 185). 

(2) Effect of One of Several Defendants Defaulting: In an action at law, only one judgment may be entered, C.P.A. 475. Thus where plaintiff is in a position to take a default judgment against one defendant but not against all, he should be allowed to sever the action, enter judgment against one and proceed against the other. Kriser v. Rodgers, 186 N. Y. S. 

316. If defendants are sued on a joint obligation and one defaults while the other defends, there can be no severance. Plaintiff must wait until a trial of the issue results in a judgment in his favor. This procedure by order of severance applies equally to the case where the default judgment can be had only upon application to the court. 

B. Upon Application to the Court: Notice of application to the court for default judgment need not be given to the defendant unless he has appeared generally, or has served a special demand for notice under R.C.P. 190. Upon the application, the plaintiff presents his summons and complaint with proof of service and affidavit of regularity showing default, and the court then takes proof of the plaintiff's damages. Evidence upon this point may be taken in court, either with or without a jury, or by means of a reference or a writ of inquiry. This procedure now applies to actions to recover damages for injury to person or property. C.P.A. 490. The former rule required, in such cases, the issuance of a writ of inquiry or the use of a jury in court. Elsey v. Int. Rrd. Co., 87 N. Y. S. 

28. (But see R.C.P. 192, subd. 3.) (1) Writ of Inquiry: A writ of inquiry is a judicial writ, directed to the sheriff of the county in which the action is triable, and commanding that officer to inquire into the damages sustained by plaintiff, with the aid of the sheriff's jury, and return the writ to the court. The sheriff's jury is made up from a specially selected list of jurors who serve for a year. 

Judiciary Law, Sec. 605-609. 

(a) How Executed:

Upon the hearing before the sheriff's

jury, the sheriff presides, and witnesses are examined as upon a trial, on the one question of damages. Upon this one question, defendant who has appeared or who has filed a special demand under R.C.P. 190, may crossexamine plaintiff's witnesses and produce testimony in his own behalf. The finding of the jury is put in certificate form and on it judgment may be entered before the clerk or after further application to the court, depending upon the direction of the court in that respect. 

C.P.A. 490. The writ of inquiry is seldom used. 

(2) In Equity Cases: Application to the court for the entry of default judgment must be made. It is no longer necessary that findings of fact and conclusions of law be drawn for the entry of such default judgment, except where the default is in a matrimonial case (R.C.P. 191), in which latter case, also, full proof in open court must be made before default judgment can be entered. C.P.A. 1174-6; 

R.C.P. 282-3. 

(3) Default Judgment After Non-Personal Service (C. 

P.A. 493; R.C.P. 192): Where a summons was served upon a defendant without the state, or otherwise than personally, if defendant does not demand a copy of the complaint, or plead, as the case requires, within twenty days after the service is complete, plaintiff may apply to the court, or a judge thereof for default judgment. The court must require proof of plaintiff's cause of action and damages. If the action is for a money judgment only, plaintiff must also show on such application: (a) that service is complete, (b) that defendant is in default, (c) that the action is for breach of contract express or implied, other than a contract to marry, or for wrongful conversion of personal property, or for injury to person or property as a result of negligence, fraud or other wrongful act; (d) in addition, if defendant is a non-resident or a foreign corporation, that a warrant of attachment granted in the action has been levied upon defendant's property.

(1′) In such case the plaintiff is required to file an inventory of the attached property, and may be required to give an undertaking that he will make restitution, if so ordered, if defendant thereafter opens his default (C.P.A. 217), defends, and succeeds in the action.

C. Default Judgment against Infant Defendant: Judgment by default against an infant defendant cannot be entered until the expiration of twenty days from the appointment of a guardian ad litem for such infant. C.P.A. 492. 

D. Relief from Default Judgment (C.P.A. 108): The court in its discretion, and upon such terms as justice requires, at any time within one year after notice thereof, may relieve a party from a judgment, order or other proceeding taken against him through his mistake, inadvertence, surprise or excusable neglect. C.P.A. 108. (Refer to discussion of C.P.A. 108 and C.P.A. 217 in Chapter IV.) 

(1) An application for relief under this section is addressed to the favor of the court, and whether the relief be granted or denied rests in the sound discretion of the court. Schlimmer v. N. Y. O. & W. R.R. Co., 212 A. D. 782. (2) Thus, the favor of the court should be withheld when the applicant, to open his default, does not show a meritorious controversy, since, the courts should not be burdened with unfounded claims to relief; nor should a just cause be delayed by the interposition of an unwarranted defense. Rothchild v. Haviland, 158 N. Y. S. 661 (where it was held that to open a default under C.P.A. 108, defendant's moving papers should contain more than a formal affidavit of merits and should state facts showing the validity and good faith of the defense). See also Zaza v. Zaza, 246 N. Y. S. 148. (3) Where one serves legal papers by mail, the whole postage must be paid, and if part is paid and part is due, the service is bad. If the attorney upon whom such papers are served refuses to accept them, he stands on a legal right, although a technical one. If the party serving the papers incurs a default by reason of the refusal to accept them, he has no legal right to have the default set aside as a matter of right, but should apply to the court's favor for such relief. Kuh v. Goldman, 104 N. Y. S. 255.

E. Default Judgment Upon Trial: C.P.A. 494a, a new sec tion, effective September 1, 1936, now provides that upon the failure of a party to appear upon or proceed to the trial of an action or proceeding in which issue shall have been joined and the cause noticed for trial, or in which

a note of issue shall have been filed and served, when such cause shall have been reached and called for trial, the pleading of such defaulting party shall be deemed abandoned and judgment may thereupon be entered without requiring application to the court, where the case is one in which the complaint of the prevailing party is such as described in C.P.A. 485 (supra) or his counterclaim is of like nature. In such case the prevailing party must file proof of the service of his complaint or counterclaim, as the case may be, and proof by affidavit of the failure of the defaulting party to appear upon or proceed to trial when the cause was reached and called for trial. 

Whereupon the clerk must enter final judgment in his favor. In a case other than those specified in C.P.A. 485, the court is required to take such proof as the case may require and render judgment accordingly. 

3. Judgment After Trial. 

A. Judgment after Verdict in Jury Trial: This is entered on an "extract from the minutes" made by the clerk of the Trial Term where the case was tried and furnished to the attorney for the successful party, who then proceeds to enter judgment in the clerk's office pursuant to the terms of the verdict without further application to the court. C.P.A. 495.

B. Judgment after Trial by Court Without Jury: As pre viously noted, this is entered upon the decision of the trial justice. C.P.A. 440, 441.

C. Judgment after Trial by Referee to Hear and Determine: 

This is entered on the "report" of the referee, separately stating his findings of fact and conclusions of law. The report must be filed with the clerk. C.P.A. 471. The judgment directed in the report is then entered by the clerk upon application of one of the parties. 

(1) Matrimonial Actions: In matrimonial actions, how ever, an application to the court is necessary before judgment on the report may be entered. This is usually in the nature of a motion to confirm the referee's report and for interlocutory judgment thereon, or for final judgment, if the action is one for a separation. The motion is made at a Special

Term. In matrimonial actions, on the ground of public policy, no default judgment may be taken except on full proof in open court of all the facts (C.P.A. 1174-6; R.C.P. 282, 283), and the filing of the stenographer's minutes, written out, with the judgment roll. (a) Where the Special Term refuses to confirm a referee's report in a matrimonial action, it may refuse to order judgment, but in such case it must order a new reference before a new referee. Perkins v. Perkins, 114 N. 

Y. S. 960. 

4. Judgment By Confession (C.P.A. 540-541): It is possible for a person to authorize the entry of judgment against himself even before action commenced against him. This is done by a confession of judgment for money due, or to become due, absolutely or contingently, and is effected by the person executing a sworn, written statement showing concisely the facts out of which the liability arose and that the sum of money for which the entry of judgment is authorized is actually and justly due or may become due upon a specified contingency. This sworn statement is aimed to prevent fraud by tending to make it difficult for a debtor to confess judgment to a friend or relative on a spurious claim and thus create a prior lien in derogation of the rights of bona fide creditors who have not yet reduced their claims to judgment.

A. Requisites: 

(1) The party himself must verify the statement. (a) Thus, the court has no power to enter a judgment by confession upon a statement made and verified by plaintiff's attorney by virtue of a power of attorney for that purpose contained in an indemnity bond or conditional bill of sale. U. S. Fid. & Guar. Co. v. Shickler, 191 

N. Y. S. 194. See also P. A. Starck Piano Co. v. 

O'Keefe, 208 N. Y. S. 350. (2) The confession should state completely and concisely the facts upon which the indebtedness arose. (a) Upon application of a junior creditor, the court may set aside such a confession of judgment for a failure to state completely and fully the facts upon which the obligation confessed arose. Chappel v. Chappel, 12 N. 

Y. 215. (3) The judgment confessed must be for money due or

to become due absolutely or contingently. (a) This would not authorize the entry of judgment by confession, before action commenced, on a tort liability. 

Burkham v. San Saun, 14 Abb. Pr. 183.

B. Joint Debtors: C.P.A. 542 provides: "One or more joint debtors may confess a judgment for a joint debt due or to become due. Where all the joint debtors do not unite in the confession, the judgment must be entered and enforced against those only who confessed it; and it is not a bar to an action against all the joint debtors upon the same demand." Confession by one partner binds the confessing party only. Scanlon v. Kuehn, 232 N. Y. S. 592.

C. Entry of Judgment: C.P.A. 543 provides: "At any time within three years after the statement is verified, it may be filed with the county clerk of the county of which the defendant was a resident at the time of making such statement. Thereupon, the clerk must enter, in like manner as a judgment is entered in an action, a judgment for the sum confessed, with costs, which he must tax, to the amount of $15.00 besides disbursements taxable in an action. The judgment must be entered in the 

Supreme Court. A judgment shall not be entered upon such a statement after the defendant's death." 

5. Judgment On Submitted Facts: Where adult parties to a controversy agree as to a certain set of facts but differ as to their legal effect, they may prepare a statement setting forth the agreed facts, acknowledge the statement like a deed to be recorded, and submit it, together with an affidavit of one of the parties that there is a real controversy, and that the submission is made to determine the actual rights of the parties. C.P.A. 546. If the action is in the Supreme 

Court, submission is made to the Appellate Division in the first instance. After argument, judgment is entered as ordered by the Appellate Division in the same way as in an action. C.P.A. 547-8. 

A. Requisites: 

(1) The ultimate as well as the evidentiary facts must all be agreed upon. (a) Thus, where the facts agreed upon give rise to conflicting inferences of fact, the court must dismiss the

proceeding and leave the parties to their remedies by action. Neary v. Brogan, 188 N. Y. 43. (2) The submission must be on a statement of facts sufficient to permit the court to render an effectual judgment. (a) Thus, where the basis of the submission is temporary injunctive relief, the court must dismiss the proceeding, since under C.P.A. 548, it has no power to grant any provisional relief. Cunard Ltd. v. Voorhis, 104 N. Y. 525. 

6. The Judgment Roll: The judgment roll constitutes the record of the action.

A. Contents: It should contain the summons and pleadings, proof of service, admissions, and the judgment, so that an inspection will reveal what parties were before the court, the issues raised, and what determination was made thereon. R.C.P. 202. (1) Incidental Papers: Such papers as motion papers, orders, (except an order which involves the merits or necessarily affects the judgment), notes of issue, etc., are not necessary parts of the judgment roll; but where the judgment is based on an attachment proceeding instead of personal service, or appearance, the papers used on the proceeding should be made. a part of the judgment roll. And since a bill of particulars amplifies or restricts a pleading, it may be included if either party demands it. Steiger v. London, 138 A. D. 246. (a) An order of reference to hear and determine, since it affects the judgment, should be included in the judgment roll, but not the motion papers on which the reference was made. Schrader v. Fraenchel, 99 N. Y. S. 137. 

B. Who Files: The judgment roll is prepared for filing by the attorney for the successful party, pursuant to the instructions contained in R.C.P. 202. Any error or irregularity does not affect the judgment (C.P.A. 109) and may be corrected by motion. The actual filing occurs when the judgment roll is delivered to the clerk who dockets it in the judgment docket of the court and issues, upon request, a transcript to be filed in as many counties of the state as desired. The docketing of the

judgment affixes a lien (except as against a municipal corporation debtor) in the amount of the judgment, upon all real estate within the county which the debtor then has or which he may, within ten years thereafter, acquire. 

C.P.A. 509, 510. The judgment docket book must contain the residence address of the judgment creditor and the full name and last known address of the judgment debtor together with the latter's title, trade or profession, if stated in the judgment. C.P.A. 501. A federal district or circuit court judgment may now be docketed by filing a transcript thereof, in the county clerk's office, in the same manner as a Supreme Court judgment. C.P.A. 

502a. See U. S. v. Harpoothian, 24 F. (2d), 646. 

7. Declaratory Judgment: C.P.A. 473 provides that the Su preme Court has discretionary power, in any action or proceeding, to declare rights and other legal relations on request for such declaration whether or not further relief is or could be claimed, and such declarations is to have the force of a final judgment. By virtue of this section it is possible to bring an action in the Supreme Court for a judicial determination of a jural status although no cause of action has yet accrued to plaintiff. The action, in matters of procedure, follows the form and practice prescribed for other equity actions in the Supreme Court. R.C.P. 210. The prayer for relief in the complaint must specify the precise rights and other legal relations of which a declaration is requested, and whether further or consequential relief (which may be coercive) is claimed, and the nature and extent of such relief. R.C.P. 211. If in the opinion of the court the parties should be left to relief by existing forms of action, or for other reasons, it may decline to grant a declaratory judgment, stating the grounds on which its discretion is so exercised. R.C.P. 212. Questions of fact arising on the trial may be ordered submitted to a jury. C.P.A. 213.

A. Object of the Declaratory Judgment: The object of the declaratory judgment is to serve some practical end in affording security and relief against uncertainty and doubt; it does not necessarily presuppose culpable conduct on the part of defendant, but it enables any party whose rights, privileges, powers or immunities, whether evidenced by a written instrument or not, have been
