disputed, endangered, threatened or placed in uncertainty to invoke the aid of a court to obtain an authoritative declaration of his rights or other legal relations. 

Loesch v. Ins. Co., 218 N. Y. S. 412, aff'd. 222 N. Y. S. 

845. To such declaration may be added coercive relief enjoining present or future acts by the defendant in violation of plaintiff's rights as declared. C.P.A. 473 does not permit of advisory opinions; it was intended to apply only in cases of "justiciable controversies" but, unfortunately, the courts differ on what constitutes the latter. Board of Ed. v. Van Zandt, 204 A. D. 856, aff'd. 

234 N. Y. 644. Post v. Met. Cas. Ins. Co., 227 A. D. 156 

Johnson v. Flynn, 160 Misc. 93. 

(1) Illustrative Cases: In Evangelical Church v. Salem, 254 N. 

Y. 161, P sought a declaratory judgment that a certain restrictive covenant affecting the use to which its property could be put had spent its force and was no longer effective. 

The form of the action was held good but relief was denied on the merits. See also Finch v. Unity Fee Co., 242 N. Y. 

589 and Francis v. Ferguson, 246 N. Y. 516 (where a declaration was sought that a covenant in a lease did not prevent P's assignment of the lease). (2) In Bauman v. Bauman, 250 N. Y. 382, a declaratory judgment was granted P, wife, that she was still the lawful wife of D, husband, although the latter had obtained a "mailorder" divorce from her and had remarried, and that such divorce and re-marriage were both illegal. Cf. Richman v. 

Richman, 266 N. Y. S. 53; Lowe v. Lowe, 265 N. Y. 197. (3) In Morecroft v. Taylor, 234 N. Y. S. 2, P was awarded a declaratory judgment that she was D's illegitimate child, where D, the mother, had consistently denied this and where there were no public records of P's birth. (In both this case and the Bauman case, the declaratory judgment protected property rights, which could not be conveniently protected by any conventional type of action). (4) In James v. Alderton, 256 N. Y. 298, P secured a purchaser for D's property on D's promise to pay P, "handsomely" when, as and if D received the purchase price from the purchaser. The purchaser paid $1,000,000 in cash and delivered a mortgage for $400,000 for the balance. P asked $80,000 for his services. D offered $6,000. P then began an action for a declaratory judgment, asking that his services be valued at $80,000. and that he be given a lien on the proceeds of the mortgage when it matured in the future. The action was dismissed, not only because P's commissions were not yet payable, but, as the court pointed out, "Under the facts of

this case, the plaintiff had an action at law for commissions. 

He needed no declaratory judgment to establish his rights. 

He chose this form of action for the purpose of securing a lien which is dependent, not upon procedure, but upon an agreement, express or implied, subjecting property to a lien. 

There being no such arrangement with the defendant, the plaintiff had no lien in law or equity. There being no lien or equities justifying one, there was no necessity for a declaratory judgment.” (5) For other illustrative cases in New York and elsewhere, see 

Borchard "The Declaratory Judgment." 8. Enforcement Of Judgment (C.P.A. 504-5): 

A. By Execution: A judgment for a sum of money, whether on the law or equity side of the court, is enforceable only by a legal remedy, such as execution, as distinguished from an equitable remedy such as contempt proceedings. 

Geery v. Geery, 63 N. Y. 252; Leerburger v. Watson, 154 N. Y. S. 577. Likewise, judgments in favor of plaintiff for replevin to recover a chattel, or in ejectment or for dower, are enforceable only by execution. C.P.A. 504. (1) However, where under the circumstances, an execution in the ejectment action would be unavailing, contempt proceedings are available. Johnson v. Purpura, 203 N. Y. S. 581 (where the sheriff in an action for ejectment was unable to remove the encroaching wall of defendant's house). (2) Although a husband may be punished for contempt for failure to pay alimony, where such payment cannot be enforced by sequestration proceedings (C.P.A. 1171, 1171a, 1172), the court may, at any time, order the arrears of alimony to be computed and docketed as a money judgment and direct that execution be issued thereon. Farquhar v. 

Farquhar, 172 A. D. 242. (a) The payment of costs or counsel fees may be enforced only by execution and not by contempt proceedings. 

Jacquin v. Jacquin, 36 Hun 378. (3) Where a final judgment directs payment by defendant, not to plaintiff, but into court, of money, not due on a contract, and where the defendant disobeys the judgment, he may be punished for contempt. C.P.A. 505; Harris v. Elliott, 163 

N. Y. 269.

B. By Contempt Proceedings: The following judgments are enforceable without execution by punishment for contempt (C.P.A. 505): (1) Final judgment, unenforceable, in part or in whole, by execution. See Mills v. Leland, 141 N. Y. S. 725;

City of Syracuse v. Hogan, 234 N. Y. 457. (2) Interlocutory judgment requiring a party to do or to refrain from doing an act. (3) Judgment requiring payment into court of money not due upon a contract. See Harris v. Elliott, supra. 

(4) Enforcement by Contempt Proceedings of Judgment or Order in Action for Divorce or Separation: In an action in New York for a divorce or separation or in an action in New York to enforce a judgment of another state for divorce or separation granted on grounds recognized in New York (Miller v. Miller, 246 N. Y. 636; C.P.A. 1161), if defendant makes default in payment of alimony awarded, contempt proceedings will lie to enforce payment. (If the foreign decree is not on grounds recognized here, plaintiff would be limited to enforcing a judgment for accrued alimony by property execution. Note, too, that "another state" does not include a foreign country. Boissevain v. Boissevain, 224 A. D. 576.) 

Note: Subd. 1, of CP.A. 1172a provides: "Any person who, by an order or judgment made or entered in an action for divorce or separation or an action for the enforcement in this state of a judgment for divorce or separation rendered in another state, is directed to make payment of any sum or sums of money and against whom an order to punish for a contempt of court has been made pursuant to the provisions of 

Sec. 1172 of this act or the judiciary law may, if financially unable to comply with the order or judgment to make such payment, upon such notice to such parties as the court may direct, make application to the court for an order relieving him from such payment and such contempt order. The court, upon the hearing of such application, if satisfied from the proofs and evidence offered and submitted that the applicant is financially unable to make such payment, may in its discretion, until further order of the court, modify the order or judgment to make such payment and relieve him from such contempt order." 

Subd. 3. "Any person may assert his financial inability to comply with the directions contained in an order or judgment made or entered in an action for divorce or separation or an action for the enforcement in this state of a judgment for divorce or sepa-

ration rendered in another state as a defense in a proceeding instituted against him, under Sec. 1172 of this act or under the judiciary law, to punish him for his failure to comply with such directions, and if the court, upon the hearing of such contempt proceeding, is satisfied from the proofs and evidence offered and submitted that the defendant is financially unable to comply with such order or judgment, it may in its discretion, until further order of the court, make an order modifying such order or judgment and denying the application to punish the defendant for contempt." 

C.P.A. 1172b provides: "Notwithstanding any inconsistent provision of this article, the provision of any judgment or order rendered or made in an action for divorce or separation, requiring the payment of moneys by the husband for the support of the wife shall be suspended and inoperative so far as punishment for contempt is concerned during the period in which the husband shall be imprisoned pursuant to any order adjudging him in contempt for his failure to comply with any provision in such order." 

C.P.A. 167a provides: "Whenever in an action for divorce or separation wherein the husband is plaintiff or wherein the wife is plaintiff but the husband interposes a counterclaim, the court or the judge thereof may refuse to grant an order to stay proceedings, where the only default is the failure of the husband to pay alimony or counsel fees due to his inability to make such payments. In no event shall a husband who has been imprisoned for contempt of court for failure to pay alimony or counsel fees or by virtue of an order of arrest under Sec. 

827 of the Civil Practice Act be stayed from proceeding with the prosecution or defense of an action where the only default is the failure of the husband to pay alimony or counsel fees." 

C.P.A. 138, subd. 18, gives trial preference to an action for absolute divorce or for separation in which an order has been made granting or denying temporary alimony.

C. Procedure on Enforcement of Judgments: 

(1) Lien of Judgment: The judgment, upon docketing, becomes a lien for ten years upon real property and chattels real in the county where docketed and which the judgment debtor then has, or, thereafter acquires, within the said period. C.P.A. 509, 510.

(a) Entry under Debtor's True Name: The judg ment must be entered and docketed against the judgment debtor under his true name. A judgment against a person described by a fictitious name becomes a lien only when the judgment and docket are amended so as to state the true name. C.P.A. 510, 511. (b) Filing of Transcripts: It is necessary to file a transcript of the judgment in counties other than that in which the original docket is made when it is desired to affect, with the judgment lien, property owned by the judgment debtor in such other counties. C.P.A. 502. 

(c) Priority of Liens: 

(1') Purchase Money Mortgage: The lien of a purchase money mortgage is superior to that of a prior judgment against the mortgagor (C.P.A. 514), provided the mortgage is bona fide, given with the purpose and intention of paying the purchase price of the land, and is executed at the same time as the purchase. Syracuse Sav. and Loan 

Co. v. Haas, 234 N. Y. S. 514. 

(2′) As Between Judgments: Where judgments are docketed at different dates, before the debtor has any property, and the debtor subsequently acquires real property, the liens of all the judgments attach simultaneously to such property and are equal liens thereon, entitled to share pro rata in the proceeds thereof, and one of several judgment creditors obtains no prior lien by superior diligence, where, in such case, he issues execution to the sheriff ahead of his fellow creditors. Hulbert v. Hulbert, 216 N. Y. 430. 

(d) Extension of Lien by Refiling: When ten years after filing the judgment roll have expired, real property or a chattel real, which the judgment debtor, or a person deriving his right or title thereto as the lienee or devisee of the judgment

debtor, then has, in any county, may be levied upon by virtue of an execution against property issued to the sheriff of that county, by filing with the county clerk a notice subscribed by the sheriff describing the judgment, the execution and the property levied upon, and, if the interest levied upon is that of an heir or devisee, specifying that fact and the name of the heir or devisee. Such notice must be recorded and indexed by the clerk as a notice of lis pendens. 

C.P.A. 512. 

(2) Executions Against Property and Person: 

(a) Issuance of Execution against Property: The general rule is that the execution may be issued at any time within five years after the entry of judgment as a matter of course. C.P.A. 650. (1) The five year period, within which execution may be issued as a matter of right, without leave of the court (under C.P.A. 650), does not begin to run until the judgment has been duly docketed, and in a foreclosure action judgment is not duly docketed until deficiency judgment, if any, is docketed. Kupper v. Frank, 30 Hun 74. (2') The right to issue execution as a matter of right within the five year period survives to the personal representatives of a deceased creditor up to the expiration of the same period. C.P.A. 651. (3′) After Expiration of Five Years: After the expiration of five years, the right still continues, subject to these qualifications: (a') that an execution was issued within the five year period and returned wholly or partly unsatisfied (Cooper v. Bailey, 74 N. Y. S. 667); or, (b') that an order has been made granting leave to issue the execution. C.P.A. 652. (4′) As against Executor or Administrator: If a judgment is recovered against a person in his representative capacity such as an executor, trustee or assignee, he is not personally liable, and his own property is not subject to execution but only that of the person he represents. C.P.A. 646. In order to issue the execution, leave must be obtained from the surrogate from whose court letters testamentary or letters of administration were issued. D. 

E.L. 151.

(b) Property Subject to Levy: 

(1') Personal Property: The sheriff may levy upon all goods and chattels of the judg ment debtor, not expressly exempted, and only such other personal property as is expressly declared by law to be subject to levy under an execution. C.P.A. 679. A "license to traffic in liquor" is not a tangible chattel and cannot be levied upon (McNeeley v. Welz, 166 N. Y. 124), nor may the judgment debtor's interest in a warehouse receipt held under a lien by another be subjected to a levy, since, such an interest is a mere chose in action collecible by the sheriff only by an equity suit in the nature of a creditor's bill. Castriotis v. Guar. Trust Co., 229 N. Y. 74. (a') Current money belonging to the judgment debtor, including bank bills issued to circulate as money, is expressly subject to levy. C.P. 

A. 685-686. 

(b') Execution Against Securities and Evidences of Debt: A new C.P.A. 687, substituted on 

September 1, 1936, now provides: "The officer to whom an execution against property is delivered must levy upon and sell in whole, or to such extent as may be necessary, any instrument generally deemed a security or any instrument for the payment of money only, belonging to the judgment debtor and found within the county, including, but not limited to any note, check, acceptance, bill of exchange, certificate of deposit, certificate of interest or participation in any profit sharing agreement or mortgage, collateral trust certificate, re-organization certificate or subscription, transferable share, voting trust certificate, certificate of interest in property, or any certificate of interest or participation in, temporary or interim certificate for, or warrant or right to subscribe to or purchase any of the foregoing. Such security or instrument shall be subject to levy and sale by such officer, whether negotiable or otherwise, whether past due or yet to become due, and whether

executed by a public or private corporation or by a private person, either within or without the state." 

(c') Transfer of Title to Certificates and Shares of Stock: P.P.L. Sec. 162, amended as of Sep tember 1, 1936, provides that title to a certificate and to the shares represented thereby can be transferred: "By delivery of a separate instrument containing a written assignment of the certificate, or an interest therein, executed by the officer who levies a property execution upon such certificate and sells the same to the purchaser on the execution sale, and such assignment plus delivery of the certificate shall effect a transfer of title to the certificate, or the interest therein levied upon and sold, and to the shares specified by such certificate, or such interest therein." The above applies even though the charter or by-laws of the corporation issuing the certificate and the certificate itself provide that the shares represented thereby shall be transferable only on the corporation books or shall be registered by a registrar or transferred by a transfer agent. (d') The interest of the judgment debtor in property subject to levy which has been pledged by him is subject to levy. C.P.A. 688. (e') A leasehold in real property having less than five years to run is treated as a chattel and may be levied upon as such. C.P.A. 708. (f') Garnishee Execution: Ten percent of the wages, earnings, profits or income from trust funds, when due and owing to the judgment debtor to the amount of $12 or more per week, may be levied upon under a garnishee execution by special order of the court and after a general execution against property has been returned unsatisfied. C.P.A. 684. The law will imply a contract for remuneration where the judgment debtor attempts to evade garnishee proceedings by pleading gratutious services. Wood v. Dock & Mill Co., 184 N. 

Y. S. 225. The earnings of the debtor may even be in the form of commissions. State employees are not exempt from the scope of the section. 

(2') Real Property: The requirement in the

case of an ordinary money judgment is that the sheriff levy upon personal property, first, to satisfy the judgment and if enough cannot be found, then the sheriff may levy on the real property of the judgment debtor. 

C.P.A. 643. If an attachment was had in the action, and the summons was personally served, or the defendant appeared, the execution runs, first, against attached personal property, second, against unattached personal property, third, against attached real property, and last, against unattached real property. Place v. Riley, 98 N. Y. 1. (Where a sheriff has the proceeds of a sale under a junior execution, it is his duty to apply the proceeds to satisfy a prior warrant of attachment, although no levy under that attachment has been made. Pach v. Gilbert, 124 N. Y. 612.) (a') Right to Redeem: Real property is sold under execution at public auction in the county of its situs. Three notices must be conspicuously posted in public places in the town or county forty-two days before the sale, and a copy published in a newspaper once a week for six weeks. Within one year, the judgment debtor may redeem by paying to the purchaser the sum paid on the sale, plus ten per cent thereof. If the judgment debtor fails to redeem, his creditors may redeem by paying the purchaser the purchase price and seven percent within fifteen months of the sale. At the end of the said fifteen months, the sheriff must execute the deed and deliver it to the person or persons entitled to it. C.P.A. 660, 712, 724-728; 748-9. 

(3′) Exempt Property: The general scheme of exemption is that trade implements and necessary household furniture belonging to a householder are exempt. A householder is the head of a family who reside together and constitute a household, although blood relationship is not necessary. If the sheriff

takes property obviously exempt, he is a trespasser. Frost v. Mott, 34 N. Y. 253.

The exemptions are provided for in C.P.A. 

665-678, and cover household utensils, books, church seats or pews, tools and implements of a mechanic, professional tools, etc., up to certain values. (a') C.P.A. 513 provides that the interest of a person holding a contract for the purchase of real property is not bound by the docketing of a judgment and cannot be levied or sold by virtue of an execution issued upon such a judgment. 

(d) Execution Against Person: 

(1') Where Available (C.P.A. 764): The right to an execution against the person depends upon the nature of the action. The test is whether the plaintiff had the right to an order of arrest under C.P.A. 826 in the action proper. Roeber v. Dawson, 3 N. Y. S. 

122. If he had, the successful party in the action also has the right to a final body execution, even for costs, against the person of the losing party. Davids v. Brooklyn 

Heights R.R. Co., 93 N. Y. S. 285. (a') An execution against the person can be issued against a woman only following an executed order of arrest. C.P.A. 765; Ritz Carlton 

Apartments v. Fried, 235 N. Y. S. 624. 

(2') How Executed: The execution against the person can be issued only after the return of an execution against property, wholly or partly unsatisfied; but if the judgment debtor is already in custody under other civil process, a prior execution against property is not necessary. C.P.A. 766. The sheriff, in executing the process, arrests the judgment debtor and commits him to the county jail until he pays the judgment or is otherwise discharged according to law. 

After thirty days' imprisonment of the debtor, the creditor may consent to a dis-

charge and revive his right to a property execution. C.P.A. 771. 

(a) Under Civil Rights Law, Sec. 72, the term of imprisonment on a body execution is limited to three months where the sum to be recovered is less than $500 and to six months where the amount is $500 or more. (This section does not apply where defendant is imprisoned for contempt for non-payment of alimony. However, see new C.P.A. 1172b). 

9. Supplementary Proceedings: 

A. Nature of Remedy: At common law, the judgment creditor's remedy ended with execution. Equity, however, sought to aid further enforcement of the judgment by means of a creditor's bill. By Article 45 of the Civil 

Practice Act, the legislature has provided a special statutory proceeding whereby a creditor may investigate the extent of the judgment debtor's property and then summarily reach such property and apply it to satisfy the creditor's judgment.

B. Amount of Judgment: The proceeding is maintainable on a judgment in any sum rendered by any court of this state. C.P.A. 773. After the creditor has obtained the judgment, the proceeding may be maintained until such judgment is satisfied, vacated, or outlawed by the statute of limitations. 

C. How Instituted (C.P.A. 774-5): 

(1) By Order of the Court: An order of the court will be granted requiring the examination where the judgment creditor shows by affidavit that the judgment is unpaid in whole or in part. However, if a proceeding was begun and closed, the creditor must also show, either, that two years have elapsed since the last examination, or, that there is reason to believe the debtor has new property or income, or is entitled to such, which may be reached under the judgment. Books, papers and records may be ordered produced. 

(2) By Subpoena: The creditor's attorney may issue a subpoena requiring the judgment debtor to appear for examination concerning his property, income, or other means for satisfying the judgment. Such

subpoena may be issued within two years from the recovery of the judgment and only in a case where the debtor has not previously been examined. C.P.A. 

775. Upon service of the subpoena the judgment debtor is enjoined from disposing of his property. 

C.P.A. 781. The provision of this last section, to such effect, must be endorsed upon the subpoena served. Books, papers and records may be subpoenaed. (3) By Warrant: Whenever it appears by affidavit that there is danger that a judgment debtor will leave the state or conceal himself therein, and that he has property which may be reached by execution, the court may issue a warrant to a sheriff to arrest the debtor. (a') If the court is satisfied that the facts in the affidavit are true, then, upon the arrest of the debtor, it may order him to give an undertaking to guarantee that he will appear before the court for examination and that he will not dispose of his property in the meantime. C.P.A. 776. 

D. Examination of Third Party: Upon proof by affidavit that any person or corporation has, or that there is reason to believe that he or it has, property of the judgment-debtor, exceeding $10 in value, or owes him such sum, the court may order such third party to appear for examination. (1) Instead of securing an order, the attorney may issue a subpoena similar to that permitted under C.P.A. 

775, against the judgment debtor. C.P.A. 779. (a) Endorsement of C.P.A. 781 upon a subpoena will enjoin the transfer of the property involved until further order of the court. Violation of the restraining provision is a contempt of court. 

E. Examination of Witnesses: Until the close of the examination of the judgment debtor, or third party, or within six months thereafter, the creditor's attorney may issue a subpoena requiring the appearance of any necessary witness, together with necessary books and papers. 

C.P.A. 782. 

F. Procedure: An order, subpoena or warrant must be served not less than three days nor more than fourteen

days before return day, by delivering to and leaving with the party to be examined a copy thereof. C.P.A. 783. 

The answers of a person examined must be under oath. 

Either party may produce witnesses and be a witness in his own behalf. C.P.A. 784. Any person who wilfully and knowingly testifies falsely concerning any material matter in the course of supplementary proceedings, is guilty of a civil contempt of court. C.P.A. 788. See 

Stafford v. Bruce, 160 Misc. 425, as to constitutionality.

A person is not excused from answering questions on the ground that it will tend to convict him of fraud, etc., but such answer cannot be used as evidence in a criminal action except one for perjury. C.P.A. 789. A judgment debtor is entitled to counsel on his examination.

G. Reaching the Debtor's Property: 

(1) Where it appears on the examination that the judgment debtor has in his possession assets belonging to him or that such assets are in the hands of another, the court may order the debtor or third party to pay the money or deliver the property to a sheriff designated or to a receiver appointed. C.P.A. 796. (2) Where it appears that a person or corporation is indebted to the judgment debtor, the court may authorize the judgment creditor to sue such person or corporation for the debt, and may enjoin the transfer of such debt until recovery of judgment in the action. C.P.A. 795. (3) After proceeding commenced, the court may make an order appointing a receiver of the property of the judgment debtor. C.P.A. 804. (4) Notwithstanding C.P.A. 684-685, the court may order the judgment debtor to pay the creditor, in installments, such portion of his income as the court deems proper in view of all circumstances. Failure to comply with the order is a civil contempt punishable under the Jud. Law Sec. 19. C.P.A. 793. See 

Lackner v. Abrams, 160 Misc. 424; Compton & Co. v. 

Williams, 248 A. D. 545 as to constitutionality. 

10. Special Proceedings. 

A. Habeas Corpus (1) Generally: The writ of habeas corpus has been de-

clared to be the great bulwark of personal liberty, guaranteed by the Constitution. (2) Where Applicable: Any person deprived of liberty within the state on any charge or pretext, except a mandate of a federal court of proper jurisdiction, a final judgment or process of a state court of competent jurisdiction, or a final order of such tribunal in a special proceeding instituted for any cause except to publish for contempt (C.P.A. 1230), may petition for the writ. (a) Thus, the writ may be demanded by a prisoner detained after the expiration of his lawful term (People ex rel. 

Tweed v. Liscomb, 60 N. Y. 559), or to whom bail is denied, wholly or in reasonable amount. (b) Likewise, a person detained as a lunatic who alleges that he was sane when committed or was committed without notice, or that his insanity has ceased, may demand the writ. People ex rel. Woodbury v. Hendrick, 215 N. Y. 339. (c) A child detained from a parent or other person having the right to custody may be brought into court on a writ of habeas corpus and its custody awarded to the proper person. People ex rel. Sisson v. Sisson, 275 

N. Y. S. 299. (1′) In such case, the writ must be issued by the Supreme Court and the welfare of the child is the prime consideration. Dom. Rel. Law, Sec. 70. People ex rel. Pruyne v. Walts, 122 N. Y. 238. Cf. C.P.A. 1170. (3) Application for Writ: The person detained, or one acting in his behalf, may apply to a judge of the Supreme Court, or to the court, or to a county judge, on a petition showing where, by whom, and on what pretext he is detained, and that he is not detained under the final judgment or process of a court of competent jurisdiction. If a judge denies a proper application for the writ, he forfeits $1,000 to the prisoner. C.P.A. 1234. (a) Form: The writ is issued in the name of the 

People of the State, under the seal of the court, and the person applying for the writ is called. the relator. The writ is directed to the warden, institution superintendent, or other person

guilty of the wrongful detention, and such person is called the defendant. The writ commands. the defendant to produce the body of the prisoner before the court or a judge at a specified time and place together with a certification as to the cause of detention. (d) Service: The writ cannot be served by a minor, but may be served on Sunday. C.P.A. 1242. 

All mistakes in the petition or writ, such as misnomers or service upon the wrong person, must be disregarded. Failure to obey the writ without cause by the person to whom the writ is directed is treated as a contempt and the offender may be immediately committed. C.P.A. 1244-1246, 1248-1250. (4) Where Certiorari Must Be Granted: If the offense for which the prisoner is detained is not bailable, the court or judge to whom application is made for a writ of habeas corpus may issue, instead, a writ of certiorari to inquire into the cause of such detention. This writ is exactly similar to that of habeas corpus except that it does not require the production of the body of the prisoner in court (C.P.A. 1261), but the defendant must file a written answer showing by what right he detains the prisoner. People ex rel. Wilson v. Flynn, 106 N. Y. S. 1141. 

(5) Proceedings on Return of Writ of Habeas Corpus: 

Proceedings on the return of the writ consist of a summary and informal inquiry into the validity of the restraint. No inquiry can be made into the propriety or justice of the imprisonment but only into the jurisdiction of the court or judge to render the judgment under which the prisoner is detained. 

Habeas corpus cannot substitute for appeal or a motion for a new trial. People ex rel. Bailey v. 

McCann, 226 N. Y. S. 449. (a) However, in examining the proof of the facts alleged in a return to a writ of habeas corpus, showing the commitment of the prisoner by a justice of the peace on a preliminary examination, the court is not precluded from going be-

hind the return, and the commitment is not conclusive as to the legality of the confinement. 

People ex rel. Bungart v. Wells, 68 N. Y. S. 59. 

Thus, where a person is arrested upon an information or affidavits which do not charge facts constituting a crime, there is a question of jurisdiction which may be determined by a writ of habeas corpus (People ex rel. Doyle v. 

Atwell, 232 N. Y. 96), and the same applies where the evidence upon which a prisoner was committed is insufficient in law to establish the commission of any crime. People ex rel. Bungart v. Wells, supra. (b) Disposition: The court on hearing argument on the writ may discharge the prisoner or admit him to bail or remand him to custody. But before an order for the discharge of a prisoner is made, notice must be given to the district attorney or to the person interested in continuing the restraint. C.P.A. 1258; People ex rel. Saporito v. Warden N. Y. Pen., 243 A. D. 450. A discharge on a writ of habeas corpus cannot be pleaded as "double jeopardy", if defendant is thereafter brought to trial. (1') A notice of appeal from a final order directing the discharge of a prisoner on a writ of habaes corpus is ineffective to stay the operation of such order or to prevent the immediate discharge of the prisoner. People ex rel. Sabatino v. Jennings, 246 

N. Y. 258. (2′) Obedience to the order for the prisoner's discharge can be enforced by an attachment against the person of the offender, who also forfeits to the prisoner $1,250, in addition to the damages caused by the disobedience. C.P.A. 1267.

B. Proceedings Against a Body or Officer (Art. 78): 

(1) Abolition of Certiorari to Review, Mandamus and 

Prohibition: Writs and orders of certiorari to review (except to review in taxation cases) mandamus and prohibition are abolished as of September 1, 1937 and the relief theretofore obtained by such writs or orders is now obtainable under the new Art. 78. 

C.P.A. 1283.

(2) "Body or Officer" Defined: The expression, "body or officer" includes every court, tribunal, board, corporation, officer, or other person, or aggregation of persons. C.P.A. 1284. (3) Relief Obtainable: Under the new proceeding the following relief is obtainable: 

(a) Review of Determination: Any act or refusal to act of a body or officer exercising judicial, quasi-judicial, administrative or corporate functions, which involves an exercise of judgment or discretion, may be reviewed. C.P.A. 1284.

The test of whether an act is judicial is whether the body or officer performing the act is authorized and required to take evidence and whether all the parties interested are entitled to notice and hearing. People ex rel. Schau v. McWilliams, 185 N. Y. 92, (where it was held that an act of a civil service commission in classifying a position as competitive was an administrative and not a judicial act). (1') Exceptions: The following determinations are not reviewable. (a') where it was made in a civil action or proceeding by a court of record or a judge thereof, (b') where it was made in a criminal matter except a criminal contempt of court, (c') where it is not a final determination, (d') where it can be adequately reviewed by appeal to a court or other tribunal, (e') where the body or officer making the determination is expressly authorized to rehear the matter upon the petitioner's application; unless the determination to be reviewed was made upon a rehearing, or a rehearing has been denied, or the time within which the petitioner can procure a rehearing has elapsed. 

C.P.A. 1285. 

(b) Compelling Performance of a Duty: Under the new proceeding the body or officer can be compelled to perform a duty specifically enjoined by law. This refers to the relief previously

obtainable in a mandamus proceeding. C.P.A. 1284. 

(c) Restraining Ultra-Jurisdictional Acts: Under the new proceeding a body or officer exercising judicial or quasi-judicial functions may be restrained from proceeding without or in excess of jurisdiction. This refers to the relief previously obtainable in a prohibition proceeding. 

C.P.A. 1284. (4) Time to Bring Proceeding: A proceeding to review a determination or to compel performance of a duty must be instituted by service of the petition within four months after the reviewable determination becomes final, or within four months of the respondent's refusal to perform his duty; or, with the court's permission, within two years where petitioner (or the person whom he represents), at the time of such determination or refusal, was under the age of twenty-one, or insane or imprisoned on a criminal charge or sentence for less than life. C.P.A. 1286. 

(5) Where Proceeding Brought: The petitioner applies for relief at a Special Term of the Supreme Court held within the judicial district embracing the county wherein respondent made the determination or refusal to perform his duty, or wherein the proceedings were brought in the course of which the matter sought to be restrained originated; or wherein it is alleged in the petition that the material facts otherwise took place. If the petition be directed against a justice of the Supreme Court the application is made to the Appellate Division of the judicial department embracing the county in which the action is triable or the special proceeding is brought, in the course of which the matter sought to be enforced or restrained, as the case may be, originated. C.P.A. 1287. 

(6) The Petition: Petitioner applies for relief on a verified petition containing a concise statement of the material facts upon which he relies and accompanied by affidavits and other written proof. The

petition demands the relief, in the alternative, or otherwise, to which petitioner believes himself en titled. C.P.A. 1288. 

(7) Service: Petitioner must give at least eight days written notice of his application for relief unless a shorter time is prescribed by an order to show cause properly granted. A copy of the petition and accompanying papers must be served with the notice or order to show cause. Service is to be made in the same manner as a summons in an action is required to be personally served, unless the order to show cause differently prescribes. Service on a court consisting of three or more judges may be made by serving a majority of them. If the respondent is a board or commission having a chairman or other presiding officer, secretary or clerk, or similar officer, service upon one of these is sufficient; otherwise, a majority of the members must be served. C.P.A. 1289. 

(8) Joinder of Parties Respondent: Whenever neces sary to accomplish substantial justice, a proceeding under this article may be maintained against an officer whose term of office has expired or against members of a board or commission whose terms of office have expired, and the successor officer or successor member of the board or commission, or any other person having custody of a record of proceedings subject to review or consideration, may also be joined as a party, at any stage of the proceeding. 

Where the proceeding is brought to restrain a body or officer exercising judicial or quasi-judicial functions from proceeding without or in excess of jurisdiction in favor of another party, the latter must be joined as a party respondent with the body or officer. C.P.A. 1290. (9) The Answer: At least two days prior to the return day, unless a different time is fixed in the order to show cause, respondent serves and files a verified answer. The answer must contain proper denials and statements of new matter, as in an action, and must

set forth the facts justifying respondent's action; but it may not contain a counterclaim. A certified transcript of the record of proceedings subject to review must be annexed to the answer; also there must be served with the answer affidavits made by a person familiar with the facts, or other written proof, showing such evidentiary facts as will entitle respondent to a trial of any issues of fact. Where the proceeding is brought to restrain a judicial or quasi-judicial body from proceeding without, or in excess of, jurisdiction in favor of a party, the latter may serve and file a separate answer or unite with the body or officer in an answer, or annex to the latter a subscribed statement that he adopts the same. C.P.A. 1291. (10) The Reply: If petitioner desires to dispute any matter, other than denials, set forth in the answer, he serves upon the respondent and submits to the court, on or before the return day, a reply as in an action. The reply may be accompanied by affidavits or other written proof. C.P.A. 1292. (11) Objections to Pleadings: The respondent may raise an objection in point of law warranting the dismissal of the petition, by setting the objections forth in his answer or by applying to the court on the return day for an order dismissing the petition as a matter of law. If the respondent intends to apply for such an order, he must serve notice thereof upon the petitioner within the time allowed for answering. In the event of the denial of such application, the court may permit the respondent to answer, upon such terms as may be just. The petitioner may move on the return day for an order striking out a defense of new matter contained in the answer on the ground that the same is insufficient in law, upon the face thereof. C.P.A. 1293. (12) Correcting Defect in Papers: Either party may request the court to correct any omissions or defects in the papers or to strike out any parts of the same upon the ground that they are not relevant or ma-

terial to the issues or that they are redundant or scandalous. C.P.A. 1294. (13) Hearing: On the return day if no triable issue of fact is raised by the pleadings a final order is made by the court. If there is a triable issue of fact it must be forthwith tried by the court without a jury or by a referee. If the proceeding is to review a determination or to compel performance of a duty, a jury trial, if demanded before the return day, may be had. In the case of proceedings instituted in the 

Appellate Division, the issue of fact is tried before a referee or at a Trial Term of the Supreme Court in a county designated by the Appellate Division. 

C.P.A. 1295. (14) Questions to be Determined on Hearing: These are: (a) whether the respondent failed to perform his legal duty, (b) whether the respondent (exercising judicial or quasi-judicial functions) is proceeding or is about to proceed without, or in excess, of jurisdiction, (c) whether the respondent had jurisdiction of the subject matter of the determination being reviewed, (d) whether the respondent made the determination in the manner authorized by law, (e) whether, in making the determination, any rule of law affecting the rights of the parties has been violated to petitioner's prejudice; (f) where the determination was made after a hearing held, and evidence taken, pursuant to statutory direction, the following may also be determined: (1') whether there was competent proof of all the facts necessary to be proved in order to authorize the determination. (2') if there was such proof, whether upon all the evidence, there was such a preponderance of proof against the existence of any of those facts that the verdict of a jury, affirming their existence, would be set aside, in a Supreme Court action, as against the weight of evidence. C.P.A. 1296.

(15) How Above Issues Determined: Where only one or more of the first five issues are raised, the court to which the application for relief is made itself disposes of the cause on the merits. Where one of the other two issues is raised, the court makes an order directing that the proceedings be transferred for disposition to a term of the Appellate Division held within the judicial department embracing the county in which the proceeding is instituted. The court may, however, even in such a case, itself pass on objections to the petition in point of law, and it may dismiss the proceeding on the merits where it appears that the petitioner is not entitled to relief because of the provisions of C.P.A. 1285 or 1286. If the court erroneously decides that the issues in the cause are not such as to call for transfer of the cause to the Appellate Division, or if the converse occurs, the Appellate Division must treat the cause, when the latter comes before it by appeal or transfer, as if the proper proceedings had been taken, and must either dispose of the issues itself, or if the papers before it are not sufficient therefor, remit the proceeding to the proper term or court to be disposed of. C.P.A. 1296. 

(16) Bringing in Third Person: Upon the application of a person specially and beneficially interested in upholding or annulling a determination to be reviewed, the court, in its discretion, may admit him as a party in the special proceedings upon such terms as justice requires. C.P.A. 1298. 

(17) Final Order: The court renders a final order grant ing the petitioner the relief to which it deems he is entitled, or dismissing the proceeding, either on the merits or with leave to renew. If the proceeding is brought to review a determination, the court may annul or confirm, wholly or partly, or modify the determination reviewed, as to any or all of the parties, and may direct appropriate action or inaction by the respondent. The court may also award such restitution as may be just. In an appropriate

case, the court may award the petitioner the damages which he has sustained. C.P.A. 1300. 

(18) Enforcement of Final Order; Enrollment: The final order may be enforced by proceedings for contempt with respect to such parts thereof as granted the primary relief sought by the petitioner. With respect to such parts thereof as award the petitioner incidental relief, such as costs, damages or restitution, the final order may be entered and docketed, and enforced as a final judgment in an action. But before the order can be enforced in any manner, an enrollment must be filed thereupon. For that purpose, the clerk must attach together and file in his office the final order, the papers upon which the order was granted, or copies thereof, and the same papers which are required by law to be incorporated into a judgment-roll in an action. C.P.A. 1303. 

C. Arbitration: 

(1) Generally: The New York Arbitration Law, enacted in 1920, makes valid, enforceable and irrevocable, except for such reasons at law or in equity as exist for the revocation of any contract, a written provision in a contract to arbitrate a controversy thereafter arising between the parties. Arbit. Law, Sec. 

2. Such an agreement to arbitrate is enforceable by a special proceeding to compel its specific performance, brought in the Supreme Court, or in any other court, specified in the agreement. (a) Thus, where a party in advance of any litigation or arbitration agrees that in the event of such proceeding it shall be subject to foreign process, then that party will not be permitted to repudiate such agreement. In re 

Marcus A. Heyman, Inc., 242 A. D. 362. (b) In Berkovitz v. Arbib & Houlberg, Inc., 230 N. Y. 261, the Arbitration Law was adjudged constitutional as not depriving a party of his right to jury trial, and the statute was held to apply to contracts pre-existing its enactment. The Court of Appeals further held, in that case, that the Arbitration Law does not affect pending actions, and that its operation does not oust the courts of their jurisdiction but merely adds another remedy to a litigant's rights.

(2) Selecting the Arbitrators: On the application of either party, the Supreme Court may appoint one or more arbitrators, or an umpire, where the agreement to arbitrate does not provide a method for selecting them, or him, or where a party fails to avail himself of such method, or where for any reason such arbitrators or umpire have not been appointed. 

Arbit. Law, Sec. 4. 

(3) Where Arbitration is Not Available: Arbitration may not be had where one of the parties is an infant or incompetent, (unless the appropriate court having jurisdiction approves) or where the controversy concerns a claim to a life estate or one in fee, except a controversy regarding partition, a boundary dispute or admeasurement of dower. C.P.A. 1448. 

(4) Procedure to Compel Arbitration: 

(a) How Begun: The proceeding is initiated by a petition to the Supreme Court, or a judge thereof, presented on eight days' notice to the party in default on the agreement to arbitrate and served personally in the same way as a summons. See In re Marcus A. Heyman, supra. (b) Hearing: Upon the return date, a hearing is had and if the court is satisfied that the party in default does not dispute the making of the arbitration agreement, it must render a final order directing the parties to proceed with the arbitration. However, if either the arbitration agreement or the question of default is controverted, a trial is had as in equity cases. If the court finds in favor of the petitioner, a similar order is made directing the parties to proceed with the arbitration. (5) Procedure Before Arbitrators: The arbitrators, whether named in the agreement or appointed by the court, must notify each party of the time and place of hearing. Each arbitrator must take an oath, before taking testimony, to faithfully and fairly perform his duties; the oath, however, may be waived by the parties. After hearing the testimony, the

arbitrators make an award in writing and acknowledged like a deed to be recorded. The award is filed with the clerk of the court or delivered to one of the parties for such filing. 

(6) Confirming, Vacating or Modifying the Award: 

Within one year after the making of the award, either party may move for an order confirming, vacating, modifying or correcting it. C.P.A. 1461, 1462. (a) The award may be vacated for fraud, evident partiality or misconduct of any arbitrator, or on the ground that the arbitrators exceeded their powers; but the court has no power to vacate the award for lack of evidence, or for any error of law or fact. (1′) Where an arbitrator, after hearings, although in good faith, made private investigations without notice to the parties, upon which investigation he admittedly based his decision, the award should be vacated on the ground of the arbitrator's misconduct, since the Civil Practice Act does not provide for ex parte investigations by the arbitrators. 

Berizzi Co. v. Krausz, 239 N. Y. 315. (b) The award may be modified or corrected for obvious errors of computation or misdescriptions, or for formal defects, or where the arbitrators have made an award on matters not submitted to them, or as not affecting the merits of the award on matters properly submitted. 

C.P.A. 1462a. (c) Judgment: A final judgment may be entered upon the order confirming or modifying the award, as upon a referee's report. Such judgment has the same force and effect as a judgment in an action, and may be treated in the same way both for the purposes of appeal or enforcement. C.P.A. 1464. (1′) Hence, any attack on the award on the ground of corruption, fraud, or other undue means can only be made upon the motion to confirm the award. Jacobowitz v. Metselaar, 268 N. Y. 130.

APPENDIX 

The following amendments are effective September 1, 1938, unless otherwise stated:

Action Against Persons Jointly Liable in Tort; Service: 

C.P.A. 1197 is amended so as to provide that where the complaint demands judgment in whole or in part for a sum of money against two or more defendants alleged to be jointly liable in contract or in tort, if the summons is served upon one or more, but not upon all, the plaintiff may proceed against the defendant or defendants served, unless the court otherwise directs; and, if he recovers final judgment it may be taken against all the defendants jointly indebted.

Personal Service Upon Partnership: 

A new C.P.A. 229a is added providing that in any action, legal or equitable, against a partnership carrying on business in this state or holding property therein, service upon any partner shall be sufficient service upon the partnership and shall authorize a judgment against the partnership and the partners actually served, and the judgment in plaintiff's favor shall operate against the property of the partnership and the separate property of the partners separately served pending actions in which the issues have not been reached for trial and the summons is re-served upon a partner. 

Statute of Limitations: Action to Recover for Violation of Privacy: 

C.P.A. 49 is amended by adding a new subd. 8 making the 3 year statute applicable to an action to recover damages for a violation of the right of privacy under Sec. 51 of the Civil Rights Law. Effective April 5, 1938. 

Statute of Limitations: Actions on Bonds and/or Mortgages Secured by Real Property: 

A new C.P.A. 47a is added providing that (1.) An action upon a bond, the payment of which is secured by a mortgage upon real property, or upon a bond and mortgage so secured, or upon a mortgage of real property, or any interest therein, must be commenced within six years after the cause of action has accrued. Where the action is brought for breach of a covenant of seizin or against incumbrances, the cause of action, for the purposes of this section only, is deemed to have accrued upon an eviction, and not before. (2.) This section shall apply to all causes of action enumerated in subdivision one, whether heretofore or hereafter accruing except that in the case of such a cause of action heretofore accrued, if, at the time this section takes effect, there remains of the time heretofore limited for the commencement of an action thereon a period longer than six years, such period shall be shortened to six years from the effective date of this section and except further that if, at the

APPENDIX 

time this section takes effect, there remains of the time heretofore limited for the commencement of an action thereon a period shorter than six years, such period shall not be enlarged by the enactment of this section. 

C.P.A. 47 is also amended so as to provide that except as otherwise provided by the new C.P.A. 47a, an action upon a sealed instrument must be commenced within 20 years after the cause of action has accrued. 

Supplementary Proceedings: 

C.P.A. 774 is amended so as to provide that proceedings may be instituted: (1) By order of the court served upon the judgment debtor, a third party or a witness (2) Within 2 years from the date of judgment by subpoena of the judgment creditor's attorney served upon the judgment debtor, a third party or a witness (3) By warrant (4) Within two years from the date of judgment by service of a subpoena on a financial institution as provided for in C.P.A. 782a.

C.P.A. 775 subd. 1 is amended so as to provide that when a proceeding to examine the debtor has been instituted and closed no further proceedings shall be instituted except by order of the court which shall be granted upon a showing (1) that one year has elapsed since the last examination or that there is reason to believe that the debtor has new property or income. An order for the debtor's examination may contain a stay order enjoining him from disposing of his property. The stay is good for 2 years unless sooner vacated. 

C.P.A. 779 is amended so as to provide that a third party order may also contain a stay which is good for 2 years unless sooner vacated. A third party subpoena may be issued at any time within 2 years from the date of the judgment and must contain a statement that it is void unless served within 2 years from the date of the judgment. Once the third party proceeding is closed no further proceedings against said third party can be had without a court order granted upon a showing that one year has elapsed since the last examination or that there is reason to believe that the third party has property of the judgment debtor or is indebted to him. 

C.P.A. 782 is amended so as to provide that upon proof by affidavit that any person or corporation has or may have information concerning the judgment debtor's property or debts owed him the court may order such a person or corporation to be examined as a witness. The order may compel production of books and papers. The attendance of such witness may also be compelled by service of a subpoena issued by the attorney for the judgment creditor within 2 years from the date of the judgment. The subpoena must contain a statement that it is void unless served within 2 years from the date of the judgment. But the foregoing provisions shall not preclude the judgment creditor from calling witnesses in any proceeding commenced against the judgment creditor or a third party.

C.P.A. 782a, a new section is added providing for the securing of information from a financial institution by subpoena with respect to deposits of the judgment debtor.

APPENDIX 

307 

Joinder of Causes of Action (The Old Law): 

Plaintiff may unite in the same complaint two or more causes of action, whether formerly denominated as legal or equitable, or both, where they are brought to recover as follows: 

1. Upon contract, express or implied, 2. For personal injuries, except libel, slander, criminal conversation or seduction, 3. For libel or slander, 4. For injuries to real property, 5. Real property in ejectment, with or without damages for the withholding thereof, 6. For injuries to personal property, 7. Chattels, with or without damages for the taking or detention thereof, 8. Against a trustee, by virtue of a contract, or by operation of law, 9. Upon claims arising out of the same transaction, or transactions connected with the same subject of action, whether or not included within one or more of the other subdivisions of this section, 10. For penalties incurred under the conservation law, 11. For penalties incurred under the agricultural law, 12. For penalties incurred under the public health law. 

Limitations on C.P.A. 258 (Old Section): 

There were three limitations contained in the section itself: (a) First limitation: The face of the complaint must show that all the causes of action united belonged to one subdivision of the section. (1) Thus two causes of action in contract are joinable. The same is true of a cause of action for false imprisonment and one for malicious prosecution (both are for personal injuries). Marks v. Townsend 97 N. Y. 590. But a cause of action for slander cannot be joined with one for false imprisonment since they fall under different subdivisions. De Wolfe v. Abraham, 151 N. Y. 186. (2′) Subdivision "9": Even if the causes of action do not fall within one of the other subdivisions, they are properly joinable, nevertheless, if they arise "out of the same transaction, or transactions connected with the same subject of action" (Subd. 9). 

Judicial interpretation of Subd. 9 was neither uniform nor satisfactory, due no doubt, to the absence of some definite test. If one automobile accident caused by D's negligence, gives rise to a cause of action for personal injuries, another, for property damages, and still another for medical expenses and loss of services, P may properly join under C.P.A. 209, and the causes of action, since they arise out of the same incident, are properly joinable under subd. 9. McInerney v. Main 81 N. Y. S. 539. But unity

APPENDIX 

of time is not the test. For example, where D assaults P and at the same time slanders him, the causes of action are not joinable under subd. 9, since the assault is one transaction independent of the slander which is another. Paul v. Ford, 102 N. Y. S. 359; 

Doyle v. American Wringer Co., 69 N. Y. S. 952. (But where

D assaults P and at the same time trespasses with respect to P's property, in an effort to get from P, property claimed by D, the assault and trespass were held properly joined under subd. 9. 

Doyle v. American Wringer Co., 69 N. Y. S. 952.) Again where

D sells P a gun which explodes and injures P, the latter cannot join a cause of action for breach of warranty and one, in n tort, for negligence in marketing a defectively constructed gun, since there is no necessary connection between the warranty and the defective construction. Reed v. Livermore, 91 N. Y. S. 986. (Cf. Turner v. Edison Storage Battery Co., 248 N. Y. 73, holding that where P buys a battery from A, defectively manufactured by B, in an action to recover damages for personal injuries from B, P cannot be compelled to elect between two causes of action joined in his complaint, one for breach of warranty and one for negligence, since the breach of warranty cause of action would not lie against the manufacturer for lack of privity and P "should not be compelled to elect between a cause of action and no cause of action"). In Ader v. Blau, 241 N. Y. 7, a cause of action against one D for wrongful death by reason of the negligent maintenance of a picket fence was held improperly joined with a cause of action against another D for causing intestate's death by reason of unskillful, surgical treatment, the court pointing out the "object" of the action was to recover damages for the wrongful death, but that the "subject" of the action as against each D was different and hence under subd. 9 the causes of action were not joinable. 

(b) Second Limitation: The causes of action united must be con sistent with each other. (1′) A cause of action for breach of warranty cannot be joined with a cause of action for recission, because of inconsistency. Seggerman Bros. Inc., v. Rosenberg Bros. & Co., 217 A. D. 7 (the first regards the contract as continuing, the other seeks its destruction). (2′) In order to prevent non-suit for failure of proof P may repeat his cause of action to recover the value of goods sold or services rendered, alleging, in one count, an agreed value, and in a second "quantum meruit" (reasonable value). Blank v. Hartshorn, 37

Hun (N. Y.) 101. In such case P cannot be compelled to elect before trial as to the counts, since proof of one or the other would entitle P to recover. Rubin v. Cohen, 113 N. Y. S. 843; 

Hermann v. Cluett, Peabody & Co., Inc., 215 N. Y. S. 220. (c) Third Limitation: The causes of action united must not require different places of trial.

APPENDIX 

309 

Counterclaims (The Old Law): 

The old C.P.A. 266 provided that the counterclaim must diminish or defeat plaintiff's recovery and must be for one of the following causes of action: (1) A cause of action arising out of the contract, or transaction, set forth in the complaint as the foundation of P's claim, or connected with the subject of the action. (a) The cause of action counterclaimed need not have existed at the commencement of the action, if the facts which give rise to it occurred up to the time of the service of the answer, the counterclaim may assert those facts. C.P.A. 266. (b) Under the above subdivision, a counterclaim in contract is interposable against a tort cause of action, and, vice-versa, if they both arise out of the same transaction. Kelly v. Wesbter, 128 N. Y. S. 58. (c) D, in an action for rent under a lease, may counterclaim for a wrongful eviction before the expiration of the lease, (the cause of action counterclaimed arises out of the contract). Ludlow v. McCarthy, 38 

N. Y. S. 1075. (d) Where P sues D for breach of a contract of employment, D may counterclaim a cause of action based on P's fraudulent representations inducing D to purchase the assets of a corporation and to employ P under the contract in suit (the counterclaim arises out of the same "transaction"). Laska v. Harris, 215 N. Y. 554. (e) Where P sues D, a female, for breach of promise to marry, D may counterclaim for money damages because of P's fraudulent representations in inducing D to make the promise (the fraud and the promise being part of the same "transaction"). Gross v. Hochstim, 130 N. Y. 

S. 315. (f) But a counterclaim based on fraud and deceit in inducing D to sell goods on credit may not be interposed in an action for malicious prosecution, even where it appears that the fraud and deceit was the cause for the false arrest, since the "transaction" is not the same. 

Rothschild v. Whitman, 132 N. Y. 472, (where the court said: “A groundless and malicious prosecution is caused by the act of commencing the action, not by the reasons given for commencing it. An illegal arrest, such as that in question, is caused by the issuing and service of the order of arrest, not by the facts recited therein. There is no relation of cause and effect between an illegal act, or the determination to do one, and the excuse alleged for doing it.") (g) In an action for the conversion of P's cordwood, D may properly counterclaim for damages sustained by reason of P severing the wood from property mortgaged to D and thus impairing D's security (the counterclaim is connected with the "subject" of P's action). Carpenter v. Ins. Co., 93 N. Y. 552.

APPENDIX 

(h) Where P sues to recover the purchase price of goods sold, D cannot counterclaim for damages resulting from the levying by P, in the action, of a warrant of attachment which was thereafter vacated but which impaired D's credit, since, the counterclaim is not based on the contract or transaction set out in P's complaint nor connected with the "subject" of P's action. Washington Trading Corp. v. Cornell, 244 

A. D. 38. (2) In an action on a contract, any other cause of action on contract existing at the commencement of the action. C.P.A. 266, Hull v. Hull, 225 N. Y. 342. (a) If P, an agent, converts property of D, his principal, which he had agreed to account for, P may waive the tort, and in an action brought by P under the contract of agency, counterclaim for breach of contract. Cort v. Stewart, 50 N. Y. 17. See also Manhattan Egg Co. v. Seaboard Co., 242 N. Y. S. 189. 

(3) Separate Judgment: 

The old C.P.A. further provided that a counterclaim, otherwise proper, was not interposable, if a separate judgment on the counterclaim could not be entered. (a) A sues B and C on a joint obligation at law. B may not counterclaim a cause of action owned by himself, individually, since no separate judgment could be entered. Hunter v. Booth, 84 A. D. 585. (See Burns v. Lopez, 256 N. Y. 123; Fox Chase Inc. v. Handel, 250 N. Y. S. 416.) (b) But if A sues B and C on a several liability, B could counterclaim a cause of action owned by him, individually, (American Guild v. Damon, 186 N. Y. 360), but not a cause of action owned by B jointly (with X).

Hopkins v. Lane, 87 N. Y. 501, (holding that D, one of three purchasers, cannot set up a counterclaim for breach of a warranty made by P to the purchasers jointly against P's cause of action on D's individual note for the purchase price.
